                                 What Follows from What:
            Learning Inference Rules from Imitation, Coherence, and the World

                                          Claude (Anthropic)∗

                                             October 3, 2026



                                                 Abstract
      Can a learner that acquires inference rules come to reason soundly, and with what justification?
      We consider a learner that imitates human inferences, is then conditioned on coherence (no
      good arguments for both P and ¬P ), and receives occasional feedback from the world. We
      answer this precisely, for formal mathematics, informal mathematics, and the checking of
      physics solutions.
         The answer is a division of labour among the learning signals, each with provable strengths
      and a provable residue.
         • Search forces worst-case soundness. Accuracy on the distribution of human
           steps says nothing about a reasoner that searches: one tonk-like rule of arbitrarily
           small probability mass makes everything derivable. Cautious acceptance over schematic
           hypotheses is sound against every adaptive prover. Its price is a combinatorial dimension
           of the hypothesis class.
         • Imitation identifies rules. Positive examples identify a schematic calculus exactly,
           at coupon-collector rates, and this generalizes to derivations of any size. But systematic
           human errors are indistinguishable from rules.
         • Coherence is eliminative negative data. Its power is exactly Post-completeness. It
           pins down classical logic and complete theories, is blind for intuitionistic logic, and is
           Popperian for arithmetic. Carnap’s categoricity problem turns out to be Gold’s problem
           in the dual space of valuations.
         • Formal mathematics. We prove an end-to-end theorem for a two-tier learner (audit,
           then assert; bold conjectures in a sandbox), and show that each ingredient is necessary.
         • Informal mathematics. We model it as a latent formalization. A bounded-gap
           verifier is sound against adaptive provers and identifies validity exactly up to informal-
           validity equivalence. Counterexample objects are exponentially more informative than
           paradoxes.
         • Physics. Contexts are given a filter semantics in which eternalism is provably impossi-
           ble. Exports from idealized contexts need certificates of finite information radius. A
           structured-solution checker is sound against adversarial solvers relative to a judgment
           layer that we prove ineliminable.
      We also settle two questions raised in the notes that motivated this work: a steeper simplicity
      penalty as a route from imitation to norms, and a “philosophical completeness theorem”.
          Every main result passed adversarial verification. Core discrete results are machine-checked
      in Lean 4, and experiments illustrate the phenomena. We argue that principled justification
      beyond proof has a definite shape: proof within contexts, certified bridges between them, and
∗
    Prepared in response to questions by Kaarel Hänni. Draft; authorship and publication to be decided by him.

      an explicit, minimized, ineliminable judgment layer, with learning signals whose one-sided
      limits are known.

                       7
1     Introduction
1.1    The question
Consider a learning process that learns which inferences are valid, and thereby learns to make
valid inferences. The motivating design has six parts.
    • It starts by imitating human inferences. These are the steps of formal or informal mathe-
      matical proofs, or of physics olympiad solutions.
    • It treats an argument as valid when it is a sequence of valid inferences.
    • It conditions the learner on coherence: it should not have good arguments for both P and
      ¬P .
    • It also conditions the learner on practical success. Occasionally the world reports a truth
      value.
    • On an inferentialist picture of meaning (Brandom, 1994), learning which inferences are
      valid is roughly learning what the words mean.
    • Arguments happen in contexts. A physics problem says “assume air pressure is 0”, and
      from background knowledge one can prove that it is not. Arguments for P and for ¬P in
      such contexts should not be penalized, yet some coherence constraint is clearly needed.
Does anything of this shape work, even with unlimited compute? Does it give rise to a reasoner
that derives many correct conclusions? Can one prove that it does? The bar rises in three steps:
    • a setup that provably works for formal mathematics;
    • one that would have worked for mathematics before we knew how to formalize proofs;
    • a principled system for checking physics olympiad solutions.
Behind all of these sits a broader question: what does principled justification look like beyond
mathematical proof?
    This report answers these questions as precisely as we could. The main results are summarized
below and collected in Table 1. Each is stated as a theorem in the body, with a full proof in the
appendix. Most theorems are paired with an impossibility result showing that its hypotheses
cannot be dropped.

1.2    The answers in brief
 (1) Search makes soundness a worst-case property (Section 3).

         • A reasoner searches for derivations whose steps its learned checker accepts. It is sound
           iff every accepted step is derivable (Lemma 3.1). One accepted bad step is fatal.
         • For every distribution over steps and every ε > 0, a single tonk-like schema with mass
           < ε makes every formula derivable (Theorem 3.2). Every PAC learner can be modified
           to stay PAC while producing outputs that trivialize the reasoner (Corollary 3.3).
         • Majority-vote ensembles of individually coherent verifiers can derive ⊥ forever without
           any member being refuted (Theorem 3.12).




                                                 8
      • Accuracy on human steps, which is what step-level reward models optimize, is therefore
        the wrong target. An experiment shows a step classifier with in-distribution AUC 0.995
        that a search procedure exploits to prove most of a set of false equations (Section 12).

(2) Cautious acceptance is the canonical sound verifier, and structure makes it
    affordable (Section 4).

      • Accepting exactly what every surviving hypothesis accepts is sound against every
        adaptive prover. It is also the largest sound policy (Theorem 4.2).
      • Its worst-case cost in human escalations is exactly a positive-elasticity dimension of
        the class (Theorem 4.4).
      • For rule schemas learned by anti-unification this cost is linear in step size. Unstructured
        classes cost exponentially (Table 4).
      • A Bayesian version is time-uniformly sound via Ville’s inequality (Theorem 4.15).

(3) Imitation identifies rules but not norms (Section 5).

      • Positive examples identify a schematic calculus exactly, at coupon-collector rates
        (Theorem 5.7).
      • Exact identification generalizes to derivations of any size (Corollary 5.11). This is a
        precise sense in which finite usage fixes inferential role for unbounded use.
      • Systematic human errors are indistinguishable from rules at every rate (Corollary 5.20).

(4) Coherence is eliminative negative data, and its power is exactly Post-completeness
    (Section 6).

      • A derivation of ⊥ from a context certified consistent is a negative bag: some step
        in it is invalid. Only bold learners ever receive this signal, with at most log2 (1/w∗ )
        detections (Theorem 6.5).
      • Every structural extension of classical consequence is classical or trivial. So one
        coherence datum pins down classical logic with no simplicity bias (Theorems 6.13
        and 6.15, machine-checked).
      • Coherence is blind among intermediate logics (Theorem 6.17). In arithmetic it is
        Popperian, detecting Π1 truth but not Σ1 truth, and runs out at Σ2 (Theorem 6.22).
      • Carnap’s categoricity problem is Gold’s problem in the dual space of valuations.
        Single-conclusion data cannot fix the classical meanings, while finitely many bilateral
        data plus one coherence datum can (Theorems 6.27 and 6.29).
      • What survives everything is the set of coherent uniform alternatives: a precise
        “Kripkensteinian residue” (Theorem 6.38).

(5) An end-to-end theorem for formal mathematics (Section 7).

      • The naive two-tier design (cautious assertion plus a bold red team) is provably unsound,
        because practice contains fallacies (Proposition 7.5).
      • The corrected learner audits, then asserts. It assigns blame through the union of
        minimal conflicts, which is Reiter’s duality (Lemma 7.8), and states soundness relative
        to a refutation depth.

                                               9
     • It is sound against every adaptive prover and red team, and it converges to the target
       modulo an explicit residue (Theorem 7.14).
     • Each ingredient is necessary (Table 9).
     • For classical propositional logic and for complete decidable theories the residue is
       empty.

(6) Informal mathematics before formalization (Section 10).

     • We model informal practice as the shadow of an unknown latent formalization. A
       bounded-gap conservative verifier is sound against adaptive provers. It must check
       links between contexts as well as steps, since Cauchy’s sum theorem is an equivocation
       (Proposition 10.7).
     • Validity is identified exactly up to informal-validity equivalence, and no finer. On-
       tology is not identifiable: standard and nonstandard reals agree on the practice
       (Theorem 10.15 and Proposition 10.17).
     • Counterexample objects need at most log2 |H| corrections, whereas paradoxes can
       need |H| − 1; the sorites is the tight case (Theorems 10.22 and 10.23).
     • The history Frege→Russell→Zermelo comes out as constrained repair selected by
       coverage of practice (Theorem 10.32).
     • A robust-core theorem explains why informal mathematics survived formalization
       (Theorem 10.35).

(7) Contexts and the principled checking of physics (Section 11).

     • Contexts denote filters of models from a declared deformation family. Suppositions
       are exact discharge. Idealizations may contradict the background.
     • Eternalism is provably impossible (Theorem 11.8). Coherence belongs on the root and
       the anchored contexts only (Theorem 11.12).
     • Designating “idealization plus full background” as coherent forces a globally para-
       consistent logic (Proposition 11.10). This is a formal version of the air-pressure
       worry.
     • Exporting a conclusion from an idealization needs information of finite radius about
       the de-idealized family: there is no free export (Theorem 11.21). Context choice is an
       attack surface (Theorem 11.27).
     • A Structured Physics Solution checker is sound against adversarial solvers relative
       to a judgment layer (Theorem 11.37). That layer, consisting of the reading of the
       problem and the adequacy of the top model, is ineliminable (Theorem 11.38).
     • “True in the context at hand” becomes supertruth over admissible completions (Theo-
       rem 11.39).
     • The approach is worked through a EuPhO problem end to end (Section 11.7).

(8) Two questions from the motivating notes.

     • A steeper simplicity penalty on function induction with private randomness does
       produce the hoped-for kink at the simple model (Theorem 8.6). But selection is a


                                           10
           rate threshold. Rare valid rules are lost and cheap fallacies kept, and the threshold
           cannot be calibrated from imitation data (Section 8).
        • The “philosophical completeness theorem” (“a mode of talking is coherent iff there is
          something it could be talking about”) holds in a generalized weak sense, for positions,
          credences and context systems. It fails in the intended sense. Bounded-arity coherence
          constraints of the kind used in consistency-based probing do not characterize coherence
          (Section 9).

   Three conclusions sit on top of these results (Section 13):
  • every learning signal is one-sided;
  • principled justification is a set of signals that jointly cover every direction of error, together
    with a declared residue;
  • mathematical and physical justification differ in degree, measured by the size of an explicit
    judgment layer, not in kind.

 Success criterion             Status
 A setup that provably         Yes, with explicit scope. The two-tier learner (Theorem 7.14) is
 works for formal              sound and exactly convergent for classical propositional logic and
 mathematics                   complete decidable theories (Corollaries 7.25 and 7.28). It is Popperian
                               for arithmetic, with sharp impossibility results beyond Σ1
                               (Theorem 7.29).
 A setup that would have       Yes, relative to realizability. A bounded-gap verifier over latent
 worked before formalization   formalizations is sound and identifies validity exactly up to
                               informal-validity equivalence (Section 10). Open: affordable soundness
                               when the learner must invent the formal language.
 A principled system for       Yes, as a checker with a declared residue. It is sound against
 checking physics olympiad     adversarial solvers relative to the reading and the top model
 solutions                     (Theorem 11.37). That residue is provably ineliminable (Theorem 11.38).
                               We deliberately built a checker, not a solver.

         Table 1: The three success criteria, and what this report establishes for each.


1.3   How the results were established
The work was carried out by many cooperating agent instances, coordinated by one orchestrating
instance. It proceeded in four stages.
  • Literature memos on eleven strands.
  • Seven theory threads, which developed the mathematics.
  • Adversarial verification. Every main result was attacked by two or three independent
    referees instructed to refute it, with brute-force computation on small cases where possible.
    The results were then repaired, and the repaired items were re-verified by fresh referees.
  • Drafting, followed by a fidelity check of each section against its verified source.
In the first verification round the referees found four false statements and about twenty significant
gaps. Each was repaired, weakened or retracted. One theorem needed a second repair round. The


                                                  11
full records are in research/verification/ of the accompanying repository, and the repaired
statements are the ones used here.
    A core of discrete results is machine-checked in Lean 4 with Mathlib (Appendix K). It covers
about fifty paper results in 14,600 lines with no sorry. Among them are:
  • the Post-completeness and coherence theorems;
  • Carnap’s problem in learning form, with the bilateral tell-tale;
  • tonk beyond the horizon, the doctrinal paradox and the halving bounds;
  • Reiter’s blame duality, and the separation between paradoxes and objects;
  • bilateral completeness and the arity counterexample;
  • the rate threshold and the rate-inversion counterexample;
  • no free export from samples, and the Lipschitz certifier.
Experiments in a small symbolic toolkit illustrate the phenomena (Section 12). They concern
school algebra with real systematic errors, propositional natural deduction, adversarial proof
search against learned verifiers, and a prototype physics checker.
    We have tried to mark scope honestly:
  • Many results are “trivial once set up”. Their contribution is the setup, and we say so.
  • Several ingredients are classical, and we credit them. Examples are Gold, Angluin, Plotkin,
    Natarajan, Rivest–Sloan, KWIK, Ville and the prior–posterior-ratio martingale, Post,
    Glivenko, Carnap and the bilateralists, chunk-and-permeate, information-based complexity,
    de Finetti and Gaifman.
  • The soundness guarantees are relative to stated assumptions, chiefly realizability of the
    target in a structured hypothesis class and truthful designation of contexts. Computational
    cost is mostly not addressed.

1.4   Reader’s guide
The report is long because it treats three domains and two side questions in full. Readers can
take the following routes.
  • Core argument: Sections 2 to 7, about the formal case, then Section 13.
  • Informal mathematics and the history of rigor: Section 10, after Section 2.
  • Contexts, idealization and physics checking: Section 11. It is largely self-contained
    given Section 2.
  • Learning theory of imitation and simplicity: Section 8.
  • Logic and the semantics of coherence: Section 9.
  • Experiments and code: Section 12.
Section 14 collects open problems. Proofs are in the appendices, one per section.




                                              12
2     Setting: judgments, steps, rules, provers, and three channels
This section fixes the vocabulary of the paper. Nothing in it is deep; the point is a set-up in which
the later theorems are short. Three questions are kept apart from the start: what is learned (a
step set, a consequence relation, or a set of valuations; Section 2.3), from what (three channels
of different logical form; Section 2.5), and against whom (an adaptive prover that searches for
wrongly accepted steps; Section 2.6). Two running examples, propositional natural deduction
and school algebra (Section 2.4), recur throughout.

2.1    Judgments, steps and closure
Fm is the set of formulas over atoms p, q, r, p0 , p1 , . . . with ¬, ∧, ∨, →, ⊥, ⊤; first-order languages
are used for arithmetic and physics. A context Γ is a finite set of formulas.
Definition 2.1 (Judgments, positions). [T1 §1.1; T2 §1] A judgment is Γ ⊢ φ (single conclusion) or
Γ ⊢ ∆ (multiple conclusion, ∆ finite); J denotes a countable set of judgments. Contexts are part
of judgments, so hypothetical reasoning, and “assume the air pressure is 0”, lives inside the step
language. A position [Γ : ∆] asserts all of Γ and denies all of ∆. Following Restall (2005), Γ ⊢ ∆
says that the position [Γ : ∆] is out of bounds; a position is coherent if it is in bounds. When
⊥ is present it counts as always denied, so [Γ : ∅] is coherent iff Γ ⊬ ⊥. Without ⊥, “coherent”
means non-trivial (Γ does not entail every formula; for a structural relation, equivalently Γ ⊬ q
for an atom q not occurring in Γ).
    Restall’s reading gives inference data and coherence data opposite polarity: an inference
says a position is out of bounds, a coherence datum certifies that one is in bounds. This is
the bilateral picture of Smiley (1996) and Rumfitt (2000) and the multiple-conclusion logic
of Shoesmith and Smiley (1978), anticipated in Carnap’s “full formalization” (Carnap, 1943).
Section 6 uses this polarity to recast Carnap’s categoricity problem as a learning problem in the
dual (valuation) space.
Definition 2.2 (Steps, rule sets, closure). [T1 §1.1] A step P ▷ j has a finite set P ⊆ J of
premises and a conclusion j ∈ J; S is the set of steps, and a rule set (step set) is any R ⊆ S.
An R-derivation π of j from B ⊆ J is a finite tree with root j whose nodes are leaves in B or
conclusions of steps P ▷ j ′ ∈ R whose premises P label their children (P = ∅ allowed); Steps(π)
is the set of steps used. The closure ClR (B) is the least X ⊇ B such that P ▷ j ∈ R and P ⊆ X
imply j ∈ X; the derived steps are Sound(R) = {P ▷ j : j ∈ ClR (P )}. The target is an unknown
rule set R∗ : its members are the valid steps, and the members of Sound(R∗ ) the derivable ones.
Lemma 2.3 (Closure facts; trivial). [T1 §1.1] (a) ClR (B) is the set of judgments with an R-
derivation from B; ClR is extensive, monotone, idempotent and finitary, and monotone in R. (b)
R ⊆ Sound(R) and ClSound(R) = ClR ; hence Sound(R) is the largest step set A with ClA = ClR .
    Proof in Appendix A.
    Two formats recur. In the formula format (Hilbert style) J = Fm and a step is φ1 , . . . , φk ▷ ψ.
In the sequent format (natural deduction in sequent style; Gentzen 1935; Prawitz 1965) judgments
are sequents and discharge is an ordinary step, e.g. →I is {Γ, φ ⊢ ψ} ▷ Γ ⊢ φ → ψ. A rule
set induces a relation on sets of formulas: A ⊢R φ iff φ ∈ ClR (A) (formula format), or iff
(A0 ⊢ φ) ∈ ClR (∅) for a finite A0 ⊆ A (sequent format). In the formula format ⊢R is a finitary
consequence relation by Lemma 2.3(a): arguments chain, so cut is built in; for the natural-
deduction calculi below, weakening and cut are admissible and the same holds. R is A-coherent
if A ⊬R ⊥.

                                                   13
Reading. Section 3 shows that a verifier is safe under derivations of every length iff everything
it accepts lies in Sound(R∗ ) (T1 Lemma 1.1). So “learning which inferences are valid” has a
sharp target: a set between R∗ and Sound(R∗ ).

2.2    Schemas and anti-unification
Steps are encoded as ground terms over a first-order signature Ω, e.g. s(and(p0 , p1 ), p0 ) for an
∧E step; object-language atoms are constants of Ω.

Definition 2.4 (Schemas). [T1 §§1.3, 5; T7 §1.1] A schema σ is a term with metavariables x, y, A,
B, . . . , and inst(σ) is its set of ground instances. σ ⪰ τ (σ is at least as general) iff τ = σθ
for a substitution θ; σ ≡ τ iff both (equality up to renaming). σ is pure if no atom constant
occurs in it; its purification σ ◦ replaces distinct atoms by distinct fresh metavariables. A guarded
schema (σ, Ψ) carries guards Ψ ⊆ Φ, a fixed finite family of decidable predicates on ground steps
(freshness; “the facts entail a ̸= 0”), and inst(σ, Ψ) is the set of instances of σ satisfying every
                                                                               S
guard in Ψ. A calculus is a finite set Σ of (guarded) schemas, with RΣ = σ∈Σ inst(σ); a tagged
step (i, s) records the schema i that it cites.

   Anti-unification is the learning algorithm of this paper. For a tuple ū = (u1 , . . . , un ) of
ground terms, A(ū) = f (A(ū1 ), . . . , A(ūr )) if all uj have root f of arity r (ūk is the tuple of k-th
arguments); otherwise A(ū) = zū , a metavariable indexed by the tuple, so equal tuples get equal
metavariables.

Theorem 2.5 (Least general generalization; Plotkin 1970; Reynolds 1970). [T1 §1.3] (known)
Every nonempty finite set P = {t1 , . . . , tn } of ground terms has a least general generalization
lgg(P ), a schema with P ⊆ inst(lgg(P )) and lgg(P ) ⪯ τ whenever P ⊆ inst(τ ). It is unique
up to renaming and computed by A(t1 , . . . , tn ). Hence inst(lgg(P )) = {inst(τ ) : P ⊆ inst(τ )}:
                                                                          T

anti-unification is the closure algorithm for single-schema instance sets.

    For a schema t let µ(t) := |t| − |vars(t)|, where |t| counts symbol occurrences (metavariables
included) and vars(t) is the set of distinct metavariables; so µ ≥ 0, µ(x) = 0, and µ(t) = |t| for
ground t.

Lemma 2.6 (Rank). [T1 Lemma 1.2] If τ = σθ then µ(τ ) ≥ µ(σ), with equality iff θ restricted to
vars(σ) is a renaming. Hence every strictly increasing generalization chain τ = σ0 ≺ σ1 ≺ · · · ≺
σm has m ≤ µ(τ ) − µ(σm ) ≤ µ(τ ).

Lemma 2.7 (When the lgg recovers the schema). [T1 Lemma 1.3] Let tj = σθj (1 ≤ j ≤ n) be
ground instances of σ. Then lgg(t1 , . . . , tn ) ⪯ σ, with ≡ iff both witness events hold: (R) for
                                                                                            ̸ y there is
each metavariable x of σ, the roots of θ1 x, . . . , θn x are not all equal; (D) for each x =
j with θj x ̸= θj y.

Proof idea. Substituting θx for the occurrences of x adds more symbols than distinct metavariables
unless θx is a metavariable, which gives the rank lemma (folklore in the Plotkin–Reynolds lattice).
Anti-unifying the tj copies σ and puts A(θ1 x, . . . , θn x) at x; this is a metavariable iff (R) holds,
and two of them coincide iff (D) fails. Full proofs in Appendix A. By the rank lemma, ascending
generalization chains are finite, so lggs exist for infinite P as well. The positive-data theory of
Sections 4 and 5 runs on counting witness events.



                                                     14
Lemma 2.8 (Guarded closure; trivial). [T1 §5, side conditions] For nonempty finite P ⊆ S let
ΨP = {ϕ ∈ Φ : ϕ ≡ 1 on P }. Then inst(lgg(P ), ΨP ) is the least set of the form inst(σ, Ψ)
containing P .

    So a learned guard is the strongest guard satisfied by every datum, and one datum that omits
a side condition deletes it (Example 2.12). Proof in Appendix A.

2.3    Hypothesis classes
Definition 2.9 (Hypothesis classes). [T2 §1, §4.1; T1 §1.2, §3] Hypotheses come in three guises.
(i) Step sets H ⊆ 2S : e.g. single schemas H1 = {inst(σ)} ∪ {∅}, tagged calculi Hktag = { i≤k {i} ×
                                                                                          S

inst(σi )}, untagged unions Hk = { l≤k inst(τl )}, finite unstructured classes. A learned step scorer
                                     S

(a process reward model) is a step set, and need not be closed under chaining. (ii) Consequence
relations ⊢ (finitary, reflexive, monotone, cut-closed), equivalently finitary closure operators C;
⊢ is structural if Γ ⊢ φ implies sΓ ⊢ sφ for every substitution s of formulas for atoms (Łoś
and Suszko, 1958; Wójcicki, 1988). Each R induces ⊢R ; many R induce the same relation.
(iii) Meanings as sets of valuations: a set V of admissible valuations v : Fm → {0, 1} (not
necessarily compositional), with Γ |=m   V ∆ iff no v ∈ V has v[Γ] = 1 and v[∆] = 0; dually,
Val(S ) is the set of valuations violating no sequent in S ′ . The version space of data D is
       ′

VS(D) = {h ∈ H : h consistent with D} (= {h : D ⊆ h} for positive data). H is realizable if it
contains the target; w is a prior and w∗ = w(R∗ ).

    Structurality is uniformity. If σ is pure, inst(σ) is closed under substitutions s for atoms,
since the atoms of σθ come from θ: s(σθ) = σθ′ with θ′ x = s(θx). So a learner that generalizes
human steps to pure schemas is structural. A schema that mentions an atom is not, and can be
unsound without trivializing: added to a complete calculus for CPC, x ∨ p0 ▷ x derives ¬p0 but
stays inside Cn(¬p0 ) (T1 Prop. 2.3, scope remark). Purification restores the link (Section 3).
    Inference data are negative data about valuations. The sequent Γ ⊢ ∆ excludes exactly the
valuations realizing [Γ : ∆]; a coherence certification or a world report is positive data about
valuations (T2 §4.1). Imitation and coherence inform the learner from opposite sides, and
Section 6 makes this precise as “Carnap’s problem is Gold’s problem in the dual space”.

2.4    Practice and the running examples
Definition 2.10 (Practice). [T7 Defs 1.1–1.2; T1 §6] The target is a finite calculus Σ∗ (the genuine
rules), with R∗ = RΣ∗ . The systematic fallacies are a finite set F of schemas, F ∩ Σ∗ = ∅, each
with an instance outside Sound(R∗ ). The practice is ΣP = Σ∗ ⊔ F , with RP = R∗ ∪ RF . Human
data are i.i.d. tagged steps: tag i ∈ ΣP has probability πi , and given i the step is an instance of i
with probability 1 − ai and otherwise an arbitrary step outside inst(i) tagged i (sporadic noise).
Alternatively, the data are a text, an enumeration of RP (Gold, 1967).

    Fallacy tags are idealized as latent but distinct: a student who affirms the consequent “cites”
a rule that is in fact affirming the consequent. If fallacies are cited under a genuine rule’s name,
the per-tag class becomes Hk (Sections 5 and 7).

Example 2.11 (Propositional natural deduction). Σ∗ is a natural-deduction calculus for CPC
(∧I/E, ∨I/E, →I, →E = modus ponens (MP), ¬I/E, ex falso, double negation). In the formula
format MP is φ, φ → ψ ▷ ψ. A student sometimes affirms the consequent, AC : ψ, φ → ψ ▷ φ;
so F = {AC}. The data are steps such as p ∧ q ▷ p tagged ∧E. The two instances p ∧ q ▷ p and

                                                 15
(r → p) ∧ p ▷ r → p already satisfy (R) and (D), so their lgg is A ∧ B ▷ A (Lemma 2.7). (In
the sequent format contexts are sets, and anti-unification modulo set structure is finitary rather
than unitary; we flag this where it matters.)

Example 2.12 (School algebra). Judgments are equations s = t between terms built from +, −,
              √
·, /, powers,    , numerals and variables, under finite sets of facts such as x ̸= 0. Terms denote
partial functions (a/0 is undefined), and s = t is true under the facts iff s, t are Kleene-equal
(both undefined, or both defined and equal) at every real assignment satisfying them. Steps are
instances of rewrite schemas such as (a + b)2 ▷ a2 + 2ab + b2 and a/a ▷ 1 with guard a =  ̸ 0, plus
trusted equational steps (symmetry, transitivity, congruence) and trusted numeral evaluation.
The student commits the freshman’s dream (a + b)2 ▷ a2 + b2 , an invalid schema, and unguarded
cancellation a/a ▷ 1, a genuine rule used without its guard (the instance x/x ▷ 1 fails at x = 0).
If the student cites “cancellation” for both uses, one datum such as (y + 1)/(y + 1) ▷ 1 under no
facts drops the guard from the learned rule (Lemma 2.8); with distinct latent tags the unguarded
rule is learned as a schema of its own. Either way, positive data learn it as a rule.

   The algebra example is experiment A and the natural-deduction example is experiment B of
Section 12.

2.5    Three channels
Definition 2.13 (The channels). [T1 §1.2; T2 §1, §2.1–2.2; T7 Defs 1.2–1.4; L3 Lemma A]
(P) Practice/imitation delivers human steps X1 , X2 , . . . , possibly tagged with rule names,
    possibly with sporadic noise (Definition 2.10). A noise-free datum lies in RP ; no datum
    says that a step is invalid.
(C) Coherence supplies a family A of designated contexts, finite premise sets certified coherent
    for the target (A ⊬R∗ ⊥), or, bilaterally, designated positions [Γ : ∆] certified in bounds
    (⊥ ∈/ ClR∗ (Γ) and ∆ ∩ ClR∗ (Γ) = ∅). By default A is fixed and known, independently
    of the target. A hypothesis h is A-coherent if A ⊬h ⊥ for all A ∈ A. A negative bag is
    Steps(π) \ Ttrust for an argument π from some A ∈ A to ⊥ (trusted steps Ttrust as in (W));
    it contains an invalid step, or chaining would give ⊥ ∈ ClR∗ ∪Ttrust (A), which designation
    excludes (for Ttrust ̸= ∅ via (WS), Lemma 2.15).
(W) The world is an evaluable fragment JW ⊆ J with truth function W : JW → {0, 1}, plus
    optional trusted steps Ttrust . Its kinds: (W1) truth values on a fragment (closed propositional
    formulas, ∆0 sentences, a decision procedure); (W2) counterexample objects, structures
    in which every line of a given argument can be evaluated; (W3) computation with fresh
    randomness, e.g. testing a polynomial identity at a point drawn after the claim is committed;
    (W4) simulation, a validated numerical solution of a less idealized model (Section 11).

    Coherence is enforced only on designated contexts: deriving ⊥ from an undesignated supposi-
tion is a reductio and is never penalized. A negative bag is a multiple-instance label (Dietterich
et al., 1997), “not all of these steps are valid”; it collapses to an ordinary negative example for a
learner that can test A ▷ ⊥ directly against cut-closed hypotheses, so the bag structure matters
for step verifiers that are not themselves cut-closed. Designations that are data about the target
carry more information (Section 6). Objects (W2) turn bags into singletons: if a structure makes
the leaves of an argument true and its conclusion false, then starting at the conclusion and
repeatedly moving to a false premise ends at a step with true premises and a false conclusion,

                                                   16
               (P) practice                   (C) coherence                   (W) world
datum          “this step is used”            an argument from a desig-       a truth value; an object; a
                                              nated context to ⊥              random test; a simulation
logical form   positive instance              negative bag (“some step is     singleton blame with objects;
                                              invalid”)                       derivation-level otherwise
blind to       systematic errors              errors coherent with the tar-   what lies outside the evalu-
                                              get                             able fragment

which alone is blamed (the descent lemma, Sections 7 and 10). Fresh randomness (W3) is sound
against adaptive provers under sufficient conditions on commitment order, degree bounds and the
field, and fails when the points are drawn before the prover searches (L3 Lemma A, Section 3);
Schwartz–Zippel bounds its error (Schwartz, 1980; Zippel, 1979).

Assumption 2.14 ((WS), sound evaluation). [T7 §1.3] For each designated [Γ : ∆], the partial
evaluation with e(j) = 1 if j ∈ Γ or W (j) = 1, and e(j) = 0 if j ∈ ∆ ∪ {⊥} or W (j) = 0, is
well defined and extends to a total V : J → {0, 1} under which no step of R∗ ∪ Ttrust has true
premises and a false conclusion.

    (WS) does not require Ttrust ⊆ Sound(R∗ ): in arithmetic the target may consist of non-logical
axioms with first-order logic trusted (Section 7), and the relevant target closure is then ClR∗ ∪Ttrust .
In formal mathematics V is truth in the intended structure M: (WS) holds when designated
positions are true of M, W is truth in M on its fragment, and target and trusted steps are
M-sound. When JW ̸= ∅ we assume the empty position [∅ : ∅] is designated, so that world-only
falsifications count (Definition 2.20).

Lemma 2.15 ((WS) and coherence; trivial). [T7 §1.3] Under (WS) every designated position is
in bounds for R∗ ∪ Ttrust , hence for R∗ . Conversely, if JW = ∅ and every designated position is
in bounds for R∗ ∪ Ttrust (for instance, in bounds for R∗ with Ttrust ⊆ Sound(R∗ )), then (WS)
holds.

    Proof in Appendix A.

Example 2.16 (Example 2.11 continued). (C) Designate A1 = {q, p → q, p → ⊥}: AC gives p,
MP then gives ⊥. The bag {q, p → q ▷ p; p, p → ⊥ ▷ ⊥} does not say which step is at fault, and
the rival diagnosis “MP is the fallacy” reads → as ← (Section 7). (W2) The valuation v(p) = 0,
v(q) = 1 makes A1 true and the line p false, so descent blames AC alone. (W1) The closed
instance ⊤, ⊥ → ⊤ ▷ ⊥ of AC has true premises and a false conclusion.

Example 2.17 (Example 2.12 continued). (C) Designate the empty fact set, with ⊥ any equation
between distinct numerals. The freshman’s dream gives (1 + 1)2 = 12 + 12 , i.e. 4 = 2 after
trusted evaluation: a singleton bag. Unguarded cancellation gives 2 · (0/0) = 2 · 1 = 2 and
2 · (0/0) = (2 · 0)/0 = 0/0 = 1, so 1 = 2; this bag also contains the genuine rules a · (b/c) ▷ (a · b)/c,
a · 0 ▷ 0 (for defined a) and a · 1 ▷ a. (W2) An evaluator that observes “undefined” blames 0/0 ▷ 1
alone. (W3) The freshman’s dream is off by 2ab, so a uniformly random point of S 2 , S ⊆ Z finite,
exposes it with probability at least 1 − 2/|S|; over F2 , where it is an identity, never: the field
matters. The instance x/x ▷ 1 fails only at x = 0, which random evaluation essentially never hits;
the object x = 0 does. In experiment A, coherence without (W) removes value errors like the

                                                   17
freshman’s dream but almost never learns guards that concern only definedness (such as a ̸= 0
for 0/a ▷ 0); almost all of what survives (96.5% of the surviving schemas for N ≥ 200) is sound
for a total reading of division over C with 1/0 = 0, a coherent alternative meaning (computed;
Section 12).

Reading. The channels are the user’s three proposed signals: imitation of human inferences, a
coherence loss, and occasional feedback from the world. They are not three noisy versions of
one signal. (P) can only confirm, (C) can only condemn sets, and (W) can condemn single steps
when it supplies objects. Each later section says what one of them fixes and what it provably
cannot.

2.6   Provers, verifiers, soundness and cost
Definition 2.18 (Protocol). [T1 §1.2; T2 §2.2] In rounds t = 1, 2, . . . a prover submits qt ∈ S
and the verifier answers at ∈ {ACCEPT, REJECT, ESCALATE}; on ESCALATE an oracle returns
1[qt ∈ R∗ ] (possibly noisy), which joins the history. The acceptance region R̂t is the set of steps
accepted outright at time t; a reasoner chains them and derives ClR̂t (B). The prover may be
randomized, adaptive and computationally unbounded; it knows the target, the practice, the
verifier’s code and the history, but not the verifier’s future coins, future human data, or the
oracle’s future (fresh) noise. It models any proof search, the learner’s own included. It is honest
if all its queries are valid.

Definition 2.19 (Soundness). [T1 Def 1.2; T7 §1.5] Fix a class H of targets and, per target, a set
S † of admissible steps (default S † = R∗ ). A verifier is δ-sound if for every target and every
prover P[∃t : at = ACCEPT ∧ qt ∈   / S † ] ≤ δ (a time-uniform guarantee); deterministically (or
0-)sound if δ = 0; and uniformly δ-sound if some event G with P(G) ≥ 1 − δ, depending
only on the human data and the verifier’s coins, has no prover ever getting a step outside S †
accepted on G. Target-soundness takes S † = R∗ for the genuine Σ∗ , practice-soundness S † = RP .
(Truth-soundness, in Section 7, means deriving only truths of the intended structure.)

    Uniform δ-soundness implies δ-soundness, since every prover’s bad event lies outside G.
Asking for S † = R∗ is stronger than the condition R̂t ⊆ Sound(R∗ ) that chaining needs; the
version-space verifiers of Section 4 meet it. Soundness is relative to the target calculus, not to
truth: improving on R∗ is the job of (C) and (W). Since coherence is Π1 -complete (T2 Thm 5.3),
no computable learner is, on every practice, both eventually complete and sound at all times
relative to the full-depth residue of fallacies: some uniformly computable family of practices
defeats it (T7 Thm 5.7, Section 7). Soundness is then stated relative to a refutation budget.

Definition 2.20 (Depth-relative soundness). [T7 Defs 1.5–1.6] Assume (WS). A d-refutation of a
finite schema set B is a derivation of total size ≤ d, relative to a designated position, whose
leaves evaluate to 1, whose steps lie in RB ∪ Ttrust , and whose conclusion evaluates to 0 (⊥,
a denied judgment, or a W -false one). B is d-clean if it has none. The depth-d residue
  (d)
Fres = {τ ∈ F : Σ∗ ∪ {τ } is d-clean} decreases in d. A verifier is depth-d sound if it is (uniformly)
δ-sound for S † = R∗ ∪ RF (d) .
                           res


Definition 2.21 (Cost). [T1 Def 1.3; T2 §2.2; T7 §1.4] (a) Escalations: the number of rounds in which
a valid query is not accepted outright (in expectation, for randomized verifiers). Esc(H | D) is
the infimum over 0-sound verifiers of the supremum over targets in H containing the data D, and

                                                 18
over honest query sequences, of this number; Esc(H) = Esc(H | ∅) is the escalation dimension.
Invalid queries are not charged. (b) Detections: rounds in which an argument π from some
A ∈ A to ⊥ with Steps(π) ⊆ R̂t is exhibited; incompleteness errors: rounds presenting a valid
step outside R̂t . (c) Mind changes: rounds with R̂t+1 ̸= R̂t ; those that remove an accepted step
are retractions (Kelly, 1996). (d) Samples N (human steps) and calls to a refutation oracle that
returns a d-refutation of a queried set or reports none.

Example 2.22 (One bad step suffices). If AC is accepted, a prover derives any φ from no
premises: ⊤ and φ → ⊤ are theorems, and AC concludes φ. If unguarded cancellation is accepted,
it derives 1 = 2 (Example 2.17). A verifier whose errors are rare on the human distribution still
accepts such a step every time a prover asks for it. This is Prior’s tonk (Prior, 1960) as an attack
on a learned checker; Section 3 turns it into theorems.

Reading. This is how the set-up takes the user’s “compute is not a constraint, but it must
actually work”. The prover is unbounded and adaptive, so soundness is a worst-case property:
reliable learning (Rivest and Sloan, 1988a), perfect selective classification (El-Yaniv and Wiener,
2010a) and the “accept” side of KWIK (Li et al., 2008a), applied to steps. Its price is paid in
escalations (human time), samples, detections and oracle calls, counted separately. Average-case
accuracy is not enough (Section 3).

2.7   Contexts: a preview
Contexts let one argue inside a supposition or an idealization without being penalized for
contradicting the world. Following McCarthy (1993), write ist(c, φ) for “φ holds in context c”;
contexts form a tree with root @, the actual or intended situation (T3 §1). A supposition SUP(A)
refines its parent by A, and discharge is exact: ist(SUP(A), ψ) iff A → ψ holds in the parent (T3
Prop. 1.5); deriving ⊥ there is a reductio of A, not an incoherence. An idealization DEF deforms
named parameters of a declared model family toward an ideal value (“air pressure → 0”). It may
contradict the root, yet does not explode while it is proper: a context denotes a filter of parameter
points of the model family, truth in it is truth on a set in that filter, and its theory is classically
closed and consistent iff the filter is proper (T3 Def. 1.3, Prop. 1.4). Coherence is enforced on the
root and on contexts anchored to it (certified, or exporting through an informative bridge into
an anchored context), never on suppositions or idealizations as such; designating “idealization
plus full background” as coherent would force every structural learner to give up a classical
rule globally (T2 Prop. 7.1), so only consistent chunks are designated (Brown and Priest, 2004).
Contexts, export certificates and the physics checker are the subject of Section 11.


3     Search makes soundness a worst-case property
The architecture studied in this paper has three parts: a learned relation V̂ that says which
steps are valid, a prover that searches for derivations all of whose steps V̂ accepts, and a training
signal that starts from human steps. This section isolates the first consequence of putting search
on top of a learned checker. Once a prover searches, the only property of V̂ that bears on the
soundness of the resulting reasoner is a worst-case one: whether its acceptance region contains a
non-derivable step. Accuracy on typical steps, on human steps, or on most instances of a rule
does not bear on it, because search can seek out exactly the steps on which V̂ is wrong.



                                                  19
    None of the results below is deep, and several are trivial once set up; what they contribute is
the setup and the quantifiers. Reasoner soundness is stepwise soundness (Lemma 3.1). Arbitrarily
small average error is compatible with a reasoner that derives everything, and every PAC learner
of calculi containing three basic introduction rules has a PAC variant, with essentially the same
sample complexity, whose every output does so (Theorem 3.2 and Corollary 3.3). In classical
propositional logic every unsound pure schema added to a complete calculus trivializes the
reasoner, so errors are total but visible to coherence (Proposition 3.5 and Lemma 3.7). Simplicity
is the wrong bias for positive data (Proposition 3.9). Average-case validity survives search in
two regimes where its randomness is drawn after the rules or claims are fixed and outside the
prover’s control, and fails in a fixed world with prover-chosen instances (Lemma 3.11). Voting
ensembles of coherent verifiers can be incoherent in a way that coherence feedback cannot localize
(Theorem 3.12). Except in Section 3.5, which measures truth in a scene, “sound” means sound
relative to the human calculus R∗ (or a target consequence relation), not relative to truth; going
beyond R∗ is the business of coherence and the world (Sections 6, 7 and 11).

3.1   Reasoner soundness is stepwise soundness
We recall the step language of Section 2. J is a countable set of judgments; contexts are part of
judgments, so hypothetical reasoning (“assume air pressure is 0”) lives inside the step language.
A step Π ▷ j has a finite set Π ⊆ J of premises and a conclusion j ∈ J; S is the set of all steps.
For A ⊆ S and B ⊆ J, ClA (B) is the least X ⊇ B such that Π ⊆ X and (Π ▷ j) ∈ A imply
j ∈ X, i.e. the set of judgments with a finite A-derivation from B. The derivable steps of the
human calculus are Sound(R∗ ) = {Π ▷ j : j ∈ ClR∗ (Π)}. A deterministic verifier V̂ has an
acceptance region A = {s ∈ S : V̂ (s) = accept}, and an unbounded prover that chains accepted
steps from premises B reaches exactly ClA (B).
Lemma 3.1 (Reasoner soundness is stepwise soundness). [T1 Lemma 1.1] For every A ⊆ S,

                  ∀B ⊆ J : ClA (B) ⊆ ClR∗ (B)           ⇐⇒        A ⊆ Sound(R∗ ).
                                                                       Lean: Cl_subset_Cl_iff_subset_Sound


Proof. (⇐) Let (Π ▷ j) ∈ A with Π ⊆ ClR∗ (B). As ClR∗ is monotone and idempotent, j ∈
ClR∗ (Π) ⊆ ClR∗ (ClR∗ (B)) = ClR∗ (B). So ClR∗ (B) is a superset of B closed under A, and
ClA (B) ⊆ ClR∗ (B) by leastness. (⇒) For (Π ▷ j) ∈ A take B = Π: j ∈ ClA (Π) ⊆ ClR∗ (Π).

    The lemma is trivial once set up; its content is that the right object is the set A, not a measure
on it. There is no “small error rate” middle ground: one accepted step Π ▷ j ∈     / Sound(R∗ ) yields,
from Π, a conclusion that R∗ does not derive. The cautious verifiers of Section 4 satisfy the
stronger A ⊆ R∗ .

3.2   Average-case accuracy cannot give soundness
Fix a propositional language with atoms p0 , p1 , . . . , the constant ⊤ and connectives ∧, ∨ (others
optional); judgments are formulas. Let R∗ contain the instances of

   ⊤I : ▷⊤,       ∧I : A, B ▷ A ∧ B,        ∧E1,2 ,     ∨I1 : A ▷ A ∨ B,         ∨I2 : B ▷ A ∨ B,

and possibly of other classically sound rules. Put ⊤(1) = ⊤, ⊤(j+1) = ⊤ ∧ ⊤(j) , and consider
the pure one-metavariable schema Tj := {A ∨ ⊤(j) ▷ A : A ∈ Fm}. Composed with ∨I2 , Tj is

                                                  20
the rule “from ⊤(j) infer anything”: a tonk (Prior, 1960; Belnap, 1962) with its trigger hidden
beyond a size horizon. For a distribution Q on S and h ⊆ S write errQ (h) := Q(h △ R∗ ).

Theorem 3.2 (Tonk beyond the horizon). [T1 Thm 2.1]
  (i) Every formula C lies in ClR∗ ∪Tj (∅), by a derivation of j + 2 steps of which only the last
      uses Tj . For non-tautologous C that step is unsound, hence not in Sound(R∗ ).
 (ii) For every probability distribution Q on S (over valid steps, invalid steps, or both), Q(Tj ) → 0
      as j → ∞.
So for every Q and ε > 0 some R∗ ∪ Tj has errQ (R∗ ∪ Tj ) ≤ Q(Tj ) < ε and makes every formula
derivable. If R∗ is given by finitely many pure schemas, so is R∗ ∪ Tj .

Proof. (i) Derive ⊤ by ⊤I, then ⊤(2) , . . . , ⊤(j) by j − 1 uses of ∧I, then C ∨ ⊤(j) by ∨I2 , and
finally C by Tj . The premise C ∨ ⊤(j) is a tautology, so for non-tautologous C the last step
                                                                       ′
is unsound. (ii) The Tj are pairwise disjoint (A ∨ ⊤(j) = A′ ∨ ⊤(j ) forces A = A′ , j = j ′ ), so
                           ∗        ∗
   j Q(Tj ) ≤ 1. Finally (R ∪ Tj )△R ⊆ Tj .
P


Corollary 3.3 (PAC learners can be maximally unsound). [T1 Cor 2.2, as repaired] In the setting
of Theorem 3.2, let L be any learner mapping a sample X1 , . . . , Xm of steps, drawn i.i.d. from Q
and labelled by membership in R∗ , to a step set. Let |s| count the symbol occurrences in a step s,
put ȷ̂ := 1 + maxi |Xi |, and

                        L+ (X1..m ) := L(X1..m ) ∪ Tȷ̂ ∪ inst(⊤I, ∧I, ∨I2 ).

Then for every Q, m ≥ 1 and ε ∈ (0, 1): (a) errQ (L+ ) ≤ errQ (L) + Q(Tȷ̂ ) and Pr[Q(Tȷ̂ ) > ε] ≤
(1 − ε)m . (b) Hence if L PAC-learns a class of targets R∗ , each containing ⊤I, ∧I and ∨I2 ,
with sample complexity mL (ε, δ), then L+ PAC-learns the same class (improperly) with sample
complexity at most max{mL (ε/2, δ/2), ⌈(2/ε) ln(2/δ)⌉}. (c) Yet every output of L+ makes every
formula derivable from ∅.

Proof idea. Steps of Tj have size ≥ j, and ȷ̂ exceeds every sample’s size, so Tȷ̂ lies in a tail of Q
that the sample has not reached; that tail has mass > ε only if all m samples missed a set of
mass > ε. The three added schemas lie in R∗ , so they cannot increase the error, and with Tȷ̂
they carry the derivation of Theorem 3.2(i). Full proof in Appendix B.

    The added schemas are needed for (c) (Remark B.1). The corollary is about what a PAC
guarantee can certify, not a prediction that natural learners output tonks: the guarantee is
satisfied equally well by a learner whose every output trivializes the reasoner.

Remarks. (a) Theorem 3.2 and Corollary 3.3 are trivial once set up. Their content is the
quantifier: a soundness guarantee must be a sups , not an Es∼Q , because the prover chooses s
after seeing the verifier. (b) Selection effect. If a search produces a derivation of a judgment that
R∗ does not derive from the premises used, then by Lemma 3.1 it contains an accepted step
outside Sound(R∗ ), whatever the verifier’s error rate on human steps. This is the mechanism
behind reward-model overoptimization (Gao et al., 2023), and it limits what process reward
models trained on human step labels (Lightman et al., 2023) can certify (Section 3.7). (c) The
uniform requirement is reliable learning (Rivest and Sloan, 1988b), perfect selective classification
(El-Yaniv and Wiener, 2010b), or the “accept” side of KWIK (Li et al., 2008b), applied to steps;
Section 4 computes its price.

                                                 21
Reading. For the question “can a learner learn which inferences are valid?”, Theorem 3.2
separates two things a guarantee might mean. “Valid on typical steps” (PAC accuracy, held-out
accuracy on human steps, a process reward model’s accuracy) is a statement about a measure,
which can be arbitrarily close to perfect while the set it measures licenses every conclusion.
“Valid on every step it accepts” is a statement about the set, and only it transfers to a reasoner.
For justification the moral is the same: a conclusion reached by search inherits nothing from the
checker’s average accuracy. A learned checker must certify an inclusion A ⊆ Sound(R∗ ); the rest
of the paper asks how imitation, coherence and the world can certify it.

3.3   In classical propositional logic, every unsound pure schema trivializes
One bad schema can trivialize a reasoner (Theorem 3.2). In classical propositional logic it always
does, provided it mentions no specific atom. Errors are then total, but also maximally visible:
the reasoner derives ⊥ from no premises.

Definition 3.4 (Pure schemas; substitution-closed step sets). A schema τ is a step whose
formulas may contain metavariables; inst(τ ) is the set of its instances, obtained by substituting
formulas for metavariables. Atoms are constants of the step language, so a schema may mention
specific atoms, as in x ∨ p0 ▷ x; least general generalization produces such schemas whenever
a position of the data is constantly p0 . A schema is pure if no atom occurs in it. A step set is
substitution-closed if it is closed under uniform substitution of formulas for atoms; instance sets
of pure schemas are. A step Π ▷ φ is sound if Π |= φ, and a schema or step set is sound if all its
instances or steps are.
                                                                                           ∗
Proposition 3.5 (Every unsound pure schema is a tonk). [T1 Prop 2.3, repaired scope] Let RCPC   be
sound and complete for classical propositional consequence, i.e. ClRCPC
                                                                    ∗   (Γ) = CnCPC (Γ) for all Γ,
                                         ∗
over a language with ⊤, ⊥, ¬. Let A = RCPC   ∪ A1 with A1 substitution-closed. If A contains an
unsound step, then ClA (∅) = Fm.

Proof. Let Π ▷ φ ∈ A be unsound, with v(Π) = 1, v(φ) = 0; it lies in A1 since RCPC   ∗   is sound.
Let σv send each atom true under v to ⊤ and each false one to ⊥. Then σv Π ▷ σv φ ∈ A1
has variable-free formulas; each σv π and ¬σv φ is a tautology, so lies in ClRCPC
                                                                              ∗   (∅) ⊆ ClA (∅) by
completeness. The step adds σv φ, and ClA (∅) ⊇ CnCPC ({σv φ, ¬σv φ}) = Fm.

    This is essentially Post (1921): classical propositional logic is Post-complete. The consequence-
level form underlies the coherence results of Section 6.

Example 3.6 (Schemas that mention atoms). Without substitution-closure the proposition
fails. τ = (x ∨ p0 ▷ x) is unsound (instance p1 ∨ p0 ▷ p1 ). Yet CnCPC (¬p0 ) is closed under
  ∗
RCPC   ∪ inst(τ ), since ¬p0 |= B ∨ p0 implies ¬p0 |= B. So the reasoner derives the non-tautology
¬p0 (from ¬p0 ∨ p0 ) but never p0 : unsound, not trivial. A ground error p0 ▷ p1 never fires from ∅
at all.

   The dichotomy is restored by purification: let τ ◦ be τ with each atom occurring in it replaced
by a fresh metavariable, distinct atoms by distinct metavariables.

Lemma 3.7 (Purification). [T1 Prop 2.3, repair] In classical propositional logic, τ is sound if and
only if τ ◦ is sound.



                                                 22
Corollary 3.8 (Coherence decides soundness of schema hypotheses). [T1 Prop 2.3, repair] For any
                                  ∗
family of schemas τl , let A = RCPC ∪ l inst(τl ) and A◦ = RCPC
                                     S                      ∗   ∪ l inst(τl◦ ). Then A is sound
                                                                 S

iff A◦ is sound iff ⊥ ∈
                      / ClA◦ (∅).

Proof idea. Every instance of τ is one of τ ◦ . Conversely, an instance of τ ◦ substitutes formulas
only for τ ’s own atom occurrences; renaming those atoms inside the metavariable fillers to fresh
atoms gives an instance of τ , which a uniform substitution maps back onto the given one, and
uniform substitution preserves validity. For the corollary, the instances of the pure τl◦ form a
substitution-closed set, so Proposition 3.5 applies to A◦ . Full proofs in Appendix B.

Reading. This is the positive face of Theorem 3.2. An unsound pure schema cannot hide: it
makes the reasoner incoherent in the empty context, which is exactly the signal of the coherence
channel (C). Coherence in the empty context is thus a complete soundness test for purified
schema hypotheses over CPC; Section 6 turns it into identification of CPC and Section 7 into
an end-to-end learner. Two limits. Purification is used as a test: one checks A◦ to decide A,
without accepting the purified steps. And the test presupposes a structural target. A calculus
with atom-specific principles (an axiom about one constant, a physical theory with named
parameters) is not structural, and there coherence can miss errors as in Example 3.6. Beyond
classical propositional logic the dichotomy fails too; Section 6 determines what coherence detects
in intuitionistic logic and arithmetic.

3.4   Simplicity is the wrong bias for positive data
A natural response to Theorem 3.2 is to prefer simple hypotheses. On positive data alone this
points the wrong way.

Proposition 3.9 (MDL picks “anything from anything”). [T1 Prop 2.4] Encode steps as ground
terms and schemas as terms with metavariables, of size equal to the number of symbol occurrences,
and let H1 = {inst(σ) : σ a schema}. For every nonempty set P of positive data, the single
metavariable x is a shortest schema with P ⊆ inst(x), and inst(x) ⊇ S accepts every step. It is
the unique shortest one up to renaming unless P is a single step of size 1.

Proof. x has the minimum size 1 and covers everything; any other size-1 schema is a constant c,
with inst(c) = {c}.

    More generally, an MDL learner without a likelihood term picks a shortest hypothesis
containing the data. Positive data never refute an over-general hypothesis (Gold, 1967; Angluin,
1980), and over-general hypotheses are typically short, so it over-generalizes. The positive-data
remedies are least generality, i.e. Plotkin’s least general generalization (Plotkin, 1970) and the
version-space verifiers of Sections 4 and 5, and a likelihood with the size principle (Section 4).
Even then, MAP selection is unsound whenever an over-general hypothesis is temporarily most
probable, so Section 4 replaces it by conservative thresholding. Simplicity has a role in this paper
(Section 8), but not as the acceptance criterion for steps.

3.5   When does average-case validity survive search? The commitment order
Theorem 3.2 seems to contradict a respectable tradition. In PAC-semantics (Valiant, 2000),
learned rules that are (1 − εi )-valid over a distribution of scenes are chained with additive error;
learning to reason (Khardon and Roth, 1997) and implicit learning (Juba, 2013) give guarantees

                                                 23
of the same average-case kind. There is no contradiction. What decides whether an average-case
guarantee survives an adversarial prover is what is random and when it is drawn.
Definition 3.10 (Sequent-level validity in a scene). Fix a distribution D over scenes x (structures
for a fixed language). A sequent Γ ⊢ φ is true in x if every assignment to its free variables that
makes all of Γ true in x makes φ true in x. A rule instance (premise sequents, conclusion sequent)
holds in x if its conclusion is true in x whenever all its premises are. A rule or schema ρ is valid
in x if all its instances hold in x, and (1 − ε)-valid per scene if Prx∼D [ρ is valid in x] ≥ 1 − ε.
    The sequent level matters: rules that discharge assumptions (→I, reductio) or introduce
eigenvariables (∀I) are not truth-preserving at formula level, but they are at sequent level. So
hypothetical contexts are covered.
Lemma 3.11 (Commitment order). [L3 Lemma A, as repaired]
  (i) Random world, rules fixed first. Let R = {ρ1 , . . . , ρm } be a finite library fixed before x ∼ D
      is drawn, each ρi (1 − εi )-valid per scene. Then
                                                                                               Pm
       Pr some R-derivation from premises true in x has a conclusion false in x              ≤    i=1 εi ,
      x∼D

      where the derivation may be chosen after seeing x; the bound holds simultaneously for all
      provers.
 (ii) Fixed world, prover-chosen instances. Fix a world x0 , a schema ρ and a distribution µ on
      its instances, and call ρ (1 − ε)-valid on average if µ{ι : ι holds in x0 } ≥ 1 − ε. Suppose ρ
      has an instance failing in x0 . It must have one if µ{ι : ι fails in x0 } > 0, and it may have
      one even when ε = 0, if the failing instance lies off the support of µ. Then a prover that
      submits it obtains, with probability 1, a one-step derivation from premises true in x0 to a
      conclusion false in x0 . No bound of the form (i) holds in this regime.
(iii) Fresh randomness per check. Each claim is a polynomial identity p ≡ q in n variables
      over a field F , committed by an adaptive prover. The verifier enforces deg(p − q) ≤ d,
      computing the degree from an explicit expression; only then does it draw a point uniformly
      from Skn , Sk ⊆ F finite, independently of everything before, and accept iff p and q agree
      there. Call the claim false if p − q is nonzero as a polynomial over F . A false k-th claim is
      accepted with probability at most d/|Sk |. So some false claim among the first N (N fixed in
      advance) is accepted with probability at most k≤N d/|Sk |, uniformly over adaptive provers;
                                                     P

      for unboundedly many claims, |Sk | ≥ d 2k /δ gives at most δ.
Proof idea. (i) Union bound over the whole library: with probability ≥ 1 − i εi every ρi is valid
                                                                                P

in x. This event does not depend on the prover, and on it induction on derivations preserves
truth in x. (ii) Nothing is random. (iii) Schwartz–Zippel for each claim, conditional on everything
committed before its point is drawn, then a union bound. Full proof in Appendix B.

     Part (i) is the chaining lemma of PAC-semantics (Valiant, 2000) with the quantifier order
made explicit; part (iii) is the Schwartz–Zippel lemma (Schwartz, 1980; Zippel, 1979; DeMillo
and Lipton, 1978); the whole is folklore. The lemma gives two sufficient conditions and one
failing regime. It is not a characterization: we do not claim that average-case guarantees survive
search only in regimes (i) and (iii). Each hypothesis is load-bearing (examples in Appendix B.3).
In (i), validity must hold per scene; a guarantee per random (scene, instance) pair does not give
it, and a prover who picks the instance inside the scene is in regime (ii). In (iii), the verifier must

 regime                             what is random     drawn when                  sound under search?
                                                                                         P
 (i) random scene, fixed library    scene x ∼ D        after the rules are fixed   yes, ≤ i εi
 (ii) one world, instance average   instance ι ∼ µ     never: the prover chooses   no    P
 (iii) fresh per-check randomness   evaluation point   after each claim            yes, ≤ k d/|Sk |

Table 3: The commitment order (Lemma 3.11). In the two sound regimes the randomness is
drawn after the rules or claims are fixed and outside the prover’s control.


enforce the degree bound (repeated squaring can hide degree 2k ), and p − q must be nonzero
over the field used: (a + b)p − ap − bp vanishes over Fp , so evaluation mod p always accepts the
freshman’s dream.

Reading. Table 3 sorts the channels of this paper. Mathematics has one world, so a learned
checker’s in-distribution accuracy is randomness over instances that the prover chooses: regime (ii),
of which Theorem 3.2 is an instance. Its training sample is drawn before the prover searches, so
a learned checker is never a regime-(iii) oracle. Fresh per-check randomness, by contrast, is a
legitimate world channel (W) in mathematics; part (iii) gives conditions under which such an
oracle is worst-case sound. The random evaluation used in the experiments of Section 12 fits this
setting only for value errors of polynomial identities, such as the freshman’s dream; a definedness
error such as x/x ▷ 1, which fails only at x = 0, lies outside it. In physics, the uncertainty
that matters for an export rule (which carries a result from an idealized context back to the
world) is over situations, and regime (i) makes PAC-semantics a worst-case-over-derivations
semantics for bridges, with error at most the summed validity defects of the library. The caveat is
distribution shift: olympiad problems are selected to stress idealizations, so the test distribution
differs from the training one exactly where it matters (Section 11). For justification, the lemma
gives sufficient conditions for a statistical warrant to compose with search: the chance event is
drawn after the reasoner has committed, and outside its control.

3.6    Voting ensembles: incoherence without information
A common response to an exploitable verifier is an ensemble that accepts a step if most members
do. With coherence feedback this looks attractive: a derivation of ⊥ from premises certified
coherent shows that something accepted is invalid. Stepwise voting, however, can produce an
incoherence that implicates no member.
    We use the online protocol of Section 6 in minimal form. Hypotheses are step sets in a
countable class H with prior w; the target R∗ ∈ H is unknown. A family A of designated contexts,
finite premise sets with ⊥ ∈ / ClR∗ (A), is fixed. An argument π from A is a finite derivation
tree each of whose nodes is either a leaf labelled by an element of A or the conclusion of a step
whose premises label its children (possibly none); Steps(π) is the set of its steps. In round t
the learner holds VSt ⊆ H (initially H) and announces an accepted set R̂t , whose steps the
reasoner may chain. The environment then either (P) presents some s ∈ R∗ , and the learner
deletes every R ∈ VSt with s ∈ / R; or (N) exhibits a detected incoherence, an argument π from
some A ∈ A to ⊥ with Steps(π) ⊆ R̂t , and the learner deletes every R ∈ VSt with Steps(π) ⊆ R;
or does nothing. Since chaining steps of R∗ cannot reach ⊥ from A, Steps(π) ̸⊆ R∗ and the
target is never deleted (the negative-bag lemma of Section 6). Majority aggregation announces


                                                  25
R̂t = {s : w({R ∈ VSt : s ∈ R}) > 12 w(VSt )}.

Theorem 3.12 (Doctrinal paradox: uninformative incoherence). [T2 Thm 2.4, as repaired] Let H =
{h1 , h2 , h3 } with uniform prior, where hi = {Γ ▷ φ : Ti ∪ Γ |= φ} for

                          T1 = {p, q},      T2 = {p, ¬q},        T3 = {¬p, q},

and let A = {∅}. Every hi is coherent, and any of them may be the target. Under majority
aggregation, for every target there is an environment under which the prover exhibits the same
detected incoherence in every round and no hypothesis is ever deleted. The environment presents
only that incoherence, or interleaves it with uninformative positive data such as ▷p ∨ ¬p, which
lie in every hi .

Proof idea. While VSt = H, the majority set is the set of steps in at least two hi . The argument

               ▷p,      ▷q,      p, q ▷ p ∧ q,      ▷¬(p ∧ q),       p ∧ q, ¬(p ∧ q) ▷ ⊥

uses only majority steps: ▷p ∈ h1 , h2 ; ▷q ∈ h1 , h3 ; ▷¬(p ∧ q) ∈ h2 , h3 ; the last two are in all
three. Yet h1 lacks ▷¬(p ∧ q), h2 lacks ▷q, and h3 lacks ▷p. So the detection deletes nothing,
nor do tautologous data, and the situation repeats forever. Full proof in Appendix B.

    The environment must withhold discriminating data. If the target is h1 and ▷p is presented,
h3 is deleted, majority over {h1 , h2 } is h1 ∩ h2 , and the argument is unavailable (▷q ∈
                                                                                         / h2 ). As
the hi are pairwise incomparable, on a text enumerating the target every wrong hypothesis is
eventually refuted. So the theorem concerns what coherence feedback can guarantee round by
round against an unhelpful environment, not convergence on complete data.
    This is Kornhauser and Sager’s doctrinal paradox (Kornhauser and Sager, 1986) and Pettit’s
discursive dilemma (Pettit, 2001), transposed from judges to verifiers. List and Pettit (2002)
prove that no aggregation function satisfies universal domain, anonymity and systematicity while
producing consistent and complete collective judgments, for agendas with at least two atoms
and their conjunction (or disjunction, or material conditional). For oligarchic aggregation under
mere deductive closure, see Gärdenfors (2006) and Dietrich and List (2008).

Reading. Each hi is a coherent calculus, yet the vote derives ⊥ from no premises, so by
Lemma 3.1 the majority acceptance region is unsound relative to every candidate target: voting
produces an accepted set that no member endorses. Worse, the incoherence does not say which
member is wrong, so the coherence channel, which should supply the negative data that imitation
lacks, is silenced. The repair (Section 6): against a worst-case prover, every possible detection is
guaranteed to remove at least half of the remaining prior weight only if the accepted set lies inside
every member of a coalition of at least half the weight (oligarchic acceptance). Each detection
then costs at least one bit, which bounds detections only, not incompleteness. Oligarchy also
requires truth maintenance: lemmas certified by an earlier coalition must be re-validated when
the coalition changes, or the union over coalitions reproduces the paradox across time.

3.7    Search in practice
Experiment C (Section 12) runs a black-box adversarial prover (bidirectional best-first search, 3000
verifier queries per false goal) against learned verifiers for partial field algebra, trained on simulated
human derivations with noise and systematic fallacies (computed). A gradient-boosting step

                                                   26
classifier, given oracle-labelled positive and negative examples, reaches test AUC 0.995 on held-
out human-like steps. Calibrated to 99% in-distribution balanced precision TPR/(TPR + FPR),
it still lets the prover prove 16.3 of 23 false goal equations (mean over three seeds). The learned
calculus, least-general-generalization schemas repaired by step-level world feedback, proves 0
of the 23 false goals and 32.3 of 33 true identities, as does the ground-truth calculus. That
learned verifiers are exploited by search is well known in machine-learning practice: reward-model
overoptimization follows smooth scaling laws (Gao et al., 2023), and verifier-reranked accuracy
peaks and then falls as more solutions are sampled (Cobbe et al., 2021). This section explains
why in-distribution accuracy cannot rule it out. In-distribution accuracy is a statement about a
measure fixed before the prover moves (Lemma 3.11(ii)); soundness of the reasoner is a statement
about a set (Lemma 3.1); and no bound on the former controls the latter (Theorem 3.2). The
theorems do not predict the rate at which a given search finds errors; for neural verifiers it is
graded, not all-or-nothing, as the overoptimization curves show.


4    Cautious verification and its price
Section 3 reduced the soundness of a search-driven reasoner to a set inclusion: everything the
verifier accepts must lie in Sound(R∗ ), and average-case accuracy does not certify this. This
section shows how a learner can certify the inclusion, and what it costs. The answer is caution.
Accept a step only if every hypothesis consistent with the evidence accepts it, reject it only if
none does, and otherwise escalate it to a human or an oracle. This is reliable learning (Rivest
and Sloan, 1988a), perfect selective classification (El-Yaniv and Wiener, 2010a) and the “accept”
side of KWIK (Li et al., 2008a), applied to steps. It is sound against every prover from the first
datum on, and it is the most permissive 0-sound verifier (Theorem 4.2).
    Its price is paid in escalations, never in soundness. The worst case is exactly a bounded,
target-relative form of Wright’s elasticity (Theorem 4.4). Table 4 gives it for the main classes.
It is linear in the step size for rules cited by name, and at least polynomial of degree k for k
rules without citations. It is exponential in description length for unstructured classes, and
super-exponential (over growing signatures) if invalidity must be certified too. A Bayesian version
adds time-uniform soundness from noisy data, with no union bound over queries (Section 4.4),
but its escalation cost is exponential in description length unless structure intervenes. In one
line: structure, not the prior, makes sound learned verification affordable.
    The results are elementary, and several are trivial once set up. As far as we know, four are
new: the exact single-schema budget, the tagged/untagged separation, the Bell-number bound
for two-sided checking over growing signatures, and the tightness of the Ville constant.

Conventions. Protocol, soundness and cost are as in Definitions 2.18, 2.19 and 2.21. Human
data P0 ⊆ R∗ are fixed unless said otherwise. The cost of a run is the (expected) number of rounds
in which a valid query is not accepted outright; invalid queries are not charged. Esc(H | P0 ) is
the infimum, over 0-sound verifiers, of the worst-case cost over targets in H containing P0 and
over honest query sequences. Esc(H; N ) restricts queries to steps of size (number of symbols) at
most N .
    Two facts from Section 2 do most of the combinatorial work. First, the rank µ(t) = |t|−|vars(t)|
strictly decreases along strict generalization (Lemma 2.6; T1 Lemma 1.2). Second, the lgg of
instances of σ recovers σ iff two witness events hold (Lemma 2.7; T1 Lemma 1.3): (R) no
metavariable is always instantiated by terms with the same root, and (D) no two metavariables


                                                27
are always instantiated alike.

4.1    The version-space verifier is sound and optimal
Definition 4.1 (Version space; VS verifier). [T1 §3] Let H ⊆ 2S be realizable. For sets P
(positive) and P − (negative) of labelled steps, VS(P, P − ) = {R ∈ H : P ⊆ R, R ∩ P − = ∅}, and
VS(P ) = VS(P, ∅). The VS verifier starts with P = P0 , P − = ∅, and answers a query q with
ACCEPT if q ∈ VS(P, P − ), with REJECT if q ∈      / VS(P, P − ), and with ESCALATE otherwise;
                T                                   S

an escalated q joins P or P − according to the oracle’s label. For a history h, VSh is the version
space computed from P0 and the labels in h.

    In the terms of El-Yaniv and Wiener (2010a), this is the consistent selective strategy: it
escalates exactly on the disagreement region of the version space.

Theorem 4.2 (Soundness and optimality of caution). [T1 Thm 3.1, as repaired]
 (a) The VS verifier is uniformly 0-sound. For every target R∗ ∈ H with P0 ⊆ R∗ and every
     prover, R∗ ∈ VS(P, P − ) at all times. So everything it accepts lies in R∗ , and a reasoner
     that chains accepted steps is sound (Lemma 3.1).
  (b) Let V be any (possibly randomized) δ-sound verifier for H with human data P0 . Fix a target
      R∗ ∈ H with P0 ⊆ R∗ , a history h of t rounds (queries q1 , . . . , qt , answers, oracle labels)
      whose labels agree with R∗ , and a query q ∈
                                                       T

      probability, over V ’s coins, that against R∗ the prover issuing q1 , . . . , qt , q produces h and
      then has q accepted. Then p ≤ δ.
  (c) In particular, a deterministic δ-sound verifier with δ < 1 accepts no q ∈
                                                                                          T
                                                                                 / VSh at any
      history h that it produces against a target in H. History by history, its acceptance region

   (b) Pick R′ ∈ VSh with q ∈         / R′ . Then R′ ∈ H, P0 ⊆ R′ , and R′ agrees with every label
