    The joint form in (b) is essential. Read conditionally on h, (b) is false. A verifier that accepts
everything with probability ε, and otherwise runs the VS verifier, is ε-sound. Yet it reaches
                                                                                          T
some histories only in that reckless mode, and after them it accepts steps outside VSh with
conditional probability 1 (Appendix C.1). With random human data the coupling must also
transport the data, giving only PR′ [data] · p ≤ δ; this is where Section 4.4 takes over. Part (a)
and case (c) are formalized in Lean (lean/InfLearn/StepSoundness.lean). The theorem is
trivial once set up; the set-up shows that the VS verifier is the largest 0-sound acceptance rule,
so its escalations are forced on every 0-sound verifier.



                                                   28
Reading. A learner can guess the calculus (the simplest or most probable hypothesis), which
is unsound under search (Section 3). Or it can certify steps, accepting only what every calculus
consistent with the evidence accepts, which is sound from the first datum without negative
data. Gold’s problem (Gold, 1967) does not threaten certification. An over-general hypothesis,
tonk included, never licenses a step by itself, because the target stays in the version space as
a guardian. Over-generality costs only completeness. Since contexts are part of judgments,
this covers hypothetical steps verbatim (→I; “assume the air pressure is 0”). As a model of
justification: a step is accepted when no hypothesis left open by the evidence defeats it, relative
to H and the human calculus, not to truth.

4.2    The escalation dimension is a positive elasticity
Definition 4.3 (Elastic chain; positive elasticity). [T1 §3] Let R ∈ H with P0 ⊆ R. An elastic
chain in R (relative to P0 ) is a sequence s1 , . . . , sm ∈ R with si ∈
                                                                       / VS(P0 ∪ {s1 , . . . , si−1 }) for
                                                                         T

every i. Equivalently, there are witnesses Ri ∈ H with P0 ∪ {s1 , . . . , si−1 } ⊆ Ri ̸∋ si . The positive
elasticity el(H, R | P0 ) is the supremum of the lengths of such chains, and el(H, R | P0 ; N )
restricts the si to size at most N .

   Wright’s finite elasticity (Wright, 1989; Motoki et al., 1991) forbids infinite sequences of this
kind and suffices for identification in the limit from text. Positive elasticity is its bounded version,
with the points in the target.

Theorem 4.4 (Escalation dimension = positive elasticity). [T1 Thm 3.2, proof as repaired] For every
class H and human data P0 ,

                               Esc(H | P0 ) =       sup     el(H, R | P0 ).
                                                R∈H, R⊇P0

The VS verifier attains it: against an honest prover for target R∗ , it never rejects and escalates
at most el(H, R∗ | P0 ) times. Conversely, if s1 , . . . , sm is an elastic chain in some R ∈ H, the
honest prover for R that queries s1 , . . . , sm in order forces every δ-sound (possibly randomized)
verifier to expected cost at least (1 − δ)m, and every 0-sound verifier to cost m almost surely.
The same holds with all steps restricted to size at most N .

Proof idea. Against an honest prover, the VS verifier escalates q exactly when q ∈      / VS(P0 ∪ E),
                                                                                          T

where E is the set of earlier escalated queries. So the escalated queries form an elastic chain
in R∗ . Conversely, the targets R and Ri label s1 , . . . , si−1 alike, so the transcripts up to round i
have the same law under both. Under Ri the step si is invalid, so it is accepted with probability
at most δ; hence the same holds under R. This is the joint form of Theorem 4.2(b). Full proof in
Appendix C.2.

    So the cost of caution depends only on the class, the target and the human data P0 , not on
the prover, the human distribution or a prior. It is KWIK’s adversarial abstention count (Li
et al., 2008a, 2011), made one-sided. Rivest and Sloan (1988a) and El-Yaniv and Wiener (2010a)
bound instead the distributional mass of abstention, a usefulness guarantee (Section 5).

Proposition 4.5 (Intersection-closed classes). [T1 Prop 3.3] (known in substance) Suppose H ∪ {∅}
is closed under arbitrary intersections. For P contained in some member, let cl(P ) be the
intersection of all members of H that contain P . Then:


                                                   29
           VS(P ) = cl(P ), the least member of H ∪ {∅} containing P . So, against an honest prover,
        T
  (i)
        the VS verifier is the closure algorithm: it accepts iff q ∈ cl(P0 ∪ E), where E is the set of
        steps escalated so far.
 (ii) el(H, R | P0 ) is the largest m for which there is a strict chain cl(P0 ) = C0 ⊊ C1 ⊊ · · · ⊊
      Cm ⊆ R of members of H ∪ {∅}.

Proof idea. An elastic chain gives the strict chain Ci = cl(P0 ∪ {s1 , . . . , si }); conversely, pick
si ∈ Ci \ Ci−1 (Appendix C.3). This is the closure algorithm of Helmbold et al. (1990); its use
for one-sided learning from positive examples goes back to Natarajan (1987). We claim nothing
new here beyond reading the chain length as an escalation count.

Lemma 4.6 (Single schemas are intersection-closed). [T1 §3, after Prop 3.3] H1 = {inst(σ) :
σ a schema} ∪ {∅} is closed under arbitrary intersections. For nonempty P , cl(P ) = inst(lggP ).

Proof idea. Two instance sets meet in the instance set of a most general common instance,
or not at all. A nonempty intersection contains a ground term, which has only finitely many
generalizations up to renaming. Full proof in Appendix C.3.

Reading. Caution climbs a lattice from below. Each escalation moves the accepted set one
strict step up, and the price is the longest climb from what the human data force to the target.
For schemas the lattice is Plotkin’s (Plotkin, 1970; Reynolds, 1970), the closure algorithm is
anti-unification, and each step up costs a unit of rank. A learner that acquires a connective’s
inferential role from its uses climbs in this way. The elasticity bounds how often it must ask
before its accepted inferences cover that role.

4.3     What caution costs: from named rules to unstructured classes
Theorem 4.7 (One schema, learned by anti-unification). [T1 Thm 3.4, as restated] Let the target be
inst(σ ∗ ) ∈ H1 , written σ ∗ inside el.
  (i) For nonempty P0 ⊆ inst(σ ∗ ): el(H1 , σ ∗ | P0 ) ≤ µ(lggP0 ) − µ(σ ∗ ).
 (ii) From no data: el(H1 , σ ∗ | ∅; N ) ≤ 1 + N − µ(σ ∗ ). Hence Esc(H1 ; N ) ≤ N + 1.
(iii) This is tight. If the signature contains two constants a, b and a unary symbol g, then for
      the universal schema σ ∗ = x, el(H1 , x | ∅; N ) = N + 1. So Esc(H1 ; N ) = N + 1.

Proof idea. (i) With gi = lgg(P0 ∪ {s1 , . . . , si }), Lemma 4.6 gives si ∈        / inst(gi−1 ), so lggP0 =
g0 ≺ · · · ≺ gm ⪯ σ ∗ strictly (by the lgg property; no assumption on the signature is needed),
and the rank lemma bounds m. (ii) From no data the first query escalates, and it has rank
at most N . (iii) Query g N −1 (a), g N −1 (b), g N −2 (a), g N −3 (a), . . . , a. The successive lggs are
g N −1 (a), g N −1 (x), g N −2 (x), . . . , x, of ranks N, N − 1, . . . , 0, and each query lies outside the
previous lgg. Full proof in Appendix C.4.

    Exhaustive search gives exactly N + 1 with two constants, and N with one (computed). After
a corpus P0 , the residual budget is at most the generality gap µ(lggP0 ) − µ(σ ∗ ). It is zero once
the corpus satisfies the witness events (Section 5). These numbers are small only because the
protocol is one-sided.




                                                     30
Proposition 4.8 (Certifying invalidity is expensive). [T1 Prop 3.5, as revised] Call a verifier two-
                                                      S
sided if it is 0-sound and rejects only steps outside VS, and count all its escalations, of valid
and invalid queries alike. Over a signature with constants a, b1 , . . . , bn and an n-ary symbol f ,
there are a target in H1 and a prover that force every two-sided verifier for H1 to escalate at least
Bell(n) times on steps of size n + 1. Over a fixed finite signature, by contrast, the two-sided VS
verifier escalates on steps of size at most N at most as often as there are such steps, 2O(N ) .

Proof idea. Let target and P0 be {f (a, . . . , a)}. For a partition π of [n], sπ puts bj and σπ a
metavariable xj on the j-th block; sπ′ ∈ inst(σπ ) iff π refines π ′ . Query the sπ finest first: each
is invalid, yet σπ is still consistent when sπ is queried. Full proof in Appendix C.5.

    Brute force gives Bell(2), Bell(3), Bell(4) = 2, 5, 15 forced escalations (computed). Certifying
validity costs at most N + 1 escalations over any signature. Certifying invalidity costs at least
Bell(N −1) over a signature with N constants and an (N −1)-ary symbol; compare the exponential
KWIK cost of conjunctions (Li et al., 2011) and the negative results for two-sided reliable learning
(Kivinen, 1995). A step checker never needs it: one-sidedness is what makes learned verification
cheap.

Theorem 4.10 (k rules without citations). [T1 Thm 3.7, scope as repaired] Let Hk = {

be the class of untagged unions of at most k schemas.

on 11–12 points with h ≤ 4 found no counterexample         (computed). For schemas h ≤ N + 1, so the

Table 4: The price of caution: escalations (human interventions on valid steps) needed by a sound
verifier against an honest prover. “Tight” means attained over a signature with two constants
and a unary symbol. All entries are proved except the conjectured bound.

    This is KWIK’s enumeration bound (Li et al., 2008a): 2L hypotheses of L bits can need
2L − 1 escalations. Single schemas of size ≤ N (with a binary symbol) also number 2Ω(N ) , yet
need at most N + 1. The price is measured by lattice height, not cardinality.

Reading. Table 4 quantifies one way in which “inventing a language” with named rules, the
user’s description of how mathematics was formalized, makes inference learnable. Formal proofs
cite their rules, and then caution costs linearly in step size, additively over rules. Informal proofs
and physics solutions cite none. The verifier must then pay at least ⌊(N − 1)/k⌋k even when one
rule is in use, since it must rule out that the steps seen so far are covered by k narrower rules.
The proved upper bound is exponential, and Θk (N k ) is conjectured. Informal steps are moreover
derived, multi-step elements of Sound(R∗ ), and Section 10 treats them separately.

4.4    Bayes-conservative verification and Ville’s inequality
A posterior relaxes the version space’s demand of truthful data, and makes very large classes
usable, which eases (but does not remove) the demand of realizability. MAP acceptance is unsound
whenever an over-general hypothesis is temporarily most probable (Section 3.4). Caution instead
thresholds the posterior probability of invalidity.
Definition 4.13 (Bayes-conservative verifier). [T1 §4] Let H be countable with prior w and
w∗ = w(R∗ ) > 0. Each R ∈ H carries a law pR on S for human data (which may model errors,
and may follow the size principle, spreading mass more thinly over larger R) and an oracle kernel
ℓR (y | q), y ∈ {0, 1}, which is deterministic if ℓR (y | q) = 1[y = 1[q ∈ R]]. The model is well
specified: human data are i.i.d. pR∗ , and oracle answers follow ℓR∗ (· | qt ). After the observations
of rounds ≤ t, the likelihood is Lt (R), the posterior is wt (R) ∝ w(R)Lt (R), and
                                            Lt (R)                                   w∗
                                                                        wt (R∗ ) =
                               X
                       Zt :=         w(R)            ,        so that                   .
                               R∈H
                                            Lt (R∗ )                                 Zt

The verifier Vδ answers q with ACCEPT iff wt ({R : q ∈
                                                     / R}) < δ, optionally with REJECT
iff wt ({R : q ∈ R}) < δr , and with ESCALATE otherwise. The VS posterior is wtVS (R) ∝
w(R) 1[R is consistent with all data].

Theorem 4.14 (VS posterior: deterministic soundness). [T1 Thm 4.1] Suppose all data are truthful:
human positives lie in R∗ , and oracle labels are correct. If δ ≤ w∗ , then Vδ with the VS posterior
never accepts an invalid step, for any prover and at any time. The guarantee is per target: it
covers every target with w(R∗ ) ≥ δ. Conversely, for every δ > w∗ there is a two-hypothesis class
on which Vδ accepts an invalid step at time 0.

   The truth is a guardian: an invalid step can be accepted only once the target’s own posterior
has fallen below δ. With noisy data this can happen, but only on one global event.

Theorem 4.15 (Time-uniform soundness). [T1 Thm 4.2, as repaired] Assume the model is well
specified and δ ≤ w∗ δ ′ .
 (a) Suppose that, conditional on Ft , the next observation has its model law: a fresh X ∼ pR∗ ,
     or an answer y ∼ ℓR∗ (· | qt+1 ). Here Ft contains the history and all of the prover’s
     information, including its internal randomness and its choice of qt+1 . So the prover cannot
     foresee human data or oracle noise, and the noise is fresh at every query. Then for every
     such prover
                                                           / R∗ ≤ δ ′ .
                                                              
                                P ∃t : Vδ accepts some qt ∈

  (b) If the oracle is deterministic, the guarantee is uniform. There is an event of probability
      at least 1 − δ ′ , depending only on the human data, on which no prover strategy ever gets
      an invalid step accepted. This holds even for a prover that knows the target and all future
      human data. Only the human data need to be i.i.d. pR∗ .

Proof idea. Under (a), each observation multiplies the R-term of Zt by a likelihood ratio of
conditional mean o:ℓR∗ (o)>0 ℓR (o) ≤ 1. So Z is a nonnegative supermartingale with Z0 = 1, and
                   P

Ville’s inequality (Ville, 1939) gives P[supt Zt ≥ 1/δ ′ ] ≤ δ ′ ; off this event wt (R∗ ) = w∗ /Zt > δ for
all t. In (b), deterministic answers multiply each term by at most 1, so Zt is bounded pathwise
by a supermartingale of the human data alone. Full proof in Appendix C.9.

    Credit. Part (a) is the prior–posterior-ratio martingale of Waudby-Smith and Ramdas
(2020), applied to validity relations: the ratio of prior to posterior at the true parameter is a
nonnegative martingale. Part (b) is an easy corollary, since truthful constraints multiply Zt by
at most 1. See Ville (1939); Shafer et al. (2011); Ramdas et al. (2023) on Ville’s inequality and
test martingales. The closest existing analogues are conservative Bayesian agents that defer to
a mentor (Cohen and Hutter, 2020; Cohen et al., 2022). New here is only the tightness of the
constant (Proposition 4.16); the application to step validity is routine.
    No union bound over queries. A prover may try 10100 steps, and one event of probability at
least 1 − δ ′ covers them all, because accepting any invalid step requires the posterior of the truth
to fall below δ. A per-step scorer has no such single event, and its errors can be found one at a
time (Theorem 3.2).


                                                    34
     The hypothesis of (a) is needed. Take H = 2{0,1} with uniform prior, R∗ = {0}, symmetric
flip noise of rate η, and δ ′ = 0.05. A prover that queries the invalid step 1 exactly when it knows
that the answer will be flipped to “valid” gets 1 accepted with probability 1.0, at both η = 0.1
and η = 0.3 (computed; Appendix C.9).

This tends to δ ′ as u, w∗ → 0. The exact values are 0.989 δ ′ at u = 0.05, w∗ = δ ′ = 0.01, and

Theorem 4.17 (Escalation bound). [T1 Thm 4.4] Let the oracle be deterministic, let the human
data satisfy the hypothesis of Theorem 4.15(a), and let δm = min(δ, δr ). Write #ESCt for the
number of escalations up to time t.
    (i) With probability at least 1−δ ′′ , simultaneously for all t, #ESCt ≤ ln(1/w∗ )+ln(1/δ ′′ ) /δm .
                                                                                                    

 (ii) With the VS posterior, deterministically, #ESCt ≤ ln(1/w∗ )/δm .
For an honest prover, or when only escalations of valid queries are counted, δm may be replaced
by δ. Without a reject option (δr = 0), the bound on escalations of invalid queries is vacuous.

Proof idea. Zt evolves multiplicatively. A human datum multiplies it by a factor of conditional
mean at most 1. Escalating a valid (invalid) q multiplies it by at most 1 − δ (1 − δr ), since q was
not accepted (rejected). Combine with Zt = w∗ /wt (R∗ ) ≥ w∗ and Ville’s inequality for the data
factors. Appendix C.11.

Corollary 4.18 (Bayes is essentially optimal on unstructured classes). [T1 Cor 4.5] On H =
{U \ {u} : u ∈ U } with the uniform prior (w∗ = 1/|U |), every δ ′ -sound verifier has worst-case
honest cost at least (1 − δ ′ )(1/w∗ − 1), while Vw∗ δ′ with the VS posterior is 0-sound and pays
at most ln(1/w∗ )/(w∗ δ ′ ). The ratio of the two bounds is ln(1/w∗ )/ δ ′ (1 − δ ′ )(1 − w∗ ) , which is
O(ln(1/w∗ )/δ ′ ) for w∗ , δ ′ ≤ 1/2. With a description-length prior w∗ = 2−L , the cost is Θ̃(2L ).

Proof. The lower bound is Theorem 4.4 applied to the chain of Theorem 4.12, which has length
1/w∗ − 1. The upper bound is Theorem 4.17(ii), and soundness is Theorem 4.14.

    The factor 1/δ ′ is an artifact of the threshold. With δ = w∗ the VS posterior accepts exactly
  VS on this class, since every consistent hypothesis has posterior at least w∗ . It is then the VS
T

verifier itself, which is exactly optimal.

Corollary 4.19 (Structure plus Bayes). [T1 Cor 4.6, as repaired] With truthful data, the VS-posterior
verifier with threshold δ never accepts an invalid step, for any prover and every target with
w(R∗ ) ≥ δ. Its escalations of valid queries number at most

Proof. Soundness is Theorem 4.14. An escalated valid q had w({R : q ∈     / R}) ≥ δ > 0, so

    This guarantee is not 0-soundness in the sense of Definition 2.19 unless every target has prior
at least δ, which fails for infinite H.

Structure, not the prior. For single schemas with a description-length prior, ln(1/w∗ )/δ ≥
ln(1/w∗ )/w∗ is exponential in |σ ∗ |, while el ≤ N + 1. The prior earns its keep elsewhere. If H
contains every finite step set (calculi with unboundedly many ground rules, so exception lists are
                                                          T
hypotheses), the bare version space never generalizes: VS(P ) = P . Under the hypotheses of
Theorem 4.15, the Bayesian verifier is sound with probability at least 1 − δ ′ for every target with
w(R∗ ) ≥ δ/δ ′ , i.e. under a description-length prior, every rule system of description length at
most log2 (δ ′ /δ). Hence a three-layer recipe:
   • a structured version-space core, for cheap escalations and exact soundness when realizable;
   • a Bayesian hierarchy over it, as insurance against unrealizability;
   • conservative thresholds, never MAP.
One caveat: Theorem 4.15 needs well-specification. Systematic human errors are exactly a
misspecification the model does not anticipate, and the posterior then concentrates on “rule plus
fallacy” as a rule (Section 5).

Reading. From noisy practice and a noisy oracle, Theorem 4.15 gives a warrant that search
cannot erode, as long as the searcher cannot foresee the data or the noise. Its error probability δ ′
is fixed in advance and does not grow with the number of steps tried, the length of derivations,
or the running time. That is what “it must actually work if run” asks of a checker that a
strong prover searches against. The warrant is fallibilist and reliabilist, conditional on a well-
specified model. The prior does not make it cheap: structure, not the prior, makes sound learned
verification affordable.

4.5   What caution buys, and what it cannot do
Learning which inferences are valid. A cautious learner learns exactly what its evidence forces
and never adopts a rule it cannot certify. It pays for completeness in escalations, and the positive
elasticity is the exact worst case. With rules cited by name and sufficiently varied human data,
positive data alone bring it to exactly the human calculus, after coupon-collector many steps,
and it is sound at every moment along the way (Section 5).
    Coherence. A cautious verifier gets no signal from coherence. Its accepted steps lie in R∗ , so
no argument from a designated context to ⊥ uses only accepted steps (T2 Prop. 2.9(a); Section 6).
Coherence informs only bold learners. Hence the two tiers of Section 7: cautious assertion, and a
bold sandbox attacked by a red team.
    Limits. Realizability is load-bearing. If the true calculus lies outside the class, for example
two untagged rules while H = H1 , the lgg over-generalizes and soundness fails. The Bayesian
hierarchy repairs this only probabilistically, at a cost exponential in the complexity of what is
missing. Soundness is relative to the human calculus, naive comprehension included if humans use


                                                 36
it, and improving on it is the job of coherence and the world (Sections 6, 7 and 10). The first-order
term model leaves out associative-commutative contexts, binders and variable-arity rules, where
the bounds of Table 4 would need re-proof. Steps inside suppositions and idealizations are
certified like any others. Which contexts may be designated, and how results are exported, is left
to Section 11.
     Justification. Certified acceptance models a warrant that survives search. A step is warranted
when every hypothesis the evidence leaves open licenses it, and no amount of adversarial search
erodes such a warrant, unlike a statistical one (Section 3). The Bayesian version trades certainty
for a single bounded chance of error, which also does not grow with search. Both are conditional
reliabilist vindications, relative to explicit assumptions (realizability; truthful or well-specified
data), and later sections show that each assumption is needed (Section 13).


5     Imitation: what positive examples fix
The first channel is imitation. The learner sees human steps, possibly with the names of the
rules they cite, and nothing else; no step is ever labelled invalid. This section asks what such
data fix when acceptance must be cautious, as search requires (Sections 3 and 4). The answer
has two halves.
    With the right hypothesis class (schemas, learned by least general generalization), positive
examples fix the rules exactly. A cautious verifier fed only human steps is sound at every time.
It becomes exactly complete after finitely many examples iff the target has a finite anchor
(Theorem 5.4). For rules that humans cite by name this happens at a coupon-collector rate
(Theorem 5.7); without citations it needs a diversity condition (Theorem 5.9). Once exact, the
verifier generalizes systematically: it accepts exactly the target calculus, on steps and derivations
of any size, whatever the distribution of the human data (Corollary 5.11). This is the formal
sense in which finite usage fixes inferential role for unbounded use.
    The other half is that positive data cannot tell what humans do from what is valid. One
mislabelled step collapses a learned rule into “anything follows” (Proposition 5.13). Sporadic
errors can be trimmed when they are rarer than the evidence for each rule’s generality, and
this threshold is necessary up to a factor of two (Theorems 5.14 and 5.15 and Corollary 5.19).
Systematic errors are indistinguishable from rules at every rate (Corollary 5.20). Removing them
needs negative information (Sections 6 and 7).
    Most results below are trivial once set up, or known in substance, and we say which. New
here, as far as we know, are three things: anchors read as the completeness condition of certified
verification; the witness-event sample complexities and their noise versions, with necessity up
to a factor of two; and the exact statement that schema-generated errors are rules. None is
deep. Together they pin down when imitation works. Throughout, the signature Ω in which
steps are encoded (Section 2.2) has at least two constants. Then a proper specialization of a
schema has strictly fewer instances, and the instance sets of all schemas have empty intersection
(Appendix D.1).

5.1    Cautious learning from positive data
Let H ⊆ 2S be a hypothesis class of step sets (Definition 2.9) containing the target R∗ , and let
the data D be a finite multiset of human steps. Unlike in Section 4, there is no oracle. Until
Section 5.6 the data are clean, D ⊆ R∗ .


                                                 37
Definition 5.1 (Cautious verifier). [T1 §5; L1 §9] The (positive-data) version space is VS(D) =
{R ∈ H : D ⊆ R}. The cautious verifier accepts exactly R̂(D) := VS(D) (with ∅ := S).
                                                                    T               T


Lemma 5.2 (Soundness and maximality; trivial). [T1 Thm 3.1, Prop 3.3, positive data] (a) If D ⊆ R∗
then R̂(D) ⊆ R∗ . So the reasoner that chains accepted steps derives only ClR∗ -consequences,
against every prover, at every time. (b) Every acceptance set contained in all R ∈ VS(D)
(i.e. sound for every target compatible with D) is contained in R̂(D). (c) D ⊆ D′ implies
R̂(D) ⊆ R̂(D′ ). (d) If H ∪ {∅} is closed under arbitrary intersections and VS(D) ̸= ∅, R̂(D) is
the least member of H ∪ {∅} containing D; for H = H1 and D ̸= ∅ it is inst(lgg(D)).
                                                             Lean: thm_3_1_a_static Lean: thm_3_1_b_static


Proof. (a) R∗ ∈ VS(D); apply Lemma 3.1. (b), (c) are immediate. (d) The intersection of all
members containing D is a member; for H1 use Theorem 2.5.

    So R̂ is the pointwise largest acceptance region that is sound for every target compatible
with the evidence. For intersection-closed classes it is the closure algorithm (Natarajan, 1987;
Helmbold et al., 1990), and it coincides with El-Yaniv and Wiener’s consistent selective strategy
and with the “accept” side of KWIK (El-Yaniv and Wiener, 2010a; Li et al., 2008a). Its soundness
is deterministic and distribution-free. Every question below is about completeness: when does
R̂(D) reach R∗ ?

5.2    Anchors: when positive data suffice
Definition 5.3 (Anchor). [T1 Thm 5.1] A finite T ⊆ R∗ is an anchor (for R∗ in H) if every R ∈ H
with T ⊆ R satisfies R ⊇ R∗ .

Theorem 5.4 (Eventual completeness iff finite anchor). [T1 Thm 5.1, as repaired] (a) For finite
D ⊆ R∗ : R̂(D) = R∗ iff D contains an anchor. (b) Let the data be a text for R∗ (finite
D1 ⊆ D2 ⊆ · · · ⊆ R∗ with t Dt = R∗ ), or i.i.d. draws from a law with support R∗ . If R∗ has an
                          S

anchor, R̂(Dt ) = R∗ for all large t (a.s. in the i.i.d. case); if not, R̂(Dt ) ̸= R∗ for every t.

Proof. (a) If D ⊇ T for an anchor T , every R ∈ VS(D) contains R∗ , so R̂(D) ⊇ R∗ ; the reverse
inclusion is Lemma 5.2(a). Conversely, if R̂(D) = R∗ , then D is itself an anchor, since every
R ∈ H containing D lies in VS(D). (b) A text eventually contains any finite T ⊆ R∗ , and then
(a) and monotonicity give exactness forever. S is countable, so i.i.d. data with support R∗ form
a text a.s.

    “Finite” matters in (a): in the class of Proposition 5.5(c), R̂(2N) = 2N but 2N has no anchor.

Proposition 5.5 (Anchors versus tell-tales). [T1 Prop 5.2, as repaired] Call a finite T ⊆ R∗ an
(Angluin) tell-tale if no R ∈ H satisfies T ⊆ R ⊊ R∗ . (a) Every anchor is a tell-tale. (b) If
H ∪ {∅} is closed under intersections, every nonempty tell-tale is an anchor; the empty one need
not be (H = {{1}, {2}}, R∗ = {1}). As existence conditions the two coincide when R∗ ̸= ∅. (c)
In general the converse fails: H = {2N} ∪ {Rn }n∈N with Rn = {0, 2, . . . , 2n, 2n + 1} is identifiable
in the limit from text, but 2N has no anchor, so on a text for 2N the cautious verifier never
becomes complete.

   Proof in Appendix D.2. Credit. Identification in the limit is due to Gold (1967), and tell-tales
characterize it for indexed families (Angluin, 1980). Anchors are the “⊆-tell-tales” of strong-
monotonic learning, where every conjecture is contained in the target (Lange and Zeugmann,

                                                  38
1992; Lange et al., 2008). As we recall their characterization (we have not re-checked its exact
form), strong-monotonic learnability is, up to effectivity, the existence of anchors. What is new
here is only the reading of anchors as the completeness condition of certified verification. For
intersection-closed classes, anchors exist iff R∗ is the closure of a finite set (L1 §2.2).
    Anchors exist in the schema classes H1 , Hktag and Hk (fixed k), by finite elasticity (Wright,
1989; Motoki et al., 1991); infinite targets have none in superfinite classes such as unions of
unboundedly many schemas (Section 5.4).

Proposition 5.6 (Finite elasticity gives anchors). [L1 §2.3, Lemma] Say H has infinite elasticity if
there are w0 , w1 , · · · ∈ S and R1 , R2 , · · · ∈ H with {w0 , . . . , wn−1 } ⊆ Rn ̸∋ wn for all n ≥ 1, and
finite elasticity otherwise. If H has finite elasticity, every R∗ ∈ H has an anchor.

    The proof (Appendix D.2) builds an infinite elasticity sequence from the failure of each
finite subset of R∗ to be an anchor. A ground step is an instance of only finitely many schemas
up to renaming (a generalization is never larger than its instances), so H1 has finite thickness,
hence finite elasticity. Finite elasticity survives unions of at most k members (Wright, 1989, as
corrected by Motoki et al. 1991). So H1 , Hktag and Hk all have anchors, as do richer classes such
as Shinohara’s length-bounded elementary formal systems with at most k clauses (Shinohara,
1994), which model learning theorem sets rather than steps (L1 §2.5).

Reading. “Learning which inferences are valid” can mean guessing R∗ in the limit, as in Gold’s
paradigm, or certifying a step: every hypothesis compatible with the evidence licenses it. Under
search only the second protects. A learner that guesses 2N in Proposition 5.5(c) converges, but
it is unsound whenever the truth is a large Rn , and a prover can find the difference. An anchor
is a finite body of usage that, given H, justifies a rule for all of its uses. This is the kind of
justification by imitation that survives adversarial search. It is relative to H, a point we return
to after Corollary 5.11.

5.3    Rates: cited rules and the coupon collector
Formal proofs cite their rules (natural-deduction rule names, Lean lemma names, Metamath
labels). Take tagged calculi Hktag (Definition 2.9) with target rules σ1 , . . . , σk , and let vi =
|vars(σi )|. The version space factors over tags (T1 Thm 3.6; Appendix D.1). So the cautious
verifier is the per-rule lgg verifier R̂(D) = i {i} × inst(lgg(D(i) )), where D(i) is the tag-i data (a

term with D(i) = ∅ contributes nothing), and rule i is identified once its data satisfy the witness
events (R) and (D) of Lemma 2.7. Human data are i.i.d. pairs (I, σI Θ) with P(I = i) = πi and
Θ ∼ Λi given I = i. For distinct metavariables x, y of σi let

    Table 5 compares the bound with simulation. The coupons are the witness events of each

Remark 5.8 (Teaching sets, corpora, and distribution dependence). [L1 §4.2, §9] Given 2vi distinct
constants, two instances of σi whose metavariables are replaced by two disjoint sets of distinct
constants satisfy (R) and (D). So a textbook with two well-chosen examples per rule is an anchor,
while a corpus needs about ln(kci /δ)/(πi ρi ) steps. No distribution-free completeness rate exists,
even for single schemas. For every m = 2h , the complete binary trees of depth log2 m with one
distinguished leaf carry a law under which every sound learner has completeness error above
1/2 after fewer than m/2 samples (Appendix D.4). Soundness is distribution-free; completeness
cannot be, and πi ρi is what the distribution must supply.

Reading. A rule used in a fraction πi of human steps, with variability ρi , is pinned down after
about ln(kci /δ)/(πi ρi ) steps; further examples add nothing. What makes this cheap is the citation
of rules, which lets the version space factor. The user’s note that mathematics was formalized
by inventing a language into which proofs can be translated has a precise counterpart here: a
language with named rules makes inference learnable from imitation at coupon-collector cost.
Without names the problem becomes a union problem, with a diversity condition (Theorem 5.9)
and, with escalation, worst-case cost at least ⌊(n − 1)/k⌋k instead of at most k(n + 1) for steps
of size at most n (T1 Thms 3.6–3.7, Section 4).

5.4    Uncited rules: a diversity condition

hypothesis, the class is superfinite in Gold’s sense, and R̂(D) = D: the cautious verifier never
generalizes (Gold, 1967). For rule i let Subi (D) := {θ|vars(σi ) : σi θ ∈ D}, with failure sets

By Lemma 2.7, a nonempty set Q of substitutions has lgg{σi θ : θ ∈ Q} ≡ σi (call Q generic) iff

Proof of (a). Let R = l≤k inst(τl ) ∈ VS(D). Partition Subi (D) by the least l with σi θ ∈ inst(τl ).
                          S

If no class were generic, each would lie in a failure set, and k failure sets would cover Subi (D). So
some class Q is generic, and its τl ⪰ lgg{σi θ : θ ∈ Q} ≡ σi . Hence R ⊇ R∗ . A failure set contains
at most one member of a pairwise-generic set, so k + 1 such members cannot be covered.
   Part (b) bounds the VC dimension of complements of k-unions of failure sets by di and applies
the ε-net theorem (Haussler and Welzl, 1987; Blumer et al., 1989) with ε = ζi (Appendix D.5).
The condition ζi > 0 asks for more instantiation variety than k rules can absorb, and some such
condition is needed:
Proposition 5.10 (When variety is too low). [T1 Remark after Thm 5.4, as repaired] (a) Suppose that
for some i the data in inst(σi ) not covered by the other k ′ − 1 rules are covered by k − k ′ + 1
specializations of σi of the forms σi [x 7→ f (z̄)] (z̄ fresh) or σi [y 7→ x], and that some instance
of σi lies outside these specializations and the other rules. Then R̂Hk (D) ̸= R∗ . (b) For k ′ ≥ 2,
low variety need not block identification: for k = k ′ = 2, R∗ = inst(p(x)) ∪ inst(q(y)) and
D = {p(a), p(b), q(a), q(b)}, R̂H2 (D) = R∗ , although x takes only 2 = k roots.
    For k ′ = 1, (a) says that if a metavariable is only ever instantiated with at most k root
symbols (and others are possible), identification fails while this persists. Proofs in Appendix D.6;
(b) is also checked by brute force (T1-code/union_remark.py). For k ′ ≥ 2 the exact threshold,
between “no cover by k failure sets” and “a cover by k − k ′ + 1 plus an uncovered instance”, is
open. So “allow up to k rules” is a real assumption about the diversity of usage: without rule
names, imitation must see each rule used in more ways than the k − k ′ + 1 spare schemas of the
class can absorb (for k ′ = 1, e.g. more than k root symbols at each metavariable). This is the
positive-data face of the escalation lower bound for untagged unions (Section 4).

5.5   Systematic generalization: finite usage fixes inferential role
Corollary 5.11 (Systematic out-of-distribution generalization). [T1 Cor 5.5] If R̂(D) = R∗ , the
verifier accepts exactly R∗ and the reasoner derives exactly ClR∗ (B) for every B, including
conclusions that need derivations far longer, and judgments far larger, than any in the data,
independently of the distribution that produced the data.
Proof. R̂(D) = R∗ gives ClR̂(D) = ClR∗ .
    The corollary is trivial; Theorems 5.7 and 5.9 supply its hypothesis, and Lemma 5.2 keeps
the verifier sound before that. The result is a learner of inference rules from imitation that is
sound at all times against adaptive search, exact after about maxi ln(kci /δ)/(πi ρi ) cited steps,
and systematic: correctness on terms of size 40 is the same event as on terms of size 10. A
learned step scorer comes with none of these guarantees (Sections 3 and 5.8).

                                                           41
Reading. Under an inferentialist picture of meaning (Brandom, 1994), the meaning of a
connective is its inferential role, the steps in which it may figure. Corollary 5.11 says that
a finite set of uses (an anchor; for cited natural-deduction rules, two generic instances per
rule) fixes that role for unbounded use. Three qualifications make this precise. (i) The role
is fixed relative to H. An anchor leaves open, within H, only hypotheses that contain R∗ , so
the cautious verifier accepts exactly R∗ ; it says nothing about hypotheses outside H. Quus-like
alternatives (Kripke, 1982) are excluded by the choice of hypothesis class, not by the data. Which
of simplicity, coherence and convention does that work is the subject of Sections 6, 8 and 13.
(ii) On clean data, overgeneralizations such as tonk are never adopted, because the lgg is the least
generalization covering usage. The learner adds to a connective’s role only what usage forces.
This is a learning-theoretic analogue of Belnap’s conservativeness requirement (Belnap, 1962),
and it is why simplicity is the wrong bias here (Proposition 3.9). (iii) The role fixed is that
of practice: a fallacy used systematically becomes part of it (Section 5.6). Contexts. Contexts
are part of judgments (Definition 2.1), so steps inside suppositions are data like any other, and
the theorems apply to them unchanged as long as contexts are encoded as first-order terms
(Remark 5.12). But they must be in the data. Theorems determine a structural consequence
relation only up to the interval between derivable and admissible rules, which is non-degenerate
for intuitionistic logic (the Kreisel–Putnam rule is admissible but not derivable). So, unless the
hypothesis class enforces a deduction theorem, assertions alone cannot in general teach reasoning
under hypotheses (L1 §§8.2–8.3).
Remark 5.12 (Side conditions). [T1 §5, extensions] With guards from a finite family Φ (Definition 2.4),
the guarded single-schema class stays intersection-closed. Its closure is the lgg with the strongest
guard true on all data (Lemma 2.8), anchors exist, and the escalation budget of Section 4 grows
by at most |Φ| (Appendix D.7). But a positive example shows that a guard held, never that it
was needed. A guard is learned only from the absence of violating uses. It is lost as soon as
one human use omits it (Example 2.12), and it cannot be learned at all if Φ cannot express it.
Set-valued contexts and binders lie outside first-order anti-unification; modulo AC it is finitary
rather than unitary, and the bounds above would need re-proof (T1 §5).

5.6    Noise: sporadic and systematic errors
Human data contain errors, and the two kinds behave completely differently.

Proposition 5.13 (One error collapses the lgg). [T1 Prop 6.1] Let D consist of ∧E1 instances
s(and(Aj , Bj ), Aj ) whose Aj do not all have the same root, plus one invalid step s(φ, ψ) tagged
∧E1 , with φ not a conjunction. Then lgg(D) = s(z, w) with z ̸= w: the learned rule accepts every
one-premise step, and from any derivable formula it derives everything.

Proof. The premise column mixes the root and with the root of φ; the conclusion column has
mixed roots because the Aj do. Both become metavariables, distinct because the columns differ
(and(A1 , B1 ) ̸= A1 ).

(computed; T1 §6) A mis-tagged “p0 ∨ p1 ▷ p0 ” does exactly this. A 15% rate of affirming the
consequent, cited as MP, turns MP’s lgg into s2 (imp(v0 , v1 ), v2 , v3 ): from A → B and anything,
infer anything. This is the tonk of Theorem 3.2, produced by learning: the lgg is least among
generalizations of all the data, errors included.
    The remedy for sporadic errors is to let hypotheses miss a few data. Counting with multiplicity,
let VSe (D) = {R ∈ H : |D \ R| ≤ e}, and with per-tag budgets VS(ei ) (D) = {R ∈ H :

                                                  42
|D(i) \ R(i) | ≤ ei for all i}, where D(i) , R(i) are the tag-i parts. The trimmed verifiers accept
T                  T
  VSe (D), resp. VS(ei ) (D).

Theorem 5.14 (Trimmed version space). [T1 Thm 6.2, as repaired] (a) For any H: if D contains at
most e invalid steps, then VSe (D) ⊆ R∗ ; likewise VS(ei ) (D) ⊆ R∗ if at most ei invalid steps
                            T                         T

are tagged i, for each i. The verifier is then sound against every prover. (b) For tagged schemas
and the per-tag verifier, suppose that for each i: (i) at most ei invalid steps are tagged i; and
(ii) the valid rule-i samples are ei -robustly generic: there are more than ei of them; for every
x, f , more than ei of them have root(Θx) ̸= f ; and for every x ̸= y, more than ei of them have
Θx ̸= Θy. Then VS(ei ) (D) = R∗ .
                   T

Proof idea. (a) R∗ lies in the trimmed version space. (b) A hypothesis in VS(ei ) covers all but at
most ei valid rule-i samples. By (ii) the rest is nonempty and still satisfies (R) and (D), so its
tag-i schema generalizes σi . Full proof in Appendix D.8.
    Two features of (b) were added in verification, with counterexamples in Appendix D.8. The
clause “more than ei of them” is the only content of (ii) for ground rules, and without it the
theorem fails for them. The budgets must be per tag: one global budget lets a hypothesis spend
everything on one rule. (computed; T1 §6) With e = 1 (resp. 2), one (resp. two) mis-tagged errors
are removed exactly; with e = 1 and two errors the lgg collapses again.
    Under i.i.d. noise the budgets can be set from the rates. Suppose each datum independently is
a valid rule-i step with probability βi (with Θ ∼ Λi ), or an invalid step tagged i with probability
αi (with arbitrary law); in terms of Definition 2.10, βi = πi (1 − ai ) and αi = πi ai .

The threshold ρi βi > αi is necessary up to a factor of two, because a noisy law for one target is
a clean law for another.
Definition 5.16 (Robust soundness). [T1 Thm 6.4] A positive-data verifier maps the data so far
(and private coins) to an acceptance set Acct . It is (δ, α)-robustly sound for H if for every R∗ ∈ H
and every law Q on S with Q(S \ R∗ ) ≤ α, i.i.d. data from Q give P[∃t : Acct ̸⊆ R∗ ] ≤ δ.
Theorem 5.17 (Indistinguishability). [T1 Thm 6.4] Let the verifier be (δ, α)-robustly sound for H,
let R∗ ⊊ R′ both lie in H, and let Q be a law on R′ with Q(R′ \ R∗ ) ≤ α. Under i.i.d. data from
Q, every q ∈ R′ \ R∗ has P[∃t : q ∈ Acct ] ≤ δ. So if δ < 1, the verifier is not complete for the
target R′ under its clean law Q.
Proof. Q is an admissible noisy law for the target R∗ , and q ∈ Acct implies Acct ̸⊆ R∗ ; the data
law is the same in both scenarios.

   This is trivial once set up, in the spirit of Kearns and Li (1993). For schemas it yields a
matching necessity. Let mi,x := maxf P[root(Θx) = f ].

                                                  43
Lemma 5.18 (Balanced splits). [T1 §6 after Thm 6.4, repaired] ρi,x ≤ 1 − mi,x ≤ 2ρi,x , and the
constant 2 is attained by the uniform law on an odd number of symbols.

Corollary 5.19 (Witness frequencies must exceed the noise rate). [T1 §6 after Thm 6.4, repaired] Let
H = Hktag and let Q be a clean law for R∗ under which a datum is a rule-i instance σi Θ, Θ ∼ Λi ,
with probability βi . If a (δ, α)-robustly sound verifier with δ < 1 has PQ [R∗ ⊆ Acct ] > δ for some
t, then for all x, f and all x ̸= y,

                     βi P[root(Θx) ̸= f ] > α      and      βi P[Θx ̸= Θy] > α.

With per-tag noise budgets, α may be read as αi .

Proof idea. If a witness frequency is at most α, then Q is an α-noisy law for the target in
which σi is replaced by σi [x 7→ f (z̄)] (resp. σi [y 7→ x]), and Theorem 5.17 applies. Proofs in
Appendices D.10 and D.11.
    So Theorem 5.15 asks for ρi,x βi > αi and ri,xy βi > αi (plus margins), while necessity asks
for (1 − mi,x )βi > αi and ri,xy βi > αi . By Lemma 5.18 the two differ by at most a factor of two
in the root frequencies and agree on the equality frequencies; the factor two in the lemma is
attained. The trade-off is unavoidable. A positive-data verifier that tolerates error rate α must
refuse every generalization supported by less than a fraction α of the data. With human error
rates around 1%, the generality of a rule used in less than about 1% of steps needs negative
information.

Corollary 5.20 (Systematic errors are rules). [T1 Cor 6.5] Let human errors be schema-generated:
the data law is Q = (1 − α)Q0 + αQerr , with Q0 supported on R∗ and Qerr on inst(τ ) for a
schema τ with inst(τ ) ̸⊆ R∗ . Suppose R′ := R∗ ∪ inst(τ ) ∈ H, as holds whenever H is closed
under adding a schema (tagged calculi with a fresh tag for τ ; untagged unions with room for one
more schema). Then Q is a clean law for R′ at every error rate α. Hence a (δ, α)-robustly sound
verifier with δ < 1 accepts each q ∈ inst(τ ) \ R∗ with probability at most δ under Q. So with
probability at least 1 − δ it never accepts all of R′ , a calculus in which τ is a genuine rule used at
the same rate.

Proof. Q(S \ R′ ) = 0; apply Theorem 5.17 to R∗ ⊊ R′ .

    So no positive-data method, with any amount of data, can both tolerate schema-generated
errors at rate α and learn genuine rules used at rate α. This also removes the protection of
the Bayes-conservative verifier (Section 4). Its time-uniform guarantee assumes a well-specified
likelihood. A model of errors as sporadic is misspecified for Q, and the clean hypothesis R′ fits
Q exactly.
Remark 5.21 (Imitation identifies practice). [T7 Prop 2.2; T1 §6] With latent but distinct fallacy
tags (Definition 2.10), the theorems above apply to the practice ΣP = Σ∗ ⊔ F tag by tag. On the
event of Theorem 5.15 the trimmed verifier accepts exactly RP = R∗ ∪ RF , including an instance
of every fallacy outside Sound(R∗ ), which a prover will query. In classical logic with a complete
Σ∗ and a pure fallacy schema, one closed instance with true premises and a false conclusion
trivializes the reasoner (T7 Prop. 2.2; cf. Proposition 3.5). If fallacies are cited under genuine
rules’ names, a learner with one schema per tag collapses the rule instead (Proposition 5.13).




                                                  44
Reading. The line between sporadic and systematic errors is exact. Positive data remove an
error only if it is rarer than the witness frequencies of the genuine rules. Rule-like errors, at
any rate, need negative information: escalation (Section 4), coherence (Section 6), or the world
(Sections 7 and 11). For the user’s program, imitation learns which inferences are used, and
which are valid only insofar as use is valid. Getting from the first to the second is what the
coherence and world channels are for, and positive data provably cannot do it. In the language
of justification, imitation can certify a step as licensed by the practice, never as licensed by
anything beyond it. This is why the cautious tier of Section 7 is sound only relative to practice,
and why coherence must act on a separate, bold tier.

5.7    An exactly solvable case: dimensions as inferential roles
Remark 5.22 (Dimension inference). [L9 §3.1, TC1] (proof sketch) The dimension of a physical quantity
is its inferential role: which sums and equations it may enter. Here learning meaning from
positive examples is solvable in closed form. Let q1 , . . . , qn be quantity symbols, and let observed
equations be polynomial (or Laurent) equations among them; each monomial has an exponent
vector in Zn . A grading is a homomorphism g : Zn → A into an abelian group (deg qi = g(ei )),
and an equation is g-homogeneous if all its monomials have the same degree. For observed
equations E, let K(E) ≤ Zn be generated by the differences of exponent vectors of monomials in
a common equation. (i) The gradings making E homogeneous are the homomorphisms vanishing
on K(E), so Zn → Zn /K(E) is the finest one: every consistent grading factors through it. It is
computed by Smith normal form (modulo torsion for rational exponents). The cautious verifier
for “dimensionally admissible” accepts exactly the equations whose differences lie in K(E) (in its
saturation, if gradings must be torsion-free). (ii) The admissible sets form an intersection-closed
class indexed by subgroups of Zn . Since these are finitely generated, every target has a finite
anchor, and Theorem 5.4 gives exact identification once the data exhibit generators of the target’s
relations. This is an instance of learning algebraic closure systems from text, where Noetherianity
is finite generation (Stephan and Ventsov 2001; L1 §2.5). (iii) Gold’s limitation appears in
miniature: positive data never refute a coarser grading (“everything dimensionless” is always
consistent), but the cautious learner never adopts one. The collapse of Proposition 5.13 appears
too: one wrong equation adds a spurious generator and merges dimensions. (iv) The residue is
conventional. The finest grading is fixed only up to isomorphism, i.e. up to the choice of base
dimensions, which no inference can see. Whether a constant such as c is dimensional is a choice
between data sets: natural units add c = 1 and coarsen the grading, and Gaussian units absorb a
dimension that SI keeps. These are coherent, empirically equivalent alternative meanings, settled
by convention (Section 13). Dimension types implement the type-checking side (Kennedy, 1994);
Buckingham’s Π theorem is the invariant-theoretic face (Buckingham, 1914). Sketch of (i)–(ii) in
Appendix D.12. This remark has not been through the project’s adversarial verification.

5.8    Imitation in practice
Experiment A (Section 12) learns school algebra over partial terms (Example 2.12) from simulated
human derivations (computed; code/results/algebra_learning.md). The target has 41 rewrite
schemas, 11 of them guarded. The corpus has sporadic noise (5% random corruptions), five
systematic fallacies (each committed with probability 0.3 per opportunity), or both. The learner
is a heuristic relative of the cautious verifier (MDL-clustered anti-unification within rule-name
buckets, support threshold two); by default it infers no guards, and a variant takes the most


                                                  45
specific guard true on all instances (Lemma 2.8). Means are over three seeds; soundness is
measured, not proved. Positive data alone gave the following. Shapes are recovered where
Lemma 2.7 predicts: a rule’s shape was recovered in 0% of (rule, run) pairs with one human
instance, 67% with two, 92% with three or four and 100% with five or more; on clean data, all
41 shapes for N ≥ 200 derivations. Generalization is systematic: on clean data at N = 500,
valid out-of-distribution steps (terms of about 40 nodes, against about 10 in human steps) are
accepted as often as in-distribution ones (1.00 and 1.00), whereas a gradient-boosted step classifier
trained on the noisy corpus with validity labels accepts 89% of valid in-distribution steps, 73%
of valid out-of-distribution ones, and about half of the invalid ones. Guards are not displayed:
on clean data at N = 500, the default learner ends with 11.7 ± 0.5 unsound active schemas
(essentially the 11 guarded rules without guards) and accepts 34% of invalid out-of-distribution
steps; most-specific guards reduce this to 0.3 ± 0.5 unsound schemas (those whose guard the
guard language cannot express), but with fallacies present the count rises to 5.3 ± 0.5, with 22%
false accepts. Fallacies reach support: at N = 500 each of the five fallacies is learned as an
active schema in 6/6 runs, with and without guard inference (Corollary 5.20). A fixed support
threshold is no soundness guarantee: under sporadic noise, spurious unsound schemas grow with
N , reaching 102 ± 11 at N = 500 with threshold one and 18.5 ± 3.6 for the untagged learner with
threshold two (3.0 on clean data); hence the linear budgets of Theorem 5.15. With noise and
fallacies together at N = 500, the cited-rule learner recovers all 41 shapes, yet 15.7 ± 0.5 active
schemas are unsound, 51% of invalid out-of-distribution steps are accepted, and a white-box
prover finds 65 ± 2 accepted invalid steps. Experiment B shows the same pattern for propositional
natural deduction (Section 12).
    In short, positive data alone recover rule shapes and generalize out of distribution, but they
never display a guard, and systematic fallacies reach support. What they fix is: rule shapes, given
an anchor (at coupon-collector cost for cited rules, under a diversity condition for uncited ones);
their extension to unbounded use; side conditions only as “strongest guard true on all data”; and
sporadic errors rarer than every witness frequency. What they cannot fix is: the choice of H, the
necessity of a guard, generalizations rarer than the error rate, and any schema-generated error.
Sections 6 and 7 show what coherence and world feedback do about the latter.


6    Coherence: what contradictions fix
Imitation cannot tell a systematic human error from a rule (Corollary 5.20). The user’s second
ingredient is a coherence loss: penalize having good arguments for both P and ¬P . In our
setting this is channel (C) of Definition 2.13: a family A of designated contexts is certified
coherent, and a derivation of ⊥ from one of them shows that one of its steps is invalid. This
section determines what that signal fixes. In brief: coherence is negative data, but only for bold
learners, and it controls only one of two error types (Section 6.1); it pins the target exactly
when the target sits at the top of its coherent version space, as classical propositional logic does
by Post-completeness, and it is blind for intuitionistic logic (Section 6.2); in arithmetic it is
Popperian (Section 6.3); Carnap’s categoricity problem is Gold’s problem in the dual space, and
is solved by data with two denials, not by coherence (Section 6.4); coherence catches tonk but
not non-conservativity (Section 6.5); what survives is the set of coherent uniform alternatives,
the Kripkensteinian residue (Section 6.6); and the use of coherence here is eliminative, which the
coherentist impossibility theorems do not touch (Section 6.7).
    Most results are known in substance or trivial once set up. Post-completeness (Post, 1921;


                                                 46
Wójcicki, 1988), Glivenko’s theorem, Gold’s limit-point argument, halving and weighted majority
(Littlestone, 1988; Littlestone and Warmuth, 1994), the multiple-conclusion answer to Carnap
(Carnap, 1943; Shoesmith and Smiley, 1978), Belnap’s conservativity (Belnap, 1962) and the
Σ1 /Π1 asymmetry of limiting inquiry (Putnam, 1965; Gold, 1965; Kelly, 1996) are classical. New,
as far as we know, are the reduction of contradictions to coalitions and the two-sided trade-off,
the denial-rank trichotomy and twelve-datum tell-tale as learning results, the rule-level analysis
of complete theories, and the systematic reading of all of these as statements about what a
coherence loss can teach.
    Cn2 is classical propositional consequence (φ ∈ Cn2 (X) iff every Boolean valuation making
X true makes φ true), Taut = Cn2 (∅), and CFm (X) = Fm is the trivial operator. Hypotheses
are step sets (Definition 2.2) or finitary consequence relations h, with h(A) = {φ : A ⊢h φ}; h is
A-coherent if A ⊬h ⊥ for all A ∈ A, read as non-triviality h(A) ̸= Fm in languages without ⊥.
Unless stated otherwise A is fixed and known, independently of the target.

6.1     Coherence as negative data
Here hypotheses are step sets R in a countable class H with prior w, R w(R) = 1, and target
                                                                          P

R∗ ; every A ∈ A is target-coherent. An argument π from A to ⊥ is a derivation of ⊥ from A;
Steps(π) is its finite set of steps.
Definition 6.1 (Online coherence protocol). [T2 §2.2] In round t the learner holds a version space
VSt ⊆ H (VS0 = H) and announces an accepted set R̂t , whose steps the reasoner may chain.
The environment, possibly adversarial, possibly the learner’s own proof search, then either (P)
presents some s ∈ R∗ , and the learner deletes every R ∈ VSt with s ∈    / R; or (N) exhibits an
argument π from some A ∈ A to ⊥ with Steps(π) ⊆ R̂t , and the learner deletes every R ∈ VSt
with Steps(π) ⊆ R; or does nothing. A (P)-round with s ∈    / R̂t is an incompleteness error; an
(N)-round is a detection.
Lemma 6.2 (Negative bags; trivial). [T2 Lemma 2.1, as repaired] Let π be an argument from a
designated A to ⊥. (a) Steps(π) ̸⊆ R∗ . (b) Every R ⊇ Steps(π) has ⊥ ∈ ClR (A), witnessed by
π. (c) The designation alone excludes every h with A ⊢h ⊥; deletion by the witness removes
only {h : Steps(π) ⊆ h}, which can be strictly smaller even for consequence relations (target
Cn2 , A = {p}, π with steps p ▷ q, q ▷ ⊥, and h the least consequence relation containing p ▷ ⊥).
Lean: negative_bag

Proof. (a) Otherwise chaining gives ⊥ ∈ ClR∗ (A). (b) Chain in R. (c) h(X) = X ∪ {⊥} if p ∈ X,
else h(X) = X.

    So a contradiction is a multiple-instance label (Dietterich et al., 1997): “not all of these steps
are valid”. For a learner that can test A ▷ ⊥ ∈ h by membership it collapses to an ordinary
negative example and π is only a checkable witness; the bag structure matters for step verifiers
that are not cut-closed, such as process reward models and ensembles.
Proposition 6.3 (Caution receives no signal). [T2 Prop 2.9(a)–(c), as repaired] (a) If R̂t ⊆ R∗ , no
detection can occur; since deletions never remove R∗ , a certified-sound learner (R̂t ⊆ VSt )
                                                                                               T

never sees one. (b) Certified soundness can be expensive. For k ≥ 1 and distinct atoms ai , pi
(1 ≤ i ≤ k), ℓ1i = pi , ℓ0i = ¬pi , let hb = {Γ ▷ φ : {ai → ℓbi i }i≤k ∪ Γ |= φ} for b ∈ {0, 1}k , and
A = {∅}. Every certified-sound learner makes 2k − 1 = |H| − 1 incompleteness errors on some
text. (c) If A is fixed and known and membership in and A-coherence of each h ∈ H are decidable,
coherence is prior knowledge (a restriction of H), not data.

                                                 47
    (a) and (c) are immediate; for (b), ▷ i (ai → ℓ1−c      ) lies in hb iff b ̸= c, so presenting it
                                             W            i
                                                       i
while hc survives is an error that deletes only hc (Appendix E.2). So coherence feedback is a
dividend paid on boldness, and with A fixed and coherence decidable it is not even data. It is an
informative signal when consistency is undecidable (Π1 ; Theorem 6.33), so that a prover must find
contradictions for the learner, and when designations are data about the target (Proposition 6.12).

Oligarchic acceptance. How should a bold learner accept, so that each contradiction teaches
it something? Not by voting: stepwise majority over coherent verifiers can derive ⊥ while
implicating no member (Theorem 3.12).

Definition 6.4 (Oligarchic halving). [T2 §2.2] OH chooses a coalition St ⊆ VSt with w(St ) ≥
1                            T
2 w(VSt ) and announces R̂t = R∈St R.

Theorem 6.5 (Oligarchic halving). [T2 Thm 2.2] Under OH with w(R∗ ) > 0: (i) R∗ is never
deleted; (ii) there are at most log2 (1/w(R∗ )) detections (at most log2 |H| for the uniform prior),
and the bound says nothing about incompleteness errors; (iii) if R∗ ∈ St , then ClR̂t (B) ⊆ ClR∗ (B)
for all B; (iv) always ClR̂t (B) ⊆ ClR (B) for all R ∈ St , so chaining never leaves what each
coalition member accepts.                                               Lean: thm_2_2 (finite classes)


Proof. (i) (P)-data lie in R∗ , and (N)-deletions spare R∗ by Lemma 6.2(a). (ii) In a detection
every R ∈ St contains R̂t ⊇ Steps(π), so all of St is deleted and w(VSt+1 ) ≤ 12 w(VSt ); (P)-rounds
never add weight. So w(R∗ ) ≤ 2−D after D detections. (iii), (iv) R̂t ⊆ R for R ∈ St .

    The halving argument is that of Barzdin and Freivalds (1972) and Littlestone (1988); new
is only the reduction of a bag to a coalition. The choice of St is the learner’s style (“simplest
half” is MDL; “boldest half” is the boldest oligarchic choice). Oligarchy needs truth maintenance:
lemmas certified by an old coalition must be re-validated, or the union over coalitions reproduces
the doctrinal paradox across time.

Proposition 6.6 (Per-round halving forces oligarchy). [T2 Prop 2.3, as repaired] Let VS be countable
with w(VS) > 0, and assume every finite P ⊆ R̂ with P ̸⊆ VS (i.e. legal for some target in

VS) can occur as Steps(π). Then every possible detection deletes at least 12 w(VS) iff R̂ ⊆ S

for some S ⊆ VS with w(S) ≥ 12 w(VS).                        Lean: prop_2_3 (finite version spaces)


    Proof in Appendix E.1. The scope is per-round halving against a worst-case prover; coalitions
of weight 13 still give log3/2 (1/w(R∗ )). Under universal domain, independence and unanimity, and
for suitably rich agendas, aggregation rules yielding deductively closed collective judgments are
oligarchic (Gärdenfors, 2006; Dietrich and List, 2008); Proposition 6.6 is the learning-theoretic
face of that result. Designations can be wrong (“air pressure is 0” plus full background knowledge
is inconsistent), so we also need a robust version.
