                                       ln 1−δ
                                           δ    1−π    1 − δ′
                                 t ≥       1  ≥     ln        .
                                       ln 1−π    π       δ

The first bound is attained.

                                                  71
Proof idea. On samples whose tag is not µ, the scenario-1 law is (1 − π) times the scenario-2 law,
so on the event Et that no µ-tag appears among the first t samples, P1⊗t = (1 − π)t P2⊗t . By (OE)
the learner’s whole view has law Pj⊗t (dx)Kt (x, ·) with one kernel Kt for both scenarios; integrate
the acceptance probability over Et . The learner that accepts τ -instances at time t iff no µ-tag
has occurred has scenario-1 error exactly (1 − π)t and scenario-2 completeness 1. Appendix F.10.
    (OE) holds for Ref d and W (Lemma 7.4(c)), for the fixed prover and red team, for learners
that do not escalate, and for TTL’s tier (Proposition 7.13(a); escalation answers are not added
to the sample). It fails for practice-following escalation, and then the conclusion is false: some
µ-instance lies outside Sound(R{σ,τ } ), a human following practice {σ, τ, µ} confirms it and one
following {σ, τ } rejects it, so one escalation separates the scenarios. Linear programming over all
tag sequences of length t ≤ 6 confirms both facts: the best learner under (OE) attains exactly
min(1, δ(1 − π)−t ) in all 108 cases, and with one practice-following escalation the cap 0 becomes
1 (computed; two_point_bounds.py). A lower bound for learners with membership queries to the
human would need the refuter to be unknown among many candidates; that is open.
    A concrete instance: A1 designated, no world, σ = ∧I, τ = AC, µ = MP. The closure of A1
under AC alone is {q, p → q, p → ⊥, p}; under {∧I, AC} it is infinite but contains no ⊥, because
∧I creates no implications and AC needs an implication with consequent q or ⊥; {∧I, MP} is
classically sound; and AC followed by MP derives ⊥.

Reading. Suppose modus ponens is rare, with frequency π. No learner that is sound whether
or not affirming the consequent is a fallacy, and that learns about the practice only from the
stream, can accept AC in the world where it is genuine before about π −1 ln(1/δ) samples: roughly
the time by which MP, the rule that would expose AC if it were a fallacy, has been seen with
confidence. TTL’s noise-free burn-in λ−1 ln(cK/δ), with λ ≤ πρMP , matches this up to the factor
ln K and the variability ρMP . This is the standard two-point rare-event bound behind PAC
sample-complexity lower bounds; the only new point is that the relevant rare event is the absence
of the refuter. Waiting is not timidity: a rule can be presumed valid only once the rules that
could impeach it have had their chance.

Theorem 7.21 (Blame: world or bilateral evidence is needed for completeness). [T7 Thm 5.6, as repaired]
(a) Two-point core. Let Σ0 ∪ {µ} and Σ0 ∪ {τ } be clean at every depth and {µ, τ } a d-conflict.
    Compare scenario 1 (target Σ0 ∪ {µ}, F = {τ }) and scenario 2 (target Σ0 ∪ {τ }, F = {µ}),
    with equal tag frequencies and a fixed prover. The data and all oracle answers have identical
    laws, and some µ-instance s, genuine in scenario 1, is accepted at any given time in
    scenario 1 with probability at most δ by every learner that is δ-sound in both scenarios. So
    µ, which lies in Colld in scenario 1, is collateral for every sound learner.
(b) Stable depth: the fallback is optimal among schema-level policies. Let d ≥ d0 and suppose
    that on G the audit falls back at B0 . Then for every g ∈ Cd (B0 ) there are an admissible
                                                                S
                                         (d)
    target M ⊆ B0 with g ∈  / M and Fres (M ) = ∅, and an instance sg ∈ inst(g) \ Sound(RM ).
    Consequently every learner that is δ-sound under every admissible target accepts sg at any
    fixed time with probability at most δ, under every admissible target, the true one included;
    every genuine g ∈ Colld (B0 ) is withheld on some instance by every such learner; and a policy
    that asserts RA′ for a possibly random A′ ⊆ ΣP , and is δ-sound under every admissible
    target, includes each schema outside TTL’s A with probability at most δ. The same holds
    on the descent path, with M := A.



                                                72
 (c) At finite depth the fallback can be strictly suboptimal (computed; thm56_finite_d.py). Take
     no world, the practice {MP, AC}, and A = {[A1 : ∅], [A2 : ∅]} with A2 = {φ, ⊥ → φ},
     φ = q ∧ (q ∧ q). The minimal refutation sizes are 9 for {MP, AC} (on A1 ) and 13 for {AC}
     (on A2 , where AC infers ⊥ from φ and ⊥ → φ), and {MP} is clean at every depth. For
     9 ≤ d ≤ 12 the only minimal conflict is {MP, AC}, the descent is blocked, and TTL returns
     A = ∅. Yet the admissible targets are only {MP} and ∅, and asserting RMP is sound under
     both (under ∅, MP is residual). For d ≥ 13 = d0 , TTL returns {MP}.

Proof idea. (a) A µ-instance outside Sound(RΣ0 ∪{τ } ) exists, or the refutation of {µ, τ } would
transfer to the clean set Σ0 ∪ {τ } (Lemma 3.1); in scenario 2 it is unsound and non-residual,
and the scenarios are indistinguishable. (b) Pick, by Lemma 7.8(b), a maximal clean M ⊆ B0
avoiding g; at stable depth it is clean at every depth, hence admissible. Appendix F.11.

The witness: → versus ←. Take A = {A1 }, no world, Σ0 = ∅, µ = MP and τ = AC. {MP}
is classically sound and A1 satisfiable; {AC} is clean (the closure above); and AC followed by
MP derives ⊥. Scenario 2 is literally the hypothesis that “→” denotes converse implication.
Under that reading AC is modus ponens for ←, MP is affirming the consequent for ←, and A1
reads {q, q ⊃ p, ⊥ ⊃ p}, which is consistent. Coherence alone cannot tell which way the arrow
points. Either of two things breaks the symmetry: world feedback, since the closed instance
(⊥ → ⊥, ⊥ → (⊥ → ⊥) ▷ ⊥) of AC is falsified; or a single denial, since in the designated position
[A1 : p] AC alone derives the denied p, and the conflict becomes {AC}. Both make scenario 2
violate (WS) (computed; hilbert_blame.py).

A Hilbert illustration (computed; hilbert_blame.py, formulas with at most 7 leaves over {p, q, ⊥},
the least bound at which S has instances). Take the practice {K, S, MP, DN, AC} with A = {[∅ :
∅], [A1 : ∅]}. The minimal conflicts are {AC, K} and {AC, MP}, the diagnoses are {AC} and
{K, MP}, and the collateral is {K, MP}. The conflict {AC, K} is a four-step derivation from no
premises: K gives ⊥ → (p → ⊥) and (p → ⊥) → (⊥ → (p → ⊥)), AC gives p → ⊥, and AC again
gives ⊥. Both conflicts are minimal at every depth beyond their sizes, and with no world and
no trusted steps every descent is blocked; so by (b), K and MP are necessarily collateral at full
depth. Whether {S, DN, AC} is clean at every depth, i.e. whether the collateral also contains S or
DN, is open; no matrix with at most four values witnesses its cleanness (exhaustive backtracking,
5.0M nodes for four values with three designated). A prior that prefers fewer fallacies, Quine’s
maxim of minimum mutilation, picks {AC} over {K, MP}; in the core {MP, AC} case it is silent
unless it uses frequency, which does not track validity (Section 8).

Reading. This is the blame problem in its sharpest form. A community whose practice contains
both modus ponens and affirming the consequent has an incoherent practice, and coherence
correctly detects it. But coherence does not say which half of the practice is the meaning of
“if”. A learner that is sound whatever the answer must give up both as rules, keeping at most
their shared instances (Proposition 7.6(c)). What settles the direction is the world, or a denial:
the bilateralists’ rejection (Smiley, 1996; Rumfitt, 2000; Restall, 2005) does syntactically what a
counterexample object does semantically.

Proposition 7.23 (Truthful designation is needed). [T7 Prop 5.8; T2 Prop 7.1, Thm 2.5] If a desig-
nated position violates (WS), descent can blame a genuine rule: with target {MP} and the bad
position [a, a → b : b], MP is blamed. Under structurality, one classically inconsistent designated
context forces the loss of a classical schema everywhere (Proposition 11.10). Proposition 7.12
bounds the damage when at most m positions, not known which, violate (WS), and Theorem 6.7
is the soft version. Nothing bounds the damage of an unknown number of mis-designations.


7.7    Specializations: what provably works for formal mathematics
Classical propositional logic. The language has ¬, ∧, ∨, →, ⊤, ⊥. Judgments are formulas,
or sequents whose contexts are lists with explicit structural rules (set or multiset contexts would
need anti-unification modulo associativity and commutativity, for which the bounds of Section 5
are not proved). All schemas are pure (Definition 3.4); by Proposition 7.19 this cannot be dropped.
Σ∗ is any finite set of classically valid pure schemas that is complete for classical consequence, F
any finite set of classically invalid pure schemas, the world is JW = closed formulas with W =
truth value (“computation”), and A consists of truthful positions and contains [∅ : ∅].

Lemma 7.24 (Closed-instance refutability; Post’s substitution). [T7 Lemma 6.1] A pure schema τ is
classically invalid iff some substitution of ⊤/⊥ for its metavariables yields a closed instance whose
premises are W -true and whose conclusion is W -false. As a one-step derivation this instance

                                                     74
has size at most |τ |, and there are at most 2v(τ ) candidates, v(τ ) the number of metavariables.
Lean: Post.schema_invalid_iff_closedRefutation


Proof. (⇐) A falsified instance is invalid. (⇒) Let a valuation v make the premises of an instance
τ θ true and its conclusion false, and put cx = ⊤ if v(θx) = 1, else cx = ⊥. By induction on τ ,
every subformula of τ [cx /x] has the value of the corresponding subformula of τ θ under v, and
closed formulas have the same value under every valuation.

    This is the substitution step of Proposition 3.5 and Theorem 6.13, and essentially Post (1921).
For sequents with a context metavariable Γ, substitute the one-element list [⊤] or [⊥] according to
  V
v( Γθ). A brute-force check on 30,000 random pure schemas (16,309 invalid) found no mismatch
(computed; lemma61.py), and the formula case is formalized in Lean.

Corollary 7.25 (TTL on classical propositional logic is exact). [T7 Cor 6.2, as repaired] Let d ≥
maxτ ∈F |τ |, and let Ref d return the first closed ⊤/⊥ instance, in a fixed enumeration, of a
schema of B with W -true premises and a W -false conclusion, and NONE if there is none. This
oracle is exact for refutations of size at most d and deterministic; its refutations are one-step and
fully evaluable, so every descent is unblocked; and its answers do not depend on d ≥ maxτ |τ |.
Then on G, with P(G) ≥ 1 − δ: (a) the minimal conflicts are exactly the singletons {τ }, τ ∈ F ,
                                   (d)
so (SBd ) holds and Colld = Fres = ∅; (b) the tier abstains before N1 and accepts exactly
R∗ afterwards, so for every premise set B the reasoner derives exactly ClR∗ (B) = CnCPC (B),
for derivations of any length and formulas of any size (Corollary 5.11), against any prover;
(c) the audit makes at most |F | + 1 oracle calls and at most |F | successful descents, and at
most (|F | + 1) σ∈ΣP 2v(σ) closed-instance checks ( σ∈ΣP 2v(σ) with caching); (d) the tier is
                 P                                      P

truth-sound at all times (Corollary 7.15).

Proof. If every schema of B is valid, a classical valuation agreeing with the truthful position and
with W falsifies no step of RB , so there is no refutation at any size; otherwise Lemma 7.24 gives
a one-step refutation of size at most |τ | ≤ d. Hence each {τ } is a conflict, and since every conflict
                                                                                               (d)
contains a fallacy (Lemma 7.4(b)) it contains a singleton conflict. So (SBd ) holds and Fres = ∅,
and Theorem 7.14(iii) gives A = Σ∗ ; completeness of Σ∗ gives (b). Each descent removes the
schemas of which the falsified step is an instance, all fallacies and at least one.

    The counts are upper bounds: one falsified step can be an instance of several fallacies, as
(⊤, ⊥ → ⊤ ▷ ⊥) is of both AC and (⊤, A → ⊤ ▷ A), and one descent removes both. The
instance checks include those of the genuine schemas, which certify the final NONE. The oracle
need not return a smallest refutation; exactness and determinism are all that the audit uses, and
part (iv) of Theorem 7.14 is trivial here.

Reading. This is the user’s first success criterion in its strongest form. From imitation of
human proofs containing systematic fallacies and sporadic slips, plus truth-table evaluation of
closed formulas, the learner identifies exactly the classical calculus the community uses, with
probability at least 1 − δ, after N1 human steps, with at most |F | refutations. On that event
it is sound against every prover at every moment, and its reasoner derives exactly classical
consequence, out of distribution.

Proposition 7.26 (Coherence alone on CPC: sound but lossy; one denial repairs it). [T7 Prop 6.3; (b) as repaired]
Let JW = ∅ and A be truthful. (a) If [∅ : ∅] ∈ A, every τ ∈ F lies in some minimal conflict once


                                                  75
                                                                             (d)
d ≥ dτ , because Σ∗ ∪ {τ } derives ⊥ from ∅ (Proposition 3.5). Hence Fres = ∅ for d ≥ maxτ dτ ,
and TTL is truth-sound. (b) The collateral can be nonempty, and then TTL’s fallback is incom-
plete, necessarily so at stable depth (Theorem 7.21(b)). Let Σ∗ be a complete Hilbert system for
the full language containing K and MP, F = {AC} and A = {[∅ : ∅]} or {[∅ : ∅], [A1 : ∅]}. Then
{AC, K} is a minimal conflict at every depth at least the size of the four-step derivation above,
and with A1 designated so is {AC, MP}. Every descent is blocked, the fallback runs at ΣP , and
K (with A1 , also MP) lies in Colld for all large d: every sound learner using coherence alone
must withhold it as a rule. (c) Bilateral repair. If for each τ ∈ F , A contains a counterexample
position [Π′ : φ′ ], where Π′ ▷ φ′ is a closed falsified instance of τ , and d ≥ maxτ |τ |, then (SBd )
holds and A = Σ∗ .

   Proof in Appendix F.13. Reading. In CPC closed evaluation refutes exactly the schemas
that coherence refutes; by Post, both refute all invalid ones. The whole difference between the
channels is localization: computation names the culprit, a contradiction only a list of suspects.
A counterexample position is a syntactic object, a partial valuation, so “bilateral coherence with
denials” and “world feedback with descent” are the same resource in two notations.

Complete decidable theories. Let T be complete and decidable, such as real closed fields
(Tarski, 1951), Presburger arithmetic (Presburger, 1929), ACFp or dense linear orders, with M |=
T . Judgments are sequents Γ ⊢ φ of first-order formulas, with W (Γ ⊢ φ) = [M |= ∀x̄( Γ → φ)],
                                                                                       V

decidable by quantifier elimination, so JW = J. Σ∗ is a finite schema calculus, sound and
complete for T -consequence on sequents, including →-introduction and ∀-introduction.

Lemma 7.27 (Validity is truth preservation). [T7 Lemma 6.4] (a) Sound(R∗ ) is exactly the set of
W -truth-preserving steps. (b) A schema is a fallacy iff it has a W -falsified instance, so each
τ ∈ F has a smallest falsified instance, of some size fτ ≥ dτ .

Proof idea. If all premises are true they are derivable, since T is complete, so ClR∗ of them is
the set of true sequents; if one is false, Σ∗ derives its false universal closure, whose negation T
proves, and then everything. Appendix F.14.

Corollary 7.28 (TTL on complete decidable theories, conditional on realizability). [T7 Cor 6.5, as repaired]
Suppose in addition that Σ∗ and F lie in a schema class for which the identification of Theo-
rem 5.15 holds, for instance first-order patterns, possibly with decidable side conditions such as
the eigenvariable condition of ∀I. Let d ≥ maxτ fτ , [∅ : ∅] ∈ A, and let Ref d search the instances
of size at most d of the schemas of B for a W -falsified one. Then everything in Corollary 7.25
                          (d)
holds verbatim: (SBd ), Fres = ∅, A = Σ∗ , truth-soundness at all times on G, and at most |F | + 1
oracle calls.

    The realizability hypothesis is not established for the listed theories. The rule ∀E, from ∀xφ
infer φ[t/x], is not a first-order pattern, because its conclusion is a substitution result; with t
arbitrary it is not a higher-order pattern in the sense of Miller (1991) either. The lgg of three ∀E
instances is (∀x(z0 +z1 = z2 )▷z3 +z4 = z5 ), with the invalid instance (∀x(0+0 = 0)▷0+0 = S0),
and more data only generalize it further (computed; ind_lgg.py). The usual axiomatizations
of RCF and ACFp contain degree-indexed families, and Presburger arithmetic an induction
or congruence schema; none is a single first-order pattern. Dense linear orders are finitely
axiomatized, so there only the quantifier rules matter. Without realizability, the identified
schema for a non-pattern rule is in general an over-general lgg. If it has an instance outside


                                                  76
Sound(R∗ ), as for ∀E and induction, the total oracle W falsifies one (Lemma 7.27(b)), and an
audit of sufficient depth removes it with singleton blame. So TTL stays sound, eventually so
under the depth schedule, but A lacks the rule and exactness fails. Two repairs are open: an
encoding with auxiliary decidable judgments Sub(φ, x, t, ψ) (“ψ is φ[t/x]”) evaluated by W ,
carried out below for induction; or an identification theorem for higher-order-pattern schemas.
    An honest caveat: why learn at all? Here W is already a sound and complete step checker, so
learning is not needed for epistemic access. TTL adds two things. It identifies which calculus the
practice uses. And it makes deployed checking fast: schema matching takes linear time, while
real quantifier elimination is doubly exponential in the worst case (Davenport and Heintz, 1988),
deciding RCF sentences is possible in exponential space (Ben-Or et al., 1986), the first-order
                                                                                           Ω(n)
theory of (R, +) already needs exponential time, and Presburger arithmetic needs 22             time
(Fischer and Rabin, 1974). The audit calls W only on the few instances needed to falsify each
fallacy. This dividend is real but modest. A restricted oracle shows the division of labour more
sharply. If W evaluates only quantifier-free sentences with rational constants and the logic is
trusted, the fallacy τ = (▷¬∃x (x · x = 1 + 1)) has no instance that this oracle can evaluate, so the
world alone never refutes it and Lemma 7.27(b) fails; designating the true sentence ∃x (x·x = 1+1)
catches it, since τ and the trusted ¬E derive ⊥, and descent backward through ¬E blames τ
alone. Over R, coherence with a designated truth catches what this computation misses; over N
the converse holds (Lemma 6.19). The real-closed-field core of olympiad algebra and geometry
would be covered by Corollary 7.28 once its realizability hypothesis is met; trigonometric steps
are not, since (R, +, ·, sin) defines Z.

Arithmetic. The language is that of PA and M = N. Ttrust is first-order logic: the logic is fixed,
and non-logical axiom schemas are learned (the logic can be learned first, at the propositional
level, by Corollary 7.25). The world is JW = ∆0 sentences with W = computation, and A consists
of truthful positions. A practice axiom schema is genuine iff all its instances are true in N, so Σ∗
may contain PA together with true extras such as Con(PA), and (WS) holds with V = truth in
N. Since R∗ consists of axiom steps, the relevant target closure is ClR∗ ∪Ttrust (Theorem 7.14(i)).
Ground axioms (those of Q, each Con(Tj ), ¬Con(PA)) are realizable; the induction schema is
not (part (e)). Parts (a)–(d) and (f) concern the audit and hold for any P̂ = ΣP .
Theorem 7.29 (Arithmetic is Popperian). [T7 Thm 6.6, as repaired in two rounds]
 (a) False Π1 axioms get singleton blame. Let τ have a false instance ∀x̄ θ(x̄) with θ ∈ ∆0
     and least counterexample n̄. The refutation “⊢ ∀x̄θ (by τ ); ⊢ θ(n̄) (by trusted ∀E)”, with
     W (θ(n̄)) = 0, has an unblocked descent that blames τ alone, by the backward rule through
     ∀E. With the prefer-unblocked oracle, τ is removed and no PA axiom is put at risk. For one
                                                             (d)
     quantified variable with m free occurrences in θ, τ ∈
                                                          / Fres once d ≥ 2|θ| + m(n + 1) + O(1);
     for k variables (k + 1) |θ| + m(n + 1) + O(k) suffices, n the largest component of n̄.
 (b) Under presumption of validity, false Σ1 axioms survive. For τ = (▷¬Con(PA)), Σ∗ = PA
     and positions with ∆0 assertions and denials, Σ∗ ∪ {τ } is clean at every depth, so τ ∈ Fres
     is asserted forever.
 (c) Popperian Σ1 -caution removes syntactically Σ1 residue. Fix a computable prenex normal
     form with negations pushed into the ∆0 matrix, and assert an instance of a practice axiom
     with Σ1 normal form ∃x̄ θ only once W has verified a witness. Then false axioms with Σ1
     normal form, ¬Con(PA) included, are never asserted, and true ones are asserted after a
     witness search, with no uniform time bound; for schematic axioms, acceptance becomes a

                                                 77
     semi-decision. The policy is syntactic: ∀y ∃p (Prf PA (p, ⌜⊥⌝)∨y ̸= y), equivalent to ¬Con(PA)
     but with a Π2 normal form, escapes it and is residual.
 (d) Beyond Σ1 there is a permanent residue. False Π2 or Σ2 axioms τ with Σ∗ ∪ {τ } clean at
     every depth (for example, Σ∗ ⊇ Q and Σ∗ ∪ {τ } ∪ A ∪ ¬D consistent for every designated
     [A : D]) admit no finite refutation and survive, and no computable policy sorts the true from
     the false ones in the limit (Theorem 6.22(e)). The same holds for randomized learners that
     are correct in the limit with probability above 12 (proof sketch).
 (e) Finite reflection-extended practices are learned under the floor, given a realizable encod-
     ing of induction. The induction schema is not a first-order pattern: its lgg has false
     instances, and the audit refutes it with singleton blame, so the asserted calculus would
     lack induction. Add auxiliary decidable judgments Sub(φ, x, t, ψ) to JW and write induc-
     tion as Sub(φ, x, 0, a), Sub(φ, x, Sx, b)▷ ⊢ a ∧ ∀x(φ → b) → ∀xφ, with the human data
     recording the Sub premises. This is a first-order pattern, recovered by the lgg of generic
     instances. Then under (Floor) a practice has at most min(K, ⌊1/(2∆)⌋) axiom schemas,
     and Tk = PA + Con(PA) + · · · + Con(Tk−1 ) is identified from positive data for every k
     within that bound. Without a floor there are no uniform sample bounds: comparing the
     targets Tk−1 and Tk , where the tag Con(Tk−1 ) has frequency π, under (OE), a learner that
     accepts ⊢ Con(Tk−1 ) at time t with probability at most δ under Tk−1 accepts it under Tk
     with probability at most 1 −(1 − δ)(1 − π)t ; so if δ + δ ′ < 1, acceptance with probability at
     least 1 − δ ′ needs t ≥ ln 1−δ
                                 δ′
                                        1
                                    ln 1−π , unbounded as π → 0. This bound is tight.
 (f) Reflection as designation. If A asserts Con(PA), then ¬Con(PA) becomes a singleton conflict.

Proof idea. (a) is Lemma 7.9. (b) PA + ¬Con(PA) is consistent by Gödel’s second theorem
(Gödel, 1931) and proves every true ∆0 sentence and no false one (Lemma 6.19). (d) reduces
to Shoenfield’s limit lemma (Shoenfield, 1959) and Post’s hierarchy theorem. (e) is the mirror
image of Theorem 7.20. Appendix F.15.
     Three computations support (a) and (e) (computed; ind_lgg.py, ind_sub_lgg.py,
two_point_bounds.py). For θ(x) = x < S 5 0 the refutation’s judgments ∀x (x < S 5 0) and
S 5 0 < S 5 0 have sizes 10 and 13. The lgg of four induction instances has the false instance
(0 + 0 = 0) ∧ ∀x(0 + 0 = x → 0 + S0 = Sx) → ∀x(0 + 0 = x), while the lgg of five generic
Sub-encoded instances equals the pattern up to renaming. And the floor bound in (e) is attained,
by linear programming over all tag sequences, in all 108 cases checked. The bound in (e) differs
from that of Theorem 7.20 because the rare tag now lives in the scenario where acceptance is
sound: the learner must see the tag at least once, so the bound scales with ln(1/δ ′ ), not ln(1/δ).
Over classes containing Tω , identification in the limit fails altogether (Theorem 6.21); Tω has
infinitely many axioms, so it lies outside the finite practices considered here.

Reading. In arithmetic TTL achieves exact, localized, Popperian falsification of false Π1 axioms;
exclusion of syntactically Σ1 errors under Σ1 -caution; identification of finite reflection-extended
practices, given a realizable induction schema; and soundness at all times relative to the stated
residue. It does not achieve, and no computable procedure can achieve, the elimination of false
Σ2 axioms consistent with the practice, or uniform sample bounds without a frequency floor; and
under presumption of validity it keeps false Σ1 axioms such as ¬Con(PA). The Gödel–Rosser
alternatives are the Kripkensteinian residue of arithmetic (Theorem 6.38(iii)), and TTL leaves
it exactly where Section 6 located it, now with explicit sample sizes, refutation budgets and

7.8   Simulation evidence
The two-tier learner on a propositional practice. The script ttl_sim.py runs TTL in
the setting of Corollary 7.25. The practice has nine genuine tagged natural-deduction rules (∧I,
∧E1,2 , ∨I1,2 , MP, modus tollens, disjunctive syllogism, double-negation elimination) and four
fallacies with their own tags (affirming the consequent, denying the antecedent, conversion, “or”
as “xor”). Sporadic noise at rate α = 0.01 is mis-tagged at random, and the trim budget is e = 2.
The audit is the per-schema closed-instance oracle. An adversarial prover enumerates every step
over a pool of ten formulas and checks it by truth table. Table 10 shows the result of re-running
the script (seed 0; it is deterministic across hash seeds).
    The positive-only cautious tier asserts the fallacies as soon as they are anchored (Proposi-
tion 7.5): it is unsound in 72 of 80 runs. TTL is unsound in none of the 80, and exact in all 20
runs at N = 250 and at N = 500. Excess noise on a fallacy tag is harmless, since whatever schema
the trimmed lgg produces for that tag is world-refuted and dropped anyway. The untrimmed
learner is kept sound by the audit, which refutes the collapsed lgg of Proposition 5.13, but it
loses whole tags, and loses more of them as N grows. The script’s scope is limited: it tests the
singleton-conflict case of CPC with a fixed budget and a per-schema world audit. It does not
exercise descent on multi-step refutations, the fallback, or the N1 formula; a simulation of the
full audit is future work.

The propositional experiment. Experiment B (Section 12) runs a related but distinct
pipeline on sequent-style natural deduction with 13 target rules: a support-thresholded version-
space learner, coherence pruning on five designated contexts with blame by a minimum-weight hit-
ting set, and optional sparse world feedback (two observed valuations). It is not an implementation
of TTL, but it exercises the phenomena of this section (computed; code/results/prop_learning.md;
final numbers in Section 12). Over the 72 runs of its main learner, the adversary derived ⊢ ⊥ in
32 runs after imitation alone, and in none after coherence pruning or after world feedback. A
bold learner over classical natural deduction, searching with Post probes (substituting ⊥/¬⊥
along a falsifying row, as in Lemma 7.24) and blind search, refuted every one of 118 invalid
structural candidate schemas by an explicit derivation of ⊢ ⊥, and accepted all 59 valid ones.
The non-structural rules ⊢ p and p ⊢ q survived, as Proposition 7.19 predicts; most unsound
rules that survive pruning in any run mention specific atoms. One observed valuation already

                                                79
pinpoints the guilty rule for schematic rules, so world feedback needed fewer pruning rounds
than coherence alone (5.0 versus 9.1 on average): localization, not refutation. Over intuitionistic
natural deduction the bold learner accepted excluded middle, double-negation elimination, re-
ductio, Peirce’s law, weak excluded middle and Dummett’s linearity, each underivable in IPC
(Kripke countermodels), and rejected the classically invalid candidates. That is Theorem 6.17 in
action: coherence pins CPC from above and is blind for IPC, so a bold extension of IPC drifts to
CPC-like logics, and only the cautious, imitation-based tier keeps the intuitionistic calculus.

7.9    Reading in the user’s terms
“Learning which inferences are valid” is learning the audited practice, in three acts done
by different channels. (1) Imitation finds the practice’s rules. Generalizing human steps to the
least general schemas that cover them, with trimming against sporadic slips, finds the schemas
the practice uses, its systematic fallacies included. It is the only channel that says anything
positive about a rule (Lemma 7.4 and Proposition 7.16). (2) Confrontation sorts the rules: an
audit, not a loss. The learner presumes the practice valid and searches for derivations, from
accepted positions, of ⊥, of a denied claim, or of a computably false claim. Each shows that
some rule used is wrong; the sound response is to withhold every rule in a minimal such set
(Lemma 7.8), and when the derivation can be checked step by step against the world the set
shrinks to the culprit (Lemma 7.9). A “coherence loss” that merely penalizes having arguments
for P and ¬P does the first half of this, and only the first half. (3) Assertion comes last: after
the audit, and after enough practice has been seen to rule out a rare rule that would expose
a fallacy (Theorem 7.20). Outside a data event of probability at most δ, the resulting checker
cannot be fooled by any prover (Theorem 7.14(i)). In propositional logic it accepts exactly the
valid steps (Corollary 7.25); in complete decidable theories it does so too, if the target calculus is
realizable in the schema class (Corollary 7.28); in arithmetic it works in Popper’s sense and no
further (Theorem 7.29).

“An argument is valid when it is a sequence of valid inferences.” This is Lemma 3.1,
and it cuts both ways. One accepted bad step poisons every argument; and chaining is also what
lets a contradiction be traced back to a step. Descent is the user’s own “go back to what caused
the contradiction”, made into an algorithm with a guarantee.

“Learning meanings” is identification up to the residue. On an inferentialist reading
(Brandom, 1994), the asserted calculus is the learned inferential role. Imitation fixes which
role the community uses. Coherence fixes it only up to the choice of which member of each
conflicting set to give up; in the MP/AC example that choice is literally whether “→” means
→ or ←, and no sound learner using coherence alone can assert modus ponens as a rule there,
although it can accept those instances of MP that are also instances of AC. This ambiguity is
not a gappy-valuation phenomenon (Section 6.4); it is a blame phenomenon: the practice is
incoherent, and coherence does not say which half of it is the meaning. The world (computation,
counterexample objects) or a denial settles the direction. That is a precise sense in which the
user’s “hooking of a model onto the world” is needed beyond inferential role: not to make the
role coherent, but to make it the intended one when practice conflicts with itself. What remains
after all three channels is the residue Fres , the fallacies coherent with the rules and invisible
to the available world. It is empty for propositional logic and, given realizability, for complete
decidable theories. In arithmetic it contains the non-standard alternatives, such as ¬Con(PA)

                                                 80
under presumption of validity, which only a designated reflection commitment or a Popperian
asymmetry removes, and past Σ1 nothing computable removes.

Justification. A step accepted by TTL carries a three-part warrant: it instantiates a schema
the practice uses robustly; that schema survived an audit of stated depth against stated designated
positions and a stated world; and the argument is a chain of such steps. None of the three parts
is a proof of validity. Together they give a guarantee relative to explicit parameters (d, A, W, δ).
Raising d changes the warrants only finitely often, sometimes restoring a rule withheld as
collateral (Theorem 7.14(iv)), and no computable procedure can avoid the possibility of change
(Theorem 7.22). This matches the user’s view that justification is an infinite endeavour, in a form
where every intermediate stage is sound relative to what has been checked. It is also Goodman’s
mutual adjustment of rules and inferences (Goodman, 1955), with each adjustment licensed by a
theorem; Section 13 takes this up. This section leaves open the query complexity of sound blame,
the full-depth status of the Hilbert rival {S, DN, AC}, blame with priors, the size of coherence
witnesses in first-order logic, and TTL with anchored physical contexts as designated positions
(see also Section 14).


8        Simplicity and normativity from imitation
Imitation identifies practice, errors included: for positive data, a systematic error generated
by a schema is a rule (Corollary 5.20). This section examines a different remedy, proposed in
K. Hänni’s notes, and answers the question it raises.1 The note asks how one can understand
“what someone means when using a word. as opposed to becoming a predictor of what they
would say”. It proposes
          “an even stronger simplicity prior than solomonoff. eg if there are explainable mistakes on a
          simple model, you want the simple model that doesn’t predict the mistakes. [. . . ] let’s just
          do a version of the simple hypothesis with noise, and then penalize the likelihood term less.”

The note anticipates an objection: in sequence prediction, a steeper penalty favours a universal
hypothesis that absorbs everything. It suggests, while “very much uncertain”, that this pathology
“is not present in the function case, if [. . . ] each input has to sample its own random bits”. It
asks whether, given the models it lists, the good model “could be a vertex of the convex hull of
the set of attainable (hypothesis complexity, expected neg log likelihood) tuples”. And it adds:
“I consider it very much plausible that there is some other pathology”.

Answers in brief. The function-case intuition and the convex-hull argument are both right:
private randomness makes every hypothesis pay for mixing per input, the product-model frontier
has the hoped-for kink (proved for parity models), and selection is a threshold on rates (risk
reduction per bit) along the lower hull (Theorems 8.3, 8.6 and 8.11 and Lemma 8.8). But there
are other pathologies. Universality returns at block granularity (Proposition 8.5); selection is by
rate, not validity, so cheap fallacies out-rate rare valid rules and guards erode (Theorem 8.15
and Propositions 8.16 and 8.18); and the right steepness is not identifiable from imitation data
(Theorem 8.17). Steeper simplicity is a good third filter beside coherence and the world, and the
note’s own alternative, “you specify properties of the thing or notion”, is a separate channel that
    1
        The note ‘beating solomonoff induction at grokking a notion’ (unpublished). Quotations are verbatim.



                                                         81
removes cheap fallacies at every steepness, provided the valid rules that expose them are kept
(Theorem 8.20 and Proposition 8.23). Section 8.7 gives the verdict in the note’s terms.
    Most results are standard or trivial once set up. As far as we know, three are mildly new:
the product-model frontier and its kink, the rate threshold for arbitrary hypotheses, and the
realizability of every convex frontier after rescaling. The pathologies and the division of labour
are easy once stated; their value lies in the precise statements.

8.1    The proposal, formalized
Inputs x ∈ X are drawn from µ, and labels lie in a finite set Y . The teacher is a channel P ∗ (y | x).
In the note’s picture, P ∗ arises from an intended norm h◦ through sporadic noise and systematic
error. Data are i.i.d. from µ × P ∗ . Normativity asks the learner to output h◦ , or its deterministic
core, rather than P ∗ . In Section 8.6, x is a proof context and y the next human step; then P ∗ is
the practice RP of Definition 2.10, viewed as a channel.

Definition 8.1 (Randomness and objective). [T5 Def 1.1, §1.3] A product hypothesis (private
randomness) is a conditional distribution h(· | x), with likelihood i h(yi | xi ). A shared-
                                                                        Q

randomness hypothesis is a distribution P (y1:N | x1:N ); sequence prediction is its causally
conditioned case. In a block hypothesis, the input at position i of a block (e.g. a document)
includes the block’s earlier pairs. The note’s loss is λℓ(h) + i≤N − log2 h(yi | xi ), with λ > 1
                                                               P

growing with N . Writing λ = cN and dividing by N gives Jˆc = cℓ + R̂N , and its population
version Jc = cℓ + R, where R(h) = Eµ×P ∗ [− log2 h(y | x)]. Let Φ(c) = inf h Jc (h). In the idealized
version, ℓ = K (prefix complexity) over total programs with rational outputs, and ph is a shortest
program for h. In the computable version, ℓ is a prefix code on a countable class. A component
costing k bits that lowers R by r has rate r/k.

    The steepness c is measured in bits per sample per bit of description. Where a result compares
the class with a semimeasure or an infinite mixture, the class is enlarged to lower-semicomputable
semimeasures and computable real-valued predictors.

Lemma 8.2 (Empirical selection tracks population selection). [T5 Lemma 1.2] In the computable
version, suppose h(y | x) ≥p2−B for all h. With probability at least 1 − δ, simultaneously for
all h, |R̂N (h) − R(h)| ≤ B (ℓ(h) ln 2 + ln(2/δ))/(2N ). On this event, each minimizer ĥ of Jˆc
satisfies, for every fixed h0 ,
                                        q
                    Jc (ĥ) ≤ Φ(c) + 2B ((ℓ(h0 ) + B/c) ln 2 + ln(2/δ))/(2N ).

In particular, a unique population minimizer with gap γ is recovered once this bound is below γ.

    This is a standard Occam-type bound; the proof is Hoeffding’s inequality with a Kraft union
bound (Appendix G.1).
    Fixed versus growing steepness. With fixed λ, c → 0, so every component of positive rate
is eventually absorbed, error or not [T4 Prop 7.1(i); L11 Prop 2.2] . Imitation with a fixed simplicity
weight therefore converges to the teacher’s practice. This is the learner’s version of Cohen’s view
that normative standards are a systematization of ordinary judgments (Cohen, 1981a). With
λ ∝ N , as the note proposes, selection is asymptotically stable. So the obstruction found below
is rate inversion, not sample size.



                                                  82
Relation to the literature. The tempered posterior ∝ 2−ℓ i h(yi | xi )η has its MAP at
                                                                            Q

the minimizer of ℓ/η + N R̂N , so the note’s λ is 1/η: a learning rate η < 1 (Grünwald, 2012;
Grünwald and van Ommen, 2017; Zhang, 2006; Bissiri et al., 2016; Bhattacharya et al., 2019).
With λ = cN the posterior tends to the non-concentrating Gibbs measure ∝ 2−(ℓ+R/c) (Catoni,
2007), whose mode the proposal selects. SafeBayes learns η from data to repair misspecification;
Theorem 8.17 suggests why no such criterion finds the note’s η, since an imitation model is well
specified for the teacher (our reading, not checked against the papers).
    Kolmogorov’s structure function hx (α) = min{log |S| : x ∈ S, K(S) ≤ α} lies, to logarithmic
precision, above the sufficiency line K(x) − α, and essentially every compatible shape occurs
(Vereshchagin and Vitányi, 2004) (exact side conditions unchecked by us); see also Gács et al.
(2001); Vereshchagin and Shen (2017). Set and probability models share randomness. The
product-model structure function ρ× (k) = min{R(h) : ℓ(h) ≤ k} need not have slope −1 and can
kink (Theorem 8.6): that is the note’s insight in this language. All sufficient statistics tie at
λ = 1, so λ = 1 + ϵ selects roughly the minimal one and larger λ selects insufficient models (proof
sketch), as the note wants, since the errors are structure. The objective cℓ + R is the algorithmic
rate–distortion Lagrangian with log-loss distortion (Vereshchagin and Vitányi, 2010); classical
two-part MDL (Rissanen, 1978; Barron and Cover, 1991; Grünwald, 2007) has λ = 1 (whether
Barron and Cover also use λ > 1 we have not checked).

8.2    No universal hypothesis in the function case
The note asks whether it is “just failing to construct the right ‘universal hypothesis’ ”. The
answer is no as long as randomness is private and inputs carry no other labels, and yes as soon
as they do.

finite class of deterministic labelers, x1 , . . . , xN inputs, and C(x) = {f (x) : f ∈ C}.

Proposition 8.4 (The sequence pathology, exactly). [T5 Prop 2.3, threshold corrected] Let M be a
universal lower-semicomputable conditional semimeasure on label sequences, with M ≥ 2−K(q)−c0 q
for every computable q (Li and Vitányi, 2008), and let cM = K(M). For every λ > 1,
              λK(M) − log M(y1:N | x1:N ) ≤ λcM + K(q) + c0 − log q(y1:N | x1:N ).
This beats q’s own objective whenever K(q) > cM + (cM + c0 )/(λ − 1).
Proof. Take logarithms in the dominance inequality and add λcM .

   As λ → ∞ the threshold tends to cM , not 0. In the function case, even the universal
conditional semimeasure m(y | x) ≍ 2−K(y|x) is a fixed predictor [T5 Thm 2.4] . For binary Y we
have K(y | x) = O(1), so R(m) ≥ log2 1−21−κU > 0 for every teacher, where κU is a machine
constant.
Proposition 8.5 (Per-block pricing: in-context universality). [T5 Prop 2.6, as repaired] Suppose the
input at position i of block b contains the block’s earlier pairs. Let (qθ ) be a finite family of
computable, rational-valued “persona” models qθ (y | x, history), with rational weights θ wθ ≤ 1.
                                                                                          P

The in-context Bayes mixture hw , with weights w(θ | history) ∝ wθ k<i qθ (yb,k | ·), is a single
                                                                       Q

function hypothesis with K(hw ) ≤ K(w, (qθ )) + O(1). On every block,
                    X                                h            X                       i
                            − log hw (yb,i | ·) ≤ min log w1θ +         − log qθ (yb,i | ·) .
                                                 θ
                      i∈b                                         i∈b

The block bound holds for every countable family, but for an infinite one hw need not be computable.
Proof. Let Zi = θ wθ k<i qθ (yb,k | ·). Then hw (yb,i | ·) = Zi+1 /Zi , so the block likelihood
                  P          Q

telescopes to Z|b|+1 /Z1 ≥ Z|b|+1 ≥ wθ i qθ (yb,i | ·), using Z1 = θ wθ ≤ 1. This holds for

history-dependent personas and inputs too, because everything is conditioned on the realized
history. For a finite rational family, hw has rational outputs and is computed by one program
from w and (qθ ).

    So hw pays log(1/w) per block, not per input. Suppose that 20% of humans each have an
idiosyncratic error, “in error contexts play wrong move j”, with j uniform among 216 moves.
Each such rule has rate about 0. Yet an O(1)-bit copier beats the norm model once a block has
two error contexts (computed; T5 c5). With two error contexts, the copier pays 3.03 bits per error
context; with sixteen, it pays 1.33; the norm model pays exactly 4.635 at any block length.

Reading. An LLM imitating whole proof documents is a block model: it can learn each author’s
systematic errors in context. A step checker whose input is a single step (Π, j) (Section 2) is a
function model. The step is therefore the granularity at which the note’s defence holds. This
gives a second, independent argument for the step-level architecture of Sections 3 and 4.

8.3    The kink: shared versus private randomness
In sequence prediction, the note says, “it’s pretty much just 1 bit paying for 1 bit”. In the
function case one might instead “see some point at which the derivative changes”. We prove this
for the note’s example, made rigorous.

                                                     84
Setting. Let X = {0, 1}d , n = 2d and µ uniform. The simple model is a parity fa (x) =
⟨a, x⟩ mod 2 with K(a) ≥ d. The error set E has |E| = pn, p < 1/4 and K(p) = O(log d)
(the last assumption   was added after verification), and the teacher is y ∗ = fa ⊕ 1E . Let
              n
LE = log2 pn ≈ nH(p), and let δE = LE − K(E | a, K(a)) be the randomness deficiency of E,
which is O(log n) for most E. Write ŝh (a′ ) for the correlation of h’s vote h(0 | x) − h(1 | x) with
       ′
(−1)⟨a ,x⟩ . The good model is fa with flip rate p; it has K ≤ d + κ′ with κ′ = O(log d), and risk
H(p). The bad model y ∗ has risk 0.
Theorem 8.6 (Kink). [T5 Thm 2.5, as repaired] There is a machine constant κ such that the following
hold.
 (a) Shared randomness has (almost) no kink. Let Pu be the uniform shared-randomness
     mixture of the 2u noisy parities that agree with a on its first d − u coordinates. Then
     |K(Pu ) − (d − u)| ≤ κ′ and − log Pu (y ∗ ) = u + nH(p) − o(1), where the o(1) term is
     nonnegative and exponentially small in n (so super-exponentially small in d). So the total
     K(Pu ) − log Pu (y ∗ ) is d + nH(p) up to ±(κ′ + o(1)) for every u, and every minimizer u∗
     of λK(Pu ) − log Pu (y ∗ ) has d − u∗ ≤ λ−1
                                              λ
                                                 (2κ′ + o(1)).
  (b) Private randomness has a kink. For every product h and t ∈ N with K(h) ≤ d − 2t −
      2 log2 (t + 1) − 2 log2 d − κ,
                                 R(h) ≥ 1 − log2 (1 + βt ) ≥ 1 − βt / ln 2,
                                                                        q
                                                                                           +
      where βt = min{2−t + 2p, (1 − 2p)2−t + εE }, with εE =                  32 ln 2 · p(δE + d + κn )/n,
       +
      δE = max(δE , 0) and κn = log2 n + 2 log2 (log2 n + 2) + κ.
  (c) The error segment. For every product h, K(h) + nR(h) ≥ d + LE − δE − 2 log2 d − κ.
Proof idea. In (a), the true component has weight 2−u and likelihood exactly 2−nH(p) . Every
other component disagrees with y ∗ on at least n/2 − pn points. In (b), Parseval shows that
at most 22t parities correlate at least 2−t with h. This is the standard list-size bound for the
Hadamard code (Goldreich and Levin, 1989; Kushilevitz and Mansour, 1993). So a short h
that correlated with fa would encode a in fewer than d bits. Jensen’s inequality turns small
correlation into risk near one bit. The slack 2p arises because h may be right on E for free.
The slack εE removes it, using the randomness of E relative to h, via Hoeffding’s inequality for
sampling without replacement (Hoeffding, 1963) and counting. Part (c) is the coding theorem.
Full proofs, with the lemmas, are in Appendix G.3.

Reading. Part (a) is the note’s “1 bit paying for 1 bit”: the shared frontier is the sufficiency
line, so a steeper penalty selects a mixture that knows almost nothing of a and learns the rest
from the data, as any Bayes mixture does; a universal mixture learns the errors the same way
(Proposition 8.4). Part (b) makes the note’s hope quantitative. A product hypothesis missing
u = d − K(h) bits gains at most about 2−u/2+O(log d) bits per sample over the coin, plus slack:
exponentially little, though not nothing (list mixtures missing u = 1, 2, 3 bits gain 0.454, 0.221,
0.110 bits (computed)). So on the scale of d bits the product frontier is flat near the coin and
drops at the simple model; that drop is the change of derivative.
    Two caveats. First, the kink is resolved only up to O(log d) bits next to the simple model.
Second, the unconditional slack 2p gives a kink only for p < 0.1421, where 1 − 2p/ ln 2 > H(p);
the slack εE gives one for every p < 1/4 once n is large and δE = O(log n). For example, at
p = 0.2 and d = 20 (taking δE = κ = 0), εE = 0.014, against 2p = 0.4.

                                                 85
8.4    Selection traces the hull: rate thresholds
Let zh = (ℓ(h), R(h)), Z = {zh } and Ĉ = conv(Z + R2≥0 ). Only finitely many h have ℓ ≤ L, and
R ≥ 0, so each Jc with c > 0 attains its minimum.

Definition 8.7 (Lower hull). [T5 §3.1, as repaired] hull− (Z) is the set of boundary points of Ĉ
supported by a line of slope −c with 0 < c < ∞. A vertex is an extreme point of Ĉ that lies in
hull− (Z); vertices belong to Z.

Lemma 8.8 (Selection traces the lower hull; standard). [T5 Lemma 3.1, as repaired] (i) For c > 0,
the minimizers of Jc are the h with zh ∈ hull− (Z) on a supporting line of slope −c, and every
point of hull− (Z) in Z arises this way. (ii) If c < c′ , and h and h′ minimize Jc and Jc′ , then
ℓ(h) ≥ ℓ(h′ ) and R(h) ≤ R(h′ ). (iii) A point z ∈ Z is the unique minimizing point for all c
in some nonempty open interval iff it is a vertex. The interval is then (s+ , s− ), given by the
absolute slopes of the adjacent edges. A hypothesis is the unique minimizer there iff, in addition,
no other hypothesis has the same point.
        Lean: RateThreshold.onLowerHull_iff Lean: RateThreshold.lemma_3_1_ii Lean: RateThreshold.lemma_3_1_iii_hyp


Proof of (i), (ii). For (i), Jc (h) = ⟨(c, 1), zh ⟩ has the same minimum over Z as over Ĉ. For (ii),
adding the two optimality inequalities gives (c′ − c)(ℓ(h′ ) − ℓ(h)) ≤ 0. Part (iii) is proved in
Appendix G.4.

   The Lean proofs of (i) and (iii) cover finite classes.

Reading. The note’s convex-hull argument is correct, with two refinements. The intended
model must be a vertex, and the only hypothesis at its point. And λ must lie in (s+ N, s− N ).
The note’s alternative, to “set abs bound on model complexity”, selects every model the penalty
selects (bound it at that model’s length), as the note observes. The converse fails: a hard bound
can also select points of the staircase ρ× that lie off the hull, though never a Pareto-dominated
point such as that of Proposition 8.16.

Theorem 8.9 (Rate threshold, separable classes; trivial once set up). [T5 Thm 3.2; T4 Prop 7.1]
Partition X into finitely many regions Xj , and let H = j Hj with ℓ = j ℓj . Then Jc =
                                                                 Q               P

   j [cℓj + Rj ], and the minimizers are exactly the products of per-region minimizers. In particular,
P

let Hj = {default, component j}, where the component costs kj > 0 extra bits and lowers Rj by
rj . Then component j is selected iff rj /kj > c; at equality both choices are optimal.
                                               Lean: RateThreshold.prod_isMin_iff Lean: RateThreshold.rate_threshold


Proof. The objective is a sum of functions of disjoint coordinates. This is the standard hard-
thresholding fact for separable penalties.

Corollary 8.12 (Every convex frontier is realizable, after rescaling). [T5 Cor 3.4, as repaired] Let
s1 > · · · > sm > 0 be rational and dj ≥ 1 integers, with j sj dj ≤ 1. Set πj = sj dj , and add a
                                                         P

fair-coin region of mass π0 = 1 − j πj . Let P be the convex polygon from (0, 1) whose successive
                                  P

edges have slope −sj and horizontal length dj , and which is flat at height π0 afterwards. Then
the lower boundary ρ̆ of the closure of Ĉ satisfies

                                P (k + C ′ ) − ϵ ≤ ρ̆(k) ≤ P (k − C ′ ) + ϵ,

where C ′ = O(                                                    1−dj /4 .
                P                                          P
                   j log dj + m log m + mκ) and ϵ =         j πj 2

    The proof is Legendre duality (Appendix G.6). Scaling every dj up by L and every sj down
by L makes the bands negligible. So every convex, decreasing, piecewise-linear frontier that
starts at height 1, has rational slopes and drops by at most one bit is realized after rescaling, up
to the bands. This is the product-model analogue of the “all shapes” theorem of Vereshchagin
and Vitányi (2004), with the same moral: whether a good model is a vertex is a property of the
data, not a theorem.

Theorem 8.13 (Parity version of the note’s example). [T5 Thm 3.5, as repaired] For every c > 0

The terms correspond to the coin, the good model and the bad model, which switch near c =

Corollary 8.14 (Identification in the parity example). [T5 Cor 3.5a, added after verification] If Jc (h) ≤
                                 ′
Φ(c) + ξ, then ŝh (a) ≥ 21−c(d+κ )−H(p)−ξ − 1 − 2p. If moreover cn > 1, then R(h) ≥ H(p) − (ξ +

     Both are proved in Appendix G.7. So for c well inside the window (≈ 1/n, ≈ (1−H(p))/d), i.e.
with cn ≫ 1 and c d ≪ 1, every near-optimal hypothesis (small ξ) correlates strongly with fa and
memorizes almost none of the errors. Near either end of the window the bounds become vacuous.
For example, at d = 20, p = 2−10 and c = 10−4 , we get ŝ ≥ 0.980 (taking κ = κ′ = ξ = 0).
     The note’s own numbers (computed; T5 c1). These are a 100-bit simple model (plus 10 bits for
the flip rate, our stand-in for the note’s “something small”), and a 10 000-bit error on a 2−10
fraction of inputs, with H(2−10 ) = 0.0112 bits per sample (about 1/100, as the note says). The
rates are 8.99 · 10−3 for the model and 1.12 · 10−6 for the error. So the good model is selected
iff c lies between these, a window spanning a factor of about 8000. At N = 106 this means
λ ∈ (1.12, 8990). Standard MDL (λ = 1) switches to the error model at N ≈ 8.9 · 105 .

Reading. The error’s rate is about 1/n: a K-random error set costs one bit per 1/n of risk,
the slowest possible rate. So the note’s example is the most favourable case for the proposal.
Normativity is achieved there because the errors are incompressible relative to the norm.

8.5    Pathologies
Theorem 8.15 (Rate blindness; trivial once set up). [T5 Thm 4.1, as repaired]
  (i) c 7→ arg min Jc depends on the data only through (µ, P ∗ ); validity enters nowhere.
 (ii) Let v be valid and e an error with rv /kv ≤ re /ke . In the setting of Theorem 8.9, if v
      is selected then so is e, for every c other than a common tie value. In the setting of
      Theorem 8.11, the same holds for near-optimal h wherever clauses (a)–(b) decide both v and
      e. This excludes slack bands around their rates and, for clause (b), needs πj ω(2) > ∆ + 8c.
(iii) h◦ is the unique minimizer on an open c-interval iff zh◦ is a vertex and no other hypothesis
      shares it.
 (iv) Take two worlds with the same (µ, P ∗ ), in which some component is an error in one and a
      rare valid rule in the other. Every imitation-only procedure gives the same output in both,
      so it is wrong in one of them.
                                                                            Lean: RateThreshold.rate_blindness




                                                   88
Proof. Parts (i) and (iv) hold because Jc is a functional of (µ, P ∗ ). Part (iii) is Lemma 8.8.
   For (ii) in the separable case: if v is selected and c is not a tie, then c < rv /kv ≤ re /ke ; and if
c = rv /kv < re /ke , then e is strictly selected. The idealized case is proved in Appendix G.8.

    The Lean proof covers (ii) in the separable case only. Part (iv) is Corollary 5.20 again. What
is new is the quantitative criterion: the penalty separates errors from rules by rate, not validity.
    For instance, a 200-bit valid rule used in 10−4 of steps, gaining at most 2.2 bits per use, is
discarded at every c that rejects the note’s 10 000-bit error (the comparison mixes two settings;
only the rates are comparable); rarely used irregular verbs likewise regularize first (Lieberman
et al., 2007).

Cheap fallacies [T5 Prop 4.2] . In step imitation (Section 8.6), a schema used in a fraction π of
steps has rate πg/ℓ. Affirming the consequent (AC: q, p → q / p) has the size of modus ponens.
The freshman’s dream (FD: (a + b)2 → a2 + b2 ) is shorter than the correct expansion.
    Take a toy with M = 64 candidate steps and slip rate ε = 0.05, so that the gain is g = 5.42
bits (computed; T5 c4). AC, at frequency 0.02 and 20 bits, then has rate 5.4 · 10−3 . That beats
proof by cases (1.4 · 10−3 ), reductio (1.6 · 10−3 ), ex falso (7.2 · 10−4 ) and induction (1.2 · 10−4 ).
So no steepness keeps every valid rule while removing every error.
    We conjecture that this is no accident (conjecture; empirical). A fallacy becomes systematic
because it is a short, natural variant of a valid pattern.

Proposition 8.16 (Rate inversion: the intended model need not be a vertex). [T4 Prop 7.3] In
Theorem 8.9, take kr = ℓ(r) and rr = πr gr , with three rules: a common valid rule A, with ℓ = 10,
π = 0.5, g = 8; a rare valid rule B, with ℓ = 40, π = 0.01, g = 8; and the freshman’s dream
F, with ℓ = 8, π = 0.05, g = 8. The rates are 0.4, 0.002 and 0.05. As c grows, the selection
passes through {A, B, F }, {A, F }, {A} and ∅, so no c selects the valid set {A, B}. In fact, in
(bits, residual bits per datum), {A, B} = (50, 0.4) is Pareto-dominated by {A, F } = (18, 0.08). So
no criterion strictly increasing in both coordinates selects it.
                                     Lean: RateThreshold.valid_not_minimizer Lean: RateThreshold.pareto_dominated

                                                              P
Proof. Apply Theorem 8.9. The residual of a set R is              / πr gr .
                                                                 r∈R

    This answers the note’s vertex question in general. The hull mechanism is right, but the
intended model is a vertex only if every valid component out-rates every error (Corollary 8.10).
That holds for rare, expensive, idiosyncratic errors. It fails for cheap systematic fallacies, which
are common in student algebra and in Euler-style manipulation.

Theorem 8.17 (The steepness is not identifiable from imitation data). [T5 Thm 4.3, as repaired]
  (i) Along the population path, R(hc ) is non-increasing as c decreases. So, for a fixed finite
      grid in the computable version with a noise floor, choosing c by held-out log-loss picks, with
      probability tending to one as N → ∞, a grid value whose selected hypothesis has least risk;
      the smallest grid value always qualifies. At finite N this can fail, through the B/c term of
      Lemma 8.2.
 (ii) For every strictly proper scoring rule, the teacher channel strictly maximizes the expected
      score at every x (Gneiting and Raftery, 2007). So every validation criterion that is an
      empirical proper score on fresh imitation data targets P ∗ , errors included.
(iii) No imitation-data statistic picks a good c in both worlds of Theorem 8.15(iv).


                                                    89
   The proof is in Appendix G.9. In the note’s example, cross-validation picks the error model:
held-out log-loss is 1.0 at c = 10−1 , 0.0112 on [10−5 , 10−3 ], and 0 at c = 10−7 (computed). A
non-proper “knee” heuristic (the interior vertex with the widest log c-interval) picks the good
model in this example. By (iii), though, no such rule is right in both twin worlds.

Reading. Choosing c is a normative act, not an estimate. The note says that “is this a chair?”
is “much closer to ‘should the person who taught me the chair notion consider it a chair?’ ” than
to what that person would say. Part (iii) makes literal the claim that this target is not a function
of P ∗ .

Proposition 8.18 (Guard erosion). [T5 Prop 4.4, hypotheses added after verification] Let σ|G guard a
schema σ (e.g. x/x → 1 if x = ̸ 0). Humans apply σ in G-contexts (frequency πG , per-step gain
g). In contexts where σ applies but G fails (frequency π¬G ), they play a non-σ step. In the
σ-applicable region a hypothesis has three options: none: uniform over the M candidates, at cost
0; unguarded: mass 1 − ε + ε/M on the σ-step, at cost kσ ; and guarded: the same as unguarded
in G-contexts, and uniform on the non-σ steps otherwise, at cost kσ + kG . Define

             πG g − π¬G log2 (1/ε)             π¬G ∆G
      ρσ =                         ,    ρG =          ,      ∆G = log2 (M/ε) − log2 (M − 1).
                      kσ                         kG

If ρG < ρσ , then for c ∈ (ρG , ρσ ) the unguarded, unsound σ is selected, although the teacher
never errs. For c < ρG the guarded rule is selected, and for c > ρσ neither is. If ρG ≥ ρσ , then
apart from ties the path goes from guarded to none, and the learner stays sound.

Proof. Relative to none, the three options are the points (0, 0), (kσ , −kσ ρσ ) and (kσ +kG , −kσ ρσ −
kG ρG ) in (bits, risk). The middle point is a vertex iff ρσ > ρG . Apply Lemma 8.8 and Theorem 8.9.
The rates are derived in Appendix G.10.

    Take πG = 0.05, π¬G = 5 · 10−4 , kσ = 22, kG = 12, M = 64 and ε = 0.05 (computed;
T5 c4). Then ρG = 1.8 · 10−4 and ρσ = 1.22 · 10−2 , so the unguarded rule is selected on
(1.8 · 10−4 , 1.22 · 10−2 ), a range that includes steepnesses at which ex falso (rate 7.2 · 10−4 ) is
kept. With πG = π¬G = 0.002 instead, ρσ < ρG and there is no unsound phase.

Reading. What a careful teacher exhibits most rarely is what goes first: side conditions,
eigenvariable conditions, edge cases. One unsound schema can trivialize a classical calculus
(Proposition 3.5). With field rules, 0/0 = 1 and 0/0 = 0 · 0−1 = 0 give 1 = 0, a negative bag
(Lemma 6.2). So coherence must restore what simplicity erodes.

Further pathologies [T5 §4.4] . Error envelopes. Errors concentrated in a cheaply describable
region are learned as an envelope (“flip rate θ on S”), at the envelope’s own rate, which can
far exceed that of the full error. Where the error density there exceeds 12 , the learner predicts
the error label throughout the region. Real systematic errors are low-complexity patterns of
exactly this kind. Parasitic errors. If K(e | v) ≪ K(e), as with FD, a one-symbol mutation of
(ab)n = an bn , then bundles can enter the hull together. A valid rule may be kept only because of
the error it subsidizes. An earlier claim, that conditional rates fix the order of entry, was false
and is retracted. Distribution dependence. A valid rule that is rare in training stays discarded
as N → ∞. Language relativity [T5 §4.4(d6), corrected] : for any separable gains rj , target set S ◦
with rj > 0 on it, and c > 0, some prefix code makes S ◦ the unique selection (give hS length


                                                  90
m + j∈S kj with nonnegative integers kj < rj /c on S ◦ and kj > rj /c off it; the Kraft sum
      P

is 2−m j (1 + 2−kj ) ≤ 1). Between universal machines marginal costs move by at most 2cU V :
       Q

negligible for 10 000-bit errors, decisive for 20-bit schemas. So the note’s “simplicity prior defined
in terms of existing understanding” cuts both ways: a language shaped by human concepts gives
human-natural errors short codes.

8.6    Inference rules: the division of labour
In situation-typed step imitation, a context x is a premise set and a goal (Section 2), and y is
the next human step among Mτ candidates. Contexts fall into computable situation types τ
of frequency πτ . In type τ the human applies a schema στ ∈ ΣP , genuine or fallacious, with
probability 1 − ε, and otherwise moves uniformly.
    Modelling type τ costs ℓ(στ ) + O(1) bits and gains gτ = log2 Mτ − Hτ per step. Its rate is
therefore ρτ = πτ gτ /(ℓ(στ ) + O(1)), and by Theorem 8.9 it is selected iff ρτ > c. The induced
verifier accepts the instances of the selected schemas. It is sound (Definition 2.19) iff no error
schema is selected and no guard has eroded.
Definition 8.19 (Admissible family, witnesses). [T5 §5.2, as repaired] The components are V ⊔ F
(valid, erroneous), with net values νj = rj − c kj . The admissible family A ⊆ 2V ⊔F is the
intersection of the filters in use: A-coherence (Definition 2.13), W-adequacy, and coherence with
designated meaning postulates Ax. Here W-adequacy means that no claim the world refutes is
derivable. The family is down-closed and contains 2V . A witness for e ∈ F is a minimal W ⊆ V
with W ∪{e} ∈  / A. It is the negative bag of Lemma 6.2, seen from the rules. Let V+ = {v : νv > 0}
and F+ = {e : νe > 0}. At a given c we impose two conditions. Error-independence: for U ⊆ V ,
U ∪ F ′ ∈ A iff U ∪ {e} ∈ A for each e ∈ F ′ . Valid dominance: every e ∈ F+ with a witness inside
V+ has β(e) > F+ ν. Here β(e) is the least value of B ν over sets B ⊆ V+ meeting all of e’s
                 P                                       P

witnesses inside V+ .
Theorem 8.20 (Division of labour). [T5 Thm 5.2, proof tightened after verification] Fix c, and assume
separability, error-independence and valid dominance at c. Then the optimal admissible sets are
exactly the admissible sets S ∗ (c) ∪ Z with Z ⊆ {ν = 0}, where
                              S ∗ (c) = V+ ∪ {e ∈ F+ : V+ ∪ {e} ∈ A}.
So, up to zero-value ties, simplicity removes exactly the errors of rate at most c, coherent or not;
coherence, the world and postulates remove exactly the errors that clash with the valid rules
actually kept (V+ , plus Ax), whatever their rate; the residue is the high-rate errors that pass
every filter; and valid rules of rate at most c are lost, whatever the other filters do.
Proof idea. An optimal set has no negative-value component. Suppose it contained an error
that is inadmissible together with V+ . Then it would have to omit some B ⊆ V+ meeting
that error’s witnesses. Valid dominance makes restoring B and dropping the error better, and
error-independence keeps the result admissible.
    Appendix G.11 gives the full proof, including the zero-value case on which the original last
step failed. A brute-force check of 17 806 instances found no violation (computed).
Corollary 8.21 (Best steepness given the other filters). [T5 Cor 5.2a, as repaired] Assume error-
independence and valid dominance at every c considered, and kj > 0. Then every optimal
admissible set contains V and no error iff
                   max{ρe : V ∪ {e} ∈ A} < c < min ρv ,         with max ∅ := 0.
                                                   v∈V


                                                 91
     The proof is in Appendix G.11. Valid dominance is essential. For a counterexample without
it, take V = {v} with rate 10−3 , and an error e of rate 2 · 10−3 , both with k = 10, whose witness
is {v}. No error survives relative to V , yet for c < 10−3 the optimum is {e}.
     So coherence and the world widen the window: the rates of incoherent or refutable errors
stop mattering. Simplicity is needed only against coherent, adequate, low-rate errors, such as
idiosyncratic false lemmas and quus-like patches (Proposition 6.37).

Proposition 8.22 (Repair by sacrifice). [T5 Prop 5.3, as repaired] Assume error-independence. Let
e ∈ F+ have a witness inside V+ , and let B ⊆ V+ be a cheapest set meeting all of e’s witnesses
inside V+ , with β(e) = B ν < νe . Then the clean set Sclean = V+ ∪ {e′ ∈ F+ : V+ ∪ {e′ } ∈ A} is
                       P

not optimal. Indeed, (V+ \ B) ∪ {e} ∪ (Sclean ∩ F ) is admissible, with value val(Sclean ) + νe − β(e).

     The proof is in Appendix G.11. Here is a computed instance (computed; T5 c4). AC’s Post
witness (Corollary 6.35), with p := ⊥ and q := A → A, needs only →I. If →I is rare, with rate
8.7 · 10−4 , then at c = 5 · 10−4 the coherent optimum is {AC, MP, ⊤I}. The learner drops →I to
keep the fallacy.
     This instance assumes A = {∅}. With a designated context such as {p0 → q0 , q0 , ¬p0 }, MP
is itself a witness partner, and AC is dropped instead. The phenomenon is Lakatos’s monster-
barring (Lakatos, 1976) run backwards: the repair is chosen by description cost, not by validity.
It refines Theorem 6.34, whose criterion must be read relative to the rules the learner keeps.

Meaning postulates. The note lists a separate route: “you specify properties of the thing or
notion sometimes”. For example, “1+1=2 won’t be true if you accidentally assign 1->rabbit and
2->chicken from a demonstration (for any reasonable meaning of plus)”. Formally, a finite set
Ax of sentences endorsed as constitutive of a notion enters as designated premises. These are
Carnap’s meaning postulates (Carnap, 1952).

 (b) Let Axnum = {1 + 1 = 2, 12 = 1, 22 = 4, 4 ̸= 2}, and keep substitution of equals. Then
