Reading: avowal versus performance. Postulates are the teacher’s avowals (“1+1=2”,
“MP is valid, AC is not”, “a chair is for sitting”), not the teacher’s performance. The steeper
penalty sees only performance. This is where the debate between Stich and Nisbett (1980) and
Cohen (1981a) lands [L11 §2] .
    Cohen grounds norms in a systematization of ordinary judgments. Stich and Nisbett had
argued, against reflective equilibrium as a test of justification, that systematic fallacies such
as the gambler’s fallacy can survive such a systematization. Performance data indeed cannot
separate a fallacy from a rule with the same profile (Theorem 8.15(iv)). Avowals are a second
channel: few bits that exclude whole high-rate classes of errors. Where an avowal is itself wrong,
as with naive comprehension, coherence catches it (Section 10).
    The note’s abstract/concrete example is a cross-notion postulate: “if something is abstract,
then it usually helps a lot to study examples to understand it”. It ties the notion to another
notion that has independent evidence, which turns a labelling error into a W-refutation. This
makes “should the person who taught me [. . . ] consider it a chair” operational. The norm is
whatever fits the notion’s avowed role, checked against the other channels.
    Table 11 puts the pieces together. The residue is the alternative meanings of Theorem 6.38
that humans actually use, such as “if” read as “iff” in closed worlds. Calling them errors is itself
a normative decision outside the data, and it may be wrong: conditional perfection is arguably
part of everyday “if”.

8.7     Verdict
Where the proposal is vindicated. Function induction with private randomness has no universal-
hypothesis pathology, by exactly the mechanism the note names: “you have to pay the likelihood
term separately at every x” (Theorem 8.3). Shared randomness pays for partial knowledge once,
product models gain exponentially little from it, so the product frontier kinks (Theorem 8.6). The
convex-hull argument is correct; the change of derivative is the vertex condition, plus uniqueness
at the vertex (Lemma 8.8). The note’s example works, asymptotically, on a window of more
than three orders of magnitude (Corollary 8.14).
    Where it fails. The penalty is validity-blind: it sorts by rate, betting that norms are high-rate
structure and errors low-rate structure (Theorem 8.15). The bet is right for idiosyncratic slips
and wrong for cheap, frequent fallacies (Proposition 8.16). It costs rare valid rules and, worse,
rarely exercised guards, so the learner can be less sound than its teacher (Proposition 8.18). The

                                                        93
good c cannot be estimated from imitation data (Theorem 8.17), and private randomness fails
whenever inputs carry the same person’s earlier labels (Proposition 8.5).
    What does the normativity work. Of the note’s other ideas, “you specify properties of the thing
or notion” is decisive: postulates are avowal rather than performance, and they remove high-rate
fallacies at every c, provided the valid rules in a witness out-rate the fallacy (Proposition 8.23).
The idea “what is it that this person is trying to teach me” calls for a teacher model with a
competence/performance split. As we recall their result, Armstrong and Mindermann (2018)
show that simplicity alone does not identify such splits. That is Theorem 8.15(iv) in another
guise.
    The division of labour comes to this: simplicity removes the expensive errors; coherence
removes the incoherent ones; the world removes the refutable ones; postulates remove those
that violate avowed properties. What survives is the meanings humans actually use. Steeper
simplicity still has a job no other channel does: removing idiosyncratic, coherent, unrefutable
errors (Table 11, first row). It is a good third filter, not a route to normativity on its own.
    In the terms of Section 13, a learner fitted to practice alone is in narrow reflective equilibrium
with its teacher, with or without a simplicity penalty. Stich’s objection applies to it exactly.
What removes coherent systematic fallacies is information that is not a function of performance:
avowed postulates, and an independent world channel. That world channel is what makes the
equilibrium wide.
    Open problems (Section 14) include kinks beyond parities, a product-model algorithmic
statistics, a data-free choice of c, competence/performance models in which joint simplicity
identifies the competence, and the best block size for a step imitator. The simulations are in
Section 12.


9    Coherence and existence: a “philosophical completeness theo-
     rem”
Sections 6 and 7 used coherence as a learning signal. This section asks what coherence means: if
a learned mode of talking is coherent, is there something it is talking about? The user’s notes
pose exactly this question (logic/a ’philosophical version’ of gödel’s completeness
theorem.md):
     “a mode of talking makes sense (is coherent) iff there is something it could be talking about”
     as long as the ‘mode of talking’ is a first-order theory and ‘something’ is a model of it, then
     this is literally the completeness theorem
     is a generalization of the completeness thm available that justifies this philosophical proposition
     more broadly?

The note suggests further instances (“probabilities describe world iff coherent”, after Christiano
et al. 2013; “maybe something could be said about vnm stuff also?”) and records a remark of
Sam’s: “completeness is an adjunction between sentences and models . . . if you are in image,
then round trip is just identity. what is this image?” Elsewhere the user adds the caveat that
“there might only be such a thing in the same sense that there is a ‘proof’ of the Gödel sentence
G” (L10 §1.5).

Answer in brief. There is a whole family of “coherent iff there is something it could be talking
about” theorems with one anatomy: completeness holds iff the points of a calculus are realized


                                                     94
by models (§9.1). Sam’s slogan is strong completeness and the user’s is weak completeness;
they differ. Every calculus is complete for models built from its own syntax, so the proposition
has content only for an independently specified semantics. Instances: positions (Stone duality,
§9.2), credences (de Finetti with counting sequents; no bounded-arity, CCS-style constraint
family characterizes coherence, §9.3), and contexts (the somethings are belief states, not worlds,
§9.4). A coherent learned calculus has a model, but a syntactic one; the Kripkensteinian residue
of Section 6 becomes a set of non-isomorphic things talked about; and a compactness and a
computability barrier show that coherence cannot in general deliver the intended model (§9.5).
The verdict is in §9.6.
    Credit. Almost everything here is a standard result, reorganized; the organization is the
contribution. The Galois connection is Birkhoff (1940) and Ore (1944); bilateral completeness
is Scott (1974) and Shoesmith and Smiley (1978); probabilistic coherence is de Finetti (1937)
and Gaifman (1964); local-models semantics is Ghidini and Giunchiglia (2001). New as far
as we know, and all elementary: the counting-sequent form of de Finetti’s theorem (probably
folklore; cf. Paris 1994), the strictness results for CCS-style constraints (Theorems 9.14 and 9.15),
the multi-context completeness theorem in exactly this form (Theorem 9.22), and the corollary
on logical inductors (Corollary 9.19). Theorems 9.25 and 9.35 are standard arguments, newly
applied. Apart from the semantic half of Theorem 9.10, which is Lemma 6.26(a) and is formalized
for the propositional language, nothing in this section is formalized in Lean; sanity checks are in
research/theory/T6-checks/.

9.1   The adjunction, and the anatomy of a completeness theorem
Definition 9.1 (Semantic frames and completeness). [T6 Def 1.1, §1.1] A semantic frame (S, M, |=)
consists of a set S of “sentences”, a set or class M of “models” and a relation |= ⊆ M × S. Put
Mod(Σ) = {m : m |= σ ∀σ ∈ Σ} and Th(K) = {σ : m |= σ ∀m ∈ K}. A deductive system is
a closure operator C on P(S) (extensive, monotone, idempotent), with closed sets (theories)
Fix(C); it is finitary if C(X) is the union of the C(X0 ) over finite X0 ⊆ X. C is sound if
C ≤ Th ◦ Mod pointwise, strongly complete if C = Th ◦ Mod, and weakly complete if every
coherent X (i.e. C(X) ̸= S) has Mod(X) ̸= ∅.

Lemma 9.2 (Galois connection). [T6 Fact 1.2] (known) K ⊆ Mod(Σ) iff Σ ⊆ Th(K). Hence
Th ◦ Mod and Mod ◦ Th are closure operators, and Mod, Th are mutually inverse inclusion-
reversing bijections between their fixed points (Birkhoff, 1940; Ore, 1944).

    Read as an adjunction between (P(S), ⊆) and (P(M ), ⊇), this is Mod ⊣ Th: the precise
content of “completeness is an adjunction between sentences and models”. The adjunction exists
for every frame; a completeness theorem is a statement about its fixed points.

Proposition 9.3 (Sam’s image). [T6 Prop 1.3] For every closure operator C: {Th(K) : K ⊆
M } = Fix(C) iff C is strongly complete.

Proof. The image of Th is Fix(Th ◦ Mod), as ThModTh = Th; and a closure operator is
determined by its fixed points, C(X) = {T ∈ Fix(C) : X ⊆ T }.
                                      T


   Trivial once set up (and soundness is not needed): the round trip fixes exactly the sets closed
under the rules iff the rules are complete.




                                                 95
Theorem 9.4 (Tautological completeness). [T6 Thm 1.4] Every closure operator C is strongly
complete for its canonical frame (S, Fix(C), ∋), whose models are the theories T , with T |= σ iff
σ ∈ T.

                            {T ∈ Fix(C) : X ⊆ T } = C(X).
                        T
Proof. Th(Mod(X)) =

Reading. If “something it could be talking about” may be any object, the user’s proposition
is trivially true: every mode of talking is about its own theories. Real completeness theorems
have content because their models (truth tables, structures, points of k̄ n , measures) are specified
before and independently of the calculus. For a learner of inference rules this is a warning in
advance: that a learned calculus is “about something” is cheap; what matters is whether it is
about something we can access independently.

Theorem 9.5 (Completeness is realization of points). [T6 Thm 1.5, as repaired] Call a theory T
a point if T ̸= S and, for some σ ∈      / T , T is maximal among theories omitting σ; write
Pt(C). Let C be finitary. (a) Lindenbaum. Every theory is the intersection of the points
containing it. (b) If C is sound, it is strongly complete iff every point is the theory of a model:
Pt(C) ⊆ {Th({m}) : m ∈ M }. (c) If C is sound, some finite F has C(F ) = S, and Th({m}) ̸= S
for all m, then C is weakly complete iff every maximal proper theory is the theory of a model.
Without soundness, (⇒) fails.

     Proof idea. (a) Zorn, using finitarity; (b) realize each point in the decomposition (a),
and conversely use complete meet-irreducibility. Full proof and the counterexample for (c) in
Appendix H.1. So every completeness proof has two steps: (i) points exist, which is Zorn and free;
(ii) points are realized by models of the independently given kind, which is where content lives
(Henkin witnesses, Zariski’s lemma, Carathéodory extension). Categorical logic has the same
slogan: Deligne’s theorem that coherent toposes have enough points is equivalent to completeness
for coherent logic (Makkai and Reyes, 1977).

Proposition 9.6 (Weak ̸= strong). [T6 Prop 1.6] IPC is sound and weakly complete, but not
strongly complete, for the Boolean valuations BV.

Proof. p∨¬p holds in all of BV but is not IPC-provable. For weak completeness use Theorem 9.5(c)
with F = {⊥}: a maximal IPC-consistent T contains φ or ¬φ for each φ (deduction theorem), so
it contains excluded middle, so it is a maximal classical theory. Alternatively, use Glivenko’s
theorem (Glivenko, 1929).

Lemma 9.7 (Classical points are maximal). [T6 reading of Prop 1.6, as repaired] Every point of Cn2
is a maximal consistent theory. (Proof by cases; Appendix H.1.)

Reading. Weak completeness needs the maximal theories to be realized, strong completeness
all points; they coincide when every point is maximal, as in classical logic. IPC has non-maximal
points, realized only by Heyting or Kripke models. This mirrors Theorem 6.17: intuitionistic
and classical logic have the same coherence data, every coherent intuitionistic position is about
a classical world, and yet those worlds do not determine intuitionistic consequence. Having
somethings for every coherent position does not mean the somethings fix what follows from what.
    Table 12 lists the family. The Nullstellensatz row is Sam’s other pointer: the weak Null-

                           n
