9.2    Positions: bilateral completeness is Stone duality
Fix any set Fm; until Theorem 9.11 connectives play no role. A sequent Γ ▷ ∆ has finite
Γ, ∆ ⊆ Fm; a valuation is any v : Fm → {0, 1}, and v |= Γ ▷ ∆ iff v falsifies some γ ∈ Γ or verifies
some δ ∈ ∆. A position [X : Y ] asserts all of X and denies all of Y ; it is incoherent for a set ⊢ of
sequents if some Γ ▷ ∆ ∈ ⊢ has Γ ⊆ X, ∆ ⊆ Y (Restall 2005; Definition 6.25). A Scott relation
is a set of sequents closed under (Ov) Γ ▷ ∆ if Γ ∩ ∆ ̸= ∅; (Wk) from Γ ▷ ∆ infer Γ ∪ Γ′ ▷ ∆ ∪ ∆′ ;
(Cut) from Γ, φ ▷ ∆ and Γ ▷ φ, ∆ infer Γ ▷ ∆. ⟨S⟩ is the least Scott relation containing S, and
Val(⊢) the set of valuations satisfying ⊢.
Theorem 9.8 (Bilateral Lindenbaum). [T6 Thm 2.2] (known) Let ⊢ be a Scott relation. (a) Every
coherent position [X : Y ] is realized by some v ∈ Val(⊢): v[X] = 1, v[Y ] = 0. (b) The maximal
coherent positions are exactly the [v −1 (1) : v −1 (0)] with v ∈ Val(⊢).
Corollary 9.9 (Strong bilateral completeness). [T6 Cor 2.3] For every set S of sequents, the
sequents valid in every valuation that satisfies S are exactly those in ⟨S⟩: Th(Mod(S)) = ⟨S⟩.
   Proof idea (Scott, 1974; Shoesmith and Smiley, 1978). Zorn gives a maximal coherent
extension, since sequents are finite; (Ov) makes it disjoint, and (Cut) makes it exhaustive: if φ
can be added to neither side, weaken the two witnessing sequents to a common context and cut
on φ. Full proofs in Appendix H.2.

                                                      97
Theorem 9.10 (The duality). [T6 Thm 2.4] (known) Give 2Fm the product topology. The Galois
connection between sets of sequents and sets of valuations has syntactic closure S 7→ ⟨S⟩ and
semantic closure V 7→ V , the topological closure. Hence ⊢ 7→ Val(⊢) is a dual lattice isomorphism
from Scott relations onto closed subsets of 2Fm , with inverse Th.    Lean: Carnap.Val_mrel (semantic

half, propositional Fm)

    The syntactic half is Corollary 9.9, the semantic half is Lemma 6.26(a): each sequent excludes
a basic clopen set. This is Stone duality (Stone, 1936) for the free Boolean algebra B on Fm,
every formula an independent generator: Γ ▷ ∆ is the clause Γ ¬γ ∨ ∆ δ of B, and Scott
                                                                   W        W

relations, closed sets of valuations and filters of B correspond. So at this level the connectives
play no role in the duality; they enter only through which closed set the rules carve out. This is
Suszko’s thesis seen as a duality: a Tarskian consequence is determined by the characteristic
functions of its theories, which are bivaluations that need not be truth-functional (Suszko 1977
stated it for structural logics).

Theorem 9.11 (Structural calculi and Lindenbaum bundles). [T6 Thm 2.6] Let Fm be a term
algebra and call ⊢ structural if it is closed under substitutions σ. (a) A Scott relation ⊢ is structural
iff Val(⊢) is closed under v 7→ v ◦ σ. (b) If ⊢ is structural, each matrix Lv = ⟨Fm, v −1 (1)⟩,
v ∈ Val(⊢), validates ⊢ under every assignment; ⊢ is exactly the set of sequents valid in all Lv ;
and every coherent position is realized in some Lv by the identity assignment.

    The proof (Appendix H.2) rests on v ◦ σ |= Γ ▷ ∆ iff v |= σΓ ▷ σ∆.

Reading. Theorem 9.8 is the cleanest form of the user’s proposition: a position is coherent
iff it partially describes an admissible valuation, “some way the sentences could all come out”.
Theorem 9.11 upgrades the something to a compositional model whose algebra is the language
itself, the multiple-conclusion analogue of Łoś and Suszko (1958) and Wójcicki (1988). Whether
the intended semantics is reached depends on the rules: with the truth-table sequents and one
coherence datum, Val(⊢) = BV (Theorem 6.29); with single-conclusion data only, the admissible
valuations include the “gappy” BV∩ (Theorem 6.27), so a single-conclusion coherence loss licenses
talk about undecided worlds.

9.3    Credences: de Finetti, counting sequents, and why local constraints fail
Let F be a finite agenda that is a union of complementary pairs {φ, φ∗ }, φ∗ ≡ ¬φ (a finite
agenda cannot be closed under ¬); BV the Boolean valuations of its atoms; v̄ = (v(φ))φ∈F . A
credence P : F → [0, 1] is negation-coherent if P (φ∗ ) = 1 − P (φ), and coherent if P = v µ(v)v̄
                                                                                        P

for a probability µ on BV, i.e. P lies in the coherence polytope ΠF = conv{v̄}. A counting
sequent (Φ, m) is a finite multiset Φ of members of F with an integer threshold m; it is valid
if every v ∈ BV makes at least m members of Φ true (with multiplicity), and P satisfies it if
                                                                           ∗
   φ∈Φ P (φ) ≥ m. An ordinary sequent Γ ▷ ∆ is the case m = 1, Φ = {γ : γ ∈ Γ} ∪ ∆.
P


Theorem 9.12 (de Finetti, with counting sequents). [T6 Thm 3.1, as repaired] For negation-coherent
P the following are equivalent: (i) P is coherent; (ii) there is no Dutch book, i.e. no λ ∈ RF with
λ · (v̄ − P ) < 0 for all v ∈ BV; (iii) for rational λ, c, if λ · v̄ ≥ c for all v then λ · P ≥ c; (iv) P
satisfies every valid counting sequent. Negation coherence is needed: P ≡ 1 satisfies (iv) but is
incoherent.



                                                   98
    Proof idea. ΠF is a rational polytope, so a point outside it violates one of finitely many
rational defining inequalities, which gives a Dutch book; clearing denominators and moving
negative coefficients from φ to φ∗ turns the inequality into a counting sequent. Appendix H.3.

Reading. The probabilistic completeness theorem says a credence is coherent iff it is a mixture
of worlds. Its proof-theoretic side is not ordinary sequents but counting sequents, graded positions
such as “at least two of these six claims hold”. An ordinary sequent gives only the union bound
   Γ (1 − P (γ)) +  ∆ P (δ) ≥ 1, the multiple-conclusion form of Adams’s uncertainty-sum bound
P                  P

(Adams, 1966).

Which consistency constraints suffice? The user’s notes on consistency-based probing
(ai/DLK/) impose negation coherence P (¬Q) = 1 − P (Q), the identity P (A ∧ B) + P (A ∨ B) =
P (A) + P (B), and modus ponens read as the tautology ¬P ∨ ¬(P → Q) ∨ Q, i.e. the union
bound P (Q) ≥ P (P ) + P (P → Q) − 1. Contrast-consistent search (CCS) trains a probe with
exactly a negation-consistency loss plus a confidence loss (Burns et al., 2023).

Example 9.13 (Small agendas). [T6 Prop 3.2, Ex 3.3] (computed) On {p, q, p ∧ q} plus complements,
P (p) = P (q) = 0.2, P (p ∧ q) = 0.9 is negation-coherent but violates the union bound of p ∧ q ▷ p.
For (P (p), P (q), P (p → q)) = (a, b, c) the coherence polytope is the tetrahedron with facets
a + c ≥ 1 (from ▷p, p → q), b ≥ a + c − 1 (modus ponens), b ≤ c (from q ▷ p → q) and the trivial
c ≤ 1: on this tiny agenda the user’s MP constraint, two other union bounds and a trivial bound
are coherence.

   This does not scale. For k ≥ 3 let Fk = {Ai , ¬Ai : i ≤ k} ∪ {Ai ∧ Aj , ¬(Ai ∧ Aj ) : i < j ≤ k},
                1
and Pk (Ai ) = k−1 , Pk (Ai ∧ Aj ) = 0, with complements on negations.

Theorem 9.14 (No bounded-arity family of constraints suffices). [T6 Thm 3.4] (a) For every
F ′ ⊆ Fk whose formulas jointly mention at most k −1 atoms, Pk |F ′ extends to a coherent credence
on Fk . (b) Pk is incoherent. Hence no family of necessary coherence constraints each depending
only on sub-agendas over fewer than k atoms characterizes coherence; in particular pairwise
(CCS-style) constraints fail, and so does every bounded arity.
                                                 1
Proof. (a) With J the mentioned atoms, put mass k−1 on each world making exactly one Aj ,
                                |J|
j ∈ J, true, and the rest, 1 − k−1  ≥ 0, on the all-false world. (b) A representing µ would make
                               W          k
the Ai pairwise disjoint, so µ( i Ai ) = k−1 > 1.

    The same holds if Fk is enlarged to all formulas in at most two atoms, valued by the two-atom
marginals of the µ in (a), which do not depend on J: Pk is “pairwise coherent” in the strongest
sense, every pair of atoms having a genuine joint distribution, and still globally incoherent. This
is the frustration in Specker’s parable of the three boxes (Specker, 1960).

Theorem 9.15 (The counting-sequent hierarchy by threshold is strict). [T6 Thm 3.5, as repaired]
(a) Pk violates the valid counting sequent Φ = {¬A1 , . . . , ¬Ak } ∪ {Ai ∧ Aj : i < j}, m = k − 1,
which repeats no formula. (b) Pk satisfies every valid counting sequent over Fk with m ≤ k − 2,
with arbitrary repetitions; in particular (m = 1) the union bound of every valid ordinary multiple-
conclusion sequent.




                                                99
    Proof. (a) In a world with t true atoms, (k − t) + 2t ≥ k − 1 members of Φ hold, as
                                                                

(t − 1)(t − 2) ≥ 0; but Φ Pk = k(k−2)  k−1 < k − 1. (b) uses only the all-false and singleton worlds
                          P

plus integrality (Appendix H.3). An exhaustive enumeration for k = 3 (503,270 valid ordinary
sequents, none violated) and an exact MILP for k = 3, 4, 5 agree.
    Scope. For any single finite agenda the finitely many rational inequalities defining ΠF (its
facets, plus its affine hull when it is not full-dimensional) give finitely many counting sequents that
characterize coherence; what Theorems 9.14 and 9.15 rule out is a bound on arity or threshold
that works uniformly across agendas (Fk needs arity k and threshold k − 1). Moreover, deciding
coherence on arbitrary agendas is NP-hard (P (φ) = 1, P (φ∗ ) = 0 is coherent iff φ is satisfiable),
so no efficiently checkable constraint family characterizes it unless P = NP. Closing the agenda
changes the picture:

Proposition 9.16 (On logically closed agendas, local additivity suffices). [T6 Prop 3.6] If F
represents a finite Boolean subalgebra of the Lindenbaum algebra and P respects equivalence, then
P is coherent iff P ≥ 0, P (⊤) = 1 and P (φ) = P (φ ∧ ψ) + P (φ ∧ ¬ψ) for all φ, ψ ∈ F ; each
constraint mentions at most three formulas, and the user’s identity follows from them.

A caution for consistency-based probing. A CCS-style probe reads credences off a model
on an unclosed list of statements and enforces negation consistency, perhaps with further pairwise
or local constraints. By Example 9.13 and Theorems 9.14 and 9.15, no fixed stock of constraint
types (not pairwise, not k-wise for fixed k, not even all union bounds of ordinary sequents)
guarantees that the probe’s credences are a mixture of worlds, i.e. that they are “about” anything
at all. Global coherence needs the agenda’s facets, or an agenda closed under the connectives
(Proposition 9.16). And even global coherence buys only some distribution over some worlds
(Theorem 9.17), not the actual one: coherence is eliminative, not confirmational (Section 6.7). A
natural experiment, which we have not run, is to train probes with negation-only, pairwise and
counting-sequent losses on agendas with k-way exclusive alternatives and measure their distance
from the coherence polytope.

First-order credences. For a countable first-order language L, P : SentL → [0, 1] is (Gaifman-
)coherent if (G1) P (φ) = 1 whenever ⊢ φ, and (G2) P (φ∨ψ) = P (φ)+P (ψ) whenever ⊢ ¬(φ∧ψ)
(Gaifman, 1964); this is the notion of the draft cited by the user (Christiano et al., 2013).

Theorem 9.17 (Coherent credences are mixtures of complete theories). [T6 Thm 3.7] (known) Let
SL be the Stone space of complete consistent L-theories, with clopens [φ] = {T : φ ∈ T }. Then P
is coherent iff P (φ) = µ([φ]) for a (unique) Borel probability µ on SL .

    Proof idea. P is a finitely additive probability on the clopen algebra; by compactness this
is automatically countably additive there, and Carathéodory extends it (Appendix H.3). By
Gödel completeness, a coherent credence is the expected truth value of φ in a random model:
the user’s proposition holds verbatim. So does the caveat: for complete T ⊇ PA + ¬Con(PA), δT
is coherent, gives 1 to every true quantifier-free sentence, and “believes” there is a proof of 0 = 1.

Theorem 9.18 (The Gaifman condition is a probabilistic ω-rule). [T6 Thm 3.8, as repaired] In the
language of arithmetic, let P be coherent, give probability 1 to every true
                                                                         
                                                                            quantifier-free sentence,
                                                               W
and satisfy the Gaifman condition P (∃x φ(x)) = supn P i≤n φ(i) . Then P = δTh(N) . No
arithmetic axioms are needed.


                                                 100
    Proof idea. Induction on logical symbols: coherence forces {0, 1}-values through ¬, ∧; numerals
carry no logical symbols, and every number is a numeral’s value (Appendix H.3). This is essentially
a consequence of Gaifman (1964): a probability with the Gaifman property is determined by its
quantifier-free values, here the true diagram of N.
Corollary 9.19 (Logical inductors’ limits are non-standard). [T6 Cor 3.9, as repaired] Let P be
a logical inductor (Garrabrant et al., 2016) over a consistent r.e. Γ ⊇ Q in the language of
arithmetic. Then P∞ violates the Gaifman condition. More generally, no limit-computable
coherent credence giving 1 to every true quantifier-free sentence satisfies it; the quantifier-free
hypothesis cannot be dropped.
   Proof idea. By Limit Coherence, P∞ is coherent and gives 1 to true quantifier-free sentences,
so Theorem 9.18 would make it δTh(N) . Non-Dogmatism refutes this (the false member of a
Gödel–Rosser pair gets positive probability), and so, independently, does Tarski’s theorem, as
Th(N) would be ∆2 . Appendix H.3.

Reading. The probabilistic “something” is always available; a standard something is fixed by
an infinitary condition that no limit-computable coherent credence of this kind meets. A Gaifman-
style probe of a language model’s arithmetic would compare P (∃x φ(x)) with maxi≤n P (φ(i)): a
large gap marks belief in a witness that is never named.
Proposition 9.20 (Finite preference data). [T6 Prop 3.10] Finitely many strict comparisons
pi ≻ qi and weak comparisons rj ≿ sj of lotteries over a finite set X have an expected-utility
representation u ∈ RX (u · (pi − qi ) > 0, u · (rj − sj ) ≥ 0) iff no λ, κ ≥ 0 with λ =
                                                                                      ̸ 0 satisfy
   i λi (pi − qi ) +  j κj (rj − sj ) = 0.
P                    P

Proof. Motzkin’s transposition theorem (Motzkin, 1936).

   This answers “vnm stuff”: the certificate is a derivation of L ≻ L by mixing (independence)
and transitivity (von Neumann and Morgenstern, 1944), money-pump-like (an interpretive gloss;
no trading is formalized), and the something is a real-valued utility.

9.4    Contexts: a completeness theorem for “true in the context at hand”
What is a coherent system of contexts (Section 11) about? We use a monotone multi-context
calculus whose labelled formulas i:φ are McCarthy’s ist(i, φ) (McCarthy, 1993), with a simplified
local-models semantics (Ghidini and Giunchiglia, 2001).
Definition 9.21 (Multi-context systems, MC, belief states). [T6 Def 4.0, as repaired; §4.1] For each
context i ∈ I fix a language Li , local models Mi with Ci = Thi ◦ Modi (e.g. classical logic; for a
learned logic Ci , the proper theories Mi = Fix(Ci ) \ {Li } with T |=i φ iff φ ∈ T ), local axioms
Ki , and a falsum ⊥i with Modi (⊥i ) = ∅. A bridge rule is j1 :φ1 , . . . , jn :φn ⇒ i:ψ. For labelled
premises Γ, Der(Γ) = (Ti )i is the least family with Ti ⊇ Ki ∪ Γi , each Ti Ci -closed, and closed
under bridges; Γ ⊢MC i:φ iff φ ∈ Ti . A belief state is c = (ci ), ci ⊆ Mi , with c |= i:φ iff every
m ∈ ci satisfies φ. It is a model if it satisfies every Ki and is bridge-compatible (if c |= jk :φk for
all premises then c |= i:ψ); Γ |=LMS i:φ iff every model of Γ satisfies i:φ.
Theorem 9.22 (Soundness and completeness; the canonical chain). [T6 Thm 4.1] Let cΓi =
Modi (Ti ) for (Ti ) = Der(Γ). (a) cΓ is a model of Γ. (b) Every model c of Γ has ci ⊆ cΓi : cΓ is
the least committal model. (c) Γ ⊢MC i:φ iff Γ |=LMS i:φ.

                                                 101
    Proof idea. cΓ |= i:φ iff φ ∈ Thi Modi (Ti ) = Ti ; and the theories Thi (ci ) of any model form a
closed, bridge-closed family containing K ∪ Γ, hence contain Der(Γ). Appendix H.4.

Corollary 9.23 (Context coherence iff existence). [T6 Cor 4.2] Let D ⊆ I be the designated
contexts (Definition 2.13). Γ ⊬MC i:⊥i for every i ∈ D iff some model c of Γ has ci ̸= ∅ for every
i ∈ D.

Proof. ⊥i ∈ Ti iff cΓi = ∅ by Theorem 9.22(a), and cΓ is the largest model by (b).

Remark 9.24 (Learned local logics). [T6 remark after Cor 4.2, added after verification] With the semantics
Mi = Fix(Ci ) \ {Li }, cΓi =
                           ̸ ∅ iff Ti =
                                      ̸ Li , so for learned local logics designated coherence must be
read as non-triviality of Ti ; this coincides with ⊥i ∈  / Ti exactly when ⊥i is Ci -explosive.
    Non-designated contexts, such as suppositions under reductio, may have ci = ∅: they are
about nothing, which is what a successful reductio shows. This is the designation discipline of
Section 6 as an existence theorem. The canonical chain is also the least equilibrium in the sense
of Brewka and Eiter (2007); with non-monotone (default) bridges equilibria can fail to exist
even when every local knowledge base Ki is consistent, so coherence alone does not guarantee
existence there (Proposition H.2, Appendix H.4).

Rules of proof versus conditionals between worlds. A world family w = (wi ) picks a
single model wi ∈ Mi of Ki for each i and satisfies each bridge as a material conditional: if
wjk |= φk for all k then wi |= ψ. Write |=W for consequence over world families.

Theorem 9.25 (Belief states, not worlds). [T6 Thm 4.5, as repaired] (a) |=LMS ⊆ |=W . (b) The
inclusion is strict: with classical local logics, Kj = {p ∨ q}, Ki = {¬r} and bridges j:p ⇒ i:r,
j:q ⇒ i:r, MC does not derive i:⊥, but there is no world family. (c) For classical propositional
local logics, |=W is classical consequence in the disjoint union of the local languages from the
                  (i)                                            V (j )
tagged axioms Ki , the tagged premises and the bridge axioms k φk k → ψ (i) (the “eternalist”
translation).

Proof. (a) A world family is a model chain of singletons. (b) Tj = Cn2 (p ∨ q) contains neither
p nor q, so no bridge fires and cΓi = Mod(¬r) ̸= ∅; but any wj satisfies p or q, forcing wi |= r.
(c) World families are the Boolean valuations of the tagged language satisfying the tagged
axioms.

    This is the context-logic instance of a standard distinction, rule of proof versus conditional
(global versus local consequence): c |= j:φ is a box over cj , and (b) is the failure of □(p ∨ q) |=
□p ∨ □q; it is why Ghidini and Giunchiglia (2001) use sets of local models. New is only its use as
a criterion for physics bridges. mcs_completeness.py confirms Theorem 9.22(c) on 400 random
three-context systems and finds 2,348 pairs where |=W strictly exceeds |=LMS .

Reading. MC reads a bridge as “export what context j establishes”; the eternalist translation
reads it as “whatever world j is about, if φ holds there, ψ holds in i’s world”. Only the first is
right for idealizations: a physics bridge (“if the idealized model establishes Q = q, then in the
world Q ≈ q ± ε”, Section 11.4) is certified for what the idealization establishes, not for case splits
over its undetermined possibilities, none of which is actual. So a context system makes sense iff
there is something it could be about holds with belief states as the somethings (Corollary 9.23)
and fails with worlds (Theorem 9.25(b)).


                                                   102
Local coherence versus a global world. The user worries that frames “needn’t be easily
reconcilable” (L10 §1.3). Even under the world reading this has a precise form.
Theorem 9.26 (Local-to-global on trees). [T6 Thm 4.6] Let the contexts be the vertices of a finite
tree, context i classical propositional over atoms Ai with a consistent closed theory Ti . Assume
running intersection ({i : a ∈ Ai } is connected for each atom a) and mutual conservativity on
edges (Ti ∩ L(Ai ∩ Aj ) = Tj ∩ L(Ai ∩ Aj )). Then every model of every Ti extends to a valuation
satisfying all Tj .
   This is the join-tree, or iterated Robinson-consistency, argument (cf. Beeri et al. 1983):
conservativity makes model sets agree on overlaps, and models glue breadth-first (Appendix H.4).
Proposition 9.27 (The frustrated triangle). [T6 Prop 4.7] On atoms {a, b}, {b, c}, {c, a} let Tab =
Cn2 (a ↔ ¬b), Tbc = Cn2 (b ↔ ¬c), Tca = Cn2 (c ↔ ¬a). Each is consistent, each pair is mutually
conservative on its shared atom, and the union is inconsistent; likewise pair marginals uniform
on {01, 10} agree on all single marginals but have no joint distribution.
Proof. An odd cycle of negations has no Boolean solution; each theory has only tautologies in a
single atom.

    This is Specker’s parable in logical form (Specker, 1960) and the simplest instance of contex-
tuality: local sections agreeing on overlaps with no global section (Abramsky and Brandenburger,
2011). Acyclicity of the cover is what rules it out (Beeri et al., 1983). So an olympiad solution’s
contexts need not describe one world, nor be reconcilable into one. What coherence of the
designated contexts guarantees is local belief states, nonempty where designated (Corollary 9.23;
cf. Remark 11.14); one world for all contexts is strictly stronger and cover-dependent.
Definition 9.28 (True in the context at hand). [T6 Def 4.8, as repaired] φ is true in context c iff
Γ ⊢MC c:φ, i.e. (Theorem 9.22) φ holds in every local model that c’s canonical belief state leaves
open. φ is true enough in c for a world claim ε iff (i) φ is true in c; (ii) a bridge c:φ ⇒ @:ε,
possibly with side conditions σ, is in the system and σ holds in the actual world (for a checker,
Γ ⊢MC @:σ); and (iii) that bridge is sound: whenever c establishes φ and σ holds in the actual
world, so does ε.
    This answers the user’s “maybe you just check that a claim is true in the context at hand. but
wtf is that???” (verification from truth.md): truth in the context at hand is supervaluational
truth over the context’s admissible local models, and a checker accepting exactly MC-derivations
accepts exactly what is true in this sense (compare Theorem 11.39). It does not justify bridges:
bridge soundness is world-relative and is certified by stability arguments or world feedback
(Section 11.4), never by coherence, which can only demand cΓi ̸= ∅ at designated contexts. The
context-tree calculus of the literature memo L7 is an MC system, and its own semantics agrees
with ours when every step and export instance is sound (proof sketch; Appendix H.4).

9.5   What coherence of a learned calculus buys, and what pins the model
      down
Let R be a learned set of rule schemas, CR the least structural finitary consequence operation
containing it, and ⟨R⟩ the least structural Scott relation. The designated contexts A ∈ A are
certified coherent: ⊥ ∈
                      / CR (A), or [A : D] is ⟨R⟩-coherent. As in Section 6, this is all the learner
knows.

                                                103
Theorem 9.29 (What coherence buys). [T6 Thm 5.1] For each designated A: (a) some CR -theory
T ⊇ A is maximal among theories omitting ⊥; (b) the Lindenbaum matrix ⟨Fm, T ⟩ validates
every rule of CR under every assignment, and the identity assignment designates all of A and
not ⊥ (Łoś and Suszko, 1958; Wójcicki, 1988); (c) bilaterally, each designated coherent [A : D]
is realized by some v ∈ Val⟨R⟩, and Lv is a matrix model of ⟨R⟩.

Proof. (a) is the Zorn step in the proof of Theorem 9.5(a), applied to the closed set CR (A) and
σ = ⊥ (Appendix H.1). (b) If φ ∈ CR (Γ) and σΓ ⊆ T , structurality gives σφ ∈ CR (σΓ) ⊆
CR (T ) = T . (c) is Theorems 9.8 and 9.11.

    (The proof of (b) works for any CR -theory T ⊇ A omitting ⊥.) So if a learned mode of
talking is coherent on a context, there is something it is talking about there. But the model’s
universe is the language: the formal content of the user’s worry about the “proof” of the Gödel
sentence. When is the syntactic something the intended one? For a matrix ⟨A, D⟩ the Leibniz
congruence ΩA (D) is the largest congruence compatible with D (a θ b and a ∈ D imply b ∈ D),
and the reduction ⟨A/Ω, D/Ω⟩ identifies elements no formula context can tell apart (Blok and
Pigozzi, 1989; Font, 2016); write Ω(T ) for ΩFm (T ).

Theorem 9.30 (The syntactic model collapses to the intended one). [T6 Thm 5.2] (a) If h : Fm →
A is a surjective homomorphism and T = h−1 (D), then

                        ⟨Fm/Ω(T ), T /Ω(T )⟩ ∼
                                             = ⟨A/ΩA (D), D/ΩA (D)⟩;

if ⟨A, D⟩ is reduced, the reduced Lindenbaum matrix of T is ⟨A, D⟩. (b) Every maximal consistent
theory of CPC has reduced Lindenbaum matrix ∼  = ⟨2, {1}⟩.

   Proof idea. Ω(T ) = h−1 (ΩA D), because a congruence compatible with T , joined with ker h,
pushes forward along h to one compatible with D (Appendix H.5). Surjectivity matters: in
general one gets the reduction of the generated submatrix.

Proposition 9.31 (What the points of a finite-matrix logic talk about). [T6 Prop 5.3, as repaired] Let
C be the consequence of a finite matrix ⟨A, D⟩. Every point of C has reduced Lindenbaum matrix
isomorphic to the reduction of a generated submatrix ⟨h(Fm), D ∩ h(Fm)⟩ (a strict homomorphic
image of a submatrix, not necessarily a submatrix); up to isomorphism there are finitely many.

Proof. C is sound and strongly complete for the homomorphisms h : Fm → A, and finitary by
compactness of AVar (the standard argument: each condition h(γ) ∈ D is clopen there, as A is
finite). So every point is some h−1 (D) by Theorem 9.5(b); apply Theorem 9.30(a) to h onto its
image.

    So for classical logic everything a coherent position can be about is, after identifying indis-
cernibles, the two truth values, because every point of CPC is maximal (Lemma 9.7) and maximal
theories are Boolean. The reason is not Post-completeness: IPC is not Post-complete, yet its
maximal consistent theories are classical (Proposition 9.6) and reduce to 2. What can differ
between calculi is the non-maximal points.

Example 9.32 (The residue as a set of things talked about). [T6 Ex 5.4] (i) Map Fm onto the
three-element Heyting chain H3 = {0 < 12 < 1} with h(p) = 12 . Then T = h−1 (1) is a non-
maximal point of IPC (maximal among theories omitting p) whose reduced matrix is ⟨H3 , {1}⟩:
one coherent intuitionistic position talks about the three-element chain while the maximal ones

                                                 104
talk about 2. (ii) First-order logic with equality plus the fallacy ∀x∃y R ▷ ∃y∀x R is coherent
(Example 6.36); its models are exactly the one-element structures, which is what the learned
fallacy is “about”.

    In general, for multiple-conclusion hypotheses, Theorems 9.10 and 9.11 put the residue
Alt(h∗ , A) of coherent uniform alternatives (Theorem 6.38) in exact correspondence with the
closed, substitution-invariant V ⊆ Val(h∗ ) realizing every designated position. Non-identifiability
of meaning is non-uniqueness of the thing talked about.

First-order term models. For a consistent first-order theory, the Lindenbaum construction
is Henkin’s (Henkin, 1949): witnesses, a complete Henkin theory, closed terms modulo provable
equality (the Leibniz reduction). The user’s DLK note says that “any assignment of truth-values
to sentences which does not violate inference rules can be extended to a full model (this is cool
because a model also needs to assign actual functions and objects . . . )”. This is correct for
complete consistent assignments: the objects are classes of witness terms. If the assignment
makes ∃x φ(x) true and every named instance false, the model needs unnamed objects, the
Gaifman failure of Corollary 9.19.

Theorem 9.33 (Prime models). [T6 Thm 5.5] (known) For complete consistent T ⊇ PA the defin-
able elements of any model of T form the prime model K(T ), unique up to isomorphism and
elementarily embeddable in every model of T (Kaye, 1991); K(Th(N)) = N; if ¬Con(PA) ∈ T ,
K(T ) is non-standard.

    For the last clause: “the least code of a PA-proof of 0 = 1” is definable, while T refutes each
standard instance Prf(n, ⌜0=1⌝), whose negation is a true ∆0 sentence (PA being consistent) and
so provable in Q ⊆ T . This is the precise form of the user’s footnote: a complete coherent theory
has a canonical thing it talks about, the objects it can define, and the “proof” is one of them, a
definable non-standard number.

Two layers of non-standardness. (i) Coherent but false: PA + ¬Con(PA) is consistent, false,
even Π1 -sound (L4 Prop 3.1); this is finitary and comes from incompleteness (coherence is not
truth, Theorem 6.22(b)). (ii) True but not categorical: Th(N) itself has non-standard models,
a limit phenomenon from compactness. Pinning comes in levels: P0 coherent (a model exists);
P1 complete (all models elementarily equivalent; for logics, Post-complete, Theorem 6.13); P2
κ-categorical; P3 categorical (unique model up to isomorphism; for calculi, all points reduce to
one matrix, Proposition 9.31).

Theorem 9.34 (Compactness barrier). [T6 Thm 5.6] (known) A first-order theory with an infinite
model has models of every infinite cardinality ≥ |L| (Löwenheim–Skolem–Tarski), so no first-order
mode of talking reaches P3 for an infinite structure.

   Every finite structure in a finite language, by contrast, is characterized up to isomor-
phism by one sentence. In the user’s icosahedron example (math is a mere string game
iff everything is.md), after the physical icosahedron explodes the mode of talking stays
coherent and its object is still pinned up to isomorphism; the caveat bites only for infinite objects.

Theorem 9.35 (Computability barrier). [T6 Thm 5.7, as repaired] Let S be a decidable set of finite
syntactic objects and K an intended class of models. If some calculus has an r.e. set of incoherent
finite sets, and a finite X ⊆ S is incoherent iff it has no model in K, then {X : X has a K-model}

                                                105
is co-r.e. (Π1 ). Consequently no such calculus exists for (a) arithmetic with K = {N} (Th(N)
is not arithmetical); (b) Diophantine equations with K = {Z} (solvability is Σ1 -complete,
Matiyasevich 1970); (c) first-order sentences in a vocabulary with a binary relation symbol, with
K the finite structures (finite satisfiability is Σ1 and undecidable, Trakhtenbrot 1950; for monadic
vocabularies it is decidable). (d) For K all structures, satisfiability is Π1 , as Gödel’s theorem
requires; for polynomial equations over k̄ it is decidable.

Proof. “No K-model” is “incoherent”, which is r.e. In (b), (c) the set is also Σ1 , hence would be
decidable; in (a) Th(N) would be Π1 , contradicting Tarski’s undefinability theorem (e.g. Boolos
et al. 2007).

    This is the textbook argument “a complete r.e. calculus makes semantic consequence r.e.”;
what is new is the framing. For any computable notion of coherence, “coherent iff there is
something it could be talking about” can hold only if having such a something is a Π1 property.
“Having some model” is Π1 ; “having the standard model”, “having an integer solution” and
“having a finite model” (in a vocabulary with a binary relation symbol) are not. The proposition
is true exactly to the extent that “something” is construed liberally.

Proposition 9.36 (Anchoring pins exactly the definable vocabulary). [T6 Prop 5.8, reading as repaired]
Let Lo ⊆ L be the anchored (world-hooked) vocabulary and T an L-theory. Any two models of
T with the same universe and the same interpretation of Lo coincide iff every symbol of L is
explicitly T -definable from Lo (Beth, 1953).

Reading. Beth’s theorem quantifies over all models of T , i.e. over every possible anchoring
structure: anchoring determines the rest of the language uniformly, whatever the anchoring
structure iff the rest is definable. Over the actual structure pinning can hold without definability:
if T says that R is a nonempty initial segment of < without a largest element, then over (N, <)
the only expansion is R = N, while over ω + ω both R = ω and R = ω + ω work. Undefinable
theoretical terms are multiply realizable over some anchoring, though perhaps not over the actual
one (the Ramsey–Lewis situation, Lewis 1970).

How are the natural numbers pinned down? The user asks this, adding “is there a
way they are the ‘lowest-complexity-structure’ which satisfy some axioms??”. Coherence and
first-order completeness cannot do it (Theorems 9.34 and 9.35). The known pins each add
something: second-order induction (Dedekind, 1888) adds quantification over all subsets, and no
complete r.e. calculus exists for its consequence, as Theorem 9.35(a) predicts; internal categoricity
(Parsons, 1990; McGee, 1997; Button and Walsh, 2018) pins the structure relative to the available
language, not the theory. Initiality (N is the initial (0, S)-algebra, the least Herbrand model of
{N (0), N (x) → N (Sx)}, van Emden and Kowalski 1976, and an initial segment of every model
of PA) adds a universal property, “nothing else is a number”, which is not first-order. The ω-rule,
or Gaifman condition, adds an infinitary rule that no limit-computable credence of the relevant
kind obeys (Corollary 9.19). World anchoring by computation adds nothing beyond coherence
with Q (Lemma 6.19). And:

Theorem 9.37 (Tennenbaum). [T6 §5.5] (known) In no countable non-standard model of PA is
addition computable, and in none is multiplication computable (Tennenbaum, 1959; Kaye, 1991).
Hence N is the unique computable model of PA up to isomorphism.



                                                 106
   This is a theorem-level “yes” to the “lowest-complexity structure” question, with two caveats:
“computable” is defined using the meta-language’s natural numbers, as the user himself notes
(logic/confusions/soundness.md); and the axioms matter, since weaker theories such as open
induction have computable non-standard models (Shepherdson, 1964). Every pin uses something
beyond finitary computable coherence: second-order quantification, a universal property, a
complexity bound measured in N, or an infinitary rule. Theorems 9.34 and 9.35 say this is forced.

9.6   Verdict
Where yes. In each row of Table 12, “coherent iff there is something it could be talking about”
is a theorem, with a realization step that is non-trivial except for positions, where points already
are valuations; for the modes of talking that matter to the learner, see Theorems 9.8, 9.12, 9.17
and 9.29, Proposition 9.20, and Corollary 9.23. Sam’s slogan holds in each strongly complete
case (Proposition 9.3 and Theorem 9.10).

Where no. (1) The something may be made of syntax (Theorems 9.4 and 9.29); the proposition
is substantive only for an independently specified semantics, and which semantics is right is
answered by which models we can independently access: the truth values, N through computation,
the world through observation. (2) Weak ̸= strong (Proposition 9.6). (3) Intended existence
needs more than coherence: layer (i) is defeated only by truth-tracking principles (reflection,
stronger theories, Popperian policies, Theorem 6.22), layer (ii) only by infinitary, second-order or
meta-level ingredients, and both limits are forced (Theorems 9.34 and 9.35). (4) The something
for a context system is a family of belief states, not of worlds (Theorem 9.25), and a global
world exists automatically only on tree-like covers with conservative overlaps (Theorem 9.26
and Proposition 9.27). (5) Probabilistic coherence is global: local constraints on an unclosed
agenda need not make a probe about any mixture of worlds (Theorems 9.14 and 9.15).

What this means for the learner. The user’s split between coherence and “hooking onto the
world” becomes two precise questions. Coherence answers “is there any world this way of talking
could be about?”: yes, canonically, but partly syntactically. Hooking answers “is it this world?”:
world feedback is positive data in valuation space (Section 6.4), and uniformly over anchorings it
pins exactly the definable vocabulary (Proposition 9.36). Neither does the other’s job. This is the
division of labour of Sections 6, 7 and 11 seen semantically: coherence removes what is incoherent
with the target, its residue Alt is a set of non-isomorphic somethings, and only world channels or
extra pins select among them. The user’s “C-model without an L-model” (logical models as
distinct from mental models.md) becomes an incoherent calculus used only through coherent
chunks: local belief states (Corollary 9.23) without a global section (Proposition 9.27). And
the computability barrier is a formal reason why there is no final formula for N: pins such as
reflection or stronger theories must be added on other grounds, matching the open-ended picture
of justification in Section 13.

Verdict. The philosophical proposition is true in a precise generalized sense: for positions,
credences, preferences, context systems and learned structural calculi, coherence is equivalent to
the existence of a model of the kind the semantics allows. It is not vindicated in the intended
sense: standard or intended existence needs categoricity, anchoring or an infinitary condition,
and no computable coherence notion can certify it beyond Π1 .


                                                107
10      Informal mathematics: learning a latent formalization
This section is the paper’s answer to the second success criterion: a setup that would have worked
for mathematical proofs before anyone knew how to formalize them. The user asks it in two
sharper forms. “How come mathematicians ended up with such a nice notion of proof? . . . this
nice structure of a formal proof is sorta there in their activities. how did it come to be there?”
And, as a hypothesis: pre-formal mathematicians used only the part of their concepts that later
survived formalization, because “the ‘rest of the concept’ . . . was untrustworthy/messy . . . and
for this reason unusable whenever people were proving things” (the robust-core hypothesis).
    The model: a pre-formal practice is the shadow of an unknown latent formalization h∗ =
(L , R∗ , ρ∗ , M∗ ), consisting of a formal language, a calculus with a library of definitions and
  ∗

lemmas, a partial, context-dependent reading of informal sentence occurrences into the language,
and an intended semantics. A human step is the ρ∗ -image of an R∗ -derivation with at most g rule
applications (an operational de Bruijn factor), plus noise. The learner sees accepted steps (P),
paradoxes derived from designated contexts (C), and objects (O): examples and counterexamples
on which informal sentences can be evaluated. We show that a conservative verifier which checks
steps and re-uses of sentences across contexts against every surviving latent formalization is sound
against adaptive provers (Section 10.2); that closure-level data identify the latent formalization
up to sameness of informal consequence on the practice’s domain and no finer, that bounded-gap
data identify its short steps but only bracket its meaning, and that ontology, and the latent
language beyond granularity, are unidentifiable (Section 10.3); that counterexample objects can
be exponentially more informative than paradoxes, whose price grows linearly with the number
of suspect steps (Section 10.4); that Frege → Russell → Zermelo is a theorem about simplicity,
coherence and coverage in a comprehension toy (Section 10.5); and that a robust core of steps
valid under every admissible sharpening survives every formalization, while selection by objects
plus Gödel completeness explains why surviving steps are derivable, though not why they are
short (Section 10.6).
    Two caveats govern the section. Every positive result assumes realizability, that the true
latent formalization lies in the learner’s fixed class, whereas the decisive historical steps (ε-δ,
uniform convergence, ideals) were inventions of language; affordable soundness with language
invention is the hardest open problem we leave (Section 10.7). And most results are trivial
once set up; their value lies in definitions under which history becomes theorems. The genuine
mathematical content is the paradox side of the object–paradox separation (Theorem 10.23
and Proposition 10.25; its novelty relative to the Littlestone-dimension literature is uncertain) and
the comprehension-toy facts (Lemma 10.31); the deep ingredients are cited (Gödel completeness,
Robinson’s transfer, Nelson’s conservativity, Incurvati and Murzi’s theorem).

10.1     The model
Definition 10.1 (Occurrences, steps, arguments, links). [T4 §1.1] Ξ is a countable set of informal
sentence strings and C a set of contexts (definitions, conventions, notation and local suppositions
in force). An occurrence is x = (c, ξ) ∈ X := C × Ξ, with str(x) := ξ. An informal step is Γ ▷ y
with Γ ⊆fin X, y ∈ X. An informal argument α is a list of lines y1 , . . . , ym ∈ X, each a premise,
an inference Γj ▷ yj with Γj among earlier lines, or a link yi ⇝ yj with i < j and str(yi ) = str(yj )
(“the same claim, used in another context”). Prem(α) is its set of premises, ym its conclusion,
and its depth the length of its longest dependency path.
     Links are the formal locus of equivocation: a sentence proved in one context is re-used in

                                                 108
another, where it may be read differently.

Definition 10.2 (Latent formalization). [T4 Def 1.1, §1.2] A latent formalization is a quadruple
h = (Lh , Rh , ρh , Mh ) consisting of a countable language Lh with a sentence ⊥; a set Rh of formal
steps Π ▷ φ over Sent(Lh ) (instances of finitely many schemas plus a library of definitions and
derived lemmas); a partial reading ρh : X ⇀ Sent(Lh ) with domain Dh ; and a class Mh of
Lh -structures for which Rh is sound and ⊥ is false.
  (i) Γ ▷ y is valid under h, written (Γ ▷ y) ∈ |=h or Γ |=h y, iff Γ ∪ {y} ⊆ Dh and ρh (y) ∈
      ClRh (ρh Γ).
 (ii) It is g-valid, (Γ ▷ y) ∈ Stgh , iff moreover ρh (y) has an Rh -derivation from ρh Γ with at most
      g rule applications and formulas of size bounded by a fixed function of g and the input.
      We assume ρh computable with decidable domain, Rh decidable, and derivations restricted
      to the input’s symbols plus a finite, computably given part of Lh depending on g; then Stgh
      is decidable and Stgh ⊆ |=h , which is all that is used.
 (iii) A link yi ⇝ yj is valid under h iff ({yi } ▷ yj ) ∈ Stgh (typically ρh (yi ) = ρh (yj ): zero
       applications).
 (iv) The admissible valuations are Vh := {vM ◦ ρh : M ∈ Mh }, with vM (φ) = [M |= φ].
 (v) h is first-order complete if Lh is first-order, Mh = Mod(Th ), and ClRh (Γ) = {φ : Th ∪Γ |= φ}.

   De Bruijn’s ratio of formal to informal proof size is roughly constant (intrinsically about 4,
Wiedijk 2000) only relative to a definitional library, as Mathias’s 4.5 × 1012 -symbol term for
Bourbaki’s “1” shows (Mathias, 2002); so g is measured over Rh with its library.

Assumption 10.3 ((BG), bounded gap). [T4 §1.3] There is a target h∗ = (L∗ , R∗ , ρ∗ , M∗ ), with
D∗ := Dh∗ , such that (BG1) h∗ ∈ H (realizability); (BG2) each accepted human step is, with
probability 1 − η, drawn from a distribution over Stgh∗ , and otherwise noise, sporadic or systematic
(instances of a fallacy schema); (BG3) human links are h∗ -valid up to the same noise.

    Steps that are valid but not g-short are routed out of (P): they are escalated with a request
to expand them (Proposition 10.11), or count as noise.

Definition 10.4 (The three channels). [T4 §1.4] (P) Practice: the stream of (BG2); escalation
answers are practice data labelled by Stgh∗ . (C) Coherence: a family A of finite designated
contexts A ⊆ X with the promise ⊥ ∈   / ClR∗ (ρ∗ A); a paradox for h is an Rh -derivation of ⊥ from
ρh A, A ∈ A, and refutes h. Suppositions are not designated, so reductio is never penalized. (O)
Objects: a finite partial valuation v : X ⇀ {0, 1} with the promise v ⊆ u for some u ∈ Vh∗ ; it
refutes h iff v|Dh extends to no member of Vh . Interactively, the learner holds an object and
queries its values (“checks the step on an example”). Computation is the special case of an
intended object M0 ∈ M∗ queried on a decidable fragment, like Euler’s six-decimal value of
   1/n2 . Convention: ρh A := ρh (A ∩ Dh ), so a sentence h cannot read asserts nothing under h.
P


    (O) is the world channel (W) of Definition 2.13 inside mathematics: objects (W2), with
computation (W1, W3) as a special case. The model fixes ρ∗ (Section 10.6 makes it vague
instead) and the class of languages.




                                                109
10.2    Sound bounded-gap verification
At time t, h is consistent with the data if it labels every practice step and escalation answer
correctly by Stgh , agrees with every link answer, and has not been refuted by a paradox or an
object datum found within the search budget so far. VSt is the set of such h. Every refutation is
a certificate, so budgeted search never refutes an unrefutable hypothesis.

Definition 10.5 (Bounded-gap conservative verifier). [T4 Def 2.1] On a query q (an informal step
or a link), Vtg answers ACCEPT iff q is g-valid, resp. link-valid, under every h ∈ VSt , with a
certificate for each; otherwise ESCALATE (ask the oracle, or ask the prover to expand q).

    Vtg is computable for finite H; for infinite H unanimity is a Π1 condition, and the effective
version is the Bayesian verifier below, truncated to finitely many hypotheses. Any budgeted proof
search that counts “not found” as “not valid” only shrinks the accepted set and so preserves the
soundness results below (T4 Prop 2.6), though not the escalation bounds.

Lemma 10.6 (Argument soundness). [T4 Lemma 2.2, repaired] If every line of α lies in Dh and every
inference and link of α (a link as the step {yi } ▷ yj ) lies in |=h , then ρh (yj ) ∈ ClRh (ρh Prem(α))
for every line yj . Hence every line is true in every M ∈ Mh that makes the premises true.

Proof. Induction on j: ρh yj ∈ ClRh (ρh Γj ) ⊆ ClRh (ClRh (ρh Prem)) = ClRh (ρh Prem) by mono-
tonicity and idempotence (Lemma 2.3); links are the case Γj = {yi }. Then use soundness of
Rh .

Proposition 10.7 (Equivocation: step-checking alone is unsound; trivial). [T4 Prop 2.3] There
are h and α such that every inference of α is h-valid and every link joins equal strings, yet
Prem(α) ▷ ym is h-invalid.
                                                  P
Proof (Cauchy’s sum theorem). Let ρh read “ fn converges” as uniform convergence in context
c1 (Cauchy’s proof) and as pointwise convergence in c2 (the statement). Lines: (1), (2) premises
                                       P
(c2 , “each fn is continuous”), (c2 , “ fn converges”); (3), (4) links of (2), (1) into c1 ; (5) the valid
inference to (c1 , “the sum is continuous”); (6) a link of (5) back into c2 . Links (4) and (6) are valid;
link (3), pointwise to uniform, is not; and {1, 2} ▷ 6 fails on Abel’s series n (−1)n+1 sin(nx)/n,
                                                                                 P

whose sum is x/2 on (−π, π), extended periodically.

   So the verifier checks links. Lakatos’s “hidden lemma” in Cauchy’s proof (Lakatos, 1976,
App. 1) is, here, an invalid link.

Theorem 10.8 (Deterministic soundness; trivial once set up). [T4 Thm 2.4] Assume (BG1) and
noise-free truthful data: practice and escalation answers lie in and are labelled by Stgh∗ , link
answers agree with h∗ , designated contexts are h∗ -coherent, object data are h∗ -admissible. Then
for every prover (randomized, adaptive, unbounded, knowing h∗ , the verifier and the history) and
every t, everything Vtg accepts lies in Stgh∗ ⊆ |=h∗ ; so every argument assembled from accepted
items has an h∗ -valid conclusion (unused premises outside D∗ dropped), true in every M ∈ M∗
satisfying its premises.

Proof. No datum can refute h∗ , so h∗ ∈ VSt , and unanimity implies g-validity under h∗ ; then
apply Lemma 10.6.




                                                   110
   There is no union bound over queries (as in Section 4): one structural fact, h∗ ∈ VSt , covers
every attempt. Incoherent survivors only shrink the intersection: Frege’s inconsistent 1903 “way
out” (Quine, 1955) would have made a conservative verifier timid, never unsound. With noisy
practice h∗ can be refuted, so we use a posterior; the threshold then involves the prior mass of
the target’s observational-equivalence class [h∗ ] (Definition 10.12).
Theorem 10.9 (Time-uniform soundness with a class prior). [T4 Thm 2.5; T1 Thm 4.2] Let H be
countable with prior w and assume (BG1). Each h has a practice likelihood ph (including noise);
practice data are i.i.d. from ph∗ , each independent of everything before it (the prover does not
foresee them); likelihoods are shadow-measurable (ph = ph′ whenever h ≡g h′ ); all other data
(escalation and link answers, paradoxes, objects) are truthful and multiply the weight of h by 1
if consistent with h, by 0 otherwise. Let Vtg,δ accept q iff the posterior gives {h : q ∈
                                                                                        / Stgh } mass
below δ, and put W ∗ := w([h∗ ]). If δ ≤ W ∗ δ ′ , there is an event of probability at least 1 − δ ′ ,
depending only on the practice data, on which no prover ever gets a query outside Stgh∗ accepted.
Proof idea. With Zt := h w(h)Lt (h)/Lt (h∗ ), shadow-measurability gives wt ([h∗ ]) = W ∗ /Zt ;
                           P

constraints only shrink Zt , which is dominated by a practice-only nonnegative supermartingale
with initial value 1. Ville’s inequality (Ville, 1939) keeps it below 1/δ ′ forever with probability
≥ 1 − δ ′ , and then wt ([h∗ ]) > δ. This is the prior–posterior-ratio martingale of Waudby-Smith
and Ramdas (2020). Full proof in Appendix I.

    W ∗ counts the ≡g -class (renamings; the ontological variants of Proposition 10.17), not the
meaning class: at gap g, soundness is paid for at the level of g-shadows, between formalizations
and meanings. Its price is escalations. Let Hg := {Stgh : h ∈ H} and let el(Hg , Stgh∗ ) be the
positive elasticity of Section 4: the supremum of lengths of sequences s1 , s2 , . . . ∈ Stgh∗ with each
si outside the intersection of the members of Hg containing s1 , . . . , si−1 .
Theorem 10.10 (Escalation costs). [T4 Thm 2.7, repaired; T1 §§3–4] Let the prover be honest and
count only escalations answered by the oracle (expansion requests yield no label). (a) With
noise-free data Vtg escalates at most el(Hg , Stgh∗ ) ≤ |H/≡g | − 1 times. (b) In the setting of
Theorem 10.9, with probability ≥ 1 − δ ′′ , Vtg,δ escalates at most (ln(1/W ∗ ) + ln(1/δ ′′ ))/δ times in
all. (c) If the n hypotheses’ g-step relations are B ∪ (U \ {u}), u ∈ U , then (a) equals n − 1 and is
attained, and with the uniform prior every δ ′ -sound verifier fails to accept (escalates or rejects),
in expectation, at least (1 − δ ′ )(n − 1) queries of some honest prover, so (b) with δ = W ∗ δ ′ is
tight up to a factor O((ln(1/W ∗ ) + ln(1/δ ′′ ))/δ ′ ).
    Proof in Appendix I, by reduction to the escalation theorems of Section 4. Expansion closes
the gap. Call h∗ step-expressive if every (Γ ▷ y) ∈ |=h∗ is the conclusion of an informal argument
from Γ whose inferences lie in St1h∗ (one rule application each) and whose links join occurrences
with equal ρ∗ -readings.
Proposition 10.11 (Expansion makes the verifier complete; trivial). [T4 Prop 2.8] If h∗ is step-
expressive and h∈VSt St1h ⊇ St1h∗ , then every h∗ -valid step has an argument all of whose steps
                T

and links Vt1 accepts.
Proof. Each inference of the expansion lies in St1h∗ , and each link is in St1h∗ with zero applications;
all lie in every survivor’s St1h .

   Under complete presentation, the hypothesis on VSt is what Theorem 10.15(d) delivers at
g = 1.

                                                  111
Reading. The architecture the user sketches, a checker that asks the arguer to “explain/justify/
derive” what it does not recognize, is sound at all times and complete via expansion once the
atomic level is learned. Learning which informal inferences are valid becomes learning a set of
latent formalizations and accepting what all of them validate; no measure over steps enters the
guarantee. For contexts, Proposition 10.7 says that a sentence true in one context is not thereby
usable in another: the re-use is an inference to be checked, the zero-length case of the export
rules of Section 11.

10.3    What practice can identify
Definition 10.12 (Equivalences). [T4 Def 3.1] h ≈ h′ iff |=h = |=h′ (this fixes Dh = {x : {x} |=h
x}); h ≈D h′ iff they agree on steps with all occurrences in D. h ≡g h′ iff Stgh = Stgh′ , Vh = Vh′ ,
and h, h′ are coherent on the same A ∈ A; ≡∞ is the same with |= for Stg .

Proposition 10.13 (No learner sees past the shadow; trivial). [T4 Prop 3.2, repaired] Suppose the
law of the practice data is a function of Stgh∗ and the history, escalation and link answers are
Stgh∗ -labels, the law of the object data (which occurrences are shown or queried, and their values)
is a function of Vh∗ and the history, A does not depend on the target, and queries come from the
learner or from provers that depend on the target only through its shadow. If h ≡g h′ , every data
sequence has the same probability under target h as under h′ , so no learner distinguishes them.
The closure-level version replaces Stg by |= and ≡g by ≡∞ .

    Proof in Appendix I. Which shadow determines which? Call Dh negation-closed if an informal
operation x 7→ ∼x (“it is not the case that . . . ”) preserves Dh with Th ⊢ ρh (∼x) ↔ ¬ρh (x),
and call v : Dh → {0, 1} a coherent valuation of |=h if v(∼x) = 1 − v(x) and v(y) = 1 whenever
Γ |=h y for a finite Γ ⊆ v −1 (1).

Lemma 10.14 (Informal completeness). [T4 Lemma 3.3, repaired] (standard corollary of compactness
                                                                        ̸ ∅. (i) Vh is exactly the
and completeness) Let h be first-order complete with negation-closed Dh =
set of coherent valuations of |=h . (ii) Γ |=h y iff Γ ∪ {y} ⊆ Dh and every v ∈ Vh with v(Γ) = 1
has v(y) = 1. (iii) A finite A ⊆ Dh is h-coherent iff v(A) = 1 for some v ∈ Vh , iff A ̸|=h y for
some y ∈ Dh . So |=h determines Vh and coherence; conversely Vh determines |=h if Vh ̸= ∅, and
coherence of all finite subsets of Dh determines it, but coherence on a fixed family A does not.

Proof idea. A coherent v makes Th ∪ {ρh (x) : v(x) = 1} finitely satisfiable (a finite inconsistent
part would force v(x) = v(∼x) = 1), so by compactness some model induces v; the rest is
completeness. Full proof in Appendix I.

Reading. This is the user’s “philosophical completeness theorem” (“a mode of talking makes
sense (is coherent) iff there is something it could be talking about”) for a practice’s informal
shadow: the coherent ways of evaluating the practice’s sentences are exactly its admissible objects.
It is a direct corollary of Lindenbaum, compactness and completeness (Gödel, 1930). His caveat
is visible in Mh = Mod(Th ): if only standard objects are presented, strengthenings such as
T + Con(T ) survive, over-generalizations of the practice’s validity relation though not errors
about truth (Section 9).
     For a step set S and D ⊆ X, let ChD (S) be the steps Γ ▷ y derivable from Γ by an argument
whose lines lie in D and whose inferences and links lie in S. h∗ is step-expressive on D∗ at gap g
if ChD∗ (Stgh∗ ) = |=h∗ (implied by step-expressiveness, for g ≥ 1).


                                                112
Theorem 10.15 (Identification up to ≈ on the practice’s domain). [T4 Thm 3.4, (d) repaired] Let
every h ∈ H be first-order complete with negation-closed nonempty domain (one fixed ∼), let T ∗
be consistent, and let A consist of subsets of D∗ .
 (a) h ≡∞ h′ iff h ≈ h′ .
 (b) Under the closure-level version of Proposition 10.13, no learner identifies the target more
     finely than ≈.
 (c) Let H be finite with h∗ ∈ H (BG1), and the presentation complete (every element of |=h∗
     appears in (P), every finite restriction of every member of Vh∗ in (O)). After finitely many
     data the survivors are exactly U ∗ := {h : h ≈D∗ h∗ }, and unanimity on |=h accepts on D∗
     exactly |=h∗ . (This concerns the ideal version space: refutation by a practice datum needs a
     certificate of non-validity, which is co-r.e.)
 (d) If instead (P) presents Stgh∗ completely, the survivors are eventually exactly

                             S g := {h ∈ H : Stgh ⊇ Stgh∗ and |=h |D∗ ⊆ |=h∗ },

      V g accepts exactly Stgh∗ , and every survivor’s meaning is bracketed: ChD∗ (Stgh∗ ) ⊆ |=h |D∗ ⊆
      |=h∗ . If h∗ is step-expressive on D∗ at gap g, then S g = {h ∈ U ∗ : Stgh ⊇ Stgh∗ }; without it,
      survivors can have strictly weaker meaning. Here all refutations are semi-decidable, so the
      budgeted VSt reaches S g .

Proof idea. The key is an upper-bracket lemma: for Dh ⊇ D∗ , some object datum refutes h iff
|=h |D∗ ̸⊆ |=h∗ , and if |=h |D∗ ⊆ |=h∗ no paradox refutes h. A step valid under h but not h∗ has
an h∗ -countermodel (Lemma 10.14(ii)) whose restriction refutes h; conversely a datum with
no h-admissible extension yields a finite h-inconsistent, hence h∗ -inconsistent, set of literals,
contradicting its origin. Practice removes hypotheses that miss a presented step. The lower
bracket is Lemma 10.6 under h. Full proof in Appendix I.

Example 10.16 (A weaker rival survives bounded-gap data). [T4 Thm 3.4(d), referee A] (computed)
Read D∗ as p0 , ¬p0 , p3 , ¬p3 ; let T ∗ = {p0 → p1 , p1 → p2 , p2 → p3 } with axiom steps plus one-step
tautological consequence, and let the rival have Th = ∅. For g ≤ 3 the g-step relations agree on D∗ ,
but (p0 ▷ p3 ) ∈ |=h∗ \ |=h , and neither objects nor paradoxes refute the rival (Mod(T ∗ ) ⊆ Mod(∅));
at g = 4 it is refuted. On 2604 random (target, rival) pairs the original statement of (d), “survivors
= {h ∈ U ∗ : Stgh ⊇ Stgh∗ }”, mismatches the true survivors 527 times, the revised one never, and
the original never on the 1545 step-expressive targets (T4-checks/verification_checks.py,
V1). Verified in Appendix I.

Reading. Bounded-gap data identify the g-step relation and bracket meaning: practice bounds
it from below, objects from above (they refute a hypothesis for validating too much, never too
little). Coarser rivals, same inferences in larger steps, survive by Gold’s asymmetry (Gold, 1967),
now about obviousness; weaker rivals miss inferences of h∗ that are not chains of short steps
through the practice’s own sentences. So a practice is guaranteed to fix the meaning of its
sentences when its long inferences decompose into short steps through them, and otherwise it
may not; expansion is the remedy.
     The equivalence classes are large: practices fix inferential role and leave ontology and latent
language open.



                                                  113
Proposition 10.17 (Ontology is unidentifiable at every gap). [T4 Prop 3.5, repaired] Let h =
(L, R, ρ, M) and h′ = (L, R, ρ, M′ ) be latent formalizations such that every member of M′ agrees
with some member of M on all sentences in ρ(Dh ), and vice versa. Then h ≡g h′ for every g,
and h ≡∞ h′ .
Proof. R, ρ are shared, so Stg , |= and paradoxes are; and vM ◦ ρ depends only on the truth values
of ρ(Dh ) in M .

    Instance (modulo the cited transfer principle). Let ρ read informal analysis into bounded sentences
of a countable sublanguage of the ∈-language of the superstructure V (R), with constants for the
standard objects the practice can name, and let R be first-order logic with bounded axioms true
in V (R). With M = {V (R)} and M′ = {∗ V (R)}, Robinson’s transfer principle (Robinson, 1966),
which rests on Łoś’s theorem, gives the hypothesis. So whether the intended reals of practice
contain infinitesimals cannot be learned from any data in this language. Boundedness matters:
the two structures are not elementarily equivalent in the full ∈-language (“iterated power sets of
R exist for every finite length” fails at nonstandard lengths).
Proposition 10.18 (ε-δ versus internal set theory). [T4 Prop 3.6, repaired] (modulo cited theorems
of Nelson) Let ρIST read informal analysis literally in Nelson’s IST (Nelson, 1977), with ev-
ery contextual constant (“let f be continuous”) read as standard (TIST := IST + {st(c)}) and
infinitesimal suppositions folded into bound variables (ξ under “dx is infinitesimal” becomes
∀dx (dx ≃ 0 ∧ dx ≠ 0 → ξ)). Let Dst be the occurrences read as sentences with standard parame-
ters only, and let hW = (ZFC + constants, ρW ), where ρW (x) is the internal sentence Nelson’s
reduction algorithm assigns to ρIST (x). Then hW ≈Dst hIST .
Proof idea. Nelson’s reduction algorithm and conservativity of IST over ZFC, with transfer for
the standard parameters. Full proof in Appendix I.

    Outside Dst nothing is claimed: f (x) = N x with N unlimited is ε-δ continuous at 0 but
fails the literal reading ∀x (x ≃ 0 → f (x) ≃ f (0)) at x = 1/N . Nor is agreement of the g-step
relations claimed: if infinitesimal proofs are shorter, noise-free bounded-gap practice generated
by hIST refutes hW as a granularity hypothesis, leaving meaning unaffected.
Proposition 10.19 (What numbers could not be). [T4 Prop 3.7] (modulo standard facts of ZF) Let Dar
be the occurrences of informal arithmetic sentences, read into the ∈-language via von Neumann
numerals (ρvN , n + 1 = n ∪ {n}) or Zermelo numerals (ρZ , n + 1 = {n}), with +, × defined
by recursion. Then (ZF, ρvN ) ≈Dar (ZF, ρZ ), although the readings differ on “1 ∈ 3”, which lies
outside Dar .
   Proof in Appendix I: transport along the recursion-defined isomorphism given by Dedekind’s
categoricity theorem (Dedekind, 1888). Benacerraf (1965) thus becomes a non-identifiability
theorem: practice fixes ≈ on its domain and nothing else.

Reading: ≈ is inferentialist meaning. Inferential role, incompatibility (here incoher-
ence, Lemma 10.14(iii)) and Sellarsian language-entry transitions (here object evaluations,
Lemma 10.14(i)) are all functions of |=h on D∗ , and soundness refers only to |=h∗ . Everything
finer (ontology, latent language, junk like “1 ∈ 3”) never changes a practice inference and is
unidentifiable from closure-level data. Granularity is finer and partly identifiable, but it is
pragmatics (which steps are acceptable), not semantics (which are valid). Whether ε-δ and
infinitesimals differ in what they are about the practice cannot settle; the verifier needs only ≈.

                                                 114
10.4     Counterexample objects versus paradoxes
Lemma 10.20 (Descent along false lines). [T4 Lemma 4.1] If v is defined on the lines of α, true
on the premises and false on the conclusion, then α contains an inference with true antecedents
and false conclusion, or a link with true source and false target, found within depth(α) moves
evaluating only antecedents of visited lines. If v is h-admissible, the item is h-invalid.
Proof. From the false conclusion, repeatedly move to a false antecedent (or link source); stop
when there is none. Premises are true, so the walk stops at an inference or link. If v = vM ◦ ρh
with M ∈ Mh , a valid item would have a true conclusion in M .
   This is Shapiro’s contradiction backtracing (Shapiro, 1983) for informal arguments, Lakatos’s
conversion of a global counterexample into a local one (Lakatos, 1976), and Easwaran’s demand
that proofs be fine enough to convert rebutting into undercutting defeaters (Easwaran, 2015).
Descent can end at a link: the blame then falls on an equivocation, not a rule (Lakatos’s “global
but not local” case).
Definition 10.21 (Correction game). [T4 Def 4.2] Let H ⊆ 2S be finite, S a finite step universe,
h∗ ∈ H. Each round the learner announces At ⊆ S; if At ̸= h∗ the environment returns (+) some
s ∈ h∗ \ At , or (−obj) some s ∈ At \ h∗ , or (−bagr ) some B ⊆ At with |B| ≤ r, B ̸⊆ h∗ ; the
learner deletes the hypotheses h ̸∋ s, h ∋ s, h ⊇ B respectively. A correction is a round with
                                                      (r)
feedback. Mobj (H) (feedback (+) or (−obj)) and Mbag (H) (feedback (+) or (−bagr )) are the
                                                              (|S|)
optimal worst-case numbers of corrections; Mbag := Mbag . The positive elasticity el(H, h∗ ) is
the supremum of lengths of sequences s1 , s2 , . . . ∈ h∗ with si ∈
                                                                  / {h ∈ H : h ⊇ {s1 , . . . , si−1 }},
                                                                    T

and el∗ (H) := maxh∗ el(H, h∗ ).
   In reality a negative channel can be silent (objects need presentable countermodels; coherent-
but-wrong hypotheses yield no paradox, Section 6); the bounds below then still bound corrections,
but unsound rounds may pass silently.
Theorem 10.22 (Objects: logarithmic). [T4 Thm 4.3] (classical) (a) Mobj (H) ≤ ⌊log2 |H|⌋; with a
prior w, weighted halving makes at most log2 (1/w(h∗ )) corrections, so with w = 2−ℓ the number of
                                                                                            (r)
counterexamples needed is at most the description length of the target. (b) Mobj (H) ≤ Mbag (H)
for all r ≥ 1, with equality at r = 1. (c) Mobj (H) = Ldim(H), the Littlestone dimension.
Proof of (a). Announce the set of steps carried by more than half of the surviving weight. Then
(+) on s ∈/ At and (−obj) on s ∈ At each keep at most half of it, and h∗ always survives. (b)
and (c) are in Appendix I.
    This is classical halving (Barzdin and Freivalds, 1972; Littlestone, 1988; Angluin, 1988); the
object game is the mistake-bound model, hence (c) (Littlestone, 1988) (confirmed on 248 random
classes, V4 (computed)).
                                                                           (r)
Theorem 10.23 (Paradoxes: linear, tightly). [T4 Thm 4.4] (a) Mbag (H) ≤ el∗ (H) ≤ |H| − 1.
(b) Single culprit: for S = {b1 , . . . , bn } and Hn = {S \ {bj } : j ≤ n}, Mobj (Hn ) = 1 and
   (r)
Mbag (Hn ) = min(r, n − 1); so Mbag (Hn ) = |Hn | − 1 against log2 |Hn |, and (a) is tight. (c) The
chain paradox: take sentences q0 , . . . , qn , suspect steps bi = (qi−1 ▷ qi ), and the designated context
{q0 , ∼qn }, e.g. qk = “a pile of n − k grains is a heap”. Let hj validate every bi with i =   ̸ j (axioms
pi−1 → pi , i ≠ j). Every hj is coherent on the designated context; every paradox uses all the bi ,
so every bag is S; every learner using paradoxes suffers n − 1 corrections; and one admissible
object pins down the culprit by descent.

                                                   115
                                            T
Proof idea. (a) The cautious learner At = VSt receives only (+) items, forming an elastic chain.
(b) Against bags, an adversary answers a rejected candidate with (+) and an accepted candidate
set C with a bag of r candidates, or of all of C if |C| ≤ r (deleting nothing). (c) The admissible
valuation of hj ∗ with q0 true and qn false makes qi true iff i < j ∗ . Full proof in Appendix I.

   Exact minimax values (T4-checks/bag_vs_object_game.py) (computed): for the single-
                                        (r)
culprit class with n = 6, Mobj = 1 and Mbag = 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 5 for r = 1, . . . , 6; for “two culprits
                                      (r)
of six” (|H| = 15), Mobj = 2 and Mbag = 2, 4, 4, 4, 4, 4. On 295 random classes Mobj ≤ Mbag ≤
el∗ ≤ |H| − 1 always held, with Mobj < Mbag in 43 (V3). “Paradoxes are worthless” (Mbag = el∗ )
is false in general: H = {∅, {0}, {0, 1}} has Mbag = 1 < 2 = el∗ (announce {0}). Real paradoxes
have few suspect steps, since steps every survivor accepts drop out of the bag.

Proposition 10.25 (Linear dependence on r is necessary). [T4 Prop 4.6] For the product of k
                                                               (r)
single-culprit blocks of size b ≥ 2 (|H| = bk ), Mobj = k and Mbag ≥ k min(r, b−1). With b = r +1:

candidate sets; equality was confirmed exhaustively for (b, k) ∈ {(2, 2), (3, 2), (2, 3)} (computed).
So adversarial multiple-instance feedback costs Θ̃(r log |H|) over classes of a given size, whereas in
the i.i.d. multiple-instance setting (Dietterich et al., 1997) bag size enters far more mildly (Sabato

Proposition 10.26 (Bag price when Mobj = 1). [T4 Prop 4.6 ] If Mobj (H) = 1 then Mbag (H) ≤ r.
                                                (r)
Conjecture 10.27 (Bag price). [T4 §4.2] Mbag (H) ≤ r · Ldim(H) for every finite H.

    Proposition 10.26 (proof in Appendix I) is the case Ldim = 1. The conjecture is tight on
single-culprit classes (r ≤ n − 1), products (r ≤ b − 1) and some two-culprit classes, and no
violation was found on 519 (class, r) pairs (bag_size_conjecture.py) or in a referee’s search,
exhaustive for |S| ≤ 4 and covering about 4.7 million random pairs with |S| ≤ 6.
    Objects can also be neutralized. A step blamed by an object can be repaired by changing R
(lemma-incorporation), changing ρ so that the monster no longer satisfies the premise (monster-
barring by redefinition), rejecting or re-interpreting the datum (monster-barring by exclusion,
monster-adjustment), or extending L (a proof-generated concept naming the hidden lemma)
(Lakatos, 1976).

Proposition 10.28 (Free barring nullifies objects; trivial). [T4 Prop 4.7] If barring object data is
free, objects constrain nothing: every hypothesis consistent with (P) and (C) can survive forever.
If objects are truthful and each barred datum costs c > 0 bits, a hypothesis that must bar m data
loses to h∗ in description length once m > (ℓ(h∗ ) − ℓ(h))/c.

                                                  116
Proof. If barring is free, (O) refutes nothing, and only (P) and (C) remain. With cost c, h scores
ℓ(h) + mc against ℓ(h∗ ), since truthful objects never force h∗ to bar.

    So objects are powerful only if certified independently (by the community, or by computation)
or if revisions of ρ are paid for, which is Lakatos’s own preference ordering.

Reading. Coherence says that some step of a paradox is bad; an object says which. The sorites
is the canonical uninformative paradox. The oligarchic halving of Section 6 bounds detected
incoherences by log2 (1/w(h∗ )), yet total corrections, counting the rejections of valid steps that
caution forces, can still be |H| − 1: coherence bounds the price of boldness, not of learning;
objects bound both. Russell’s paradox, by contrast, is a bag of size one; what it could not do is
choose the generalization (Section 10.5). A learner of inference rules must generate and evaluate
objects, not only search for ⊥.

10.5    Frege → Russell → Zermelo
Definition 10.29 (Repair setting). [T4 §5.1] I is a set of rule instances; Coh ⊆ P(I) is a coherence
predicate, downward closed and of finite character; R ⊆ P(I) is a finite class of uniformly specified
restrictions with description lengths ℓ. Practice at time t is a finitely supported weight wt : I →
[0, ∞), with coverage covt (c) := i∈c wt (i). Budget-t ⊥-search refutes c iff c has a refutation of
                                  P

size ≤ t; incoherent c has one of size k(c) < ∞. MDLt := arg min{ℓ(c) : c ∈ R, supp(wt ) ⊆ c},
and REPt is the lexicographic arg max of (covt , −ℓ) over the members of R not refuted with
budget t.

Theorem 10.30 (Abstract repair; trivial). [T4 Thm 5.1, repaired] (a) If I ∈ R is strictly shortest,
MDLt = I for all t. (b) Coherent members are never refuted; incoherent c is refuted for t ≥ k(c).
(c) For t ≥ t0 := max{k(c) : c ∈ R incoherent}, REPt maximizes (covt , −ℓ) over the coherent
members of R. (d) If wt /∥wt ∥1 → w∞ in ℓ1 and the coherent maximizer of cov∞ is unique,
REPt is eventually equal to it. (e) If c1 , c2 ∈ R are coherent and c1 ∪ c2 is not, no coherent set
contains both, and while supp(wt ) ⊆ c1 ∩ c2 only ℓ chooses between them.

    Proof in Appendix I. The content is in an instance that makes every step of the story
checkable. In first-order logic with ∈, =, let Comp(φ) := ∀a∀b∃y∀x (x ∈ y ↔ φ(x, a, b)), with φ
from the menu Φ of Table 13. A formula is stratified (as in Quine’s NF, Quine 1937) if it has a
typing t with t(v) = t(u) + 1 for u ∈ v and t(u) = t(v) for u = v; x ∈ x has none. R consists
of NC (the whole menu), POS (positive formulas), STRAT (stratified; NF-like), SEP (of the
form x ∈ a ∧ ψ), and Z := SEP ∪ {EMP, PAIR, UNI} (Zermelo-like). The description lengths are
stipulated: ℓ(NC) < ℓ(POS) < ℓ(SEP) < ℓ(STRAT) < ℓ(Z), and outcomes depend on this choice.

Lemma 10.31 (Comprehension facts). [T4 §5.2, F1–F6] (F1) NC is incoherent (Comp(R) with
x := y refutes it in four lines). (F2) POS is coherent, even for all positive formulas. (F3)
STRAT ∩ Φ is coherent. (F4) Z ∩ Φ is coherent. (F5) POS, STRAT, Z are pairwise jointly
incoherent. (F6) SEP ⊊ Z, and POS, STRAT, Z are pairwise ⊆-incomparable.

Proof idea. (F2) one element u ∈ u; (F3) N with x ∈ y iff x ∈ e(y) for a bijection e onto the
finite and cofinite sets; (F4) the hereditarily finite sets; (F5) {V, ZR} and {S, CMP} each yield
Russell’s set. Full proof in Appendix I.
