Residual defects of the prototype. The first version accepted V6–V9: C1 allowed α in the
answer, C3 checked only the oscillation
                                    √     over α of answer over certified√form, never its offset from
1, and C4 never enforced ϵ < 2/ 5 or σmin − ∆/2 > m (beyond 2/ 5 the upper bound turns
negative and was silently dropped). The repaired version is still a toy. (i) It hard-codes one
top model and two bridge schemas. (ii) SymPy and floating-point evaluation of the budget at a
corner stand in for a kernel and interval arithmetic; that the corner is worst rests on monotonicity
of the bounds, argued, not machine-checked. (iii) C3 accepts only answers symbolically identical
to the certified form, so it is incomplete; a fuller checker would add a rigorously enclosed offset
|answer/certified − 1| to the budget. (iv) V2 and V5 are rejected because the SPS declares the
violation; the semantic protection is that C4 recomputes side conditions from root data and C3
catches answer-changing undeclared imports (V11). (v) V0 is a completeness gap, and V4 is
caught only because the specification is formal. The episode is a small instance of the lesson of
Section 3: plausible syntactic checks accepted four simple attacks, which were rejected only once

                                                     140
every check was made semantic.

What remains judgment. The photo-to-model reading (a specular cylinder, lit over its full
height, its unsupplied reflectance, the meaning of “illuminance surplus”, the α range) and the
open world (taper, tilt, floor roughness, the seat’s shadow). Each effect could be added as a
declared deformation with its own bridge, moving it from judgment to checking; what cannot be
moved is the claim that these are all the effects the setters intend (Theorem 11.38). The one
checkable test of the reading is the round trip: render the predicted floor pattern and compare
it with the photograph. A second example: IPhO 2025 T2 C.4 is a germ-root problem whose
leading-order W ∗ = 19.8 mJ differs from the exact optimum 20.1 mJ by 1.3%, inside the marking
interval [19, 21] mJ, so the tolerance-aware check of Theorem 11.26 passes (L9 §8.3) (computed).

11.8    The four logical roles of physicists’ checks
The brief listed dimensional analysis, limiting cases, symmetry and conservation as “coherence
checks”. They play at least four logical roles (L9 §3), and only the first two are coherence in the
sense of Section 6:

                 Table 17: Logical roles of the physicist’s checks (after L9 §3.7).
Role                    Checks                     What it certifies          Soundness status
1. invariance of the    dimensions (unit group);   homogeneity,               worst case, decidable;
rule set                declared context           equivariance of derived    necessary only
                        symmetries                 equations
2. in-model theorem     conservation laws          a computation inside one   worst case within the
                                                   local model                model; silent on exports
3. regression against   limiting cases             agreement with solved      sound only for regular
another context                                    sub-models                 limits (Norton)
4. export side          order of magnitude; “the   a bridge at a stated       sound with explicit
condition               neglected term is small”   tolerance                  budgets; heuristic
                                                                              otherwise


    Dimensions are a type system: dimensions form a free abelian group and inference is
unification modulo abelian groups (Kennedy, 1994); the Buckingham Π theorem (Buckingham,
1914) turns “express in terms of” into a type signature (in IPhO 2025 A.1 the force must be
mg Φ(ρSh/m, P0 S/(mg)), and the official (m + ρSh)g is Φ = 1 + π1 ). Dropping a small Π
group assumes complete similarity, which fails in intermediate asymptotics (Barenblatt, 1996),
so “Π2 ≪ 1, ignore it” is a bridge (role 4), not a type fact. Symmetry needs uniqueness, as the
user notes: a symmetric problem implies only a symmetric set of solutions. Context symmetries
are context-indexed (time reversal holds without drag), and a declared symmetry gives free
negative data: a non-equivariant candidate rule is refuted by a symmetry image of one of its own
accepted instances (L9 TC2; dimension inference as learning meaning from positive examples
is discussed in Section 5). Limiting cases are cross-context regression tests whose validity is
Norton’s continuity (Proposition 11.5(c)). Order-of-magnitude checks are export side conditions
(Theorem 11.26). Qualitative checks are one-sided refutations, as in qualitative simulation,
which is sound but incomplete (Kuipers, 1986). The closest precedent for the in-model layer is
the Andes tutor (VanLehn et al., 2005), which checks each equation for units and against an
author-supplied model, and never checks bridges, because the author fixes the idealizations.

                                                   141
What is and is not established. Contradictory contexts are fine, and realizability says
which must be consistent. “True in the context at hand” is truth on the context’s filter in
every admissible completion, and checking it reduces to kernel steps, stable imports, certified
bridges and a reading. Reductio in inadequate frameworks needs a consistency certificate, and
with approximate premises a margin. Untrusted solvers are handled for everything except the
reading, whose characteristic attack, silent narrowing, no internal signal sees. Not established
(Section 14): any theorem about learning readings (the deep part, J1); frame realizability under
germ semantics; idealizations inside suppositions and non-value exports; automated proofs of the
regularity constants of Theorems 11.31 and 11.32; a checker beyond one worked problem. The
mini-checker illustrates the checks on one problem and twelve adversarial variants; it is not a
system, and its own soundness is argued, not proved.


12     Experiments
This section reports seven small computational experiments. They illustrate theorems of the
earlier sections in settings where the target calculus is known, validity can be decided or tested,
and adversaries can be run. They are not evidence that the methods scale, whether to real human
mathematics, to neural verifiers, or to domains where world feedback is scarce. Experiments A–C
use the Python package cil in code/; D–G are check scripts of the theory notes, re-run for this
section. All are deterministic given the seeds. A–C use seeds 0, 1, 2 throughout, and “a ± b” is
the mean and the population standard deviation over these three seeds, not a confidence interval;
rows that pool several values of N give the standard deviation over the pooled runs. Full tables
are in the reports code/results/*.md. An independent review re-ran everything and fixed two
reporting errors in B and a latent one in A; no stored result changed (computed).

12.1    A: algebra, from imitation to a sound calculus, and the meadow
Setup. The target has 41 oriented rewrite schemas for field algebra over partial terms (Ex-
ample 2.12). Eleven carry guards built from defined, nonzero and nonneg atoms on schematic
variables, such as x/x → 1 if nonzero(x); numeral arithmetic is trusted, not learned. A simulator
writes N human derivations in which every valid step instantiates a target schema and cites its
name. Noise is sporadic (5% random mutations under random rule names), fallacies (each
opportunity for one of five systematic
                                  √ fallacies taken with probability 0.3: the freshman’s dream,
unguarded x/x → 1, unguarded x2 → x, (a + b)/(c + d) → a/c + b/d, −(a + b) → −a + b),
both, or none. The learner clusters rewrite cores by MDL and takes Plotkin’s least general
generalization (Plotkin, 1970) of each cluster, bucketing by the cited rule (tagged) or by shape
(untagged). The verifier is conservative: it accepts a numeral evaluation or an instance of an
active schema with human support m ≥ 2 whose guard follows from the context. A Lakatos-style
loop (Lakatos, 1976) of at most six rounds probes the schemas, derives with them, detects
contradictions, blames and repairs (minimal guard, else split the cluster, else delete). In mode
numeral the only signal is coherence (C): two derivations of distinct numerals from one ground
term, or a derivation contradicting arithmetic. Modes bag and step add a world oracle (W), on
whole derivations with blame by spectrum fault localization (Abreu et al., 2007), or on single
steps. The oracle compares partial values with Kleene equality at boundary and random rational
points (Schwartz, 1980; Zippel, 1979). Held-out valid and invalid steps in distribution (ID, about
10-node terms) and out of it (OOD, about 40 nodes) are labelled by an independent 60-point
oracle, and a white-box attack targets every active schema.

  imitation only             30.0 ± 0.0 15.7 ± 0.5   0.506 / 0.507   1.00 / 1.00     65 ± 2        0

schema and guard recovered; unsound: active schemas failing the soundness test; FA and C:
acceptance of held-out invalid and valid steps; exploits: accepted invalid steps found by the white-
box attack. Of the 11 guarded rules, 0, 3, 11 and 11 are recovered exactly. The gradient-boosting
baseline (34 features, threshold 0.5) is trained on the same corpus with validity labels.

Imitation and world feedback. Section 5.8 reports the positive-data part: shapes are
recovered once a rule has a few instances (in 67% of rule–run pairs with two, 100% with five or
more) and generalize out of distribution, but no guard is ever displayed, all five fallacies reach
support at N = 500 (Corollary 5.20), and spurious schemas from sporadic noise grow with N
(Theorem 5.15). The first row of Table 18 is the outcome. With step-level world feedback, 0 of
the 90 runs of the coherence grid (N from 20 to 500, three noise settings, both learners, three
seeds) end with an unsound schema, the largest false-accept rate on any held-out set is 0.000, and
for N ≥ 200 the loop recovers 40.6 ± 0.8 of the 41 schemas exactly, guards included, at ID/OOD
completeness 0.993 ± 0.015 / 0.992 ± 0.018. Bag-level feedback leaves an unsound schema in
2/90 runs (for example 0 · (x − a) → 0). The guarantee is only as good as the red-teaming of
one’s own rules: with four probes per schema and round, bag feedback leaves 2.0 ± 0.8 unsound
schemas; with thirteen, none.




Figure 1: Experiment A: unsound active schemas against the number of human derivations N
(noise both, mean of three seeds), tagged learner (left) and untagged learner (right) (computed).
Imitation alone (grey) gets worse with N ; coherence without a world (blue) removes about half;
world feedback (orange, green) leaves one schema in one run.


Coherence without a world converges to the meadow. Pure coherence removes the errors
that change the value of a numeral expression. In paired runs of the tagged learner it deleted the
freshman’s dream in all 11 runs in which imitation had produced it, (a + b)/(c + d) → a/c + b/d

                                                     143
in 17/17 and −(a + b) → −a + b in 20/21, and repaired unguarded x/x → 1 into the guarded
rule in 31/35. It almost never learned a guard that concerns only definedness: a · 0 → 0 ended
with a guard weaker than defined(a) in all 54 runs with N ≥ 100. Of the 90 pure-coherence runs,
88 end with an unsound schema (Figure 1).
    What survives has a meaning. Every unsound learned schema was re-tested in a total
semantics: complex values, x/0 := 0, the principal square root, non-integer powers set to 0.
This is a version of the meadows of Bergstra and Tucker (2007), and the convention of Lean’s
Mathlib (Section 13). All 41 target rules hold in it, all five fallacies fail in it, and seven of
the 11 guards are unnecessary in it. For N ≥ 200, 304 of the 315 schemas that survive pure
coherence (96.5%) are sound in it. Of the other eleven, ten mention an object constant (as in
x · z → x; pure coherence compares only numeral expressions) and one is a value error. For
N < 200 the share is 86.3%, since a smaller calculus derives fewer contradictions. Conversely,
apart from one run, the guards
                           √      that pure coherence did learn are guards the total semantics
also needs: x/x → 1 and x2 → x came out exact√whenever √         learned in general form, and in
                                                             √
Experiment C the coherence-only calculus guards ab → a b in every seed by exactly the
one atom, nonneg(a), that the total semantics requires. So pure coherence converged, not to the
                                                                                        √
intended partial calculus, but to a coherent calculus for another meaning of “/” and “ ”. This
is the algebraic counterpart of the Kripkensteinian residue of Theorem 6.38: a coherent uniform
alternative that agrees with everything positive examples and contradictions can supply. Only a
channel that observes “undefined”, here the world oracle at the corner point x = 0, separates the
two meanings (Example 2.17).

Checks. Soundness of schemas is a random test with 250 assignments that include undefined
and irrational values. The review re-tested every active schema of five final calculi with 20,000
random assignments plus a grid of special values and found no unsound one. Fresh seeds 3, 4, 5
(N = 200) reproduce the pattern: 14 to 15 unsound schemas after imitation, 8 to 9 after pure
coherence, none after world feedback.

12.2    B: natural deduction, Post probes and the bold extension of IPC
Setup. Formulas use atoms p, q, r (s only out of distribution) and →, ∧, ∨, ¬, ⊥; steps are
sequent-style natural-deduction inferences (Example 2.11). The target is classical natural
deduction with 13 rules, the assumption rule carrying the side condition A ∈ Γ as a mem guard;
IPC drops reductio. Simulated humans prove random tautologies backwards. Their fallacies,
each taken with probability 0.3 and cited as →E, are affirming the consequent (AC), denying the
antecedent (DA) and an illegitimate discharge (ID); noise corrupts 5% of steps. The learner vs
buckets steps by cited rule, anti-unifies them under an MDL criterion, adds most-specific mem
guards and verifies with support m = 2. Coherence pruning uses five designated contexts: ∅ and
four satisfiable premise sets. Negative bags (Lemma 6.2) come from blind search for Γd ⊢ ⊥ and
from Post probes, which substitute ⊥ or ¬⊥ for a rule’s metavariables, prove its premises and
refute its conclusion (Proposition 3.5 and Lemma 7.24). Blame is a minimum-weight hitting set,
weighted by the human steps the cheapest repair loses. The world phase adds two observed
valuations of the 16. Rule soundness is decided exactly by truth tables. An adversary searches
with the learned rules for derivations of 34 invalid goals, ⊢ ⊥ among them; sound completeness is
the share of 25 random tautologies the sound active rules prove within budget (target calculus:
0.88 ± 0.03).



                                              144
  phase                  unsound     tonk-like    exploits /34 ⊢ ⊥ rules /13 sound compl. world queries
  imitation only        45.6 ± 19.3 30.2 ± 12.2   34.0 ± 0.0   9/9   13.0     0.87 ± 0.04       0
  + coherence, no world 1.2 ± 0.9       0.0       1.7 ± 4.1    0/9   13.0     0.87 ± 0.04       0
  + sparse world         0.2 ± 0.4      0.0          0.0       0/9   13.0     0.87 ± 0.04   910 ± 309

Table 19: Experiment B, learner vs, noise both, N ∈ {50, 100, 200}: 9 runs, sd over the 9 runs
(computed). Tonk-like: unsound rules whose conclusion is unconstrained by the premises; ⊢ ⊥:
runs in which the adversary derives ⊢ ⊥; rules: target rules recovered exactly or with a stronger
guard. Held-out invalid steps accepted: 0.86 ± 0.02, 0.00 ± 0.01, 0.00.


Imitation, coherence, world. Imitation recovers all 13 rules, but on noisy data MDL anti-
unification merges corrupted steps that share a skeleton into tonk-like rules (Prior, 1960), and
the adversary proves all 34 invalid goals (Table 19). Coherence alone cuts the unsound rules
from 45.6 to 1.2 and closes every route to ⊢ ⊥, at no cost in completeness. Over all 72 runs of
vs (N from 5 to 200, four noise settings), 47 runs have an unsound rule after imitation, 19 after
coherence and 6 after world feedback, and ⊢ ⊥ is derivable in 32, 0 and 0. On clean data no
phase has an unsound rule for N ≥ 50, and the assumption rule’s side condition is learned from
positives, as most specific guard. A fallacy counts as surviving if an active unsound rule licenses
it or a special case of it. For N ≥ 50, AC survives imitation in all 18 runs with fallacies, DA
in 14 and ID in 10, and coherence removes all of them except one closed instance of DA that
mentions atoms, outside the structural hypothesis of Corollary 6.35; world feedback removes that
one too. At N ≤ 10 coherence leaves unsound rules in 4 of 18 runs, AC itself among them: a
small learned calculus may lack the rules needed to derive the contradiction. Plotkin’s learner
without clustering (aggressive, 18 runs) produces 4.8 ± 2.3 tonk-like rules per run and lets the
adversary derive ⊢ ⊥ in every run; after pruning, in none. Blame needs a prior (Theorem 7.21):
for this learner at N = 50 on noise both, blaming the least-supported rule recovers 0.3 ± 0.5
target rules and no completeness, while blaming by cost of the cheapest repair recovers all 13
with completeness 0.87 ± 0.04.

Post-completeness probes. A bold learner over classical natural deduction adds a candidate
schema iff no derivation of ⊢ ⊥ is found with it. Of 177 structural candidates (19 hand-picked, 8
pairwise LGGs of target rules, 150 random), the 59 classically valid ones were all accepted, and
the 118 invalid ones were all refuted by an explicit derivation of ⊢ ⊥, as Theorems 6.13 and 6.15
predict. The derivations are short: median 2.5 and maximum 6 steps for the LGGs of target
rules, median 6 and maximum 17 for the random schemas. For AC, the probe A := ⊥, B := ¬⊥
gives
           ⊥ ⊢ ⊥ (ax), ⊥ ⊢ ¬⊥ (¬I), ⊢ ⊥ → ¬⊥ (→I), ⊢ ¬⊥ (¬I), ⊢ ⊥ (AC).
Non-structural rules escape (Proposition 7.19): ⊢ p is coherent with every designated context
and falls only to an observed valuation, and p ⊢ q falls to the designated context {p, ¬q}. Of
the 45 unsound rules that survive pruning in any run, 38 mention specific atoms. Offered
tonk’s two rules one at a time, the bold learner accepts whichever comes first and rejects the
other (Proposition 6.31). One observed valuation is a complete test for a schematic rule with k
metavariables only if all 2k literal instantiations are probed. The code probes at most eight, and
the three schematic rules that survive the world phase in some run each have k ≥ 4; probing all
instantiations removed them. What world feedback adds is exact blame, since a refuted step
names its rule: 5.0 ± 2.1 pruning rounds against 9.1 ± 5.9 without it (N ≥ 20, 36 runs).

                                                    145
The bold extension of intuitionistic logic. Over intuitionistic natural deduction the bold
learner accepted excluded middle, double-negation elimination, reductio, Peirce’s law, weak
excluded middle and Dummett’s linearity, each underivable in IPC (a Kripke countermodel
was found for each), and rejected the classically invalid candidates by derivations of ⊢ ⊥.
Completeness on classical tautologies rose from 0.72 to 0.80 (classical natural deduction: 0.84).
The same happens when IPC is first learned: from 60 simulated intuitionistic proofs, vs with
coherence recovers the 12 rules (10 exactly, 2 with a stronger guard), never learns reductio, has
no classically or Kripke-unsound rule, and its bold phase then accepts the same six principles.
This is Theorem 6.17: coherence cannot separate the intermediate logics, so a maximal coherent
structural learner never outputs IPC, and the residue of Theorem 6.38(ii) shows up as a drift
toward CPC. Only the cautious, imitation-based tier keeps IPC.

Caveats. The designated contexts are certified satisfiable with ground-truth satisfiability, a
modelling assumption (Assumption 2.14), and “coherent” means that no derivation of ⊥ was
found within budget. Most-specific guards make ∧E and ∨E stronger than the target, since
the simulated humans only eliminate from open assumptions: sound, but incomplete out of
distribution. Fresh seeds 3 and 4 (N = 50, both) reproduce the pattern.

12.3    C: an adversarial prover against statistical and rule-learned verifiers
Setup. All verifiers are trained on the same N = 500 noisy algebra derivations of Experiment A,
except one trained on a clean corpus. The statistical verifiers, akin to process reward models,
are logistic regression, gradient-boosted trees and a random forest on hashed subterm-shape
n-grams, rule signatures and tree-edit features. They receive oracle labels for positives and for
negatives (refuted human steps, perturbations, fallacy instances), so they get more supervision
than the positive-only rule learner. Thresholds are calibrated in distribution to a balanced
precision TPR/(TPR + FPR); “@pX” means precision X. The rule-learned verifiers are A’s
learner without guards (lgg_pos), with most-specific guards (lgg_ms; lgg_ms_clean on the
clean corpus), after pure coherence, and after world feedback; the ground-truth calculus is a
control. The prover sees only accept or reject. It runs a bidirectional best-first search whose
proposals are target rules, fallacies and near-miss schemas with guards ignored, arithmetic,
random mutations, and goal-directed leaps and grafts, with 3000 verifier queries for each of 23
false goal equations and 1500 for each of 33 true identities. A derived equation counts as false
when exact evaluation at 5 fixed points refutes it, so these counts are lower bounds.

Accuracy in distribution does not transfer to the search distribution. The boosted-
tree verifier reaches test AUC 0.995 on held-out human-like steps. Calibrated to in-distribution
balanced precision 0.9925, it still lets the prover prove 16.3 ± 2.4 of the 23 false goals, with a
median of 514 queries to a false proof (Table 20). At the strictest threshold, with no false accept
on the calibration set, recall falls to 0.45; it then proves 4.7 of 33 true identities, and still 1.7
false goals. All 11 statistical configurations derive false equations under search. Of their 1082
invalid proof edges, 81% come from goal-directed leaps, grafts and random mutations, and only
1% from the known fallacies: steps that look like human steps and are not. Figure 2 plots the
probability of a false proof against the budget and against in-distribution precision. This is
Theorem 3.2 and Lemma 3.11(ii) in a closed domain: in-distribution accuracy is a property of
a measure fixed before the prover moves, soundness a property of a set. That learned reward
models are exploited by optimization is documented at scale, notably by the overoptimization

scaling laws of Gao et al. (2023); the experiment adds a domain in which every exploited step
can be certified invalid.




Figure 2: Experiment C (computed). Left: fraction of the 3 × 23 (seed, false goal) searches
that found a false proof within B verifier queries, for boosted trees at five thresholds and the

Rule-learned verifiers fail only through named schemas. All 154 invalid proof edges
of the rule-learned verifiers are licensed by a schema that the soundness test flags; the most
used is the noise-born projection x + y → y, which alone collapses arithmetic (1 = 1 + 1 = 2).
A counterexample deletes or guards such a schema and removes its whole family of exploits; a
classifier has no such unit of repair. After world feedback the learned calculus proves no false goal
and 32.3 of 33 true identities, like the ground-truth calculus. With clean data and an adequate
guard language positive data suffice: lgg_ms_clean proves no false goal, as the version-space
verifier of Theorem 4.2 should, although in one seed an over-specialized schema 0/(y + 3) → 0,

                                                      147
whose true guard the guard language cannot express, is active and was not reached. Together, the
four rule-based verifiers with no false goal derive no false equation in 900,305 queries. Coherence
without a world leaves A’s residue: in-distribution balanced precision 1.0000, yet 5 false goals
per seed, all definedness errors such as 0/x = 0 and (x − x)/y = 0, each true when 1/0 = 0.
In-distribution precision does not rank verifiers by soundness, even among rule-based ones.

Patching the classifier with its own exploits. The natural remedy is adversarial data
collection, as for PRM800K (Lightman et al., 2023): harvest the certainly invalid accepted steps
from the searches on the 23 false goals, add them as negatives, retrain and recalibrate. Over
four rounds (boosted trees at p0.99, seeds 0 and 1 only), false goals proved on the harvested set
fell from 15.5 to 0.0 and false equations per thousand queries from 195.7 to 14.3. The patched
verifier still proved 1.5 of 12 fresh false goals and 2.0 of 35 goals against a second, differently
configured prover, while true identities proved fell from 28.0 to 24.5. Retraining is an effective
practical mitigation; its soundness is relative to the attacks it was patched against. All of this is
one small closed domain, an informed black-box prover (“not proved” is not “unprovable”) and
small baselines on hand-built features; the experiment does not measure how often larger learned
verifiers are exploited, and the theorems do not predict that rate.

12.4    D: the two-tier learner
ttl_sim.py runs the two-tier learner on a propositional practice with nine genuine and four
fallacious tagged rules and 1% sporadic noise, against a prover that enumerates and truth-table
checks every step over ten formulas; re-running it reproduced Table 10 exactly (computed). The
positive-only cautious tier is unsound in 72 of 80 runs (Proposition 7.5); the two-tier learner is
unsound in none and exact in all 40 runs with N ≥ 250 (Theorem 7.14 and Corollary 7.25). Only
the singleton-conflict case of CPC with a per-schema world audit is simulated; Experiment B is a
related pipeline, not an implementation of the two-tier learner.

12.5    E: a physics mini-checker
sps_checker.py runs the checks of Definition 11.35 on the chair-leg solution of Section 11.7;
Table 16 lists the outcomes, which a re-run reproduced (computed). The honest solution is
accepted with certified error at most 0.0888 within its 10% tolerance, and all twelve variants are
rejected, one of them (the honest solution with a 5% tolerance) as a completeness gap. The first
version of the checker accepted four simple attacks (V6–V9) until each syntactic check was made
semantic: relative soundness (Theorem 11.37) is only as good as the checks, a small instance of
the lesson of Section 3.

12.6    F: simplicity
Five scripts check the quantitative claims of Section 8 (Table 21), and each confirms its statement:
a simplicity penalty with private randomness separates a good model from memorized errors on
a window of steepness c, but it is blind to validity, so with realistic rates no c keeps every valid
rule and removes every error.

12.7       G: the comprehension toy
comprehension_toy.py runs the repair setting of Definition 10.29 on the menu of Table 13
(computed; re-run). As a sanity check of Lemma 10.31, positive comprehension has the model
u ∈ u, and none of the seven menu sets proved inconsistent by hand (Russell’s set alone, and
the pairs and triples behind (F5)) has a model with at most three elements, which is consistent
with the lemma but no proof of it. For every practice the unconstrained MDL choice is naive
comprehension, Frege’s Basic Law V, which coherence refutes. The coverage-maximal coherent
repair then follows the practice, as Theorem 10.32(iv) states:

         practice (weights)                          POS     STRAT       SEP     Z    selected
         INT 5, UNI 5, PAIR 3, DIFF 4, EMP 1           13         18       9    18    STRAT (tie, broken by ℓ)
           + V 1 (Frege’s universal extension)         14         19       9    18    STRAT
           + ZR 3 (Zermelo’s diagonal subsets)         14         19      12    21    Z
           same, with V 4                              17         22      12    21    STRAT

The entries are coverages. On the Dedekind–Cantor operations stratification and separation tie
and description length decides; once diagonal subsets outweigh the universal set, Z wins; reverse
the weights and stratification wins. The theorem’s full case analysis (93 sub-supports) is checked
separately by T4-checks/run_all.sh.

12.8       What the experiments do and do not show
They show the mechanisms of the theorems at work in small, fully checkable domains: positive
data fix shapes but not guards (A, B); systematic errors reach support (A, B, D); coherence refutes
uniform errors through Post’s substitution and leaves a residue of coherent alternative meanings
(the meadow in A and C, intermediate logics in B); a world channel that can see the residue
removes it (A–C); and accuracy in distribution says nothing about soundness under search (C).
They show no more than that. Realizability is assumed: valid human steps are exact instances

                                                            149
of the target rules, the guard language contains the true guards, and the simulated humans
are the target plus known fallacies and noise; real practice satisfies none of this (Section 10).
Soundness is measured, not proved: in A and C it is random testing, and “0” means that no
counterexample was found; B decides rule soundness exactly, but its coherence verdicts and
adversaries are budgeted searches. World feedback is cheap in algebra: random evaluation is an
almost perfect oracle for polynomial identities, and a run of A uses about 104 queries; in physics,
and in mathematics with undecidable fragments, B’s two observed valuations or the certified
bridges of Section 11 are the more realistic picture. Few seeds: three per configuration (two for
C’s retraining), although fresh seeds reproduce the patterns of A and B. Toy scale: closed small
domains, heuristic and informed adversaries, small statistical baselines. Nothing here measures
how often large neural verifiers are exploited, only why accuracy cannot rule it out.

Reproduction. Python 3.11 with numpy, scipy, scikit-learn, matplotlib, mpmath and pytest
(sympy for some checks). The full-grid JSON results are checked in, and –report-only rebuilds
every report and plot of A–C from them in seconds; –quick runs a smoke test of each in one to
two minutes. Wall-clock times on a shared 4-core machine:
cd code
python3 -m pytest -q                             # 85 tests, 1.5 min
./run_all_quick.sh                               # everything quick, 16 min
python3 experiments/exp_algebra_learning.py      # A, 19 min, 3 workers
python3 experiments/exp_prop_learning.py --workers 6         # B, 58 min
python3 experiments/exp_adversarial_prover.py    # C, 22 min
python3 experiments/exp_adversarial_prover.py --retrain      # 14 min
cd ../research/theory
(cd T7-checks && python3 ttl_sim.py)             # D, 22 s
(cd T3-checks && python3 sps_checker.py)         # E, 1 s
(cd T5-checks && for f in c?_*.py; do python3 $f; done)      # F
(cd T4-checks && python3 comprehension_toy.py)   # G

code/README.md lists every script with its reproduction status in the review.


13     Justification beyond proof
The investigation began with a question about principled justification. What does it look like,
once we go beyond mathematical proof? This section steps back from the theorems and says
what they show about that question. It also says what they do not show. Throughout, “we”
means the authors of this report. The view argued for is deliberately modest. The theorems do
not deliver a theory of justification. They make precise one shape that justification can take,
and they locate exactly where that shape runs out.

13.1    The setup is Goodman’s circle, made into a learning problem
The setup we studied has three parts:
   • learn inference rules from accepted inferences;
   • revise them when they generate unacceptable conclusions;
   • revise accepted inferences when they conflict with rules one is unwilling to give up.
This is, almost verbatim, Goodman’s account of the justification of deduction and induction:

                                               150
       “Rules and particular inferences alike are justified by being brought into agreement
       with each other. A rule is amended if it yields an inference we are unwilling to accept;
       an inference is rejected if it violates a rule we are unwilling to amend.” (Goodman,
       1955, ch. III)
In our vocabulary, the channels correspond to Goodman’s account as follows:
   • Imitation (P) supplies the accepted particular inferences.
   • Rule schemas, learned by least generalization, are the rules.
   • Coherence (C) is the mechanism by which a rule “yields an inference we are unwilling to
     accept”. A derivation of ⊥ from a context certified consistent is a negative bag, and some
     rule in it must go.
   • World feedback (W) adds a channel whose errors are independent of human errors.
Without (W), the process is narrow reflective equilibrium. With (W) it is wide reflective
equilibrium in the sense of Daniels (1979). Daniels’ “independence constraint” is, formally, the
conditional-independence assumption that the coherence impossibility theorems show to be
necessary (Section 6.7).
   What our theorems add to Goodman’s picture is a set of vindications with explicit conditions.
Under stated channel assumptions, mutual adjustment is sound against every adaptive arguer,
with explicit costs:
   • realizability of the target in a structured hypothesis class;
   • truthful designation of the contexts that are certified consistent;
   • a world channel of the right kind.
For each assumption there is a matching impossibility result showing that dropping it breaks
the guarantee (Section 7). This is the sense in which the theorems are conditional reliabilist
meta-justifications of reflective equilibrium. They are not a non-circular foundation for logic. As
Dummett (1973) observed, any justification of deduction is explanatory rather than suasive: it
uses deduction. What they provide is an explicit list of what the process relies on, together with
proofs that it relies on nothing else.
   Two features of the guarantees matter philosophically.
   • First, they are anytime, not merely in the limit. Limit theorems are Reichenbachian
     vindications. They fall to the objection of Salmon (1967) that infinitely many methods
     share the same limit while disagreeing at every finite stage. Our soundness guarantees hold
     at every time, with probability over the data (Theorem 4.15). Our costs are explicit finite
     quantities: escalations, detections, samples. This also matches a recurring theme in the
     notes that prompted this work: suspicion of limit framings, and the view that thinking and
     mathematics are “infinite endeavors” with no final formula.
   • Second, they are worst case over arguers. A step checker that is accurate on the distribution
     of human steps can be exploited by search (Theorem 3.2). Justification that is to survive an
     untrusted or adversarial generator must be uniform over what the generator may propose.
     This is the case that motivates the question in the first place.

13.2     What each signal can do, and the residue it leaves
The main results combine into a division of labour. Table 22 summarizes it.

                                                 151
    Two observations about this table seem to us to be the real philosophical content of the
investigation.

Every signal is one-sided. Each learning signal we studied can refute errors in only one
direction:
   • Positive data never refute an over-generalization (Gold).
   • Coherence never refutes over-caution: a cautious learner never sees a contradiction (Propo-
     sition 6.3). Nor does it refute a coherent over-generalization (Theorem 6.9).
   • Coherence among exported tolerances never refutes a tolerance that is too wide. Widening
     an interval cannot create a contradiction (Theorem 11.28).
   • The demand that a problem be well posed refutes readings that are too wide, but it rewards
     readings that are too narrow (Proposition 11.40).
Each one-sided signal is an attack surface for an adversarial arguer, and it calls for a complementary
signal that refutes errors in the other direction. Principled justification, on this picture, is not
one master criterion. It is a set of signals that together cover every direction of error, plus an
explicit statement of the directions that remain uncovered. Table 15 lists the pairs for physics.
The same structure appears in mathematics. Imitation proposes and caution guards. Coherence
condemns, but only bold hypotheses. Objects localize.

Each residue has a name, and different philosophies disagree only about the residue.
Outside the residue of coherent, uniform, world-adequate alternatives, every position we know of
counts the learner as correct:
   • the realist;
   • the Peircean, for whom truth is the limit of inquiry;
   • the Dummettian, for whom truth is warranted assertibility;
   • Carnap’s tolerance;
   • Quine’s pragmatism.
Inside the residue, they disagree. The realist sees undetectable error. The Peircean or Dummettian
sees no fact of the matter. Carnap sees a free choice of language. Quine sees a choice made on
pragmatic grounds. The theorems make the residue precise in several cases:
   • It is empty for classical propositional logic.
   • It contains all intermediate logics for an intuitionistic practice.
   • It contains PA + ¬Con(PA) for arithmetic.
   • In the algebra experiment it contains the total semantics with 1/0 = 0, which pure coherence
     converged to (Section 12). This is, as it happens, exactly the convention adopted by Lean’s
     Mathlib and by Isabelle/HOL, where x/0 = 0.
A theorem can say exactly where these philosophies part ways. It cannot settle who is right
inside the residue. We think that is the correct division of labour between mathematics and
philosophy here.



                                                152
    One consequence deserves to be stated on its own. Rules can be identifiable when truths are
not. The bilateral tell-tale identifies classical meanings from finitely many data (Theorem 6.29).
Yet no computable learner on the same channels decides Σ2 truth in the limit (Theorem 6.22).
This undercuts the simple form of Dummett’s acquisition argument. That argument holds that a
meaning one could acquire must be manifestable in use, so meaning cannot outrun decidability.
But a learner can acquire the meaning-constitutive rules exactly, while the truth values they
determine remain beyond any computable reach.

13.3    Rules are not premises
The tortoise of Carroll (1895) refused to infer Z from A, B and “if A and B then Z” until that
inference was itself added as a premise, and so on forever. For a learner the lesson has a precise
form:
   • A learned rule is a schema. Its validity is a universally quantified claim over all instances,
     of Π form.
   • No finite amount of world feedback verifies such a claim. Feedback can only refute it.
   • The justification for using a learned rule therefore cannot be further premises. It has to be
     a guarantee about the process that accepted it: cautious acceptance, and an anchor that
     has been observed.
This is why the right acceptance policy for learned rules is Popperian. Accept tentatively. Assert
only what every surviving hypothesis licenses. Treat each refutation as permanent. It is also
why self-certification must never feed back into acceptance:
   • a learned verifier that “proves its own soundness” has provided no evidence that distinguishes
     it from an unsound verifier sharing the certifying rule;
   • in arithmetic, by Löb’s theorem a theory that proves the reflection schema ProvT (⌜φ⌝) → φ
     for its own provability predicate, for all φ, proves everything. By contrast, the corresponding
     reflection rule is admissible for Σ1 -sound theories.
Reflection is a principled source of genuinely new inferences, beyond imitation, that a learner
who has accepted a calculus is committed to. Where it is used, it must enter as a stratified rule,
never as a self-referential premise.

13.4    What principled justification beyond proof looks like
Putting the pieces together, the results suggest the following shape for a principled justification
of a claim about something other than pure mathematics. A physics olympiad answer is the
running case.
   1. Proof inside contexts. Each step inside a context is checked against a calculus. The
      calculus is formal where possible. Where it is learned, acceptance is cautious, so the check
      is sound against an arguer who searches for weaknesses (Sections 4 and 10).
   2. Certified bridges between contexts. Moving a conclusion from an idealized context
      to the claim of interest is not a logical step. It needs a certificate carrying information of
      finite radius about how the quantity behaves away from the idealization. Facts about the
      idealized model alone never suffice (no free export, Theorem 11.21). Side conditions are


                                                153
       evaluated where they are true, in the parent context, and contexts are declared deformations.
       Otherwise an arguer can “stipulate” its way to anything (Theorem 11.27).
  3. An explicit judgment layer. What remains is provably ineliminable (Theorem 11.38):

         • that the problem was read correctly, i.e. the admissible worlds were not narrowed;
         • that the top model is adequate for the world claims;
         • that the trusted base is trustworthy.

       A principled justification does not hide this layer. It minimizes it and declares it.
  4. Correctness as supertruth. For underspecified questions, “true in the context at hand”
     means true in every admissible completion of the intended model, to the demanded precision.
     This is the largest acceptance criterion that stays sound under uncertainty about the reading
     (Theorem 11.39). Answer-invariance under unspecified features, which experienced solvers
     notice and rely on, is exactly a certificate of supertruth.
  5. Learning signals with known limits. Every learned component comes with a statement
     of the signal that trained it and the residue that signal leaves. The learned components are
     the calculus, the bridge validity regions and the tolerances.
   Measured against this shape, mathematical proof is the limiting case in which:
  • the judgment layer has shrunk to the reading of the formal statement and trust in the
    axioms;
  • every bridge is exact;
  • the calculus is fixed rather than learned.
Informal mathematics before formalization sits in between. It was a bounded-gap practice over a
latent calculus, as the robust-core hypothesis suggests. In it, counterexample objects did most of
the corrective work, and paradoxes were rare and uninformative (Section 10). Physics adds a
nonempty, explicit judgment layer and non-logical bridges, but the shape is the same. On this
view the difference between mathematical and physical justification is one of degree, measured
by the size of the declared residue, not one of kind.

13.5     What this says about meaning
The inferentialist premise of the question was that learning which inferences are valid is roughly
learning meanings. The results support a qualified version:
  • Inferential roles are learnable, exactly, from finite use. A finite anchor fixes the
    inferential role of a vocabulary for unbounded use (Corollary 5.11). This is a precise, if
    modest, answer to the rule-following question of how finite training fixes an infinite norm.
    The answer is: through structure (schemas) plus a small set of telling instances. The
    skeptic’s “quus” survives only as a non-uniform patch, which uniformity rules out, or as a
    coherent uniform alternative, which is a named residue.
  • More than role is not identifiable. Practice, coherence and object data identify a latent
    formalization exactly up to informal-validity equivalence, and no further (Theorem 10.15).
    Ontology is not identifiable: standard and nonstandard reals are observationally equivalent



                                                154
       on the practice (Proposition 10.17). Neither is the choice of set-theoretic reduction
       (Proposition 10.19).
   • Coherence buys existence, but not intended existence. Every coherent mode
     of talking has something it could be talking about. That is a generalized completeness
     theorem, true for positions, credences and context systems alike (Section 9). But that
     something may be a syntactic or nonstandard model. Intended existence needs one of three
     additional things:

         – categoricity;
         – anchoring of the vocabulary to the world (Beth definability);
         – an infinitary condition, such as the probabilistic ω-rule.

       None of these can be certified computably beyond Π1 .
Inferential role is one aspect of meaning, and the project captures that aspect precisely. Other
aspects are not captured by anything studied here:
   • the “hooking” of a conception onto the world;
   • the activities a concept supports;
   • the questions it lets one ask.
We do not claim otherwise.

13.6     Answers to the questions that prompted the investigation
The investigation was prompted by a set of questions. Most of them are recorded in notes that
are publicly available. Table 23 lists the main ones with pointers to where they are answered or
partly answered.


14      Limits and open problems
We collect the main limitations of the results and the open problems they leave. The hardest of
them is the first.

1. Affordable soundness when the learner must invent language. Every soundness
guarantee here is relative to realizability: the target calculus, or the latent formalization, must lie
in the hypothesis class. History did not respect any fixed class. It invented uniform convergence,
ideals, ε–δ and the fundamental group as proof-generated concepts.
   • A universal, language-inventing class restores realizability, but sound verification then costs
                         ∗
     on the order of 2K(h ) escalations (Theorems 4.12 and 10.10).
   • What is needed is a class that is closed under the definitional extensions mathematics
     actually made, and that still has small escalation dimension. Equivalently, we need a theory
     of when concept invention, for example by anti-unification over definitions or by naming
     the hidden lemma found by descent, is cheap.




                                                 155
2. Computational cost. Our bounds count samples, escalations, detections and refutation-
oracle calls, not computation.
  • Membership in the version-space intersection for untagged unions is likely NP-hard.
  • Consistency with negative bags is a multiple-instance problem. Its hardness is the formal
    shadow of the obfuscated-arguments problem in debate.
  • Refutation search is only semi-decidable. Our depth-relative soundness (Theorem 7.22) is
    the honest form of what can be had.
A complexity-theoretic version of the two-tier theorem would be valuable. It would identify
which audits are polynomial and for which classes.

3. The reading layer. In physics the irreducible judgment is the reading: which admissible
worlds the problem text determines (Theorem 11.38).
  • Determinacy refutes only over-wide readings (Proposition 11.40).
  • What positive data identify the intended admissible class? We conjecture that official
    solutions together with marking schemes form a tell-tale within a class of “minimal
    completions consistent with the intended imports”.
  • Learning readings soundly is, we think, the central open problem for principled checking
    beyond mathematics.

4. Sharp combinatorics. Several bounds have gaps:
  • The binomial conjecture for k-unions of intersection-closed classes (Conjecture 4.11) would
    close the gap for untagged schema learning.
                                 (r)
  • The bag-price conjecture Mbag ≤ r · Ldim (Conjecture 10.27) would give the exact exchange
    rate between paradoxes and counterexample objects.
  • The query complexity of sound blame, i.e. computing the union of the minimal conflicts
    with a conflict oracle, is open.

5. Coherence beyond Post-completeness.
  • For which first-order or modal targets does every invalid uniform rule have a short coherence
    witness in a natural designated context?
  • Is there a hierarchy of coherence-witness complexity?
  • Which logics and theories are coherence-pinned, meaning that the residue is empty for
    natural families of designated contexts?
  • For arithmetic: is there a principled learner over reflection progressions that converges to
    “the right amount of reflection” under a natural feedback notion?

6. Simplicity and normativity.
  • For which classes of simple models does the product-model frontier kink, beyond parities?
  • Is there a principled, data-free choice of the simplicity coefficient, for example one tied to
    the cheapest postulate-violating error?

                                              156
  • Which assumptions on a performance model, such as errors being time-bounded shortcuts,
    make joint description length identify competence despite the known non-identifiability of
    preferences from behaviour?

7. Probabilistic and contextual coherence.
  • Find a finitely presented proof system for valid counting sequents that is complete for de
    Finetti coherence.
  • Prove a probabilistic local-to-global theorem for context systems on tree covers.
  • Decide whether contextual frustration between idealized contexts should be penalized or
    tolerated.

8. Scale.
  • Our experiments are illustrations in small symbolic domains with realizable targets, cheap
    world feedback and few seeds.
  • Whether learned calculi with cautious acceptance remain affordable at the scale of real
    mathematical corpora (for example Lean or Metamath step data with injected fallacies at
    measured human rates) is an empirical question we have not addressed.
  • We have deliberately not built a strong prover, and we would recommend that any such
    work keep the checker separate from the generator.

A     Proofs for Section 2
Theorem 2.5 is due to Plotkin (1970) and Reynolds (1970). All other statements of Section 2
are proved here. The rank lemma and the recovery lemma are T1 Lemmas 1.2–1.3, which the
referees reported correct with no change needed; the other three are routine.


                                                171
A.1    Proof of Lemma 2.3 (closure facts)
(a) Let D be the set of judgments with an R-derivation from B. D contains B (one-node
derivations) and is closed under R: if P ▷ j ∈ R and P ⊆ D, graft derivations of the finitely
many members of P under a new root j. So ClR (B) ⊆ D. Conversely, if X ⊇ B is R-closed,
induction on the height of derivations shows that every node of every derivation from B lies
in X; so D ⊆ ClR (B). Extensivity and monotonicity in B and in R hold because a derivation
from B using steps of R is also one from any B ′ ⊇ B using steps of any R′ ⊇ R. Idempotence: a
derivation from ClR (B) becomes a derivation from B after grafting a derivation from B above
each leaf. Finitarity: a derivation has finitely many leaves, so it is a derivation from a finite
B0 ⊆ B.
    (b) A single step P ▷ j ∈ R is a derivation of j from P , so R ⊆ Sound(R), and monotonicity
in the rule set gives ClR (B) ⊆ ClSound(R) (B). Conversely, ClR (B) is closed under Sound(R):
if P ⊆ ClR (B) and j ∈ ClR (P ), then by (a) j ∈ ClR (ClR (B)) = ClR (B); it contains B, so it
contains ClSound(R) (B). Finally, Sound(A) = {P ▷ j : j ∈ ClA (P )} depends only on ClA , so
ClA = ClR gives A ⊆ Sound(A) = Sound(R); and Sound(R) itself has the closure ClR .

A.2    Proof of Lemma 2.6 (rank)
Throughout, |t| is the number of symbol occurrences in t, metavariable occurrences included,
and vars(t) is the set of distinct metavariables of t. A renaming of a set X of metavariables is a
substitution whose restriction to X is an injective map into metavariables. If τ = σθ with θ a
renaming of vars(σ), then σ = τ θ−1 , so σ ≡ τ ; conversely, σ ≡ τ means equality up to such a
renaming, and then µ(σ) = µ(τ ).
    Let τ = σθ. Let x1 , . . . , xk be the distinct metavariables of σ, and let xi occur mi ≥ 1 times
in σ.
    Size. Replacing each of the mi occurrences of xi (one symbol) by θxi (with |θxi | symbols)
gives
                                                   k
                                                   X                  
                                    |τ | = |σ| +         mi |θxi | − 1 .
                                                   i=1

   Metavariables. Every metavariable of τ occurs in some θxi , so
                                                    k
                                                    X
                                     |vars(τ )| ≤         |vars(θxi )|,
                                                    i=1

with equality iff the sets vars(θxi ) are pairwise disjoint.
   Difference. Since |vars(σ)| = k,
                                        k h
                                        X                                     i
                                                               
                       µ(τ ) − µ(σ) ≥         mi |θxi | − 1 + 1 − |vars(θxi )| .                 (1)
                                        i=1

We bound each summand.
   • If θxi is a metavariable, then |θxi | = 1 = |vars(θxi )| and the summand is 0.
   • Otherwise θxi contains at least one function or constant symbol and at least one occurrence
     of each of its metavariables, so |θxi | ≥ |vars(θxi )| + 1. Using mi ≥ 1, the summand is at
     least (|θxi | − 1) + 1 − |vars(θxi )| ≥ 1.


                                                    172
Hence µ(τ ) ≥ µ(σ).
    Equality. If µ(τ ) = µ(σ), then every summand in (1) is 0 and the inequality there is tight.
The first forces every θxi to be a metavariable; the second forces the sets vars(θxi ) = {θxi } to
be pairwise disjoint, i.e. the θxi to be pairwise distinct. So θ restricted to vars(σ) is a renaming.
Conversely, if it is a renaming, then |τ | = |σ| and |vars(τ )| = |vars(σ)|, so µ(τ ) = µ(σ).
    Chains. Suppose σl+1 ≻ σl strictly, i.e. σl = σl+1 θl and σl ̸≡ σl+1 . Then θl is not a renaming
of vars(σl+1 ) (otherwise the two would be equivalent), so µ(σl ) > µ(σl+1 ), i.e. µ(σl ) ≥ µ(σl+1 ) + 1.
Summing along τ = σ0 ≺ σ1 ≺ · · · ≺ σm gives µ(τ ) ≥ µ(σm ) + m, and µ(σm ) ≥ 0.

A.3     Proof of Lemma 2.7 (when the lgg recovers the schema)
Let σ have metavariables x1 , . . . , xv , let θ1 , . . . , θn be ground substitutions (n ≥ 1), and let
tj = σθj . For each metavariable x of σ write cx = (θ1 x, . . . , θn x) for its column, and let η be the
substitution η(x) := A(cx ).
    Claim. For every subterm σ ′ of σ, A(σ ′ θ1 , . . . , σ ′ θn ) = σ ′ η.
    By induction on σ ′ . If σ ′ = x is a metavariable, both sides are A(cx ). If σ ′ = f (σ1′ , . . . , σr′ )
(with r = 0 for a constant), then every σ ′ θj = f (σ1′ θj , . . . , σr′ θj ) has root f , so by definition
A outputs f applied to A of the argument columns (σk′ θ1 , . . . , σk′ θn ), which by the induction
hypothesis are σk′ η. Hence the output is f (σ1′ η, . . . , σr′ η) = σ ′ η.
    Taking σ ′ = σ and using Theorem 2.5,

                                lgg(t1 , . . . , tn ) = A(t1 , . . . , tn ) = ση ⪯ σ.

    When η is a renaming. By the definition of A:
   • η(x) = A(cx ) is a metavariable iff the entries of cx do not all have the same root, i.e. iff (R)
     holds at x. If (R) fails at x, η(x) is a compound term or a constant.
   • If (R) holds at x and at y, then η(x) = zcx and η(y) = zcy , and these coincide iff cx = cy ,
     i.e. iff (D) fails for the pair x, y.
So η restricted to vars(σ) is a renaming iff (R) holds for every x and (D) holds for every pair
x ̸= y.
    Conclusion. If (R) and (D) hold, η is a renaming and lgg(t1 , . . . , tn ) = ση ≡ σ. If either fails,
η is not a renaming of vars(σ), so by Lemma 2.6 µ(ση) > µ(σ); since equivalent schemas have
equal rank, lgg(t1 , . . . , tn ) ̸≡ σ, and it is strictly more specific.

A.4     Proof of Lemma 2.8 (guarded closure)
P ⊆ inst(lgg(P )) by Theorem 2.5, and every ϕ ∈ ΨP holds on P , so P ⊆ inst(lgg(P ), ΨP ). Let
P ⊆ inst(σ, Ψ). Then P ⊆ inst(σ), so σ ⪰ lgg(P ) and inst(lgg(P )) ⊆ inst(σ). Every ϕ ∈ Ψ holds
on all of P , so Ψ ⊆ ΨP and ϕ∈ΨP ϕ−1 (1) ⊆ ϕ∈Ψ ϕ−1 (1). Intersecting, inst(lgg(P ), ΨP ) ⊆
                             T                 T

inst(σ, Ψ).

A.5     Proof of Lemma 2.15 ((WS) and coherence)
This is the remark following (WS) in T7 §1.3, in the form repaired after verification (without the
clause Ttrust ⊆ Sound(R∗ )). Write R+ = R∗ ∪ Ttrust . Let [Γ : ∆] be designated and V as in (WS).
V makes every member of Γ true, and no step of R+ has true premises and a false conclusion, so
the set of V -true judgments is an R+ -closed superset of Γ and contains ClR+ (Γ). Since V makes


                                                        173
⊥ and every member of ∆ false, ⊥ ∈   / ClR+ (Γ) and ∆ ∩ ClR+ (Γ) = ∅. As ClR∗ (Γ) ⊆ ClR+ (Γ)
(Lemma 2.3(a), monotonicity in the rule set), the position is also in bounds for R∗ .
   Conversely, let JW = ∅ and let [Γ : ∆] be a designated position that is in bounds for R+ .
Then e is well defined, because Γ ⊆ ClR+ (Γ) is disjoint from ∆ ∪ {⊥}. Let V be the indicator
function of ClR+ (Γ). It extends e: it is 1 on Γ and 0 on ∆ ∪ {⊥}. The set ClR+ (Γ) is closed
under R+ , so no step of R+ has V -true premises and a V -false conclusion, and (WS) holds.
For the parenthetical instance: if Ttrust ⊆ Sound(R∗ ), then R∗ ⊆ R+ ⊆ Sound(R∗ ), so by
Lemma 2.3(a),(b) ClR+ = ClR∗ , and being in bounds for R∗ is the same as being in bounds for
R+ .


B      Proofs for Section 3
Lemma 3.1, Theorem 3.2, Proposition 3.5 and Proposition 3.9 are proved in full in the main text.
This appendix proves the remaining results of Section 3 and verifies the claims of Example 3.6.
Notation is as in Section 3. Throughout, |= is classical propositional consequence, and a step
Π ▷ φ over formulas is sound if Π |= φ.

B.1     PAC learners can be maximally unsound
Proof of Corollary 3.3. For N ≥ 0 let S≥N := {s ∈ S : |s| ≥ N }.
    Step 1: Tj ⊆ S≥j . Every step of Tj has the premise A ∨ ⊤(j) , and ⊤(j) contains j occurrences
of ⊤. So every step of Tj has size at least j.
    Step 2: the tail bound. The sets S≥N decrease in N and have empty intersection, so Q(S≥N ) ↓ 0
by continuity from above. Fix ε ∈ (0, 1) and let Nε := min{N ≥ 0 : Q(S≥N ) ≤ ε}.
    • If ȷ̂ ≥ Nε , then Q(Tȷ̂ ) ≤ Q(S≥ȷ̂ ) ≤ Q(S≥Nε ) ≤ ε, by Step 1 and monotonicity.
    • If ȷ̂ < Nε , then Nε ≥ 2, because ȷ̂ ≥ 1. By minimality of Nε , Q(S≥Nε −1 ) > ε. Moreover
      ȷ̂ < Nε means |Xi | ≤ Nε − 2 for every i, i.e. no sample lies in S≥Nε −1 . Since the Xi are
      i.i.d. from Q, this event has probability (1 − Q(S≥Nε −1 ))m < (1 − ε)m .
Hence Pr[Q(Tȷ̂ ) > ε] ≤ Pr[ȷ̂ < Nε ] ≤ (1 − ε)m .
     Step 3: the error decomposition. Write h = L(X1..m ) and Y = inst(⊤I, ∧I, ∨I2 ) ⊆ R∗ . An
element of (h ∪ Tȷ̂ ∪ Y ) \ R∗ lies in h \ R∗ or in Tȷ̂ , because Y ⊆ R∗ . An element of R∗ \ (h ∪ Tȷ̂ ∪ Y )
lies in R∗ \ h. So (h ∪ Tȷ̂ ∪ Y ) △ R∗ ⊆ (h △ R∗ ) ∪ Tȷ̂ , and errQ (L+ ) ≤ errQ (L) + Q(Tȷ̂ ) pointwise
in the sample. This proves (a).
     Step 4: PAC. Let m ≥ max{mL (ε/2, δ/2), ⌈(2/ε) ln(2/δ)⌉}. Then Pr[errQ (L) > ε/2] ≤ δ/2,
since as usual the guarantee of L holds for every sample size m ≥ mL (ε, δ). By Step 2 applied
with ε/2, Pr[Q(Tȷ̂ ) > ε/2] ≤ (1 − ε/2)m ≤ e−mε/2 ≤ δ/2. By Step 3 and a union bound,
Pr[errQ (L+ ) > ε] ≤ δ. Both events are computed from the same sample, so no extra samples
beyond the maximum are needed. The outputs of L+ need not lie in the hypothesis class of L,
so the guarantee is improper. This proves (b).
     Step 5: trivialization. Every output of L+ contains Y ∪ Tȷ̂ . The derivation in the proof of
Theorem 3.2(i), with j = ȷ̂, uses only steps of Y (namely ⊤I, ∧I and ∨I2 ) and one step of Tȷ̂ . So
ClL+ (X1..m ) (∅) = Fm for every sample. This proves (c).

Remark B.1. Step 5 is where the three added schemas are needed. If L ≡ ∅ and they are omitted,
the output is Tȷ̂ alone. Every step of Tȷ̂ has a premise, so ∅ is closed under it and ClTȷ̂ (∅) = ∅.

Proof of Lemma 3.7. Let τ have metavariables x̄ = x1 , . . . , xk , and let pi1 , . . . , pil be the distinct
atoms occurring in τ . Let y1 , . . . , yl be fresh metavariables and τ ◦ := τ [pit := yt ]t≤l . Write
τ [x̄ := Ā] for the instance of τ obtained by substituting the formulas Ā for x̄, and similarly for τ ◦ .
     (⇐) Every instance of τ is an instance of τ ◦ : τ [x̄ := Ā] = τ ◦ [x̄ := Ā, ȳ := (pi1 , . . . , pil )]. So if
  ◦
τ is sound, so is τ .
     (⇒) Assume τ is sound, and let ι = τ ◦ [x̄ := Ā, ȳ := B̄] be an instance of τ ◦ . Then ι is τ with
each occurrence of pit in τ itself replaced by Bt , and each xs replaced by As . Occurrences of pit
inside the As are left alone. There are infinitely many atoms, so we can choose distinct atoms
r1 , . . . , rl that occur nowhere in Ā, B̄ or τ . Let ρ be the renaming pit 7→ rt (t ≤ l), identity on
all other atoms, and put A′s := ρ(As ). Then ι′ := τ [x̄ := Ā′ ] is an instance of τ , hence sound.
      Let σ be the simultaneous uniform substitution with σ(pit ) = Bt , σ(rt ) = pit (t ≤ l), and
identity on all other atoms. We claim σ(ι′ ) = ι. The formulas of ι′ are built from the skeleton
of τ , whose atoms are among the pit and include no rt , with the fillers A′s plugged in for the
metavariables.
   • On the skeleton, σ replaces each pit by Bt , exactly as the substitution ȳ := B̄ does in τ ◦ .
   • Each filler A′s = ρ(As ) contains no pit , since ρ renamed them all. It contains rt exactly
     where As contains pit , because As contains no rt . All its other atoms are fixed by both ρ
     and σ. Hence σ(A′s ) = As .
So σ(ι′ ) = τ ◦ [x̄ := Ā, ȳ := B̄] = ι.
    Finally, uniform substitution preserves classical validity. Suppose Π′ |= φ′ and let v be any
valuation. Define v ′ (p) := v(σ(p)). By induction on formulas, v ′ (ψ) = v(σψ) for every ψ. So if v
satisfies σΠ′ , then v ′ satisfies Π′ , hence φ′ , and v satisfies σφ′ . Applied to ι′ , this shows that
ι = σ(ι′ ) is sound.

B.3    The commitment-order lemma
Recall Definition 3.10. An R-derivation is a finite tree of sequents in which each node is either
a leaf marked as a premise (chosen freely by the prover) or, together with its children, forms
an instance of some rule of R: the children are the instance’s premise sequents (none, for a
premise-free rule) and the node is its conclusion sequent. The conclusion of the derivation is the
sequent at the root.

Proof of Lemma 3.11(i). Let G := {x : ρi is valid in x for every i ≤ m}. By the union bound
over the whole library,
                                        m
                                        X                                   m
                                                                            X
                       Pr [x ∈
                             / G] ≤           Pr [ρi is not valid in x] ≤         εi .
                       x∼D                    x∼D
                                        i=1                                 i=1

Fix x ∈ G and any R-derivation whose premises are all true in x. We show by induction on
height that every sequent in the tree is true in x. Premise nodes are true by assumption. Every
other node is the conclusion of an instance ι of some ρi , whose premise sequents are its children
(possibly none) and are true in x by the induction hypothesis. Since x ∈ G, ρi is valid in x, so ι
holds in x and its conclusion is true in x. In particular the conclusion of the derivation is true
in x.
     So the event “some R-derivation from premises true in x has a conclusion false in x” is
contained in the complement of G. Its probability (its outer probability, if it is not measurable)
                      P
is therefore at most i εi . The event G is determined by x and the library R alone. Because R
is fixed before x is drawn, G does not depend on the prover, and the bound holds simultaneously
for every prover, including one that sees x and chooses any number of derivations of any length.
The sum runs over the whole library, not over the rules a particular derivation uses.

Remark B.2 (Per-pair guarantees do not give per-scene validity). Suppose each scene in the
support of D falsifies exactly one of M instances of a rule ρ, and the instance is drawn uniformly
from these M . Then Pr(x,ι) [ι holds in x] = 1 − 1/M , but ρ is valid in no scene, so its per-scene
validity is 0. A prover that sees x submits the falsified instance; this is regime (ii) inside each
scene.

Proof of Lemma 3.11(ii). Let ι0 be an instance of ρ that fails in x0 : its premise sequents are
true in x0 and its conclusion sequent is false in x0 . The tree consisting of ι0 alone (its conclusion
at the root, its premises as leaves) is a {ρ}-derivation from premises true in x0 with a conclusion
false in x0 . A prover that knows x0 submits it, and nothing in this regime is random, so this
happens with probability 1. If µ{ι : ι fails in x0 } > 0, such an ι0 exists. If µ is supported on
instances that hold in x0 , the average validity is 1, i.e. ε = 0, but a failing instance off the support
of µ may still exist, and the same prover uses it. So no bound in terms of ε holds.

   For part (iii) we use the Schwartz–Zippel lemma (Schwartz, 1980; Zippel, 1979; DeMillo and
Lipton, 1978): if f ∈ F [z1 , . . . , zn ] is nonzero of total degree at most d and S ⊆ F is finite, then
Pra∼Unif(S n ) [f (a) = 0] ≤ d/|S|.


                                                    176
Proof of Lemma 3.11(iii). Formalize the protocol as follows. In round k the prover, which may
be randomized, adaptive and computationally unbounded, commits to explicit expressions for
pk , qk ∈ F [z1 , . . . , zn ]. The verifier computes an upper bound on deg(pk −qk ) from the expressions
and rejects if it exceeds d. Otherwise it draws ak uniformly from Skn , independently of everything
so far, and accepts iff pk (ak ) = qk (ak ).
     Let Hk be the σ-algebra generated by everything that happens before ak is drawn: earlier
claims, points and answers, and the prover’s coins. The claim (pk , qk ) is Hk -measurable, and ak
is independent of Hk . Let Ek be the event that claim k passes the degree check and pk − qk is
nonzero in F [z1 , . . . , zn ], and Acck the event that it is accepted. By the Schwartz–Zippel lemma,
applied conditionally on Hk ,
                                                                              
               Pr[Ek ∩ Acck | Hk ] = 1Ek ·        Pr         (pk − qk )(a) = 0 ≤ d/|Sk |.
                                              a∼Unif(Skn )


Taking expectations, Pr[Ek ∩ Acck ] ≤ d/|Sk |. A false claim (one with pk − qk ̸= 0 over F ) that
fails the degree check is rejected, so a false claim is accepted only on Ek ∩ Acck . By the union
bound,                                                              X
                   Pr ∃k ≤ N : claim k is false and accepted ≤           d/|Sk |,
                                                                           k≤N

which is N d/|S| when Sk = S for all k. If |Sk | ≥ d 2k /δ for all k ≥ 1, then k≥1 d/|Sk | ≤
                                                                                        P
P        −k = δ, which bounds the probability that any false claim in an unbounded sequence is
  k≥1 δ2
accepted. The prover’s strategy enters only through the Hk , so the bounds hold uniformly over
provers.

Why the conditions of (iii) are needed.
                                                                           k
   • Degree enforcement. Over F = F2k with S = F , the claim z 2 ≡ z can be written with k
                                    k
     squarings. The polynomial z 2 − z is nonzero of degree 2k , but it vanishes on all of F , so
     random evaluation always accepts. The bound d/|S| is vacuous here, since d = 2k = |S|.
     A verifier that read the degree off the length of the expression, roughly k, would wrongly
     believe its error to be about k/2k .
                          p, f = (a + b)p − ap − bp = 0<i<p pi ai bp−i is nonzero over Z, but p
                                                                 P     
   • The field. For prime
                     p
     divides every i with 0 < i < p, so f = 0 in Fp [a, b]. Evaluation mod p accepts this false
     integer identity with probability 1. Over Q with S ⊆ Z, the lemma applies with d = p.
   • Order of commitment. If the evaluation points were drawn before the prover committed to
     its claims, and visible to it, the prover could choose a nonzero p − q that vanishes at those
     points. This is regime (ii) again.

B.4    The doctrinal paradox
Proof of Theorem 3.12. Step 1: each hi is a coherent, cut-closed calculus. The sets T1 , T2 , T3
are satisfied by the valuations (v(p), v(q)) = (1, 1), (1, 0), (0, 1) respectively. For every set Γ,
Clhi (Γ) = CnCPC (Ti ∪Γ). For ⊇: if Ti ∪Γ |= φ, compactness gives a finite Γ0 ⊆ Γ with Ti ∪Γ0 |= φ,
so Γ0 ▷ φ ∈ hi and φ ∈ Clhi (Γ). For ⊆: CnCPC (Ti ∪ Γ) contains Γ and is closed under hi , since
Π ⊆ CnCPC (Ti ∪ Γ) and Ti ∪ Π |= φ imply Ti ∪ Γ |= φ. In particular Clhi (∅) = CnCPC (Ti ), which
does not contain ⊥ because Ti is satisfiable. So every hi is A-coherent and is a possible target.
    Step 2: memberships. The following table records which hi contain each step of π.

                                                  177
                        step                  h1 (p, q)   h2 (p, ¬q)   h3 (¬p, q)
                        ▷p                       ✓            ✓            –
                        ▷q                       ✓            –            ✓
                        p, q ▷ p ∧ q             ✓            ✓            ✓
                        ▷¬(p ∧ q)                –            ✓            ✓
                        p ∧ q, ¬(p ∧ q) ▷ ⊥      ✓            ✓            ✓

The checkmarks are immediate: p ∈ T1 , T2 ; q ∈ T1 , T3 ; ¬q |= ¬(p ∧ q) and ¬p |= ¬(p ∧ q); and
the third and fifth steps are valid outright. For the dashes: T3 |= ¬p, T2 |= ¬q and T1 |= p ∧ q,
and each Ti is satisfiable, so T3 ̸|= p, T2 ̸|= q and T1 ̸|= ¬(p ∧ q).
    Step 3: π is a legal detection that deletes nothing. The five steps form an argument π from ∅
to ⊥: the first two yield p and q, the third yields p ∧ q, the fourth yields ¬(p ∧ q), and the
fifth yields ⊥. With uniform weights, s is in the majority set over H iff s lies in at least two
of the hi , since 2/3 > 1/2 > 1/3. By the table, every step of π lies in at least two hi , so
Steps(π) ⊆ R̂maj , the majority set over H. Also by the table, Steps(π) ̸⊆ hi for each i. So the
deletion set {R ∈ H : Steps(π) ⊆ R} is empty.
    Step 4: induction on rounds. Fix any target hi∗ . Consider the environment that, in each
round, exhibits π or presents the positive datum ▷p ∨ ¬p, in any order. We show VSt = H for
all t. Initially VS1 = H. If VSt = H, then R̂t = R̂maj ⊇ Steps(π). So π is a legal (N)-datum,
since ∅ ∈ A, and by Step 3 it deletes nothing. The datum ▷p ∨ ¬p lies in every hi , so it is a legal
(P)-datum for the target and deletes {R : ▷p ∨ ¬p ∈        / R} = ∅. Hence VSt+1 = H. So VSt = H
and R̂t = R̂maj for all t, and π is available in every round.

Consequences recorded in the main text.
    • The vote is unsound relative to every target. By Step 3, ⊥ ∈ ClR̂maj (∅). By Step 1,
       ⊥∈
        / Clhi (∅) for each i. By Lemma 3.1 with B = ∅, R̂maj ̸⊆ Sound(hi ) for each i.
    • Discriminating data break the paradox. Let the target be h1 and present ▷p ∈ h1 . This
      deletes h3 , since ▷p ∈ / h3 . Over VS = {h1 , h2 }, with weight 1/3 each, a step is a strict
      majority iff it lies in both, so R̂ = h1 ∩ h2 . Since ▷q ∈
                                                               / h2 , π is no longer available. The
      hi are pairwise incomparable: ▷p ∧ q lies only in h1 , ▷p ∧ ¬q only in h2 , and ▷¬p ∧ q
      only in h3 . So on a text enumerating all of the target, each wrong hypothesis is eventually
      deleted by a datum it lacks, and from then on the uninformative detections stop.


C      Proofs for Section 4
The following are proved in full in the main text: Theorem 4.2(a) and (c), Theorem 4.12,
Theorem 4.14, and Corollaries 4.18 and 4.19. This appendix proves the remaining results, checks
the examples of Section 4, and records the evidence for Conjecture 4.11. All statements are the
versions repaired after adversarial verification (T1 Verification log). Where a repair changed a
statement or a proof, this is noted.
    Throughout, a verifier’s internal randomness is a single draw ω ∼ Λ on a space ΩV , made
before the first round. Nothing is lost by this, since coins drawn later can be drawn up front.
Steps are ground terms, |t| is the number of symbol occurrences of t, and µ(t) = |t| − |vars(t)|.
We use the rank lemma (Lemma 2.6) and the characterization of the lgg (Theorem 2.5).




                                                  178
C.1     Optimality of the VS verifier
Fix human data P0 and a deterministic, non-adaptive prover issuing q1 , q2 , . . . . For a step set T
used as the target, the transcript after i rounds is τiT (ω) = (e1 , . . . , ei ) with ej = (qj , aj , yj ).
                                           T (ω), q , ω), and y = 1[q ∈ T ] if a = ESCALATE,
It is defined recursively by aj = V (P0 , τj−1     j               j       j           j
while yj = ∗ (no label) otherwise. A history h = (e1 , . . . , et ) is consistent with a step set R if
yj = 1[qj ∈ R] whenever yj ̸= ∗.

Lemma C.1 (Coupling through the coins). Let h be a history of t rounds that is consistent with
                                                                                              ′
two step sets T and T ′ , with queries q1 , . . . , qt . Then for every ω, τtT (ω) = h iff τtT (ω) = h.
                                                                        ′
Proof. We show by induction on i ≤ t that τiT (ω) = h≤i iff τiT (ω) = h≤i . The case i = 0 is
trivial. Suppose the claim holds for i − 1. If neither transcript equals h≤i−1 , neither extends to
h≤i . If both equal h≤i−1 , the verifier’s answer ai = V (P0 , h≤i−1 , qi , ω) is the same under both
targets. If ai differs from the answer recorded in h, neither transcript equals h≤i . If it agrees
and is not ESCALATE, both transcripts equal h≤i . If it agrees and is ESCALATE, the labels are
1[qi ∈ T ] and 1[qi ∈ T ′ ]. Both equal the label recorded in h, by consistency, so both transcripts
equal h≤i .

Proof of Theorem 4.2(b). Since q ∈          / VSh , there is R′ ∈ VSh with q ∈
                                                                             / R′ . Then R′ ∈ H
                                          T

and P0 ⊆ R′ , and R′ contains every step labelled positive in h and no step labelled negative,
so h is consistent with R′ . By hypothesis, h is consistent with R∗ . Let the prover issue
q1 , . . . , qt , qt+1 := q. For T ∈ {R∗ , R′ } put

                       ET := {ω : τtT (ω) = h and V (P0 , h, q, ω) = ACCEPT}.

By Lemma C.1, ER∗ = ER′ as subsets of ΩV , so p = Λ(ER∗ ) = Λ(ER′ ). On ER′ , with target R′ ,
                    / R′ is accepted in round t + 1. V is δ-sound, and R′ is an admissible target,
the invalid query q ∈
so Λ(ER′ ) ≤ PR′ [∃s : as = ACCEPT ∧ qs ∈  / R′ ] ≤ δ.

    This joint-probability form replaces the original statement, which bounded the acceptance
probability at h and is false when read conditionally (referee report A-1). The deterministic
form (c) follows because Λ(E) ∈ {0, 1} when Λ is a point mass. Any history that a deterministic
verifier produces against a target is consistent with that target, since the oracle is truthful.
    The reckless verifier. Let H = {R1 = {a, c}, R2 = {a, b, c}, R3 = {a, b}} and P0 = ∅. The
verifier draws B ∼ Bernoulli(ε). If B = 1 it accepts every query; otherwise it runs the VS verifier.
   • It is ε-sound. When B = 0 no invalid query is ever accepted, by Theorem 4.2(a), so the
     probability of an invalid acceptance is at most P[B = 1] = ε.
   • Take target R2 , and let the prover query c and then b. Initially VS = {a}, so when
                                                                                  T

     B = 0 the query c is escalated. The history h = ((c, ACCEPT, ∗)) therefore has probability
     exactly ε. It carries no label, so it is consistent with every hypothesis, and VSh = H with
       VSh = {a} ̸∋ b.
     T

   • Given h (that is, B = 1), b is accepted with probability 1. The joint probability is ε ≤ δ = ε,
     as (b) says.
A simulation reproduces this (T1-code/thm31b_counterexample.py).
    Random human data. Suppose instead that P0 is random, with a countable range and a
target-dependent law, and independent of ω. Let d be an observed value with d ⊆ R′ , where R′ is


                                                   179
as above with VSh computed from P0 = d. The coupling, applied conditionally on P0 = d, gives

          p(d) := PR∗ [h, then q accepted | P0 = d] = PR′ [h, then q accepted | P0 = d].

Hence PR′ [P0 = d] · p(d) ≤ δ, which is the weaker bound stated in the main text.

C.2    The escalation dimension
Proof of Theorem 4.4. Upper bound. Let V be the VS verifier, and let R∗ ∈ H with P0 ⊆ R∗ .
Consider any honest prover, which may be adaptive.
 (1) V never rejects. By Theorem 4.2(a), R∗ ∈ VS, so every query q ∈ R∗ lies in
                                                                                       S
                                                                                           VS.
 (2) Every label is 1. So P − = ∅ throughout, and P = P0 ∪ E, where E is the set of queries
     escalated so far.
 (3) Let s1 , s2 , . . . be the escalated queries in order. When si is escalated, si ∈         VS(P0 ∪
                                                                                           T
                                                                                     /
     {s1 , . . . , si−1 }), and si ∈ R∗ .
So the escalated queries form an elastic chain in R∗ , and since nothing is rejected, the cost equals
their number, which is at most el(H, R∗ | P0 ). As V is 0-sound, Esc(H | P0 ) ≤ supR el(H, R | P0 ).
     Lower bound. Let V be δ-sound. Let s1 , . . . , sm be an elastic chain in some R ∈ H with
P0 ⊆ R, with witnesses Ri . The prover queries s1 , . . . , sm in this order, which is honest for
target R. Fix i. For j < i we have sj ∈ R and sj ∈ Ri , so the oracle’s label on sj , if requested,
is 1 under both targets. Induction on rounds, as in Lemma C.1, gives τi−1      R (ω) = τ Ri (ω) for

     Size restriction. The same argument applies verbatim when all queries and chain elements
