    The original proof of the lower bound cited the conditional reading of Theorem 4.2(b). The
direct coupling above replaces it (referee report A-4). The cost of a randomized verifier is its
expected cost (Definition 2.21).

Proof of Lemma 4.6. (1) Two schemas. Let σ, τ be schemas, renamed apart. A ground term t
lies in inst(σ) ∩ inst(τ ) iff t = σθ = τ θ′ for ground substitutions θ, θ′ . Since the variables are
disjoint, θ ∪ θ′ then unifies σ and τ . If σ and τ are not unifiable, the intersection is empty.
Otherwise let η be a most general unifier. Every unifier factors through η, so t = σηρ for
some ρ, and t ∈ inst(ση). Conversely, every ground instance σηρ = τ ηρ lies in both sets. So
inst(σ) ∩ inst(τ ) = inst(ση) ∈ H1 .
    (2) Arbitrary families. Let F ⊆ H1 . The empty family has intersection S = inst(x) ∈ H1 .
If F = ∅, it lies in H1 . Otherwise F contains a ground term t, and every member of F
   T                                      T

is inst(σ) with σθ = t for some θ. Then |σ| ≤ |t|, and every function or constant symbol of σ
occurs in t. So, up to renaming, there are finitely many such σ, and F has finitely many distinct
members. Its intersection is a finite intersection, which lies in H1 by (1).
    (3) Lggs of arbitrary nonempty sets. Let P ̸= ∅. For finite F ⊆ F ′ ⊆ P we have lggF ⪯ lggF ′ ,
since F ⊆ F ′ ⊆ inst(lggF ′ ). By the rank lemma, a strictly increasing chain of lggs has length
at most µ(lgg{t}) = |t| for any t ∈ P . So there is a finite F0 ⊆ P such that lggF ≡ lggF0
for every finite F with F0 ⊆ F ⊆ P . Put g := lggF0 . For each s ∈ P , lgg(F0 ∪ {s}) ≡ g,
so s ∈ inst(g). If P ⊆ inst(τ ), then F0 ⊆ inst(τ ), so g ⪯ τ . Thus g is the lgg of P , and
inst(g) = {inst(τ ) : P ⊆ inst(τ )}.
           T

    (4) The closure. For nonempty P , the members of H1 that contain P are the sets inst(τ ) with
P ⊆ inst(τ ) (the empty set does not contain P ). So cl(P ) = inst(lggP ) by (3), or by Theorem 2.5
when P is finite.

C.4     One schema
Proof of Theorem 4.7. (i) Let s1 , . . . , sm be an elastic chain in inst(σ ∗ ) relative to a nonempty
P0 ⊆ inst(σ ∗ ). Put gi := lgg(P0 ∪ {s1 , . . . , si }) for 0 ≤ i ≤ m. By Lemma 4.6, VS(Q) =
                                                                                                    T

inst(lggQ) for nonempty Q. So the elasticity condition says si ∈             / inst(gi−1 ), while si ∈ inst(gi ).
Now P0 ∪ {s1 , . . . , si−1 } ⊆ inst(gi ), so gi−1 ⪯ gi by the lgg property, and the instance sets differ,
so gi−1 ≺ gi strictly. This uses the lgg property and no assumption on the signature; mere
inclusion of instance sets would not give generality over a poor signature (referee report A-6).
Also gm ⪯ σ ∗ , since P0 ∪ {s1 , . . . , sm } ⊆ inst(σ ∗ ). By the rank lemma µ(gi−1 ) ≥ µ(gi ) + 1 for
each i, and µ(gm ) ≥ µ(σ ∗ ). Summing, µ(lggP0 ) = µ(g0 ) ≥ µ(σ ∗ ) + m.
      (ii) Let s1 , . . . , sm , m ≥ 1, be an elastic chain in inst(σ ∗ ) relative to ∅, with |si | ≤ N . Then
s2 , . . . , sm is an elastic chain relative to {s1 }, because the version spaces involved are the same.
By (i), m−1 ≤ µ(s1 )−µ(σ ∗ ). Since s1 is ground, µ(s1 ) = |s1 | ≤ N . So m ≤ 1+N −µ(σ ∗ ) ≤ N +1,
as µ ≥ 0. Taking the supremum over targets and applying Theorem 4.4 with the size restriction
gives Esc(H1 ; N ) ≤ N + 1.
      (iii) Let N ≥ 1, and consider the steps

                     t0 = g N −1 (a),   t1 = g N −1 (b),    tj = g N −j (a) (2 ≤ j ≤ N ),

all of size at most N and all in inst(x). Let Lj = lgg(t0 , . . . , tj ). Then L0 = t0 . For j ≥ 1, every
ti with i ≤ j has the form g N −j (ui ). The ui have mixed roots: they are a, b when j = 1, and
include u0 = g j−1 (a) and uj = a when j ≥ 2. So anti-unification copies the prefix g N −j and
places a metavariable at the bottom, giving Lj ≡ g N −j (z) and µ(Lj ) = N − j. Each tj escapes:

                                                      181

        / inst(L0 ) = {g N −1 (a)}.
   • t1 ∈

                                / inst(g N −j+1 (z)), whose instances begin with N − j + 1 copies
     of g.
By Lemma 4.6, t0 , . . . , tN is therefore an elastic chain of length N + 1 in inst(x). Together
with (ii) and µ(x) = 0, el(H1 , x | ∅; N ) = N + 1, and Esc(H1 ; N ) = N + 1 by Theorem 4.4.

    Computation. Exhaustive search over all ground terms of size at most N (T1-code/elast.py)
gives a maximal elasticity of 4, 5 at N = 3, 4 over {a, b, g, p}, which is N + 1. With a single
constant, over {c, g, p} at N = 3, 4, 5, it gives 3, 4, 5 = N . We re-ran both for this paper.

C.5    Two-sided checking
Proof of Proposition 4.8. Let the signature have constants a, b1 , . . . , bn and an n-ary symbol f .
Take the target R∗ = inst(f (a, . . . , a)) = {f (a, . . . , a)} and P0 = R∗ . For a partition π of [n]
with blocks indexed 1, . . . , |π|, let π(j) be the index of the block containing j, and put

                         sπ := f (bπ(1) , . . . , bπ(n) ),         σπ := f (xπ(1) , . . . , xπ(n) ).

Three facts:
 (1) f (a, . . . , a) ∈ inst(σπ ).
 (2) sπ′ ∈ inst(σπ ) iff π refines π ′ . Indeed, sπ′ is an instance iff the assignment xπ(j) 7→ bπ′ (j) is
     well defined, i.e. iff π(j) = π(k) implies π ′ (j) = π ′ (k).
 (3) Each sπ has size n + 1 and is invalid.
List the Bell(n) partitions as π1 , π2 , . . . along a linear extension of refinement with the finest
first, so that if πu properly refines πv then u < v. The prover queries sπ1 , sπ2 , . . . in this order.
     Let V be two-sided, and consider the query sπv . Every label received so far is negative, except
for the positives in P0 , and it concerns some sπu with u < v. Such a step is not an instance of
σπv : by (2) that would require πv to refine πu , which for u < v and πu ̸= πv contradicts the
order. With (1), inst(σπv ) is in the current version space and contains sπv . So sπv ∈ VS, and
                                                                                              S

V cannot reject it. V is 0-sound and sπv is invalid, so V cannot accept it. Hence V escalates
every one of the Bell(n) queries.
     Fixed signature. The two-sided VS verifier escalates q only if q ∈ VS \ VS. Once escalated,
                                                                         S      T

q is labelled and joins P or P − . From then on q ∈ VS (accepted) or q ∈
                                                        T                       S
                                                                              / VS (rejected), so no
step is escalated twice. A ground term of size n is a string of n symbols in Polish notation, so
over a finite signature Ω there are at most n≤N |Ω|n ≤ (|Ω| + 1)N steps of size at most N .
                                                 P


   The proposition originally claimed super-exponential cost without qualification. That
needs a signature that grows with N , and the fixed-signature bound was added (referee
report A-7). Computation. A brute-force search over all depth-one schemas f (t1 , . . . , tn )
with ti ∈ {a, bj , metavariables}, together with x, queried in order of decreasing number of
blocks, gives 2, 5 and 15 forced escalations for n = 2, 3, 4. These are Bell(2), Bell(3), Bell(4)
(T1-code/checks37.py, re-run).

C.7     Untagged rules
Proof of Theorem 4.10(i). Assume N ≥ k + 1, and let n + 1 := ⌊(N − 1)/k⌋ ≥ 1. For a ∈
{0, . . . , n}k put sa := p(g a1 c, . . . , g ak c), which has size 1 + j (aj + 1) ≤ 1 + k(n + 1) ≤ N .
                                                                       P

                                                                                                 P

an earlier one. Then a′ ≥ a, so a′j > aj for some j; otherwise a′ ≤ a componentwise, and
                         P        P

with a ≥ a this would give a′ = a. Put

                    Ra′ :=

Every earlier sa′ lies in the j-th schema for a j with a′j ≥ aj + 1. The current sa lies in none, since
its j-th argument has only aj copies of g. So Ra′ witnesses that sa escapes, and the sequence is an
elastic chain of length (n+1)k relative to P0 = ∅. By Theorem 4.4, Esc(Hk ; N ) ≥ ⌊(N −1)/k⌋k .

Proof of Theorem 4.10(ii). Let k ≥ 2, and let s1 , . . . , sm be an elastic chain relative to ∅, with
|si | ≤ N and witnesses Ri = l≤k inst(τi,l ). Unions of fewer than k schemas are padded by
                              S


                                                           183
repetition (only the witness of s1 can be empty, and it plays no role, since π1 partitions ∅). Let
πi be the partition of [i − 1] that assigns j to block l when l is the least index with sj ∈ inst(τi,l ).
It has at most k blocks.
    Chains of indices. Call i0 < i1 < · · · < ir a chain if, for each b ≥ 1, the indices i0 , . . . , ib−1
all lie in one block of πib , say block l. Then τib ,l covers si0 , . . . , sib−1 , and it misses sib because
sib ∈
    / Rib . So sib ∈
                   / instlgg(si0 , . . . , sib−1 ) ⊆ inst(τib ,l ), and hence

                            lgg(si0 ) ≺ lgg(si0 , si1 ) ≺ · · · ≺ lgg(si0 , . . . , sir )

strictly. The first term is ground, with µ ≤ N . By the rank lemma, µ drops by at least one at
each step and stays nonnegative. So r + 1 ≤ N + 1: every chain has length at most N + 1. This
is the argument of Theorem 4.7, and it needs no common target schema.
    The recursion. Let g(d) be the largest m for which there is a sequence π1 , . . . , πm , with
πi a partition of [i − 1] into at most k blocks, all of whose chains have length at most d. A
two-element index set {i0 < i1 } is always a chain, so g(1) = 1. For d ≥ 2, split [m − 1] into the
blocks of πm . Fix a block B, and restrict each πj with j ∈ B to B ∩ [j − 1]. This gives a sequence
of the same kind indexed by B. Its chains are chains of the original sequence, and appending m
to any of them gives a chain again, because B is one block of πm . So its chains have length
at most d − 1, and |B| ≤ g(d − 1). Hence m − 1 ≤ k g(d − 1). So g(d) ≤ 1 + k g(d − 1), and
g(d) ≤ u<d k u = (k d − 1)/(k − 1). With d = N + 1, m ≤ (k N +1 − 1)/(k − 1), and Theorem 4.4
        P

gives the bound on Esc(Hk ; N ).

Proof of Theorem 4.10(iii). Identify a, b with 0, 1, so that steps are points x ∈ {0, 1}n . A linear
flat schema denotes a subcube, which fixes some coordinates and leaves the others free. For
points y, p and a set J of coordinates, say y agrees with p on J if yj = pj for all j ∈ J. The pair
(J, p|J ) with |J| = k is a pattern, and a set P of points realizes it if some y ∈ P agrees with p
on J.
     Escape criterion. Let P be the set of earlier chain elements. A subcube Q with p ∈         / Q fixes
some coordinate j to 1 − pj , since otherwise p ∈ Q. So Q lies in the half-cube {xj = 1 − pj },
which is itself a subcube avoiding p. Hence p escapes iff some union of at most k half-cubes
{xj ≠ pj }, j ∈ J, covers P . That holds iff no y ∈ P agrees with p on J. Padding J to size k
(possible since n ≥ k) preserves this.   So p escapes iff some pattern of p is not realized     by P .
     Upper bound. There are 2k nk patterns. The first chain element realizes nk of them. Each
                                                                                        

later element escapes, so it realizes at least one pattern not realized     before, and realized patterns
                                                           k        n
stay realized. So the chain has length at most 1 + (2 − 1) k .
     Lower bound. The target is the full cube f (x1 , . . . , xn ). Query all points of weight at most k
in order of non-decreasing weight. Let x have weight w ≤ k, and choose J ⊇ supp(x) with
|J| = k. An earlier point y that agrees with x on J has yj = 1 on supp(x), so supp(y) ⊇ supp(x).
Its weight is at most w, so y = x, which is impossible      for an earlier, distinct point. So every such
                                            Pk     n
point escapes, and the chain has length w=0 w .
     Repeated metavariables. A flat schema with repeated metavariables denotes the points that
satisfy its constants and the equalities xi = xj for positions sharing a metavariable. If such a
schema avoids p, then either a constant position j has pj ̸= the constant, so the schema lies in
{xj ̸= pj }, or all constants agree with p and some pair i, j sharing a metavariable has pi ̸= pj ,
so the schema lies in {xi = xj }. Each such set is determined by at most two coordinates of p,
and each of its points disagrees with p on one of them. If p escapes, there is therefore a set J
of at most 2k coordinates on which no earlier point agrees with p. Padding J to size 2k               (for
                                                                                         2k      n
n ≥ 2k) and counting patterns as above gives a chain length of at most 1 + (2 − 1) 2k . The

                                                        184
class contains the linear one, and elastic chains stay elastic in a larger class, so the lower bound
persists.

    The original summary said “Θ(nk ) for flat schemas”. That is proved only for linear flat schemas,
and it needs n ≥ k (referee report A-9). Computation. The exact values quoted in Section 4.3
come from T1-code/elast.py (deep schemas), subcube.py (subcubes) and abstr2.py (the
partition abstraction), and were re-run for this paper. Separately, explicit elastic sequences of
length ⌊(N − 1)/k⌋k were checked for (k, n) ∈ {(2, 1), (2, 2), (2, 3), (3, 1), (3, 2)} (checks37.py).

C.8     The binomial conjecture: the case k = 2, h = 3, and the evidence
Proposition C.2 (Conjecture 4.11 for (k, h) = (2, 3)). [T1 Conj 3.8, case proved during verification] Let
C be a family of subsets of U , closed under arbitrary intersections, with U ∈ C and of height 3.
Then every elastic sequence for the class of unions of one or two members of C, relative to ∅, has
length at most 6 = 42 .
                      


Proof. Reduction. Let C0 = C. Its points lie in every member, so they never escape and
                                   T

never occur in an elastic sequence. Removing C0 from U and from every member preserves
intersection-closure, height and elastic sequences. So we may assume C0 = ∅.
    Ranks. Let the rank r(X) of a member be the length of the longest strict chain of members
from ∅ to X. If X ⊊ Y are members, then r(X) < r(Y ). Since r(U ) = 3, every member X ̸= U
has r(X) ≤ 2. The only member of rank 0 is ∅. Members of rank 1 are atoms and members of
rank 2 are planes.
    Fact 1. If X, Y are members with X ̸⊆ Y , then X ∩ Y is a member with r(X ∩ Y ) < r(X). So
the intersection of a plane with a member not containing it is ∅ or an atom, and the intersection
of an atom with a member not containing it is ∅.
    Let s1 , . . . , sm be elastic, with witnesses Yi1 ∪ Yi2 (taking Yi1 = Yi2 for a single member) that
cover s1 , . . . , si−1 and miss si .
    Claim A: each atom contains at most one si . Suppose si , sj ∈ A with i < j. Then si ∈ Yjl
for some l, and sj ∈     / Yjl , so A ̸⊆ Yjl . By Fact 1, A ∩ Yjl = ∅, contradicting si ∈ A ∩ Yjl .
    Claim B: each plane contains at most three si . Let X be a plane and sj the last sequence
element in X. The earlier elements of X are covered by Yj1 ∪ Yj2 . Since sj ∈        / Yjl , X ̸⊆ Yjl , so each
X ∩ Yjl is ∅ or an atom. By Claim A, each contains at most one sequence element. So X has at
most two elements before sj .
    By Claims A and B, every member other than U contains at most three sequence elements.
    The contradiction. Suppose m ≥ 7. The members Y71 , Y72 miss s7 , so they differ from U , and
together they cover s1 , . . . , s6 . Each covers at most three, so each covers exactly three, disjointly.
Atoms cover at most one, so both are planes; call them X and Y . Moreover, X contains no
element covered only by Y , since it would then contain four sequence elements, contrary to
Claim B; and likewise for Y . Say s6 ∈ Y . The witness Z1 ∪ Z2 of s6 misses s6 and covers the
three X-elements, which all lie among s1 , . . . , s5 .
    Let Z be a member with s6 ∈         / Z. If Z ̸⊇ X, then Z ∩ X is ∅ or an atom, so Z covers at most
one X-element. If Z ⊋ X, then r(Z) ≥ 3, so Z = U ∋ s6 , which is impossible. Hence a member
that covers two or more X-elements equals X. One of Z1 , Z2 covers at least two X-elements;
say Z1 = X. Then X contains neither of the two Y -elements among s1 , . . . , s5 , so Z2 must cover
both. But s6 ∈ Y \ Z2 , so Z2 ∩ Y is ∅ or an atom, which covers at most one of them by Claim A.
This is a contradiction, so m ≤ 6.


                                                     185
   This argument was sketched by a referee and written out in full by the author of T1 (referee
report A-5). The same verification pass made three corrections to the evidence:
   • An earlier claim that “a natural one-step decomposition proof fails on some extremal
     sequences” had no surviving script. It is withdrawn and plays no role here.
   • The run with height h ≤ 5 used a universe of 14 points. The elasticity cannot exceed the
     number of points, so “14 versus the conjectured 15” is a ceiling artifact, not evidence.
   • The graphic-matroid      data were added. The ground set of M (Kh+1 ) is the edge set of Kh+1 ,
            h+1
     with 2 points. Its flats are the edge sets that are unions of complete graphs on the
     blocks of a partition of the vertices. Flats are closed under intersection, and the rank of
     the matroid is h, so the family has height h. T1-code/graphic.py computes the 2-union
     elasticity as 3, 6, 10, 15 = h+1
                                    2   for h = 2, 3, 4, 5, so every edge can be placed in an elastic
     sequence. For k = 3 it computes 6 and 10 for K4 and K5 , which is the number of edges.
     We re-ran these computations for this paper.

C.9    Time-uniform soundness
Lemma C.3 (Ville’s inequality (Ville, 1939)). If (Mt )t≥0 is a nonnegative supermartingale with
EM0 ≤ 1, then P[∃t : Mt ≥ 1/δ ′ ] ≤ δ ′ for every δ ′ ∈ (0, 1].
Proof of Theorem 4.15. Observations and likelihoods. In round u there is at most one observa-
tion Ou : a human datum X ∈ S, an oracle answer y ∈ {0, 1} to an escalated query qu , or nothing.
For each R let λR (Ou ) be pR (X), ℓR (y | qu ) or 1 accordingly, and let Lt (R) = u≤t λR (Ou ).
                                                                                  Q

of round u + 1. That type is Fu -measurable: Vδ is deterministic, and qu+1 and the schedule
of human data are Fu -measurable. In particular λR∗ (Ou+1 ) > 0 almost surely, so Lt (R∗ ) > 0
almost surely.
    (a) Supermartingale. Observation spaces are countable. For each R,

                                                     186
Since the Xj are i.i.d. pR∗ , the computation of (a), in the filtration Gn = σ(X1 , . . . , Xn ), shows
that Z H is a nonnegative supermartingale with Z0H = 1. Let G = {supn ZnH < 1/δ ′ }. It depends
only on the human data, and P(G) ≥ 1 − δ ′ by Lemma C.3. On G, for every prover strategy and
every arrival schedule, Zt < 1/δ ′ for all t, and the argument of (a) shows that no invalid step is
ever accepted. The prover may know R∗ , the verifier and all of X1 , X2 , . . . in advance. It can
choose only the timing of the data, and the bound holds for every timing.

    The hypothesis of (a) was implicit in the original statement. The referees showed that it is
needed, and it was made explicit (referee report B-2).
    A prover that foresees the noise. Let H = 2{0,1} with the uniform prior, so w∗ = 1/4, and
let R∗ = {0}. There are no human data. The oracle flips the true label with probability η,
independently at each query, so ℓR (y | q) = 1 − η if y = 1[q ∈ R] and η otherwise. Set δ ′ = 0.05
and δ = w∗ δ ′ . The prover knows in advance whether the next answer will be flipped. If it will
be, the prover queries the invalid step 1, and the answer reads “valid”. If not, it queries the
valid step 0, and the answer again reads “valid”. So every answer is “valid”, and each answer
to a query of 1 multiplies the likelihood ratio of {0, 1} to {0} by (1 − η)/η > 1. No answer
ever lowers that ratio. Flips occur infinitely often, so the posterior mass of the hypotheses
that exclude 1 eventually falls below δ, and the next query of 1 is accepted. The simulation
T1-code/prescient.py gives acceptance probability 1.0 at η = 0.1 and at η = 0.3, against the
bound 0.05. This prover violates the hypothesis of (a), because Ft would have to contain the
future noise. In (b) no such prover exists, because there is no noise to foresee.

D      Proofs for Section 5
Lemma 5.2, Theorem 5.4, Theorem 5.9(a), Corollary 5.11, Proposition 5.13, Theorem 5.17 and
Corollary 5.20 are proved in full in the main text. This appendix proves the remaining results
of Section 5. The proofs follow T1 §§5–6 in the form repaired after verification; the repairs are
noted where they occur. The last subsection is a sketch and is labelled as such.
    Notation is as in Sections 2 and 5. Steps are ground terms over a signature Ω with at least
two constants a = ̸ b. For a schema σ, vars(σ) is its set of metavariables and v = |vars(σ)|. A
substitution θ for σ maps vars(σ) to ground terms. Anti-unification is the map A of Section 2.2,
and lgg is computed by it (Theorem 2.5).

                                                  188
D.1     Three elementary facts
Lemma D.1. (F1) If τ = σθ and θ restricted to vars(σ) is not a renaming, then inst(τ ) ⊊ inst(σ).
(F2) The intersection of the instance sets of all schemas is empty. (F3) For tagged calculi Hktag
and clean data D, VS(D) = i≤k {i} × R̂i , where R̂i = inst(lgg(D(i) )) if D(i) ̸= ∅ and R̂i = ∅
                   T           S

otherwise.

Proof. (F1) Inclusion holds because instances of τ are instances of σ. For strictness there are two
cases. First, suppose some θx is not a metavariable, say with root g. Choose a constant c ̸= g; it
exists because there are two constants. Let t = σθ′ with θ′ x = c and θ′ ground elsewhere. At a
position of σ where x occurs, t carries c, while every instance of τ carries a term with root g. So
t∈/ inst(τ ). Second, suppose every θx is a metavariable. Then θ is not injective on vars(σ), say
θx = θy with x ̸= y. Let θ′ x = a and θ′ y = b. Every instance of τ has equal subterms at the
positions of x and y, and σθ′ does not. So σθ′ ∈  / inst(τ ).
    (F2) The ground schemas a and b have disjoint instance sets {a} and {b}.
    (F3) Write I for the family of single-schema instance sets. Then Hktag = { i {i}×Ai : Ai ∈ I},
                                                                               S

and D ⊆ i {i} × Ai iff D(i) ⊆ Ai for every i. So VS(D) is the set of all such unions with
                S

(A1 , . . . , Ak ) ∈ i VSi , where VSi := {A ∈ I : D(i) ⊆ A}. Each VSi is nonempty, since it
                    Q

contains inst(σi ). Hence (i, s) ∈ VS(D) iff s ∈ Ai for every choice in the product, iff s ∈ VSi .
                                   T                                                         T

Finally, VSi = inst(lgg(D(i) )) for nonempty D(i) by Theorem 2.5, and VSi = ∅ for empty
              T                                                               T

D(i) by (F2).

   (F3) is the positive-data case of the factorization used for T1 Thm 3.6. The verification noted
that Hktag is not literally intersection-closed, and the factorization is what the argument actually
uses.

D.2     Proofs of Propositions 5.5 and 5.6
Proof of Proposition 5.5. (a) If T is an anchor and T ⊆ R ∈ H, then R ⊇ R∗ , so R ̸⊊ R∗ .
     (b) Let T ̸= ∅ be a tell-tale, and suppose T ⊆ R ∈ H with R ̸⊇ R∗ . Then R ∩ R∗ ⊇ T is
nonempty, so it is a member of H by intersection-closure of H ∪ {∅}. And T ⊆ R ∩ R∗ ⊊ R∗ ,
which contradicts the tell-tale property. For H = {{1}, {2}} and R∗ = {1}: no member lies
strictly inside {1}, so ∅ is a tell-tale; {2} ⊇ ∅ does not contain R∗ , so ∅ is not an anchor. Here
H ∪ {∅} is intersection-closed. For the existence claim, if T is a tell-tale and r ∈ R∗ , then T ∪ {r}
is a nonempty tell-tale (fewer members contain it), hence an anchor; anchors are tell-tales by (a).
    (c) The class is an indexed family of uniformly recursive sets. {0} is a tell-tale for 2N, since
every Rn contains the odd number 2n + 1 and so no member lies strictly inside 2N. Rn is a
tell-tale for itself. By Angluin’s theorem (Angluin, 1980) the class is identifiable in the limit from
text. Every finite T ⊆ 2N lies in Rn for any n with 2n ≥ max T , and Rn ̸⊇ 2N. So 2N has no
anchor, and by Theorem 5.4(b) the cautious verifier is never complete on a text for 2N. The
same example shows that “finite” is needed in Theorem 5.4(a): every member containing 2N is
2N, so R̂(2N) = 2N.

Proof of Proposition 5.6. If R∗ = ∅, then ∅ is an anchor. Otherwise suppose R∗ has no anchor,
and pick w0 ∈ R∗ . Given w0 , . . . , wn−1 ∈ R∗ , the finite set {w0 , . . . , wn−1 } ⊆ R∗ is not an anchor.
So some Rn ∈ H contains it with Rn ̸⊇ R∗ ; pick wn ∈ R∗ \ Rn . Then {w0 , . . . , wn−1 } ⊆ Rn ̸∋ wn
for all n ≥ 1, which is infinite elasticity.



                                                   189
    This is the lemma of L1 §2.3 in a direct form. The facts used after the proposition in the
main text are standard: finite thickness implies finite elasticity, and the union theorem of Wright
(1989) with the corrected definition of Motoki et al. (1991).

ρ > 0, some ground term t with root ̸= f is in the support. The instance of σi with x 7→ t lies in
inst(σi ) but not in R̂i . So P[R̂(D) ̸= R∗ ] ≥ (1 − πi ρ)N .

D.4     Proof of the claims in Remark 5.8
Teaching sets. Let θ1 , θ2 map the vi metavariables of σi to 2vi pairwise distinct constants. For
each x, θ1 x ̸= θ2 x are distinct constants, so their roots differ and (R) holds. For x ̸= y, θ1 x ̸= θ1 y,


                                                    190
so (D) holds. By Lemma 2.7, lgg(σi θ1 , σi θ2 ) ≡ σi . In a tagged class this set (one per rule) is an
anchor, by (F3).
    No distribution-free completeness rate (the example is from L1 §9). Let g be binary, h ≥ 1,
m = 2h , and let uj (1 ≤ j ≤ m) be the complete g-tree of depth h with leaf j equal to a and
all other leaves equal to b. Let J ⊆ [m] with |J| ≥ 2, and anti-unify {uj : j ∈ J}. All internal
positions carry g and are copied. At leaf ℓ ∈ J the column contains a (from uℓ ) and b (from any
other uj , j ∈ J), so it becomes a metavariable zℓ . Columns at distinct leaves ℓ, ℓ′ ∈ J differ (at
index ℓ one has a, the other b), so zℓ ̸= zℓ′ . At leaves ℓ ∈/ J the column is constantly b. Hence
the lgg has distinct metavariables exactly at the leaves in J and b elsewhere. Its instances among
the u’s are exactly {uj : j ∈ J}, because uk with k ∈   / J has a at a leaf where the lgg has b. For
|J| = 1 the lgg is uj itself.
    Take the target inst(x) ∈ H1 and the uniform law Q on {u1 , . . . , um }. A sample of n < m/2
draws hits a set J of at most n indices, and by the above R̂(D) ∩ {u1 , . . . , um } = {uj : j ∈ J}.
So Q(R∗ \ R̂(D)) ≥ (m − n)/m > 1/2 for every such sample. Any learner whose acceptance set is
sound for every target in H1 compatible with its data accepts a subset of R̂(D) (Lemma 5.2(b)),
so its completeness error is at least as large. Since h is arbitrary, no bound on the number of
samples that is independent of Q can guarantee completeness error at most 1/2.

D.5     Proof of Theorem 5.9(b)
Fix a rule i with vi ≥ 1. Write M := vi + v2i and M ′ := vi2 + 1 ≥ M . Consider the range space
                                                    

on substitutions (a countable set) whose ranges are
                                      
                                Ci := F1 ∪ · · · ∪ Fk : F1 , . . . , Fk ∈ Fi .

     Step 1: traces of Fi . Let θ1 , . . . , θn be substitutions. For fixed x, the sets {θ : root(θx) = f }
for different f are pairwise disjoint. So their traces on {θ1 , . . . , θn } are pairwise disjoint subsets, of
which at most n are nonempty, giving at most n + 1 distinct traces. Each pair x ̸= y contributes
one set, hence one trace. So Fi has at most vi (n + 1) + v2i ≤ M (n + 1) distinct traces on n
points.
     Step 2: traces of Ci . The trace of F1 ∪ · · · ∪ Fk is determined by the traces of F1 , . . . , Fk . So
Ci has at most (M (n + 1))k ≤ (M ′ (n + 1))k traces on n points.
     Step 3: VC dimension. If Ci shatters d points, then 2d ≤ (M ′ (d + 1))k , i.e. ϕ(d) :=
d − k log2 (M ′ (d + 1)) ≤ 0. Let L := log2 (4kM ′ ) ≥ 2 and di = 2kL. Then ϕ(di ) > 0 iff
(4kM ′ )2 > M ′ (2kL + 1), iff 16k 2 M ′ > 2kL + 1. This holds because L < 4kM ′ gives 2kL + 1 <
8k 2 M ′ + 1 ≤ 16k 2 M ′ . Moreover ϕ′ (d) = 1 − k/((d + 1) ln 2) > 0 for d ≥ di ≥ 4k. So ϕ(d) > 0 for
all d ≥ di , and the VC dimension of Ci is less than di .
     Step 4: the ε-net theorem (Haussler and Welzl, 1987; Blumer et al., 1989). Let a range space
have VC dimension at most d, let µ be a probability measure, and let 0 < ε, δ ′ < 1. If
                                              n4           2 8d       13 o
                                   m ≥ max          log2     ,  log 2     ,
                                                ε          δ′ ε        ε
then with probability at least 1 − δ ′ an i.i.d. µ-sample of size m meets every range of µ-measure
at least ε. We use the theorem in this “measure ≥ ε” form. The symmetrization proof gives it
with the same constants. Alternatively, apply the standard “measure > ε” form with ε = ζi /2,
at the cost of a constant factor. (This boundary point was noted in verification.)
    Step 5: conclusion. Apply Step 4 with µ = Λi , ε = ζi , d = di and δ ′ = δ/k ′ . The sum in the
statement exceeds the maximum. By definition of ζi , every range in Ci has Λi -measure at least

                                                        191
ζi . So with probability at least 1 − δ/k ′ the ni rule-i substitutions meet every range in Ci , i.e.
they are not contained in any union of k failure sets. They are a subset of Subi (D), so neither is
Subi (D). For a ground rule, one sample makes Subi (D) nonempty, and Fi = ∅. A union bound
over the k ′ rules and part (a) finish the proof.

D.6     Proof of Proposition 5.10
This proposition replaces T1’s original remark after Thm 5.4, which claimed that at most k roots
for a metavariable makes identification fail forever. Verification refuted that claim for k ′ ≥ 2,
and part (b) is the counterexample.
    Failure sets and specializations. For a metavariable x of σi and f ∈ Ω of arity r, let
z̄ = z1 , . . . , zr be fresh distinct metavariables. Then inst(σi [x 7→ f (z̄)]) = {σi θ : root(θx) = f }
and inst(σi [y 7→ x]) = {σi θ : θx = θy}. So covering substitutions by failure sets is the same as
covering the corresponding instances by these specializations.
    (a) Let σ1′ , . . . , σk−k
                           ′
                               ′ +1 be the specializations in the hypothesis, and let



                                                                        inst(σl′ ).
                                       [                       [
                                R :=          inst(σj ) ∪
                                       j̸=i                 l≤k−k′ +1

This is a union of at most k schema instance sets, so R ∈ Hk . Every datum lies in R∗ . A datum
outside inst(σi ) lies in some inst(σj ) with j ̸= i. A datum in inst(σi ) lies in another rule or,
by hypothesis, in some inst(σl′ ). So D ⊆ R, R ∈ VS(D) and R̂Hk (D) ⊆ R. The instance of σi
assumed to lie outside R is in R∗ \ R̂Hk (D).
    The case k ′ = 1. If x is instantiated in the data with roots among f1 , . . . , fk only, the k
specializations σ1 [x 7→ fj (z̄)] cover the data. Any instance whose x has another root lies outside
them. So identification fails for as long as this persists.
    (b) Let R = inst(τ1 ) ∪ inst(τ2 ) ∈ VSH2 (D), with D = {p(a), p(b), q(a), q(b)}; possibly τ1 = τ2 .
If some τl covers a p-term and a q-term, then τl ⪰ lgg(p(·), q(·)), which is a metavariable because
the roots differ. So τl is a metavariable and R is everything. Otherwise each τl covers only
p-terms or only q-terms of D. All four terms are covered, so one schema covers both p-terms and
the other both q-terms. Then τ1 ⪰ lgg(p(a), p(b)) = p(x) and τ2 ⪰ q(y) (up to the order of the
two schemas). In both cases R ⊇ R∗ . Since R∗ ∈ VSH2 (D), R̂H2 (D) = R∗ . If the substitution
law of x is supported on {a, b}, the two failure sets {root(θx) = a} and {root(θx) = b} have full
measure, so ζ1 = 0.

D.7     Proofs for Remark 5.12
Let Φ be finite and let the target be G∗ := inst(σ ∗ , Ψ∗ ) ̸= ∅. Write cl(P ) := inst(lgg(P ), ΨP ) for
nonempty P , the least guarded instance set containing P (Lemma 2.8).
    Anchors exist. Ascending generalization chains are finite (Lemma 2.6), so the lgg of the
(possibly infinite) set G∗ exists and equals lgg(T0 ) for some finite T0 ⊆ G∗ . For each ϕ ∈ Φ that
is not true on all of G∗ , pick tϕ ∈ G∗ with ϕ(tϕ ) = 0. Let T := T0 ∪ {tϕ }. Then lgg(T ) = lgg(G∗ ),
because lgg(T0 ) ⪯ lgg(T ) ⪯ lgg(G∗ ) = lgg(T0 ). Also ΨT is exactly the set of predicates true on
all of G∗ . Hence G∗ ⊆ inst(lgg(T ), ΨT ) = cl(T ). Conversely, cl(T ) ⊆ G∗ , since G∗ is a guarded
instance set containing T . So cl(T ) = G∗ , and every guarded instance set containing T contains
G∗ : T is an anchor.
    Chains. Let cl(P0 ) = C0 ⊊ C1 ⊊ · · · ⊊ Cm ⊆ G∗ be closures (the chain of closures of the data
plus escalated steps, for nonempty initial data P0 ⊆ G∗ ), Cj = inst(gj , Ψj ) with gj = lgg(Cj ) and

                                                      192
Ψj = ΨCj . Inclusion gives gj−1 ⪯ gj and Ψj−1 ⊇ Ψj . If both were equalities, then Cj−1 = Cj .
So each step either strictly generalizes the schema (at most µ(g0 ) − µ(σ ∗ ) times, by Lemma 2.6,
since gm ⪯ lgg(G∗ ) ⪯ σ ∗ ) or strictly shrinks the guard set (at most |Φ| times). By T1 Prop. 3.3
and Thm 3.2 (Section 4) this bounds the escalations by µ(lgg(P0 )) − µ(σ ∗ ) + |Φ|.

D.8    Proof of Theorem 5.14
(a) If D has at most e invalid steps, then |D \ R∗ | ≤ e, so R∗ ∈ VSe (D) and VSe (D) ⊆ R∗ . The
                                                                               T

per-tag case is identical. Soundness against every prover follows by Lemma 3.1, as in Lemma 5.2.
    (b) By (i), R∗ ∈ VS(ei ) (D), so VS(ei ) (D) ⊆ R∗ . Conversely, let R ∈ VS(ei ) (D) and fix i.
                                      T

Its tag-i component is inst(τ ) for a schema τ , or empty. Let Vi be the multiset of valid rule-i
samples and Vi′ := Vi ∩ inst(τ ). Then |Vi \ Vi′ | ≤ |D(i) \ R(i) | ≤ ei .
   • Vi′ ̸= ∅, because |Vi | > ei . In particular the tag-i component is not empty.
   • For each x and f , more than ei members of Vi have root(Θx) ̸= f , and at most ei were
     removed. So some member of Vi′ has root(Θx) ̸= f . Since this holds for every f , the roots
     at x in Vi′ are not all equal, and (R) holds for Vi′ .
   • Likewise, for each x ̸= y some member of Vi′ has Θx ̸= Θy, and (D) holds.
By Lemma 2.7, lgg(Vi′ ) ≡ σi . Since Vi′ ⊆ inst(τ ), τ ⪰ lgg(Vi′ ) ≡ σi , so inst(τ ) ⊇ inst(σi ). For
a ground rule, Vi′ is a nonempty multiset of copies of σi , so τ ⪰ σi directly. Hence R ⊇ R∗ for
every R ∈ VS(ei ) (D), and VS(ei ) (D) = R∗ .

D.9    Proof of Theorem 5.15
                                                                                               2
We use Hoeffding’s inequality for X ∼ Bin(N, p) and s > 0: P[X − N p ≥ sN ] ≤ e−2N s and
                          2
P[X − N p ≤ −sN ] ≤ e−2N s . Fix a rule i and write α = αi , β = βi , ρ = ρi and ∆ = ∆i , so that
ρβ = α + 2∆ and ei = ⌊(α + ∆)N ⌋.
   Noise count. The number X of invalid steps tagged i is Bin(N, α). Since ei + 1 > (α + ∆)N ,
                                                                                2
                   P[X > ei ] = P[X ≥ ei + 1] ≤ P[X − αN ≥ ∆N ] ≤ e−2N ∆ .

    Witness counts, vi ≥ 1. For each metavariable x, let Gx attain ρi,x (Appendix D.3). Let Cx+
and Cx− count the valid rule-i data with root(Θx) ∈ Gx and ∈  / Gx , respectively. For each pair
x≠ y, let Cxy count those with Θx = ̸ Θy. These are 2vi + v2i = ci counts. Each is Bin(N, p)

                                                 193
with p ≥ βρi,x ≥ βρ, resp. p = βri,xy ≥ βρ. So pN ≥ (α + 2∆)N and (α + ∆)N ≤ pN − ∆N .
Hence, for each count C,
                                                                                   2
                 P[C ≤ ei ] ≤ P[C ≤ (α + ∆)N ] ≤ P[C − pN ≤ −∆N ] ≤ e−2N ∆ .
Suppose all ci counts exceed ei . For any f , the side of the split Gx that does not contain f consists
of samples with root ̸= f . So #{root(Θx) ̸= f } ≥ min(Cx+ , Cx− ) > ei . The pair conditions hold
directly, and the number of valid rule-i samples is at least Cx+ > ei . So the valid rule-i samples
are ei -robustly generic.
    Ground rules. With ρ = 1, the single required event is that the number of valid rule-i samples,
                                                                                              2
which is Bin(N, β) with β = ρβ, exceeds ei . Its failure probability is at most e−2N ∆ by the
same computation; here ci = 1. This covers the gap noted in verification (B-17).
                                                                            2
    Conclusion. Outside an event of probability at most (1 + ci )e−2N ∆i for each i, hypotheses (i)
and (ii) of Theorem 5.14(b) hold for all rules, and the trimmed verifier accepts exactly R∗ . Take
a union bound over i.

D.10     Proof of Lemma 5.18
Let F be a probability law on the countable set Ω, m = maxf F (f ) (attained, because only finitely
many atoms exceed any positive mass), fmax a maximizer, and ρ = maxG min(F (G), 1 − F (G)).
    ρ ≤ 1 − m. For any G, the side of the split that does not contain fmax has mass at most
1 − m, so the minimum is at most 1 − m.
    1 − m ≤ 2ρ. If m ≥ 1/2, the split G = {fmax } gives min(m, 1 − m) = 1 − m, so ρ ≥ 1 − m. If
m < 1/2, enumerate Ω and add symbols to G one at a time until F (G) ≥ (1−m)/2. This happens
at a finite stage, since the partial sums tend to 1. Before the last addition F (G) < (1 − m)/2,
and the last symbol has mass at most m. So finally (1 − m)/2 ≤ F (G) < (1 + m)/2, hence
1 − F (G) > (1 − m)/2, and ρ ≥ (1 − m)/2.
    Tightness. For the uniform law on 2r + 1 symbols, m = 1/(2r + 1), and the best split has r
against r + 1 symbols, so ρ = r/(2r + 1). Then 1 − m = 2r/(2r + 1) = 2ρ.
    T1 originally stated the constant 3; verification sharpened it to 2 and found it tight (B-18;
random laws gave ratios up to 1.974).

D.11     Proof of Corollary 5.19
Suppose βi P[root(Θx) ̸= f ] ≤ α for some x, f . Let R′′ be R∗ with its tag-i component replaced
by inst(σi [x 7→ f (z̄)]), which lies in Hktag . By (F1), R′′ ⊊ R∗ . Q is a law on R∗ , and the
Q-mass of R∗ \ R′′ is the probability that a datum is a tag-i instance with root(Θx) ̸= f ,
namely βi P[root(Θx) ̸= f ] ≤ α. Apply Theorem 5.17 to R′′ ⊊ R∗ . Every q ∈ R∗ \ R′′ ̸= ∅
has PQ [∃t : q ∈ Acct ] ≤ δ. So PQ [R∗ ⊆ Acct ] ≤ δ for all t, which contradicts the hypothesis.
The pair case uses σi [y 7→ x] in the same way. All of the alternative’s “noise” sits on tag i, so
robust soundness is only needed for laws whose noise on tag i is at most α; this is the per-tag
reading.
    The factor βi in the displayed conditions was added in verification (B-18). α is a fraction of
all data, while the probabilities are under Λi .

D.12     Sketch for Remark 5.22
(proof sketch) The claims are elementary. They have not been through the project’s adversarial
verification, so we record only the argument.

                                                 194
   (i) An equation whose monomials have exponent vectors a1 , . . . , ar is g-homogeneous iff
g(aj − a1 ) = 0 for all j. So all of E is homogeneous iff g vanishes on the generators of K(E), iff it
vanishes on K(E). The projection π : Zn → Zn /K(E) is such a grading. By the homomorphism
theorem every such g factors uniquely as ḡ ◦π. An equation is homogeneous under every consistent
                                          T
grading iff its differences lie in g ker g = ker π = K(E).
   Smith normal form. If M is an integer matrix whose rows generate K(E) and U M V =
diag(d1 , . . . , dr , 0, . . . ) is its Smith normal form, with U, V unimodular, then Zn /K(E) =  ∼
                n−r . If the target group is torsion-free, g(cv) = 0 with c ̸= 0 forces g(v) = 0. So g
    j Z/dj Z ⊕ Z
L

vanishes on the saturation {v : cv ∈ K(E) for some c ̸= 0}, and the finest torsion-free grading is
(Zn /K(E))/torsion.
    (ii) Let Adm(L) be the set of equations whose differences lie in L ≤ Zn . Then Adm(L) ∩
Adm(L′ ) = Adm(L ∩ L′ ), and Adm determines L: the equation 1 = q v (Laurent monomials) is
in Adm(L) iff v ∈ L. The version space of E is {Adm(L) : K(E) ≤ L}, so R̂(E) = Adm(K(E)).
For the target L∗ = ker g ∗ , choose generators v1 , . . . , vs (subgroups of Zn are finitely generated).
The equations 1 = q vj form an anchor. More generally, a finite E ⊆ Adm(L∗ ) is an anchor
iff K(E) = L∗ : if K(E) ⊊ L∗ , then Adm(K(E)) is consistent with E and misses Adm(L∗ ).
Theorem 5.4 then gives the stated identification. Claims (iii)–(iv) of the remark are direct
readings of these facts.

E.3    Proof of Proposition 6.12
(a) For the target, Γ ⊬∗ φ iff Γ∪{¬φ} is target-coherent, by classical reductio. So the designations
eventually enumerate (codes for) every finite-premise sequent not in ⊢∗ , and the text enumerates
⊢∗ : together they form an informant. Let (hi ) enumerate H, and let the learner output the least
i such that hi contains every sequent seen in the text and is coherent on every designated context


                                                  196
seen. Let i∗ be the least index of the target and j < i∗ , so hj ̸= ⊢∗ . If ⊢∗ ̸⊆ hj , the text eventually
shows a sequent of ⊢∗ \ hj , and j is rejected from then on. Otherwise pick Γ ▷ φ ∈ hj \ ⊢∗ . Then
Γ ∪ {¬φ} is target-coherent, hence eventually designated, and it is hj -incoherent by reductio in
hj ; so j is rejected from then on. The target’s index i∗ is never rejected. As there are finitely
many j < i∗ , the output is eventually i∗ forever. If membership in the hi is uniformly decidable,
both tests are decidable (coherence of hi on A is non-membership of A ▷ ⊥), so the learner is
computable.
     (b) {pj } is hn -coherent iff {¬pi : i < n}∪{pj } is consistent iff j ≥ n, and it is hω -incoherent for
every j. The chain is strictly increasing with union hω , and all members are ∅-coherent. Consider
the computable learner that outputs hj for the least j such that {pj } has been designated, and hω
if there is none (the enumeration learner of (a) also works). On target hn , exactly the singletons
{pj } with j ≥ n can be designated, and {pn } eventually is; from then on the least designated
j is n, and the learner outputs hn . On target hω no singleton {pj } is ever designated, and the
learner outputs hω throughout.
     Repair note. This proposition was added after verification; the referee’s counterexample (b)
showed that the first version of Corollary 6.11, stated without “A fixed”, was false.

E.4    Proofs for Section 6.2: Post-completeness
Theorem 6.13, pure {→}. Let φ ∈ C(X) \ Cn2 (X) and v a Boolean valuation with v[X] = 1,
v(φ) = 0. Put σv (p) = p0 → p0 if v(p) = 1 and σv (p) = p0 otherwise. By induction on ψ,
σv ψ ≡2 p0 → p0 if v(ψ) = 1 and σv ψ ≡2 p0 if v(ψ) = 0. For atoms this is the definition.
For ψ = α → β: if v(α) = 0, then σv ψ ≡2 p0 → (·), and both p0 → (p0 → p0 ) and p0 → p0
are tautologies; if v(α) = v(β) = 1, then σv ψ ≡2 ⊤ → ⊤ ≡2 ⊤; if v(α) = 1, v(β) = 0, then
σv ψ ≡2 ⊤ → p0 ≡2 p0 . Hence σv X ⊆ Taut ⊆ C(∅) and, by structurality, σv φ ∈ C(σv X) ⊆ C(∅),
with σv φ ≡2 p0 . So p0 ∈ Cn2 ({σv φ}) ⊆ C(C(∅)) = C(∅). Substituting any ψ for p0 and using
σC(∅) ⊆ C(∅) gives C(∅) = Fm.

Proposition 6.14. Cai is a closure operator, and it is structural because σX is non-empty iff
X is. In the {∧, ∨} language Cn2 (∅) = ∅, since the all-false valuation falsifies every formula; so
Cai ⊇ Cn2 . Now let C ⊋ Cn2 be structural. Case C(∅) ̸= ∅. Pick φ ∈ C(∅) and substitute p0 for
every atom. Every one-variable lattice term is ≡2 p0 , so p0 ∈ Cn2 ({σφ}) ⊆ C(C(∅)) = C(∅), and
by structurality C(∅) = Fm: C = CFm . Case C(∅) = ∅. Take φ ∈ C(X) \ Cn2 (X) (so X ̸= ∅)
and a Boolean v with v[X] = 1, v(φ) = 0. Let σ send the atoms true under v to q and the false
ones to r (distinct atoms). Each σψ is a lattice term in q, r, and its value at (q, r) = (1, 0) is
v(ψ). Up to ≡2 , the lattice terms in q, r are q, r, q ∧ r and q ∨ r. So σψ ∈ {q, q ∨ r} up to ≡2
for ψ ∈ X, and σφ ∈ {r, q ∧ r}. Hence σX ⊆ Cn2 ({q}) and r ∈ Cn2 ({σφ}), so
          r ∈ C({σφ}) ⊆ C(C(σX)) = C(σX) ⊆ C(Cn2 ({q})) ⊆ C(C({q})) = C({q}).
Substituting χ for q and ψ for r gives ψ ∈ C({χ}) for all χ, ψ, so C(Y ) = Fm for every non-empty
Y : C = Cai .

Theorem 6.15. (a) Suppose ⟨D⟩ = Cn2 . Every h ∈ S(D, A0 ) is structural and contains D,
hence contains ⟨D⟩ = Cn2 ; by Theorem 6.13, h ∈ {Cn2 , CFm }, and A0 excludes CFm . Conversely,
suppose ⟨D⟩ ⊊ Cn2 (note ⟨D⟩ ⊆ Cn2 because Cn2 is structural and contains D). Then ⟨D⟩ is
structural, contains D, and is A0 -coherent because Cn2 is; so ⟨D⟩ and Cn2 are distinct members
of S(D, A0 ).

                                                   197
     Explicit finite bases. For a language containing ¬ and → (possibly also ∧, ∨), with distinct
atoms p, q, r, take the axioms ▷p → (q → p), ▷(p → (q → r)) → ((p → q) → (p → r)),
▷(¬p → ¬q) → (q → p) and modus ponens p, p → q ▷ q; if ∧, ∨ are present, add their
clauses as →-axioms: ▷p ∧ q → p, ▷p ∧ q → q, ▷p → (q → p ∧ q), ▷p → p ∨ q, ▷q → p ∨ q,
▷(p → r) → ((q → r) → (p ∨ q → r)). For pure {→} take the first two axioms, Peirce’s law
▷((p → q) → p) → p and modus ponens. For languages without →, such as {¬, ∧} and {¬, ∨},
finite bases exist because every two-element matrix logic is finitely based (Rautenberg, 1981, as
we recall the result; we have not re-checked its exact statement).
     The least structural consequence relation generated by a set of sequents is given by derivations
from substitution instances of those sequents. With the axioms and modus ponens above, these
are derivations in a Hilbert system with modus ponens as its only rule: Łukasiewicz’s axioms for
{¬, →}, extended by the usual axioms for ∧ and ∨, and for {→} the axioms K, S and Peirce’s
law, a variant of the Tarski–Bernays axiomatization (K and S give intuitionistic implication,
and Peirce’s law makes it classical). These systems are complete for tautologies (Kleene, 1952;
Mendelson, 1964); for the ∧, ∨ axioms Kalmár’s lemma goes through because the system proves
p, q ⊢ p ∧ q, ¬p ⊢ ¬(p ∧ q), ¬q ⊢ ¬(p ∧ q), p ⊢ p ∨ q, q ⊢ p ∨ q and ¬p, ¬q ⊢ ¬(p ∨ q). Since modus
ponens is the only rule and the first two axioms are present, the deduction theorem holds. So if
X |= φ, compactness gives a finite {x1 , . . . , xn } ⊆ X with |= x1 → (· · · → (xn → φ)), which is
provable, and n applications of modus ponens derive φ from X. Hence ⟨D⟩ = Cn2 .
     (b) Let j < i∗ , so hj =
                            ̸ Cn2 . If hj ̸⊇ Cn2 , some sequent of Cn2 \ hj eventually appears in
the text, and j is ineligible from then on. If hj ⊇ Cn2 , then hj = CFm by Theorem 6.13, and
A0 ⊢hj ⊥ makes j ineligible from the start. i∗ is always eligible. So the learner’s output is
non-decreasing, never exceeds i∗ , and is eventually i∗ ; in particular it changes its mind at most
i∗ times. Uniform decidability makes the eligibility tests computable; no property of the ordering
was used.
     (c) Cn2 ⊕{▷p} with p not in A0 : its consequences of A0 are Cn2 (A0 ∪{p}), which is consistent,
and it contains every sequent of Cn2 . CFm contains every sequent. Cai contains every sequent of
Cn2 in the {∧, ∨} fragment and is non-trivial on ∅.

The sequent-rule basis fails (countermodel). Take the language {¬, →, ∧} and let D′
consist of the three {¬, →} axioms, modus ponens, and the sequent rules p ∧ q ▷ p, p ∧ q ▷ q,
p, q ▷ p ∧ q. We show ▷p → (q → p ∧ q) ∈      / ⟨D′ ⟩; this countermodel is due to the verifier of T2
and was re-checked by script. For every assignment s of truth values to atoms define vs on all
formulas, simultaneously for all s, by recursion on complexity: vs (p) = s(p); vs treats ¬ and
→ classically; and vs (α ∧ β) = 1 iff α ∈ Θ∗ and β ∈ Θ∗ , where Θ∗ = {φ : vs (φ) = 1 for all s}.
This is well defined, since membership of α, β in Θ∗ depends only on values at lower complexity.
Every substitution instance of a {¬, →} tautology lies in Θ∗ , because each vs evaluates ¬, →
classically and treats the substituted subformulas as atoms. Θ∗ is closed under modus ponens (if
vs (α) = vs (α → β) = 1 for all s, then vs (β) = 1 for all s), under ∧-introduction (if α, β ∈ Θ∗
then vs (α ∧ β) = 1 for all s) and under ∧-elimination (if α ∧ β ∈ Θ∗ then α, β ∈ Θ∗ by definition).
So ⟨D′ ⟩(∅) ⊆ Θ∗ . But p ∈    / Θ∗ , so vs (p ∧ q) = 0 for every s; at s(p) = s(q) = 1 this gives
vs (p → (q → p ∧ q)) = 0. Hence p → (q → p ∧ q) ∈      / Θ∗ , and ⟨D′ ⟩ =
                                                                        ̸ Cn2 .

Renaming is not enough (L5 Prop 2.5). Let C(X) = Cn2 (X) if Cn2 (X) contains no atom,
and C(X) = Cn2 (X ∪ At) otherwise, where At is the set of atoms. C is extensive; it is monotone
because Cn2 (X) ⊆ Cn2 (Y ) for X ⊆ Y ; and it is idempotent, since in the first case Cn2 (Cn2 (X))


                                                198
has no atom and in the second C(X) already contains every atom. For a renaming ρ (atoms to
atoms) we have ρC(X) ⊆ C(ρX): in the first case because Cn2 is structural, and in the second
because Cn2 (ρX) contains ρp for an atom p ∈ Cn2 (X), so C(ρX) = Cn2 (ρX∪At) ⊇ ρCn2 (X∪At).
C contains p ▷ q, and C(∅) = Taut; so C is ∅-coherent, has the classical theorems, and differs
from Cn2 . It is the least renaming-invariant closure operator above Cn2 containing p ▷ q: any
such operator puts every atom into C ′ (X) as soon as Cn2 (X) contains one.

Proposition 6.16(a). Extensive and monotone: clear. Idempotent: if σX ⊆ Th, then
σCadm (X) ⊆ Th by definition, so every φ ∈ Cadm (Cadm (X)) has σφ ∈ Th. Structural: if
φ ∈ Cadm (X) and σ(τ X) ⊆ Th, then applying the definition to σ ◦ τ gives σ(τ φ) ∈ Th; so
τ φ ∈ Cadm (τ X). Cadm (∅) = Th because Th is substitution-closed. Maximal: if C is structural
with C(∅) = Th, φ ∈ C(X) and σX ⊆ Th, then σφ ∈ C(σX) ⊆ C(C(∅)) = Th. For finite X,
the defining condition is admissibility of X/φ.

Proposition 6.16(b),(c). (b) Cn2 is structural with theorems Taut, so Cn2 ⊆ Cadm by (a).

In the {∧, ∨} fragment Th = ∅, so the condition “σX ⊆ Th” never holds for non-empty X:
Cadm (X) = Fm for X ̸= ∅ and Cadm (∅) = ∅, i.e. Cadm = Cai .

1997; Iemhoff, 2001). The two relations have the same theorems by (a). For the coherence

(Theorem 6.17(a)), and a fortiori A ⊢IPC adm ⊥.


E.5    Proof of Theorem 6.17
(a) Glivenko’s theorem (Glivenko, 1929) gives ⊢CPC ¬ψ iff ⊢IPC ¬ψ. With ψ = A and the
                                                                                           V

deduction theorem (in both logics), A ⊢CPC ⊥ iff ⊢CPC ¬ A iff ⊢IPC ¬ A iff A ⊢IPC ⊥ (for
                                                               V                   V

A = ∅ neither side holds). An intermediate L is sandwiched between IPC and CPC.
    (b) CPC is a structural proper extension of IPC, coherent on every classically consistent
context; by (a) it has the same coherence data. So IPC is never a maximal coherent structural
hypothesis.
    (c) We use the following facts about Jankov formulas (Jankov, 1968); see Chagrov and
Zakharyaschev (1997, ch. 9). There is an infinite family B1 , B2 , . . . of finite subdirectly irreducible
Heyting algebras with Bi ∈  / SH(Bj ) for i ̸= j, and each Bi has a formula χ(Bi ) such that a
Heyting algebra B refutes χ(Bi ) iff Bi ∈ SH(B). At most one Bi is the two-element algebra
2 (two copies would violate the antichain condition); drop it, so |Bi | > 2 for all i. Let Lk be
the least structural consequence relation containing ⊢IPC and the axioms ▷χ(Bi ) for i ≤ k, and
      S
Lω = k Lk .
• Strictly increasing. The matrix consequence |=Bk+1 of (Bk+1 , {1}) is structural and contains
  ⊢IPC , by soundness of IPC for Heyting algebras. Bk+1 validates χ(Bi ) for i ≤ k, since

• Intermediate. SH(2) consists of 2 and the trivial algebra, and Bi is neither, so 2 validates
  every χ(Bi ). Hence every Lk ⊆ |=2 = CPC. By Proposition 6.10, Lω is a consequence relation;
  it is structural as a union of structural relations, and IPC ⊆ Lω ⊆ CPC.
• Impossibility. By (a), for every k ≤ ω the set of Lk -coherent finite contexts is the set of
  classically consistent ones. So whether A is fixed, or designations are target-dependent data,
  the environment can present one and the same designation stream δ for every target in
  {Lk : k ≤ ω}. Folding δ into the learner turns any learner for this class into a text learner for
  the chain L1 ⊊ L2 ⊊ · · · together with its union, which is impossible by the locking-sequence
  argument of Corollary 6.11.
   (d) The target is ⟨G⟩ for a finite set G of sequents. Since Dn is contained in the structural
target, so is ⟨Dn ⟩. Once the text has shown every sequent of G, ⟨Dn ⟩ ⊇ ⟨G⟩. From then
on every conjecture is correct (its syntactic index, e.g. Dn , may keep changing), which is
BC-identification.

E.7    Proof of Theorem 6.22
(a) If π is true, PA + π ⊆ Th(N) is consistent. If π is false, ¬π is a true Σ1 sentence, so PA ⊢ ¬π
by Σ1 -completeness, and PA + π is inconsistent.
    (b) A true σ gives PA + σ ⊆ Th(N). By Gödel’s second theorem PA + ¬Con(PA) is consistent,
although ¬Con(PA) is a false Σ1 sentence.
    (c) On a Π1 sentence π the learner says “true” at stage t unless a PA-proof of ¬π with code
≤ t exists. By (a) and soundness of PA, such a proof exists iff π is false; so the verdict changes at
most once and converges to the truth. On a Σ1 sentence σ it says “false” unless a PA-refutation
of ¬σ (equivalently a proof of σ) with code ≤ t exists; apply (a) to the Π1 sentence ¬σ.
    (d) At stage t the learner accepts φn iff no PA-proof with code ≤ t refutes the conjunction
of φn with the sentences among φ0 , . . . , φn−1 accepted at stage t. By induction on n: once
the statuses of φ0 , . . . , φn−1 have stabilized, the status of φn is a Π1 question (consistency
of a fixed finite extension of PA), answered by proof search with at most one change. So all
statuses converge. In the limit φn is accepted iff it is consistent with PA plus the accepted earlier
sentences: this is Lindenbaum’s construction along the enumeration, so the limit is a complete
consistent extension of PA, and it depends on the enumeration. If φ0 = ¬Con(PA), no refutation
of PA + ¬Con(PA) exists (Gödel II), so φ0 is accepted forever and the limit contains a false Σ1
sentence. On the enumeration Con(PA) ∧ 0=0, ¬Con(PA), Con(PA), . . . , the first sentence is
consistent with PA (it is true) and stays accepted; the second is inconsistent with it, which proof
search finds, so it is rejected.
    (e) On a computable presentation, the learner’s guesses about each sentence form a computable
function g(φ, n). If they converged to the truth value on every Σ2 sentence, then by Shoenfield’s
limit lemma (Shoenfield, 1959) the set of true Σ2 sentences would be ∆2 ; but it is Σ2 -complete,
hence not ∆2 , by Post’s hierarchy theorem.
    Repair note. The first version of (d) said that the naive learner accepts whichever of Con(PA)
and ¬Con(PA) comes first; the verifier’s enumeration above refutes that, and (d) now states the
Lindenbaum form.



                                                  201
E.8     Proof of Proposition 6.24
(a) If limits exist on C, then for φ ∈ C: limt Pt (φ) = 1 iff ∀q ∈ Q+ ∀s ∃t ≥ s Pt (φ) > 1 − q, a Π2
condition. The set of true Σ2 sentences is Σ2 -complete, so it is not Π2 (else it would be ∆2 ), and
no convergent computable credences have it as their success set.
    (b) By the convention on empty quantifier blocks every Π1 sentence is Π2 . If weakly coherent
credences gradually verified all Π2 sentences, they would be Π1 -convergent, and Theorem 6.23
would give a true Π2 sentence with lim inf t Pt = 0, contradicting Pt → 1 on true Π2 sentences.
    (c) Enumerate the sentences of Σ2 form, σ = ∃x∀y R(x, y) with R in ∆0 (quantifier blocks
coded as single variables; an empty block is a dummy variable), with indices ind(σ). At stage t:
• xt (σ) is the least x ≤ t with R(x, y) for all y ≤ t, or t + 1 if there is none;
• ct (σ) is the last stage s ≤ t with xs (σ) ̸= xs−1 (σ), or −1 if there is none;
• at (σ) := t − max(ind(σ), ct (σ)), the age of the current witness, for t ≥ ind(σ);
• It is the symmetric set of pairs (σ, σ ′ ) of Σ2 sentences with indices ≤ t that have a PA-proof
  of ¬(σ ∧ σ ′ ) with code ≤ t (including σ ′ = σ);
                                               ′
• Pt (σ) := min 1 − 2−at (σ) , min{2−at (σ ) : (σ, σ ′ ) ∈ It , at (σ ′ ) ≥ at (σ)} .
                                                                                   

Set Pt := 0 on sentences not of Σ2 form, and on σ before stage ind(σ).
     Weak coherence. If PA ⊢ ψ → ¬φ and one of them is not of Σ2 form, it has credence 0
and the sum is at most 1. Otherwise (ψ, φ) ∈ It from some stage on. At such a stage, say
at (ψ) ≤ at (φ); then Pt (ψ) ≤ 2−at (φ) and Pt (φ) ≤ 1 − 2−at (φ) , so the sum is at most 1. False
σ. Every x is eventually refuted, so xt (σ) → ∞ and changes infinitely often. At each change
at (σ) = 0 and Pt (σ) ≤ 1 − 20 = 0. So Pt (σ) ̸→ 1. True σ. Let x∗ be its least witness. Once every
x < x∗ is refuted and t ≥ x∗ , xt (σ) = x∗ forever. Let T0 = max(ind(σ), last change); for t > T0 ,
at (σ) = t − T0 → ∞. A competitor σ ′ with (σ, σ ′ ) ∈ It is PA-incompatible with the true σ, hence
false since PA is sound. If ind(σ ′ ) > T0 then at (σ ′ ) ≤ t − ind(σ ′ ) < at (σ). The finitely many
competitors with index ≤ T0 are false, so each changes its witness at some stage c > T0 , after
which at (σ ′ ) ≤ t − c < at (σ). So from some stage on no competitor enters the inner minimum,
and Pt (σ) = 1 − 2−at (σ) → 1. Non-convergence. By (a), these credences cannot converge on every
Σ2 sentence.
     The construction is due to L3’s referee and was sanity-checked by script (L3-scripts/
sigma2_weak_coherence.py). It assigns 0 to every sentence not of Σ2 form, so it is dogmatic
on the strictly Π2 truths, consistent with Theorem 6.23.

E.9     Proofs for Section 6.4: Carnap’s problem
For finite Γ, ∆ write U (Γ, ∆) = {v : v[Γ] = 1, v[∆] = 0}; these sets form a clopen basis of 2Fm ,
and a valuation satisfies the sequent Γ ▷ ∆ iff it lies outside U (Γ, ∆). So Val(S) is closed for
every set S of sequents.

and v violates it. If v ∈ V violates a valid Γ ▷ ∆, then U (Γ, ∆) is a neighbourhood of v, so it
contains some u ∈ V , which violates the sequent too; contradiction.
    (b) Finitary: if φ ∈ CV (X), the closed sets V ∩ {v : v(φ) = 0} and {v : v(x) = 1},

sequent iff CV (v) = v (identifying v with its true-set). Fix(CV ) = V ∩ : a fixed point X equals

    (c) An intersection of classical theories is a theory, and Fm is the empty intersection.
Conversely, a consistent theory T is the intersection of the Boolean valuations extending it: if

Theorem 6.29. (a) Each schema enforces one row condition. For ¬: φ, ¬φ▷ excludes both
true and ▷φ, ¬φ excludes both false, so v(¬φ) = 1 − v(φ). For ∧: the two eliminations give
v(φ ∧ ψ) ≤ min, introduction gives ≥. For ∨: the introductions give v(φ ∨ ψ) ≥ max, and
φ ∨ ψ ▷ φ, ψ gives ≤. For →: ▷φ, φ → ψ gives v(φ → ψ) = 1 when v(φ) = 0; ψ ▷ φ → ψ gives
it when v(ψ) = 1; modus ponens gives v(φ → ψ) = 0 when v(φ) = 1, v(ψ) = 0. So v satisfies
all instances iff it is a homomorphism into the two-element Boolean algebra. (With ⊥, ⊤ in the
language, ⊥▷ and ▷⊤ fix their values.) BV = Val(TT) is closed, and |=m         BV determines BV by
Lemma 6.26(a).
     (b) For any substitution σ and sequent s = Γ ▷ ∆, v satisfies σs iff v ◦ σ satisfies s. Let
v ∈ V . Every instance of a schema in TT is σ(TTj (p, q)) for some σ and j, and v ◦ σ ∈ V satisfies
TTj (p, q); so v satisfies all instances and V ⊆ Val(TT) = BV. For equality, take any v ∈ V
(there is one, as V ̸= ∅) and any u ∈ BV. Let σ(p) = p0 ∨ ¬p0 if u(p) = 1 and σ(p) = ¬(p0 ∨ ¬p0 )
otherwise. Then v ◦ σ ∈ V ⊆ BV is Boolean and agrees with u on atoms (v is Boolean, so
v(p0 ∨ ¬p0 ) = 1), hence v ◦ σ = u and u ∈ V .
     (c) The learner that outputs BV once the eleven sequents TT(p, q) and the coherence datum
have appeared is correct by (b), and it never needs to change its mind. V = ∅ satisfies every
sequent vacuously, so without the coherence datum no finite set of positive data excludes it.
     (d) Pointwise refutation. A text for LBV := |=m BV eventually presents every instance of TT,
and by (a) every non-Boolean valuation violates one. No tell-tale. Fix a Boolean u0 , and for
a formula χ let vχ′ agree with u0 except at χ. Then vχ′ (¬χ) = u0 (¬χ) = 1 − u0 (χ) = vχ′ (χ),
so vχ′ ∈ / BV = BV. BV ∪ {vχ′ } is closed, and by Lemma 6.26(a) its relation Lχ satisfies
Val(Lχ ) = BV ∪ {vχ′ } =  ̸ BV, so Lχ ⊊ LBV . Given a finite T ⊆ LBV , choose χ occurring in no
sequent of T ; then vχ′ agrees with u0 on every formula T mentions and satisfies T , so T ⊆ Lχ .
Non-learnability. Suppose a learner, computable or not, BC-identifies (a fortiori if it EX-identifies)
every meaning in a class containing BV and all BV ∪ {vχ′ } from texts of their relations, plus the
coherence datum [ : ], which is in bounds for every member and so carries no information. By the
locking-sequence argument (Blum and Blum, 1975; Angluin, 1980), which needs no effectivity,
there is a finite σ of LBV -data such that the learner conjectures BV on every extension of σ
by LBV -data. Choose χ not mentioned in σ; then σ consists of Lχ -data, and on a text for
Lχ beginning with σ the learner conjectures BV ̸= BV ∪ {vχ′ } forever. Density. A Boolean
valuation is determined by its values on atoms, and the map from atom assignments to BV
is a homeomorphism from 2ω , which has no isolated points. So BV \ {u0 } is dense in BV; by
Lemma 6.26(a) it has the same multiple-conclusion relation, and a basic open set meets BV iff
it meets BV \ {u0 }, so the two meanings have the same in-bounds positions. Composing any
Boolean v with the constant substitution along u0 yields u0 , so BV \ {u0 } is not structural.
     Repair note. The first version of (d) ended “identification in the limit still holds via the text”.
That was false and has been retracted; (d) now proves non-identifiability. The Lean formalization
proves (d) for BC-identification from texts with pauses and arbitrary learners.



                                                  203
Proposition 6.30. Row by row, with designated set {1}. ¬: explosion p, ¬p ▷ q at p = 1,
q = 0 forces f¬ (1) = 0; then p ▷ ¬¬p at p = 1 forces f¬ (0) = 1. ∧: p ∧ q ▷ p forces f∧ (0, b) = 0,
p ∧ q ▷ q forces f∧ (a, 0) = 0, and p, q ▷ p ∧ q forces f∧ (1, 1) = 1. ∨: the introductions force
f∨ (a, 1) = f∨ (1, b) = 1, and disjunctive syllogism at p = q = 0 (where ¬p = 1) forces f∨ (0, 0) = 0.
→: modus ponens forces f→ (1, 0) = 0; q ▷ p → q forces f→ (a, 1) = 1; ¬p ▷ p → q at p = 0
forces f→ (0, b) = 1. So only the standard matrix can validate all twelve rules, and it does.
A brute-force check over all 4 · 163 matrices confirms that exactly one matrix validates them
(T2-checks/matrix_check.py).

E.10     Proofs for Section 6.5: tonk and conservativity
Proposition 6.32. Intuitionistic implication with ¬-introduction, ¬-elimination and ¬¬-
elimination is a standard natural-deduction system for the {→, ¬} fragment of classical logic
(Prawitz, 1965), so it is coherent and proves Peirce’s law ((p → q) → p) → p. Peirce’s law is not
intuitionistically valid: in the two-world Kripke model w ≤ w′ with p true only at w′ and q true
nowhere, p → q fails at both worlds, so (p → q) → p holds at w vacuously, while p fails at w.
So the extension is not conservative over IPC→ . ¬¬-elimination alone is conservative. Let τ
erase negations (τ (¬φ) = τ (φ), τ commuting with →, identity on atoms). It maps each instance
of ¬¬φ ▷ φ to the identity step τ φ ▷ τ φ and each instance of an IPC→ rule to an instance of
the same rule, and it fixes ¬-free formulas. So a derivation of a ¬-free sequent in the extension
maps to an IPC→ -derivation of the same sequent. Conservativity and coherence. If the base is
non-trivial and coherence means non-triviality on the old vocabulary, a conservative extension
proves no new old-vocabulary sequent, so it is non-trivial; likewise if ⊥ with ex falso is in the
base. If ⊥ is new and has no rules, IPC→ + {▷⊥} adds no ⊥-free theorem and so is conservative,
yet derives ⊥.

Theorem 6.33. (a) “R ⊬ ⊥” says that no finite derivation in R ends in ⊥, a Π1 condition.
For hardness, given e build a finite system Re that derives the successive configurations of the
computation of φe (e) (elementary formal systems simulate Turing machines; Post 1943; Smullyan
1961), and add the rule “halting configuration ▷⊥”. Then Re ⊬ ⊥ iff φe (e) diverges, which
reduces the Π1 -complete complement of the halting set.
    (b) Membership: “for every old-vocabulary s and every derivation of s in B + N there is a
derivation of s in B” is Π2 . Hardness, from the Π2 -complete set Tot = {e : φe total}. Let B
be a universal elementary formal system that derives T (x̄, ē) iff φe (x) ↓ (unary numerals x̄, ē)
and derives Num(n̄) for exactly the unary numerals, designed so that T -strings never occur as
premises of its rules. Its productions must also match only predicate-tagged strings over the old
alphabet; in pure Post-string format a projection such as xy ▷ y could otherwise strip a new
symbol, while in an elementary formal system with predicates this is automatic. Let Ne add
a new symbol c with the rules ▷c ē and c y, Num(x) ▷ T (x, y) (schematic in x, y). The only
derivable c-string is c ē, so the new old-vocabulary theorems are exactly T (n̄, ē) for n ∈ N, and
since T -strings are never premises nothing further follows. So the old-vocabulary theorems of
B + Ne are Thm(B) ∪ {T (n̄, ē) : n ∈ N}, and B + Ne is conservative over B iff φe (n) ↓ for every
n, iff e ∈ Tot.
    (c) A set is decidable in the limit by a computable learner iff it is ∆2 (Shoenfield, 1959;
Putnam, 1965; Gold, 1965); a Π1 set is decided in the limit with at most one mind change; a
Π2 -complete set is not ∆2 . Refutability in the limit corresponds to Π2 (Kelly, 1996).



                                                 204
     (d) If B is decidable, “every old-vocabulary theorem of B + N is a theorem of B” quantifies
  universally over derivations and then checks a decidable property: Π1 .

  E.11     Proof of Theorem 6.38
  The characterization of Alt as the never-refuted G-closed hypotheses is Theorem 6.9(ii), restricted
  to G-closed h.
      (i) A structural h ⊇ Cn2 is Cn2 or CFm by Theorem 6.13, and CFm is incoherent on every
  context, so on the non-empty A.
      (ii) ⊇: if h is structural with IPC ⊆ h ⊆ CPC and A is classically consistent, then A ⊬CPC ⊥,
  so A ⊬h ⊥. ⊆: let h ⊇ IPC be structural and suppose Γ ⊢h φ with Γ finite and Γ ̸|= φ. Take a
  Boolean v with v[Γ] = 1, v(φ) = 0, and let σv map each atom to ⊥ → ⊥ or ⊥ according to v. As
  shown in the proof of Proposition 6.16(c), IPC proves every classically true variable-free formula
  and refutes every classically false one. So IPC proves each member of σv Γ and proves ¬σv φ. By
  structurality σv Γ ⊢h σv φ; by cut with the IPC-theorems σv Γ, ∅ ⊢h σv φ; with ∅ ⊢h ¬σv φ and
  σv φ, ¬σv φ ⊢IPC ⊥, ∅ ⊢h ⊥. By monotonicity h is incoherent on every A ∈ A, which is non-empty.
  So every coherent structural h ⊇ IPC lies inside CPC.
      (iii) With G deductive closure and A = {∅}, the hypotheses above the target are the
  deductively closed theories containing PA, and ∅-coherence is consistency. Designating {Con(PA)}
  removes PA + ¬Con(PA). If every finite set of true sentences is designated, a theory containing
  a false φ is incoherent on {¬φ}, and a theory T ⊆ Th(N) is coherent on every true context; so
  exactly the unsound ones are removed. The theories Tk of Theorem 6.21 contain PA and are
  sound, so they remain, and by that theorem text plus true-context coherence cannot discriminate
  among them.
      Repair note. Part (ii) was strengthened from “⊇” to equality after verification, with the σv
  argument above; part (iii) was made precise (G and A specified).


  F      Proofs for Section 7
  The proofs of Proposition 7.5, Lemma 7.11, Lemma 7.24 and Corollary 7.25 are complete in the
  main text; this appendix adds the details they cite. Throughout, (WS) is assumed for every
  designated position unless a statement says otherwise, V (or V[A:D] ) denotes a total valuation as
  in (WS), and “refutation” means a refutation in the sense of Definition 7.2, with size counting
  the symbols of distinct judgments. Two facts are used repeatedly.
(F1) Cleanness is closed downward. If B ′ ⊆ B, every refutation of B ′ is a refutation of B, because
     RB ′ ⊆ RB . Likewise for step sets.
(F2) Transfer. If every step of a set S ′ lies in Sound(R) and π is a refutation with steps in
     S ′ ∪ R ∪ Ttrust , then replacing each S ′ -step of π by an R-derivation of its conclusion from its
     premises, and compressing (Definition 7.2(a)), gives a refutation with steps in R ∪ Ttrust , from
     the same leaves to the same conclusion, of some finite size. In particular, if R is clean at every
     depth, then every refutation using S ′ transfers to a refutation of R, which is impossible.

  F.1     Proof of Lemma 7.4
  Fix a designated position [A : D] and its valuation V , and let X = V −1 (1). Since V falsifies no
  step of R∗ , X is closed under R∗ , so ClR∗ (X) = X. Hence if P ▷ j ∈ Sound(R∗ ) and P ⊆ X,


                                                   205
then j ∈ ClR∗ (P ) ⊆ ClR∗ (X) = X: V falsifies no step of Sound(R∗ ).
    (a) Let S ⊆ Sound(R∗ ), and let π be a derivation relative to [A : D] whose leaves have
e-value 1 and whose steps lie in S ∪ Ttrust . By induction along the sequence π, every judgment
of π is V -true. A leaf is e-true, hence V -true because V extends e. A judgment produced by a
step of S ∪ Ttrust from earlier, V -true judgments is V -true, by the claim for S and by (WS) for
Ttrust . So the conclusion of π is V -true, and since V extends e it is not e-false. Hence π is not a
refutation; as π, its size and the position were arbitrary, S is clean at every depth. For B ⊆ Σ∗ ,
RB ⊆ R∗ ⊆ Sound(R∗ ) (Lemma 2.3(b)).
    (b) A member of Conf d has a d-refutation, so by (a) it is not contained in Σ∗ ; as it is contained
in ΣP = Σ∗ ⊔ F , it meets F .
    (c) The definition of a d-refutation of B mentions only e (which is determined by A and W ),
Ttrust , d and B. So Conf d is a function of these data. Both tie-breaking rules choose among the
d-refutations of B using only their sizes, a fixed lexicographic order, and whether their descent is
blocked; the last depends only on π and e, because e+   π is computed from e and the trusted steps
of π.
    (d) (⇒) The proof of (a) used (WS) only for the position of π. (⇐) Suppose e = e[A:D] is well
defined and Σ∗ is clean at every depth relative to [A : D]. Let Y = ClR∗ ∪Ttrust (A ∪ W −1 (1)) and
let V be the indicator of Y . Since Y is closed under R∗ ∪ Ttrust , V falsifies no step of R∗ ∪ Ttrust ,
and V is 1 on every e-true judgment. Let j be e-false and suppose j ∈ Y . By Lemma 2.3(a), j has
a finite (R∗ ∪ Ttrust )-derivation tree from A ∪ W −1 (1), that is, from e-true leaves. Compressing
it gives a refutation of Σ∗ relative to [A : D] of some finite size, contradicting cleanness. So V is
0 on every e-false judgment, V extends e, and (WS) holds for [A : D]. Nothing in the argument
used that Σ∗ is the target.

F.2    Details for Proposition 7.5
The first two claims are Theorems 5.14 and 5.15 applied to the tagged practice, together with
Definition 2.10, which gives every fallacy an instance outside Sound(R∗ ). For the CPC claim, let
Πc ▷ jc be the closed falsified instance of the pure fallacy τ (Lemma 7.24). Each closed formula
has the same value under every valuation, so every member of Πc and ¬jc are tautologies, and
a complete Σ∗ derives them from ∅. The step adds jc , and {jc , ¬jc } classically entails every
formula, which Σ∗ derives. For the contrast: if Σ∗ is complete and s = (p ▷ q) for atoms p, q, the
set of tautologies is closed under s because p is not a tautology, so ClR∗ ∪{s} (∅) = ClR∗ (∅).

F.3    Proof of Lemma 7.8
(a) M ⊆ U is clean iff it contains no member of K, iff (by upward closure and finiteness) it contains
no member of C, iff U \ M meets every member of C, i.e. is a transversal. Complementation
reverses inclusion, so maximal clean sets correspond to minimal transversals.
    (b, ⇐) Let x ∈ C ∈ C and T = (U \ C) ∪ {x}. Let E ∈ C. If E ̸⊆ C, then E meets U \ C. If
E ⊆ C, then E = C by minimality of C, so x ∈ E. Hence T is a transversal, and it contains a
minimal transversal T ′ . Since T ′ meets C and T ′ ∩ C ⊆ T ∩ C = {x}, we get x ∈ T ′ .
    (b, ⇒) If x ∈ T for a minimal transversal T , then T \ {x} is not a transversal, so some E ∈ C
has E ∩ T = {x}, and x ∈ E.
    (b, second claim) By (a), x lies outside some maximal clean set iff x lies in some minimal
transversal, iff x ∈ C. Every clean set is contained in a maximal one, and ∅ is clean, so there
                    S




                                                 206
are maximal clean sets and their intersection is U \ C. It is clean because cleanness is closed
                                                          S

downward.
    (c, ⇐) If C = {{x1 }, . . . , {xr }}, every transversal contains {x1 , . . . , xr }, which is itself a
transversal.
    (c, ⇒) Let T be the unique minimal transversal. By (b), T = C. For each x ∈ T , minimality
                                                                    S

gives some E ∈ C with E ∩ T = {x}; since E ⊆ C = T , E = {x}. A member C ∈ C with
                                                      S

|C| ≥ 2 would then strictly contain the singleton member {x} for any x ∈ C, contradicting
minimality.
    The Lean formalization (InfLearn.Blame) proves (a) as isMaxClean_iff and
setOf_isMaxClean_eq_image, (b) as exists_minTransversal_mem_iff, iInter_maxClean_eq
and isClean_sdiff_blameSet, and (c) as existsUnique_minTransversal_iff, for a finite uni-
verse; upward closure is needed only inside U , and ∅ ∈  / K only for the intersection claims.
