F.5    Proof of Lemma 7.9
Agreement. We show by induction on the order in which the fixed-point computation assigns
values that every value of e+ π agrees with V . Initial values come from e, which V extends. If the
forward rule assigns 1 to the conclusion of a trusted step whose premises have value 1, those
premises are V -true by induction, and since V falsifies no trusted step the conclusion is V -true.
If the backward rule assigns 0 to a premise π0 of a trusted step whose conclusion has value 0 and
whose other premises have value 1, then by induction the conclusion is V -false and the other
premises are V -true; if π0 were V -true the step would be falsified by V , so π0 is V -false. Hence
e+
 π never assigns both values to a judgment, and if it does, no total V as in (WS) exists for
[A : D].
    (a) Each move of the descent goes from a node of value 0 to one of its premises, which occurs
strictly earlier in the sequence π; so the descent terminates. Leaves have e-value 1, hence e+π -value
1, while every visited node has value 0; so every visited node is the conclusion of some step of π.


                                                  207
Since the descent is unblocked, it ends by outputting a step s all of whose premises have value 1
and whose conclusion has value 0 (a step without premises is output at once). By agreement,
the premises of s are V -true and its conclusion V -false. By (WS), s ∈        / Ttrust and s ∈/ R∗ ; as π is
a refutation of B, s ∈ RB .
    (b) The worklist assigns each judgment of π a value at most once and re-examines a trusted
step only when one of its judgments receives a value, so the fixed point costs O(|π|φ) operations,
with one evaluation of e per judgment. The descent looks at the premises of each visited node
once, and visits at most |π| nodes. The number of judgments is at most |π| ≤ d.
    (c) If some genuine σ had s ∈ inst(σ), then s ∈ R∗ , contradicting (a).
    (d) Let σ ∈ B with s ∈ inst(σ). Let J1 be the list of judgments of π with e+          π -value 1, in the
order in which they received the value (those with initial e-value 1 first). Each member of J1 is
e-true, and then is treated as a leaf, or received value 1 by the forward rule as the conclusion
of a trusted step of π whose premises precede it in J1 . The premises of s lie in J1 . Let c0 be
the conclusion of s. Define c0 , c1 , . . . : if ci is e-false, stop; otherwise ci received value 0 by the
backward rule from a trusted step ti of π of which ci is a premise, whose conclusion ci+1 had
received value 0 strictly earlier, and whose other premises lie in J1 . Since assignment times
strictly decrease along the chain, it terminates at some e-false ck , and the ci are pairwise distinct.
They are not in J1 , because e+ π is consistent.
    Let πσ be the list J1 , followed by c0 (justified by s), c1 (justified by t0 ), . . . , ck (justified
by tk−1 ). Its judgments are distinct; each non-leaf judgment is the conclusion of a step whose
premises occur earlier; its leaves are e-true; its steps are s ∈ inst(σ) and trusted steps; and its
last judgment ck is e-false. So πσ is a refutation of {σ} relative to [A : D]. All its judgments are
judgments of π, and size counts distinct judgments, so |πσ | ≤ |π| ≤ d. Hence {σ} is a d-conflict.
(Under a tree-size convention this bound could fail, because the premise derivations of s and the
side premises of the chain may share judgments.)
    The Lean theorem InfLearn.Blame.LineArg.descent proves the descent for a line-list
argument under a total evaluation, with the walk length bounded by any height function that
decreases along dependencies, and LineArg.descent_not_mem proves that the step found lies
outside every rule set whose steps preserve truth. Trusted-step propagation and part (d) are not
formalized.

F.6     Proof of Proposition 7.12
(a) At a good position p, every schema moved into Blamedp is a fallacy, by Lemma 7.9(c) applied
relative to p. So a genuine schema collects votes only from bad positions, of which there are at
most m. The bonus for [∅ : ∅] is used only when that position is known to be good, and then it
blames only fallacies. So every genuine schema has at most m votes and lies in A.
    (b) Every call at p either ends p’s loop or moves at least one schema out of Bp . Indeed, if
the call returns a refutation r, ep is well defined, e+  p,r is consistent and the descent is unblocked,
then the descent outputs a step s whose premises have value 1 and whose conclusion has value
0, by the argument of Lemma 7.9(a), which used only consistency and unblockedness. If s
were trusted, the forward rule would have given its conclusion value 1 as well, contradicting
consistency. So s is untrusted and lies in RBp , and some σ ∈ Bp with s ∈ inst(σ) is moved.
Hence the loop at p makes at most |Bp | + 1 ≤ |ΣP | + 1 calls, and at most |F | + 1 at a
good position, where only fallacies move. With m′ ≤ m bad positions the total is at most
(|A| − m′ )(|F | + 1) + m′ (|F | + |Σ∗ | + 1) ≤ |A|(|F | + 1) + m|Σ∗ |.
    (c) Let p be complete. Its loop ended with NONE, so its final Bp is d-clean relative to p;


                                                    208
and since p is good, only fallacies were moved, so Σ∗ ⊆ Bp . If τ ∈ F \ Blamedp , then τ ∈ Bp ,
and Σ∗ ∪ {τ } ⊆ Bp is d-clean relative to p by (F1). Contrapositively, each complete p relative
to which Σ∗ ∪ {τ } is not d-clean gives τ a vote. A τ ∈ A has at most m votes, which gives the
claim. For m = 0 with all positions complete, A = ΣP \ p Blamedp lies inside every final Bp , so
                                                             S

it is d-clean relative to every position; it contains Σ∗ , and each of its fallacies is residual. This is
the conclusion of Lemma 7.11(a).
     Why per-position loops (computed; prop32_voting.py). An earlier version ran one smallest-
refutation query per position and voted on the results. With one bad position [a, a → b : b],
target {MP} and F = ∅, it made one successful descent, against its claimed bound (m + 1)|F | = 0.
With m = 1, the truthful positions p1 = [q, p → q, ¬p : p, ¬q] and p2 = [¬r, r → t : ¬t] and the
practice {MP, AC, DA}, the smallest refutation at p1 always blames AC and hides DA, so DA
got one vote and survived although it is refutable at two positions. The loops above exclude
already-blamed schemas, so DA is blamed at both positions and removed, while AC, refutable at
one position only, may survive.

F.7    Proof of Proposition 7.13
(a) By Definition 7.10, A is computed at t = N1 from P̂ , a function of the first N1 human steps,
and from answers of Ref d , which are deterministic functions of (B, d, A, W, Ttrust ) (Lemma 7.4(c)).
Phase-1 acceptance is membership in RA . The sandbox, the red team and the prover interact
with the tier only through queries, which do not change A, and escalation answers are not added
to the sample. A promoted κ is a macro, answered by expanding it into an RA -derivation,
so the primitive accepted steps remain RA and ClRA is unchanged. If promoted rules were
accepted as primitive steps instead, then κ ⊆ Sound(RA ) ⊆ Sound(R∗ ∪ RF (d) ), so the accepted
                                                                                  res
set would satisfy only this weaker inclusion; the closure would be unchanged (Lemma 2.3(b)).
But d-cleanness of the enlarged step set can fail. If some τ ∈ A is residual at depth d with
a shortest refutation of size 2d, a promoted κ whose instances package the first half of that
refutation into single steps removes its intermediate judgments and yields a refutation of size at
most d. Primitive promoted rules would also need truth maintenance under the depth schedule.
     (b) Let h0 ∈ HS be clean at every depth with w(h0 ) > 0. A detection exhibits a refutation π
of the step set Bt = St and deletes every h ⊇ Steps(π) \ Ttrust . If h0 were deleted, the steps
                          T

of π would lie in h0 ∪ Ttrust and π would be a refutation of h0 , which is impossible. So h0 is
never deleted. In a detection, every h ∈ St contains Bt ⊇ Steps(π) \ Ttrust , so all of St is deleted
and w(VSt+1 ) ≤ 12 w(VSt ). After D detections w(h0 ) ≤ w(VS) ≤ 2−D , so D ≤ log2 (1/w(h0 )).
For the robust bound, run the proof of Theorem 6.7 with h0 in place of R∗ : every detection
multiplies by β the weight of a superset of St , so the total weight shrinks by a factor at most
 1+β

     Step 5: depth schedule. For B ⊆ ΣP let mB be the least size of a refutation of B (∞ if

refutation of size mB for d ≥ mB : it changes at most once. Under the prefer-unblocked rule

                                                                     S
                                                        P
by Conf d = {B : Ref d (B) ̸= NONE}. There are 2|Σ | sets B, each with at most two change

for d ≥ d∗1 . At each stage, Steps 2 and 3 apply with the current d; truth maintenance ensures

                                        (d)
each Σ∗ ∪ {τ } no longer changes, so Fres = Fres , and the tier is sound relative to R∗ ∪ RFres . The
example in the main text shows that A need not be monotone.

Proof of Corollary 7.15. On G, every step the reasoner chains lies in RA ⊆ R∗ ∪ RF (d) , in Ttrust ,
                                                                                        res
or (for the extended reasoner) is a world-channel step. The first two kinds preserve truth in M
by hypothesis. A world-channel step has all its judgments in JW and is accepted only if it is
not W -falsified; if its premises are true in M, they are W -true, so its conclusion is not W -false,
hence W -true, hence true in M. By induction along derivations, everything derived from M-true
premises is M-true.

F.9    Proofs of Propositions 7.16 to 7.19
Proof of Proposition 7.16. Every subset of Σ2 is clean at every depth, so Σ1 and Σ2 both satisfy
(WS) (Lemma 7.4(d)), and the oracle answers are the same under both (Lemma 7.4(c)). The
learner’s view consists of these answers, the fixed prover’s queries and its own coins, so it has the
same law under both targets. Let the prover query some q ∈ RΣ2 \ Sound(RΣ1 ) in every round.
If the learner ever accepts q with probability above δ, it is not δ-sound for Σ1 ; otherwise, under
Σ2 , it fails with probability at least 1 − δ to accept the genuine step q. In the example, ∨I1 is not
derivable from the conjunction rules, while A ∧ B ▷ B ∧ A is derivable by ∧E2 , ∧E1 and ∧I.

Proof of Proposition 7.17(a). The prover is fixed. The learner’s inputs (human data, oracle
answers, W , escalation answers) have the same law under every Σ ∈ T : the data law and the
practice are shared, oracle answers by Lemma 7.4(c), and escalation answers follow the shared
practice. Coupling the learner’s coins, the whole run up to and including the answer to q has the
same law, so p(h, q) does not depend on Σ. If q ∈ / Sound(RΣ ∪ RF (d) (Σ) ) for some admissible Σ,
                                                                    res
the event “h, then q accepted” is an unsound acceptance under Σ.
     TTL passes. On G the audit runs on P̂ = ΣP , identically in all admissible scenarios. Fix
Σ ∈ T , with valuations VΣ from (WS). If a descent on π outputs s, then e+ π agrees with VΣ , since
the agreement argument of Lemma 7.9 needs only a total valuation that extends e and falsifies
no trusted step. So s has VΣ -true premises and a VΣ -false conclusion, and s ∈    / RΣ ; hence no
schema of Σ is removed by descent, and Σ is contained in the returned set (descent path) or in B0
(fallback). Let x ∈ A \ Σ. On the descent path Σ ∪ {x} ⊆ A is d-clean by (F1). In the fallback,



                                                 211
if Σ ∪ {x} ⊆ B0 were not d-clean it would contain some C ∈ Cd (B0 ), with C ̸⊆ Σ because Σ is
                                                      (d)
clean, so x ∈ C, contradicting x ∈ A. Either way x ∈ Fres (Σ), so RA ⊆ RΣ ∪ RF (d) (Σ) .
                                                                                        res


    Part (b) of Proposition 7.17 collects facts proved elsewhere: Proposition 7.6(b); Theo-
rem 6.5(iii) for the coalition, whose members all contain a falsified fallacy instance under the
stated priors (so the coalition-unanimous set does too), while a prior with more than half its
mass on R∗ forces R∗ ∈ St and hence soundness; and Proposition 3.9.

Proof of Proposition 7.18. Both worlds have the practice {∧I, AC} with the same frequencies
and instance laws, so a learner without refutation evidence has a view with the same law in both.
Let the prover query an AC instance outside Sound(R∧I ) under W1 , such as q, p → q ▷ p; it is
genuine under W2 . Accepting it with probability above δ is unsound under W1 ; otherwise the
learner is incomplete under W2 . For the separation, under W1 the instance (⊤, ⊥ → ⊤ ▷ ⊥) has
true premises and a false conclusion, while under W2 its premise ⊥ ↔ ⊤ is false.

Proof of Proposition 7.19. (a) By time t at most t refutations have been exhibited, each a finite
derivation implicating finitely many instances; a fallacy with infinitely many invalid instances
keeps one that is not refuted. (b) is Proposition 6.37. (c) With every finite set of steps a
hypothesis, the intersection of the version space of data D is D itself (Section 5.4).

F.10     Proof of Theorem 7.20
Existence of s, and admissibility. If every τ -instance were in Sound(R{σ,µ} ), the refutation of
{µ, τ } would transfer, by (F2), to a refutation of {σ, µ}, which is clean at every depth. So some
τ -instance s lies outside Sound(R{σ,µ} ). The target {σ, µ} of scenario 1 is clean at every depth,
hence satisfies (WS), and its fallacy τ has the instance s outside Sound(R{σ,µ} ); the target {σ, τ }
of scenario 2 is clean at every depth and has F = ∅. In scenario 2, s is a genuine instance.
     The inequality. Let Pj be the law of one human sample (a tag and a step) in scenario j.
On samples whose tag is not µ, P1 = (1 − π)P2 as measures: the frequencies are (1 − π)qσ and
(1 − π)qτ against qσ and qτ , and the per-tag laws coincide. Let Et be the event that none of the
first t samples has tag µ. Then P1⊗t restricted to Et equals (1 − π)t P2⊗t , and P2⊗t (Et ) = 1.
     Human samples are i.i.d. and independent of everything generated before them. By (OE),
every other input of round r is drawn from a kernel applied to the history, the same kernel in
both scenarios. By induction over rounds, the joint law of the samples x = (x1 , . . . , xt ) and the
other inputs y through time t is Pj⊗t (dx) Kt (x, dy), with one kernel Kt for both j. Whether s is
accepted at time t is a function of (x, y); let ϕ(x) = Kt (x, {accept s at t}) ∈ [0, 1]. Then
                            Z                           Z
     P1 (accept s at t) ≥         ϕ dP1⊗t = (1 − π)t         ϕ dP2⊗t = (1 − π)t P2 (accept s at t),
                             Et                         Et

so P2 ≤ δ(1 − π)−t whenever P1 ≤ δ.
                                                                                      ′
    The bounds. P2 ≥ 1 − δ ′ requires (1 − π)t ≤ δ/(1 − δ ′ ), i.e. t ln 1−π
                                                                          1
                                                                             ≥ ln 1−δ
                                                                                   δ , which is positive
when δ + δ ′ < 1. Since 1−π 1
                               = 1 + 1−π π       1
                                           , ln 1−π     π
                                                     ≤ 1−π , which gives the second inequality. It fails
                 ′              ′                  1
without δ + δ < 1: for δ = δ = 0.6 and π = 2 the two sides are −0.585 and −0.405.
    Tightness. Let the learner accept τ -instances at time t iff no µ-tag occurs among the first t
samples and an independent coin with bias min(1, δ(1−π)−t ) comes up heads. It does not escalate,
so it satisfies (OE). In scenario 1 it accepts s at time t with probability (1−π)t min(1, δ(1−π)−t ) ≤
δ; in scenario 2, where µ never occurs, with probability min(1, δ(1 − π)−t ). Linear programming


                                                  212
over all learners that depend only on the tag sequence, for t ≤ 6, confirms that this is optimal in
all 108 cases checked (computed; two_point_bounds.py).
    Practice-following escalation. By (F2) with τ and µ exchanged, some µ-instance sµ lies
outside Sound(R{σ,τ } ), hence outside the scenario-2 practice R{σ,τ } , while it lies in the scenario-1
practice. A learner that escalates sµ in round 1 and accepts s in every later round iff the answer
was “reject” accepts s with probability 0 in scenario 1 and 1 in scenario 2. So without (OE) the
conclusion fails.

F.11     Proof of Theorem 7.21
(a) The practice Σ0 ∪ {µ, τ } and its frequencies are the same in both scenarios, so the data and
practice-following escalation answers have the same laws, and oracle answers are target-blind
(Lemma 7.4(c)). Both targets are clean at every depth, hence satisfy (WS), and by (F2) each
target’s fallacy has an instance outside the closure of the other’s calculus; in particular some
µ-instance s lies outside Sound(RΣ0 ∪{τ } ). In scenario 2, µ is not residual, because Σ0 ∪ {τ, µ}
                                    (d)
contains the conflict {µ, τ }; so Fres = ∅ there, and accepting s is unsound, which δ-soundness
caps at probability δ. The fixed prover queries s, and the acceptance probability at any given
time is the same in scenario 1, where s is genuine. Finally {µ} and {τ } are clean by (F1), so
{µ, τ } ∈ Cd and µ ∈ Colld in scenario 1.
    (b) Since d ≥ d0 , a subset of ΣP is d-clean iff it is clean at every depth.
    Choice of M . Apply Lemma 7.8(b) with U = B0 and K = Conf d ∩ 2B0 , whose minimal
members are Cd (B0 ): since g ∈ Cd (B0 ), some maximal d-clean M ⊆ B0 omits g. M is clean at
                                  S

every depth, so it satisfies (WS) by Lemma 7.4(d) (e is well defined because (WS) holds for the
true target).
    Admissibility of M . Let x ∈ ΣP \ M . If x ∈ B0 \ M , then M ∪ {x} is not d-clean by
maximality; if every x-instance were in Sound(RM ), (F2) would turn a refutation of M ∪ {x}
into one of M , which is impossible. If x ∈ ΣP \ B0 , then x was removed by a descent on some
refutation π, with output s ∈ inst(x). The valuation VM extends e and falsifies no trusted step,
so e+
    π agrees with it (agreement in Lemma 7.9); hence s has VM -true premises and a VM -false
conclusion, and since VM preserves truth along RM -derivations, s ∈     / Sound(RM ). Either way x
has an instance outside Sound(RM ).
      (d)
    Fres (M ) = ∅. For x ∈ B0 \ M this is maximality. For x ∈          / B0 , {x} is a d-conflict by
Lemma 7.9(d), applied in the true scenario, so M ∪ {x} is not d-clean by (F1).
    Witness and conclusion. g ∈ B0 \ M , so the first case gives sg ∈ inst(g) \ Sound(RM ). All
admissible scenarios share the data law and the oracle answers, the prover is fixed, and escalation
answers follow the shared practice; so the probability that sg is accepted at a given time is the
same under every admissible target. Under M , δ-soundness relative to RM ∪ RF (d) (M ) = RM
                                                                                       res
bounds it by δ. For the schema-level statement, a schema x outside A = B0 \ Cd (B0 ) lies
                                                                                       S

in Cd (B0 ) (handled above) or outside B0 , and then its descent output is a witness outside
   S

Sound(RM ) for any maximal clean M ⊆ B0 , which is admissible as shown. A policy that includes
x in A′ accepts that witness when the prover queries it, so includes x with probability at most
δ. On the descent path take M = A: at stable depth A is clean at every depth, every schema
                                                                                      (d)
outside A has a descent witness outside Sound(RA ) by the second case, and Fres (A) = ∅ by
Lemma 7.9(d); so A is admissible and the same argument applies.
    (c) The claims were computed by thm56_finite_d.py (well-founded derivations, size counting
distinct judgments) and can be checked by hand. On A1 , AC infers p from q and p → q, and MP


                                                  213
infers ⊥ from p and p → ⊥; the distinct judgments q, p → q, p, p → ⊥, ⊥ have 1+3+1+3+1 = 9
symbols. On A2 , AC infers ⊥ from φ and ⊥ → φ, with 5 + 7 + 1 = 13 symbols. The closure
of A1 under AC alone is {q, p → q, p → ⊥, p}, so {AC} has no refutation on A1 ; and {MP}
is classically sound while both contexts are satisfied by q = 1, p = 0. So for 9 ≤ d ≤ 12,
Cd = {{MP, AC}}. With no world and no trusted steps e is defined only on the assertions and
on ⊥; at ⊥ the producing MP step has the unevaluated premise p and no premise of value 0, so
the descent is blocked, and the fallback returns ∅. The subsets of the practice that are clean at
every depth are ∅ and {MP}; both are admissible, since AC is classically invalid and MP has
instances outside Sound(∅). Under ∅, MP is residual at every depth, so asserting RMP is sound
under both. For d ≥ 13, {AC} is a conflict, so {MP, AC} is no longer minimal; with the default
oracle the blocked refutation of size 9 triggers the fallback with Cd = {{AC}}, and with the
prefer-unblocked oracle the refutation on A2 has an unblocked descent that removes AC; either
way A = {MP}.

F.12     Proof of Theorem 7.22
Construction. Let Σ0 be a finite elementary formal system (Post, 1943; Smullyan, 1961) with
judgments starte that simulates the universal machine on input e from starte and contains the
rule halt ▷ ⊥, as in Theorem 6.33(a). Σ0 has no rules without premises, so without a start
judgment nothing is derivable; in particular Σ0 is clean on [∅ : ∅], and the step ▷starte is not
in Sound(RΣ0 ). Let τe = (▷starte ) and ΣPe = Σ0 ∪ {τe }, with fixed computable frequencies and
instance laws. If φe (e) diverges, ΣPe is clean and is the target, with F = ∅. If φe (e) converges,
Σ0 ∪ {τe } derives ⊥ from ∅, the target is Σ0 , F = {τe } and τe ∈ / Fres ; accepting ▷starte is then a
                 ∗
step outside R ∪ RFres = RΣ0 .
     Uniformity. For each e only one case occurs, so this is not a two-scenario argument; what
is used is that the data laws and the oracle answers Ref d (for every d, by brute force) are
computable uniformly in e.
     Reduction. Let S = {e : ∃t P(the learner accepts ▷ starte by time t) > 12 }. If φe (e) diverges,
the prover queries the genuine step ▷starte in every round, and (b) and continuity of measure give
e ∈ S. If φe (e) converges, (a) gives P(ever accepts) ≤ δ < 12 , so e ∈
                                                                      / S. Hence S is the complement
of the halting set {e : φe (e) converges}. But for a computable learner, with computable data laws,
oracle and prover, the probability of acceptance by time t is lower semicomputable uniformly in
(e, t), so S is Σ1 . This also holds if the learner escalates, because escalation answers follow the
practice ΣPe , which is the same in both cases and decidable by matching. The complement of the
halting set is not Σ1 : a contradiction.

F.13     Proofs for classical propositional logic
Proof of Proposition 7.26. (a) inst(τ ) is substitution-closed and contains an unsound step, and
R∗ is complete for CPC, so by Proposition 3.5 ClR∗ ∪inst(τ ) (∅) contains ⊥. A derivation of ⊥ from
∅ is a refutation of Σ∗ ∪ {τ } relative to [∅ : ∅], so dτ is finite. For d ≥ dτ , Σ∗ ∪ {τ } ∈ Conf d
                                                                    (d)
contains some C ′ ∈ Cd , and τ ∈ C ′ by Lemma 7.4(a). Hence Fres = ∅ for d ≥ maxτ dτ , and by
Theorem 7.14(iii) and Corollary 7.15 TTL asserts only instances of Σ∗ , which are classically
valid.
    (b) {AC, K} is a conflict. K = A → (B → A) gives ⊥ → (p → ⊥) (with A = ⊥, B = p) and
(p → ⊥) → (⊥ → (p → ⊥)) (with A = p → ⊥, B = ⊥). AC, B, A → B ▷ A, applied to these
two gives p → ⊥; applied to p → ⊥ and ⊥ → (p → ⊥) it gives ⊥. This is a refutation relative to


                                                 214
[∅ : ∅]. It is minimal at every depth beyond its size. {K} ⊆ Σ∗ is clean (Lemma 7.4(a)). {AC} is
clean at every depth on these positions: from ∅ it derives nothing, since AC has premises, and
the closure of A1 under AC alone is {q, p → q, p → ⊥, p}. Likewise, with A1 designated, AC
followed by MP derives ⊥, and {MP} is clean, so {AC, MP} is a minimal conflict. Every descent
is blocked. With no world and no trusted steps, e is defined only on the assertions of the position
and on ⊥. An output step would need premises among the assertions (or none) and conclusion ⊥.
No genuine step is such a step, because the positions are satisfiable and Σ∗ is sound; and no AC
step is, because AC with premises in A1 yields only p, and AC needs premises. So the fallback
runs at B0 = ΣP , K (and with A1 also MP) lies in Colld for all large d, and Theorem 7.21(b)
applies.
     (c) τ derives the denied φ′ from the asserted Π′ in one step, a refutation of size at most
|τ | ≤ d (a closed ⊤/⊥ instance has at most the symbols of τ ). So {τ } ∈ Conf d for each τ ∈ F ,
every conflict contains a fallacy and hence a singleton conflict, and the minimal conflicts are
                                             (d)
exactly these singletons. Then Colld = ∅, Fres = ∅, and Lemma 7.11 gives A = Σ∗ on the descent
path, and also in the fallback, where Σ∗ \ Colld (B0 ) ⊆ A ⊆ Σ∗ and Colld (B0 ) ⊆ Colld = ∅.

    The bounded computations behind the Hilbert illustration (hilbert_blame.py, formulas
with at most seven leaves over {p, q, ⊥}; with six leaves S has no instances, and the conflicts
are the same) agree: on {[∅ : ∅], [A1 : ∅]} the minimal conflicts are {AC, K} and {AC, MP} with
diagnoses {AC} and {K, MP}; with the bilateral position [A1 : p] the only minimal conflict is
{AC}; and the closed instance (⊥ → ⊥, ⊥ → (⊥ → ⊥) ▷ ⊥) of AC is falsified. The search for
a matrix witnessing that {S, DN, AC} is clean at every depth (validating S and DN, with AC
designation-preserving, ⊥ undesignated and A1 satisfiable) found none with at most four values
(matrix_witness.py, matrix4.py).

F.14    Proofs for complete decidable theories
We take Σ∗ to be a standard sequent calculus: besides being sound and complete for T -consequence,
it has →-introduction, ∀-introduction, ¬-elimination (deriving ⊢ ⊥ from ⊢ ψ and ⊢ ¬ψ), ex falso
and weakening, as derived rules at least.

Proof of Lemma 7.27. (a) Let Π ▷ j be a step. All premises W -true. Each premise Γ ⊢ φ has a
universal closure true in M; since T is complete, T proves it, and Σ∗ derives the premise from ∅.
Hence ClR∗ (Π) = ClR∗ (∅), which by soundness and completeness of Σ∗ and completeness of T
is the set of W -true sequents; so j ∈ ClR∗ (Π) iff W (j) = 1. Some premise Γ ⊢ φ W -false. By
→I and ∀I, Σ∗ derives from it the sentence ⊢ ∀x̄(γ1 → · · · → γn → φ), which is false in M. T
refutes it, so Σ∗ derives its negation; then ¬E, ex falso and weakening derive every sequent, and
ClR∗ (Π) = J. So Π ▷ j ∈ Sound(R∗ ) iff some premise is W -false or j is W -true, i.e. iff the step
preserves W -truth.
    (b) A schema is a fallacy iff it has an instance outside Sound(R∗ ), iff by (a) it has an instance
with W -true premises and a W -false conclusion. The smallest such instance is a one-step
refutation of {τ } ⊆ Σ∗ ∪ {τ } relative to [∅ : ∅], so dτ ≤ fτ .

Proof of Corollary 7.28. Under the realizability hypothesis Theorem 7.14 applies; (WS) holds
with V = truth in M (of universal closures), the positions being truthful. The oracle is exact:
if B is not d-clean it contains a fallacy (Lemma 7.4(b)), whose smallest falsified instance has
size fτ ≤ d and is found; if B is d-clean the search cannot find a falsified instance of size at
most d, which would be a d-refutation. Each {τ }, τ ∈ F , is then a conflict, and the proof of

                                                215
Corollary 7.25 applies verbatim; truth-soundness follows from Corollary 7.15, since R∗ preserves
truth in M by Lemma 7.27(a).

     The restricted oracle. Suppose W evaluates only quantifier-free sentences with rational
constants, and first-order logic is trusted. The fallacy τ = (▷¬∃x (x · x = 1 + 1)) is a step without
premises whose conclusion is not quantifier-free, so it has no instance that W can evaluate, and
Lemma 7.27(b) fails for this oracle. The position [∃x (x · x = 1 + 1) : ∅] is truthful in R. In the
refutation “⊢ ¬∃x(x · x = 1 + 1) (by τ ); ⊢ ⊥ (by trusted ¬E, with the asserted ∃x(x · x = 1 + 1))”,
the backward rule through ¬E gives ¬∃x(. . . ) the value 0, and the descent outputs τ ’s step: {τ }
is a singleton conflict. Without trusted logic the conflict is the bag {τ, ¬E}; with the bilateral
position [∅ :⊢ ¬∃x(x · x = 1 + 1)] it is {τ }, by a one-step refutation. An earlier version designated
[x · x = 1 + 1 : ∅], which under the universal-closure semantics asserts the false ∀x (x · x = 2) and
so violates (WS).

F.15     Proof of Theorem 7.29
(a) In the refutation, τ ’s step has no premises and ∀E is trusted. The conclusion θ(n̄) is a false
∆0 sentence, so it has value 0; the backward rule through ∀E (whose only premise is ∀x̄θ; for
k variables, through each of the k instantiation steps) gives ∀x̄θ the value 0; and the descent
outputs τ ’s step, whose (empty) premise set has value 1 vacuously. The descent is unblocked, so
by Lemma 7.9(c) only schemas having ⊢ ∀x̄θ as an instance are removed, and these are fallacies;
no axiom of PA is one, since its instances are true. With the prefer-unblocked oracle, as long
as τ remains in B there is an unblocked refutation of size at most d, so the oracle returns an
unblocked one, every call removes at least one fallacy and no genuine schema, and the loop does
not fall back before τ is removed. Size. For one variable, |∀xθ| = |θ| + 2, and θ(n) replaces each
of the m occurrences of x (one symbol) by the numeral S n 0 (n + 1 symbols), so |θ(n)| = |θ| + mn.
The total is 2|θ| + mn + 2 ≤ 2|θ| + m(n + 1) + O(1). For k variables the refutation has k + 1
judgments, each of size at most |θ| + m(n + 1) + O(k). For θ(x) = x < S 5 0 (|θ| = 8, m = 1,
n = 5) the sizes are 10 and 13, total 23. Under the default tie-breaking rule a smaller blocked
refutation through learned axioms could trigger the fallback, and those axioms could become
collateral; and if the refutation ran through Q’s axioms by coherence instead of through W , the
bag would contain them. So computation is subsumed by coherence as a source of refutations
(Lemma 6.19), but not as a source of blame.
     (b) PA ⊬ Con(PA) by Gödel’s second incompleteness theorem (Gödel, 1931), so PA+¬Con(PA)
is consistent. A refutation of Σ∗ ∪ {τ } relative to a position with ∆0 assertions and denials
would derive, in first-order logic, from PA + ¬Con(PA) and true ∆0 leaves, either ⊥ or a false ∆0
sentence. Q ⊆ PA proves every true ∆0 sentence and refutes every false one (Lemma 6.19), so
in either case PA + ¬Con(PA) would be inconsistent. Hence Σ∗ ∪ {τ } is clean at every depth,
τ ∈ Fres , and by Theorem 7.14(iii) τ ∈ A at every depth.
     (c) A false sentence with Σ1 normal form ∃x̄ θ has no witness, so W never verifies one and the
instance is never asserted. A true one has a witness, which enumeration finds after finitely many
W -evaluations, with no computable bound on its size. The disguise ∀y ∃p (Prf PA (p, ⌜⊥⌝) ∨ y ̸= y)
is logically equivalent to ∃p Prf PA (p, ⌜⊥⌝), i.e. to ¬Con(PA), because y ̸= y is refutable; its prenex
normal form is Π2 , so the policy does not apply; and PA plus it is consistent and ∆0 -sound, so
by the argument of (b) it is residual.
     (d) Cleanness. Suppose Σ∗ ⊇ Q and Σ∗ ∪ {τ } ∪ A ∪ ¬D is consistent for every designated
[A : D]. A refutation relative to [A : D] would derive, from Σ∗ ∪ {τ } ∪ A and true ∆0 leaves,


                                                  216
either ⊥, or some δ ∈ D, or a false ∆0 sentence. Q proves the true ∆0 leaves and refutes false
∆0 sentences, so in each case Σ∗ ∪ {τ } ∪ A ∪ ¬D would be inconsistent. Impossibility. Let (φe )
enumerate the Σ2 sentences, let Σ∗ = PA, and consider the practices PA∪{⊢ φe }, with the practice
data given as a computable text. Suppose a computable learner, using the data, Ref d at every
depth and the ∆0 oracle, eventually and permanently asserts ⊢ φe if φe is true, and eventually
and permanently withholds it if φe is false and PA ∪ {⊢ φe } is clean at every depth. Define g(e, s):
if a refutation of PA ∪ {⊢ φe } has been found by stage s of a systematic search (computable, since
each Ref d is), output “false”; otherwise output the learner’s current verdict. A true φe makes
the practice sound, hence clean (Lemma 7.4(a)), so the search never succeeds and g converges to
“true”. A false φe either has a refutation, which the search eventually finds, or is clean, and then
the learner converges to “false”. So lims g(e, s) exists and is the truth value of φe , which makes
Σ2 -truth ∆2 by Shoenfield’s limit lemma (Shoenfield, 1959). But the set of true Σ2 sentences is
Σ2 -complete and therefore not Π2 , let alone ∆2 , by Post’s hierarchy theorem (Theorem 6.22(e)).
Randomized learners (proof sketch). If the learner is randomized and correct in the limit with
probability above 12 , the sets {e : P(eventually and permanently withholds ⊢ φe ) > 12 } and the
corresponding set for assertion are Σ2 , and the same combination would make Σ2 -truth ∆2 .
     (e) Tag count. For every tag, ∆ ≤ (ρi βi − ᾱi )/2 ≤ ρi βi /2 ≤ πi /2, since ρi ≤ 1 and βi ≤ πi . As
P
   i πi = 1, there are at most 1/(2∆) tags. Identification. The axioms of Q and each Con(Tj ) are
ground, and fall under the convention ρi = ci = 1; the Sub-encoded induction rule is a first-order
pattern whose lgg on generic instances recovers it (ind_sub_lgg.py). So Theorem 5.15 applies
and Theorem 7.14 gives P̂ = ΣP . All practice schemas are genuine (every Con(Tj ) is true), so
F = ∅ and the audit, which never removes genuine schemas, returns A = ΣP . Instances of the
encoded rule with a false Sub premise are harmless: Sub judgments are concluded by no practice
schema, so they enter derivations only as leaves, which must be W -true. Without the induction
encoding, the lgg of induction instances is over-general and has false instances (ind_lgg.py); for
the computed one, ∀x(0 + 0 = x → 0 + S0 = Sx) is derivable by trusted equality logic from the
true ∆0 leaf 0 + S0 = S(0 + 0), so backward propagation through the trusted →E and ∀E gives
an unblocked descent, and the audit removes the identified schema with singleton blame.
     The floor bound. Accepting ⊢ Con(Tk−1 ) is unsound under Tk−1 : by Gödel’s second theorem
Con(Tk−1 ) is not a theorem of the consistent r.e. theory Tk−1 , so the step is not derivable in the
target calculus with trusted logic, even with all true Sub judgments as premises, and it lies outside
R∗ ∪ RFres = RTk−1 . (It is true, so this is soundness for the target calculus, Theorem 7.14(i), not
truth-soundness.) Let Et be the event that the tag Con(Tk−1 ) does not occur among the first t
samples. As in Appendix F.10, Pk⊗t restricted to Et is (1 − π)t Pk−1     ⊗t      ⊗t
                                                                             , Pk−1 (Et ) = 1, and under
(OE) the acceptance probability given the samples is the same function ϕ in both scenarios. So
                Z                Z
Pk (accept) =        ϕ dPk⊗t +         ϕ dPk⊗t ≤ (1−π)t Pk−1 (accept)+1−(1−π)t ≤ 1−(1−δ)(1−π)t .
                Et               Etc


Requiring Pk ≥ 1 − δ ′ gives (1 − π)t ≤ δ ′ /(1 − δ), i.e. t ≥ ln 1−δ           1
                                                                              
                                                                         δ′ ln 1−π , positive when

δ + δ < 1. Tightness: the learner that, by one coin at time 0, accepts the step from the
start with probability δ and otherwise accepts it once its tag has occurred is uniformly δ-sound
under Tk−1 , where the tag never occurs, and under Tk accepts at time t with probability
δ + (1 − δ)(1 − (1 − π)t ) = 1 − (1 − δ)(1 − π)t . An earlier version transplanted the bound of

                                                    217
     (f) With [Con(PA) : ∅] designated, τ = (▷¬Con(PA)) and the trusted ¬E derive ⊥ from the
asserted Con(PA). The backward rule gives ¬Con(PA) the value 0, and the descent outputs
τ ’s step, so {τ } is a singleton conflict (compare Theorem 6.38(iii): designating true sentences
removes the unsound alternatives).


G      Proofs for Section 8
Several results of Section 8 are proved in full in the main text: Propositions 8.4, 8.5 and 8.16,
Theorem 8.9, and Corollary 8.10, Lemma 8.8(i)–(ii), and Theorem 8.15 outside its idealized
case. The language-relativity construction is also given in full there. This appendix proves the
remaining results, and states and proves the two lemmas on which the kink rests. The proofs
follow T5 in the form repaired after verification, and the repairs are noted where they occur.
    Conventions:
    • Logarithms are base 2 unless written ln.
    • κ denotes a machine constant. It absorbs O(1) terms and may grow from one result to the
      next, but it is fixed before d, n or m.
    • ph is a shortest program for h, and K(ph ) = K(h) + O(1) (Li and Vitányi, 2008).
    • Prefix complexities of concatenated self-delimiting descriptions add, up to O(1).

G.1     Proof of Lemma 8.2
Both minimizers exist. By Kraft, {h : ℓ(h) ≤ L} has at most 2L elements. Fix h0 . Losses lie in
[0, B], so R(h0 ), R̂N (h0 ) ≤ B. Any h with ℓ(h) > ℓ(h0 ) + B/c therefore has
                        Jc (h), Jˆc (h) ≥ cℓ(h) > cℓ(h0 ) + B ≥ Jc (h0 ), Jˆc (h0 ).
So both infima are minima over a finite set. In particular a population minimizer hc exists, and
both ℓ(hc ) and ℓ(ĥ) are at most ℓ(h0 ) + B/c.
   Uniform deviation. For a fixed h, − log h(yi | xi ) are i.i.d. in [0, B]. By Hoeffding’s inequality,
                                                                         2   2
                                 P |R̂N (h) − R(h)| ≥ ε ≤ 2e−2N ε /B .
                                                          


Setting the right side equal to δ2−ℓ(h) gives ε = εN (h) := B (ℓ(h) ln 2 + ln(2/δ))/(2N ). A union
                                                                     p

bound over h, using h 2−ℓ(h) ≤ 1, shows that with probability at least 1 − δ all deviations are
                      P

at most εN (h) simultaneously.
    Excess risk. On this event,
                Jc (ĥ) ≤ Jˆc (ĥ) + εN (ĥ) ≤ Jˆc (hc ) + εN (ĥ) ≤ Φ(c) + εN (hc ) + εN (ĥ).
                                     p
Both εN terms are at most B ((ℓ(h0 ) + B/c) ln 2 + ln(2/δ))/(2N ).
   Uniqueness. If Jc (h) ≥ Φ(c) + γ for every h ̸= hc , then a bound below γ forces ĥ = hc .

G.3     The kink
The setting is that of Section 8.3. For a product h put sh (x) = h(0 | x) − h(1 | x) and
                ′

Lemma G.1 (List decoding; standard). [T5 Lemma 2.5a] For t ∈ N, |{a′ : ŝh (a′ ) ≥ 2−t }| ≤ 22t .
Hence, if ŝh (a) ≥ 2−t , then
                           K(a | ph ) ≤ 2t + 2 log2 (t + 1) + 2 log2 d + O(1).

                                                      219
Proof. Parseval gives a′ ŝh (a′ )2 = Ex [s2h ] ≤ 1, so at most 22t coefficients are at least 2−t . Given
                       P

specify d and t self-delimitingly, followed by the 2t-bit index of a in the list. The 2 log2 d term is
needed because a program for h need not determine d. For real t, apply the lemma to ⌈t⌉.

   The list-size bound is the standard Parseval (Johnson-type) bound for the Hadamard code, as
used by Goldreich and Levin (1989) and Kushilevitz and Mansour (1993). Only its combination
with the coding theorem is used here.

Proof of Theorem 8.6. (a), upper bounds.
   • Pu is computable from the d − u known bits of a together with d, u and p. So K(Pu ) ≤
     d − u + κ′ , using K(p) = O(log d).
   • The true parity is a component of weight 2−u . Its likelihood is exactly (1 − p)n−pn ppn =
     2−nH(p) , because |E| = pn. Hence − log Pu (y ∗ ) ≤ u + nH(p).
   (a), lower bound on − log Pu (y ∗ ). Every other component fa′ disagrees with y ∗ on w ≥
n/2 − pn points. This is because fa ⊕ fa′ has weight n/2, and flipping E changes at most pn of
them. So the likelihood of fa′ is

                      2−nH(p) (p/(1 − p))w−pn ≤ 2−nH(p) (p/(1 − p))n(1/2−2p) ,

and therefore − log Pu (y ∗ ) ≥ u + nH(p) − log2 1 + 2u (p/(1 − p))n(1/2−2p) . The exponent is
                                                                                 

positive because p < 1/4. The last term is below 2−2800 already at d = 10, p = 1/64 (computed).
    (a), lower bound on K(Pu ). Given a program for Pu and (d, u), compute the a′ that maximizes
Pu (fa′ ), the probability of the label vector fa′ .
   • Every a′ with the true (d − u)-prefix has Pu (fa′ ) ≥ 2−u (1 − p)n , from its own component.
   • Every other a′ is at Hamming distance exactly n/2 from every component, so Pu (fa′ ) =
     (p(1 − p))n/2 .
   • The first is larger, because n2 log2 1−p  n
                                           p > 2 ≥ d ≥ u.
So the argmax has the true prefix, and d ≤ K(a) ≤ K(Pu ) + u + O(log d).
   (a), selection. For every u,

                    λK(Pu ) − log Pu (y ∗ ) ≥ λ(d − u − κ′ ) + u + nH(p) − o(1),

while the value at u = d is at most λκ′ + d + nH(p). A minimizer u∗ therefore satisfies
(λ − 1)(d − u∗ ) ≤ 2λκ′ + o(1). In the computable version the code length is exactly d − u.
The objective at u then exceeds the objective at u = d by (λ − 1)(d − u) + (od − ou ), where
ou := u + nH(p) + log Pu (y ∗ ) = log2 1 + a′ ̸=a qa′ /qa ≥ 0, the sum running over the other
                                             P

components of Pu with likelihoods qa′ , is non-decreasing in u because the families are nested. So
for λ > 1 it is minimized only at u = d.


                                                  221
   (b). Suppose ŝh (a) ≥ 2−t . Then, by Lemma G.1,

      d ≤ K(a) ≤ K(h) + K(a | ph ) + O(1) ≤ K(h) + 2t + 2 log2 (t + 1) + 2 log2 d + O(1).

This contradicts the hypothesis on K(h) once κ exceeds the O(1). So ŝh (a) < 2−t .
  • Lemma G.2 gives R(h) ≥ − log2 ( 21 + 2−t−1 + p) = 1 − log2 (1 + 2−t + 2p).
  • Lemma G.3, with K(h) ≤ d (so εh ≤ εE ) and 1 − 2p > 0, gives R(h) ≥ 1 − log2 (1 + (1 −
    2p)2−t + εE ).
  • Finally, log2 (1 + z) ≤ z/ ln 2.
   (c).
  • Ph (y) := x h(yx | x) is a semi-distribution on {0, 1}n , computable from ph and d. A
                 Q

    program for h need not determine d; omitting this was the error corrected after verification.
  • By the conditional coding theorem, K(y ∗ | ph , d) ≤ − log Ph (y ∗ ) + O(1) = nR(h) + O(1).
    So K(y ∗ ) ≤ K(h) + nR(h) + 2 log2 d + O(1).
                                                                 ∗
  • From y ∗ one computes a. The correlation of (−1)y with χa is 1 − 2p, and with any other
    χa′ it is | − 2E[χa⊕a′ 1E ]| ≤ 2p < 1 − 2p. Then E = {y ∗ ̸= fa }. So K(a, E) ≤ K(y ∗ ) + O(1).
  • Symmetry of information gives K(a, E) = K(a) + K(E | a, K(a)) + O(1) ≥ d + LE − δE −
    O(1).
Combining the last three items proves (c).

G.4       Proof of Lemma 8.8(iii)
Fix z = (ℓz , rz ) ∈ Z and c > 0, and write φc (y) = ⟨(c, 1), y⟩. Compare z with the other points of
Z:
  • Points to the left, Z< = {z ′ : ℓ′ < ℓz }, a finite set. Here φc (z) ≤ φc (z ′ ) iff c ≤ σ(z ′ ) :=
    (r′ − rz )/(ℓz − ℓ′ ), strictly iff c < σ(z ′ ).
  • Points to the right, Z> = {z ′ : ℓ′ > ℓz }. Here φc (z) ≤ φc (z ′ ) iff c ≥ τ (z ′ ) := (rz −r′ )/(ℓ′ −ℓz ),
    strictly iff c > τ (z ′ ).
  • Other points with the same ℓ have a different r, and z beats them iff rz < r′ , for every c.
Let s− := minZ< σ, with s− = ∞ if Z< = ∅, and s+ := max(0, supZ> τ ). If supZ> τ > 0,
the supremum is attained, because τ (z ′ ) ≤ rz /(ℓ′ − ℓz ) and only finitely many points have
ℓ′ ≤ ℓz + rz /s for any s > 0.
    So the set of c > 0 at which z is the unique minimizing point is the open interval (s+ , s− )
when rz < r′ for all same-ℓ points, and is empty otherwise.
    If (s+ , s− ) ̸= ∅, then z is a vertex. Take c in the interval.
  • On Z + R2≥0 , φc is uniquely minimized at z, because φc (z ′ + v) > φc (z ′ ) for v ≥ 0, v ̸= 0.

  • A point of Ĉ is a finite convex combination of points of Z + R2≥0 . Its φc -value equals φc (z)
    only if every point in the combination is z. So z is the unique minimizer of φc over Ĉ.
  • Hence z is an extreme point of Ĉ, supported by a line of slope −c, i.e. a vertex of hull− (Z).
   If z is a vertex, then (s+ , s− ) ̸= ∅. First, extreme points of conv(S) lie in S, and a point
z + v with v ̸= 0 is the midpoint of z ′ + v/2 and z ′ + 3v/2. So vertices lie in Z.
 ′



                                                     222
   • Since z ∈ hull− (Z), some c0 > 0 has z minimizing φc0 . So s+ ≤ c0 ≤ s− , and same-ℓ points
     have r′ > rz .
   • Suppose s+ = s− = c0 . Then some zL ∈ Z< has σ(zL ) = c0 , since Z< is finite, and some
     zR ∈ Z> has τ (zR ) = c0 , since the supremum is attained when positive.
   • Then zL , z and zR lie on one line of slope −c0 , with ℓL < ℓz < ℓR . So z is a proper convex
     combination of zL and zR , and not extreme. This is a contradiction.
     The interval and the hypothesis-level claim. The interval is (s+ , s− ), where s∓ are the absolute
slopes of the edges to the adjacent vertices; at a risk-minimal vertex s+ = 0. Hypotheses with
the same point have equal Jc for every c, so a hypothesis is the unique minimizer on the interval
iff no other hypothesis has its point.
A computed check (computed; T5 c3(ii), test repaired after verification): 300 random point sets and
400 values of c each gave no minimizer off the hull, no monotonicity violation, and every vertex
selectable.

G.6     Proof of Corollary 8.12
The coin region. On the coin region every hypothesis has cross-entropy at least one bit per unit
mass, with equality iff it is uniform there. So R(h) ≥ π0 + j≥1 πj Rj (h), and the region plays
                                                                P

no role in the complexity argument. The lower bound of Theorem 8.11 therefore holds with π0
added. The witnesses hS are uniform on the coin region, at an extra O(1) bits to recognize it.
Hence |Φ(c) − Ψ(c)| ≤ cC ′ + ϵ with Ψ(c) := π0 + j min(c dj , πj ), for all c ≥ 0. At c = 0 both
                                                       P

sides equal π0 .
    Legendre duality. Φ(c) = inf y∈Ĉ ⟨(c, 1), y⟩ is the support function of Ĉ. The lower boundary
ρ̆ of its closure is convex and lower semicontinuous, so ρ̆(k) = supc≥0 [Φ(c) − ck]. Likewise
P (k) := supc≥0 [Ψ(c) − ck].

   • Ψ is concave and piecewise linear, with breakpoints at c = sj and slope        j:sj >c dj between
     them.
   • Its conjugate P is the polygon described in the statement: it starts at P (0) = Ψ(∞) = 1,
     has an edge of slope −sj and length dj for each j in decreasing order of sj , and is flat at
     π0 afterwards. For k < 0, P (k) = +∞.
   The sandwich. Then

G.7    Proof of Theorem 8.13
Upper bound. We list the three models.
   • The coin has K ≤ κ and R = 1.
   • The good model has K ≤ d + κ′ and R = H(p) exactly, since |E| = pn.
   • The error-memorizing model y ∗ has K ≤ d + LE + κ log n (index E among the pn-subsets,
     and give pn) and R = 0.
   Lower bound. Fix h and t.
   • If K(h) ≤ d − τt , then Theorem 8.6(b) gives Jc (h) ≥ R(h) ≥ 1 − βt / ln 2.
   • Otherwise, Theorem 8.6(c) and R ≥ 0 give Jc (h) ≥ cK + max(0, (Ktot − K)/n) with
     K = K(h) > d − τt . As a function of K this is piecewise linear: its slope is c − 1/n below
     Ktot and c above. Note that Ktot − d + τt = LE − δE + s(t) ≥ 0, because LE − δE = K(E |
     a, K(a)) ≥ 0.
        – If c ≥ 1/n, the minimum over K ≥ d − τt is at K = d − τt , with value c(d − τt ) +
          (LE − δE + s(t))/n ≥ c(d − τt ) + (LE − δE − κ)/n.
        – If c < 1/n, the minimum is at K = Ktot , with value cKtot .
In every case Jc (h) is at least the minimum of the three lower-bound terms.
    The 2 log2 d in Ktot comes from the corrected Theorem 8.6(c), and the coin term uses the
sharper βt . Neither changes the switch points beyond their O(log) slack.
Proof of Corollary 8.14. Correlation. By the upper bound of Theorem 8.13, R(h) ≤ Jc (h) ≤
c(d + κ′ ) + H(p) + ξ. Lemma G.2 gives 2−R(h) ≤ 1+ŝ2h (a) + p, i.e. ŝh (a) ≥ 21−R(h) − 1 − 2p.
Substitute the bound on R(h).
    Memorization. By Theorem 8.6(c), K(h) ≥ Ktot − nR(h), so
                                  Jc (h) ≥ cKtot − (cn − 1)R(h).
Combine this with Jc (h) ≤ c(d + κ′ ) + H(p) + ξ and with LE ≥ nH(p) − log2 (n + 1). This gives
      (cn − 1)R(h) ≥ c(LE − δE − 2 log2 d − κ − κ′ ) − H(p) − ξ ≥ (cn − 1)H(p) − cΛ − ξ.
Divide by cn − 1 > 0.
   Numbers for illustration (computed; T5 repair_checks): at d = 20, p = 2−10 and κ = κ′ = ξ = 0,
c = 10−4 gives ŝ ≥ 0.980 and R ≥ H(p) − 2.8 · 10−5 , and c = 10−3 gives ŝ ≥ 0.955.

                                                 225
G.8    Proof of Theorem 8.15(ii) in the idealized setting
Let h be near-optimal, and let c be such that each of v and e satisfies the hypothesis of clause
(a) or that of clause (b) of Theorem 8.11. Suppose v is learned, i.e. ŝh,v ≥ 21 .
   • Then v does not satisfy the hypothesis of (a), so it satisfies that of (b). In particular
     πv ≥ πv ω(dv /4) > c dv , i.e. πv /dv > c.
   • If e satisfied the hypothesis of (a), then πe < c de . That would give πe /de < c < πv /dv ,
     contradicting πv /dv ≤ πe /de .
   • So e satisfies the hypothesis of (b), and is learned.



G.9    Proof of Theorem 8.17
(i).
   • Monotone risk. By Lemma 8.8(ii), for grid values c1 < ci and any population minimizers h
     at c1 and h′ at ci , R(h) ≤ R(h′ ).
   • Eventually exact. For each grid value, Lemma 8.2 gives Jc (ĥc ) → Φ(c) in probability.
     Only finitely many hypotheses have ℓ ≤ ℓ(h0 ) + B/c, so the gap between Φ(c) and the
     best non-minimizer is positive. Hence, with probability tending to one, ĥc is a population
     minimizer for every grid value simultaneously.
   • Uniform validation. Held-out log-losses of the finitely many selected hypotheses converge
     uniformly to their risks, since losses lie in [0, B].
So, with probability tending to one, selection by held-out log-loss picks a grid value whose selected
hypothesis has least risk. By the first item, which holds for any choice of minimizers (including
ties at a hull breakpoint), the smallest grid value always attains this least risk, and it is the
unique choice when its risk is strictly smallest. At finite N the second item may fail at small c,
where the bound of Lemma 8.2 is weakest. That is the caveat in the statement.
    (ii). Strict propriety holds pointwise in x (Gneiting and Raftery, 2007). Integrating over µ,
the teacher channel strictly maximizes the expected score among channels that differ from it
on a set of positive µ-mass. With a growing held-out set, empirical scores converge uniformly
over finitely many candidates. So the criterion selects, among candidates, one with the largest
expected score, i.e. the smallest divergence from P ∗ in the score’s divergence.
    (iii). Any statistic of imitation data has the same law in the two worlds of Theorem 8.15(iv),
while the good c differs between them.

G.10     The rates in Proposition 8.18
Measure log-loss relative to the option none, which gives every candidate probability 1/M .
   • G-contexts, unguarded or guarded. The σ-model gains g per step, by the definition of the
     per-step gain. Mass πG .
   • ¬G-contexts, unguarded. The teacher’s step is a non-σ step. It receives probability ε/M
     instead of 1/M , a loss of log2 (1/ε) per step. Mass π¬G .
   • ¬G-contexts, guarded. The teacher’s step receives 1/(M − 1) instead of the unguarded
     ε/M , a gain of ∆G = log2 (M/ε) − log2 (M − 1) per step over the unguarded option. Note

                                                226
      ∆G > log2 (1/ε), by log2 MM−1 ; this corrects the earlier bound “at most π¬G log2 (1/ε)/kG ”
      for the guard’s rate.
So the unguarded option lowers risk by πG g − π¬G log2 (1/ε) = kσ ρσ at cost kσ . The guard lowers
it by a further π¬G ∆G = kG ρG at cost kG . These are the three points used in the main-text
proof. If the guarded option also models the teacher’s alternative step in ¬G-contexts, its gain
there only increases, and the argument is unchanged with the larger ∆G .
    For the case ρG ≥ ρσ : the middle point lies on or above the chord from (0, 0) to the guarded
point. The lower hull of the three points is then that chord, with slope −(kσ ρσ + kG ρG )/(kσ + kG ),
and apart from ties the selection jumps from guarded to none at that c. In the second numerical
instance of the main text the jump is at c = (0.0022 + 0.0087)/34 ≈ 3.2 · 10−4 .

G.11     Proofs for the division of labour
                                                       P
Throughout, the components are finite, val(S) = j∈S νj , and by separability minimizing Jc
over admissible sets is maximizing val over A. Two facts are used repeatedly.
   • If V+ ∪ {e} ∈
                 / A, then e has a witness inside V+ . Indeed, a minimal W ⊆ V+ with
     W ∪ {e} ∈/ A is minimal among all subsets of V .
   • Conversely, a witness inside V+ makes V+ ∪ {e} inadmissible, by down-closure.

Proof of Theorem 8.20. Step 0: S ∗ (c) is admissible. Apply error-independence with U = V+ .
    Step 1: optimal sets have no negative components. Let S be admissible and optimal. Removing
the components with ν < 0 keeps admissibility (down-closure) and raises the value. So S ⊆ {ν ≥
0}.
    Step 2: optimal sets contain no error inadmissible with V+ . Let E1 := (S ∩F )\{e : V+ ∪{e} ∈
A}, and suppose E1 ̸= ∅.
   • Each e ∈ E1 has a witness inside V+ . Every such witness W is nonempty, because {e} ⊆ S
     is admissible.
   • Since (S ∩ V ) ∪ {e} ⊆ S is admissible, S ∩ V contains no witness of e. So B := V+ \ S
     meets every witness of every e ∈ E1 inside V+ . In particular B ̸= ∅, and B ν > 0.
                                                                              P

   • If E1 contains some e ∈ F+ , then B ν ≥ β(e) > F+ ν ≥ E1 ν, using valid dominance
                                          P                P         P

     and E1 ⊆ {ν ≥ 0}. Otherwise E1 ⊆ {ν = 0}, and E1 ν = 0 < B ν.
                                                   P         P

   • Let S ′ := V+ ∪ ((S ∩ F ) \ E1 ). It is admissible by error-independence with U = V+ .
     Since S ∩ V ⊆ V+ ∪ {ν = 0}, we have S∩V ν = V+ ν − B ν, and therefore val(S ′ ) =
                                            P          P       P

     val(S) + B ν − E1 ν > val(S).
              P      P

This contradicts optimality, so E1 = ∅.
    Step 3: comparison with S ∗ (c). Hence S ∩ V ⊆ V+ ∪ {ν = 0} and S ∩ F ⊆ {e : V+ ∪ {e} ∈
A, νe ≥ 0}. So val(S) ≤ val(S ∗ (c)). Since S ∗ (c) is admissible, it is optimal, and val(S) =
val(S ∗ (c)). Every element of S ∗ (c) has positive value, and S is contained in S ∗ (c) ∪ {ν = 0}, so
equality forces S ⊇ S ∗ (c) and S \ S ∗ (c) ⊆ {ν = 0}. Conversely, every admissible S ∗ (c) ∪ Z with
Z ⊆ {ν = 0} has the optimal value.
    Correction after verification. The original last step argued that “adding V+ \ S keeps
admissibility”. That can fail when S contains zero-valued valid rules. For example, take
V = {v+ , v0 } with ν(v0 ) = 0, and an error e whose only witness is {v+ , v0 }. Then S = {v0 , e} is
admissible, but S ∪ {v+ } is not. The direct comparison in Step 3 avoids this.

                             / A iff it fails at least one of them. An error with νe ≤ 0 (rate at
most c) is excluded by simplicity, up to zero-value ties. A valid rule with νv ≤ 0 is not in V+ ,
whatever the filters.

H      Proofs for Section 9
The following results are proved completely in the main text: Propositions 9.3, 9.20, 9.27 and 9.31,
Theorems 9.4, 9.14, 9.25, 9.29 and 9.35, and Corollary 9.23, and Theorem 9.15(a). In Example 9.13
the first claim is a one-line check (the union bound of p ∧ q ▷ p is (1 − 0.9) + 0.2 = 0.3 < 1), and
the facet list is computed (research/theory/T6-checks/prob_coherence.py); it is also easily
verified by hand from the four vertices (0, 0, 1), (0, 1, 1), (1, 0, 0), (1, 1, 1). Theorems 9.33, 9.34
and 9.37 and Beth’s theorem in Proposition 9.36 are cited, not reproved; the non-standardness
clause of Theorem 9.33 and the examples after Proposition 9.36 are argued in the main text.
Everything else is proved below. Throughout, “theory” means closed set.

H.1     The abstract frame
Proof of Lemma 9.2. Both sides of K ⊆ Mod(Σ) ⇐⇒ Σ ⊆ Th(K) say that m |= σ for all
m ∈ K, σ ∈ Σ. Both maps are inclusion-reversing. Taking K = Mod(Σ) gives Σ ⊆ ThMod(Σ),
and taking Σ = Th(K) gives K ⊆ ModTh(K); so both composites are extensive and monotone.
Applying the inclusion-reversing Mod to Σ ⊆ ThModΣ gives ModThModΣ ⊆ ModΣ, and the
converse is extensivity of ModTh at ModΣ; so ModThMod = Mod, and dually ThModTh = Th.
Idempotence of both composites follows. The fixed points of Th ◦ Mod are exactly the sets
Th(K), those of Mod ◦ Th the sets Mod(Σ), and the two identities show that Mod and Th are
mutually inverse on them. Fixed-point sets of closure operators on power sets are closed under
arbitrary intersections, so they are complete lattices.

Proof of Theorem 9.5. We first record a consequence of finitarity: the union U of a nonempty
chain of closed sets is closed. Indeed C(U ) = {C(U0 ) : U0 ⊆ U finite}, each finite U0 lies in a
                                                S

single member T of the chain, and C(U0 ) ⊆ C(T ) = T ⊆ U .
    (a) Let T be closed and σ ∈  / T . The family of closed T ′ ⊇ T with σ ∈/ T ′ is nonempty (it
contains T ) and, by the observation, closed under unions of nonempty chains. By Zorn’s lemma
it has a maximal member Tσ . Then Tσ =   ̸ S, and every closed T ′ ⊋ Tσ contains σ by maximality;
so Tσ is a point containing T . Hence {P ∈ Pt(C) : P ⊇ T } ⊆ σ∈T         / Tσ ⊆ T , since τ ∈
                                          T                           T
                                                                                             / T
implies τ ∈/ Tτ . The reverse inclusion is trivial. If T = S there is no point above T and the
empty intersection is S.
    (b) (⇐) Soundness gives C(X) ⊆ Th(Mod(X)). Conversely, by (a), C(X) = i Pi for the
                                                                                      T

points Pi ⊇ C(X). By hypothesis Pi = Th({mi }), and mi ∈ Mod(X) because X ⊆ Pi . So
Th(Mod(X)) ⊆ i Th({mi }) = C(X).
                 T

    (⇒) Let P be a point, with witness σ. By strong completeness, P = C(P ) = Th(Mod(P )) =
  m∈Mod(P ) Th({m}). If Mod(P ) = ∅ the right-hand side is S ̸= P ; so Mod(P ) ̸= ∅. Each

Th({m}) with m ∈ Mod(P ) is closed, by soundness, and contains P . If each of them strictly

contained P , each would contain σ, and so would their intersection P , which is impossible. So
P = Th({m}) for some m.

closed set. (⇐) If X is coherent, C(X) is proper and extends to a maximal proper closed T ;
by hypothesis T = Th({m}), and m ∈ Mod(X). (⇒) Let T be maximal proper closed. Since
C(T ) = T ̸= S, weak completeness gives m ∈ Mod(T ). Then T ⊆ Th({m}) ̸= S, and Th({m})
is closed by soundness; maximality gives T = Th({m}). Soundness is needed for (⇒). Let
S = {a, b, ⊥} with closed sets ∅, {a}, S (so C is finitary and C({⊥}) = S), and let M = {m}
with Th({m}) = {a, b} ̸= S. The C-coherent sets are ∅ and {a}, both satisfied by m, so C
is weakly complete; {a} is maximal proper closed but differs from Th({m}) = {a, b}, which
is not closed, so C is unsound (C({b}) = S ̸⊆ ThMod({b}) = {a, b}). repair_checks.py (A)
reproduces it and finds no failure of (b) or (c) on 1,602 random sound finite frames.

Details for Proposition 9.6. Soundness. Every Boolean valuation is a model of IPC, so each
Th({v}) is IPC-closed. Failure of strong completeness. p ∨ ¬p is true in every Boolean valuation,
and refuted in the three-element Heyting algebra by h(p) = 12 , so IPC ⊬ p∨¬p. Weak completeness.
The hypotheses of Theorem 9.5(c) hold with F = {⊥}, and Th({v}) ̸∋ ⊥. Let T be a maximal
proper IPC-closed set; proper means consistent, as {⊥} is explosive. If φ ∈/ T , then CIPC (T ∪ {φ})
is closed and strictly larger than T , hence equals Fm; so T, φ ⊢IPC ⊥ and, by the deduction
theorem, ¬φ = φ → ⊥ ∈ T . So φ ∈ T or ¬φ ∈ T , whence φ ∨ ¬φ ∈ T for every φ. As
consequence relations CPC = IPC + EM, where EM is the set of instances of excluded middle;
so CCPC (T ) = CIPC (T ∪ EM) = CIPC (T ) = T ̸∋ ⊥. Thus T is CPC-consistent, and maximal
CPC-consistent (any CPC-consistent superset is IPC-consistent), hence T = v −1 (1) for a Boolean
v. Theorem 9.5(c) concludes.

Proof of Lemma 9.7. Let T be maximal among Cn2 -theories omitting φ, and suppose neither ψ
nor ¬ψ is in T . By maximality φ ∈ Cn2 (T ∪ {ψ}) and φ ∈ Cn2 (T ∪ {¬ψ}). By proof by cases,
Cn2 (T ∪ {ψ}) ∩ Cn2 (T ∪ {¬ψ}) = Cn2 (T ) = T , so φ ∈ T , a contradiction. Hence T is complete
(it contains ψ or ¬ψ for every ψ). Since T ≠ Fm it is consistent, and a complete consistent
theory is maximal consistent.

H.2     Positions
Proof of Theorem 9.8. (a) Order the coherent positions extending [X : Y ] componentwise. A
union of a nonempty chain [Xk : Yk ] is coherent: an incoherence witness Γ ▷ ∆ with Γ ⊆ Xk ,
                                                                                                    S

∆ ⊆ Yk involves finitely many formulas, so it lies in a single [Xk : Yk ]. Zorn gives a maximal
     S

coherent [X ∗ : Y ∗ ]. Disjoint: if φ ∈ X ∗ ∩ Y ∗ , then {φ} ▷ {φ} ∈ ⊢ by (Ov) witnesses incoherence.
Exhaustive: suppose φ ∈   / X ∗ ∪ Y ∗ . By maximality both [X ∗ ∪ {φ} : Y ∗ ] and [X ∗ : Y ∗ ∪ {φ}] are
incoherent, so there are Γ1 , φ ▷ ∆1 and Γ2 ▷ φ, ∆2 in ⊢ with Γi ⊆ X ∗ , ∆i ⊆ Y ∗ . (If φ does not
occur in a witness, that witness already shows [X ∗ : Y ∗ ] incoherent.) By (Wk) both hold with
context Γ1 ∪ Γ2 , ∆1 ∪ ∆2 ; (Cut) on φ gives Γ1 ∪ Γ2 ▷ ∆1 ∪ ∆2 ∈ ⊢, which witnesses incoherence
of [X ∗ : Y ∗ ]: a contradiction. So v = χX ∗ is a valuation with v −1 (0) = Y ∗ . It satisfies every
Γ ▷ ∆ ∈ ⊢, since otherwise Γ ⊆ X ∗ and ∆ ⊆ Y ∗ . And v[X] = 1, v[Y ] = 0.
    (b) For v ∈ Val(⊢), the position [v −1 (1) : v −1 (0)] is coherent, because v satisfies every sequent
of ⊢. It is maximal: a proper extension puts some φ on both sides, which (Ov) makes incoherent.
Conversely, a maximal coherent position is, by the proof of (a), of this form.

                                                  230
Proof of Corollary 9.9. Soundness, ⟨S⟩ ⊆ Th(Mod(S)): the sequents valid in every v ∈ Mod(S)
contain S and form a Scott relation. Every valuation satisfies the (Ov) instances and preserves
(Wk); for (Cut), if v[Γ] = 1 and v[∆] = 0, the premises force v(φ) = 0 and v(φ) = 1. Completeness.
If Γ0 ▷ ∆0 ∈/ ⟨S⟩, then [Γ0 : ∆0 ] is ⟨S⟩-coherent, since a witness inside it would yield Γ0 ▷ ∆0 by
(Wk). Theorem 9.8 gives v ∈ Val⟨S⟩ ⊆ Mod(S) realizing [Γ0 : ∆0 ], i.e. violating Γ0 ▷ ∆0 .

Proof of Theorem 9.10. Syntactic closure. By Corollary 9.9, Th ◦ Mod on sets of sequents is
S 7→ ⟨S⟩, so its fixed points are exactly the Scott relations. Semantic closure. For V ⊆ 2Fm ,
Mod(Th(V )) = V . The set Mod(Th(V )) is an intersection of the sets {v : v |= Γ ▷ ∆}, each
of which is clopen (it depends on finitely many coordinates); so it is closed, and it contains V ;
hence it contains V . Conversely, let w ∈
                                        / V . Some basic open neighbourhood of w, determined
by finitely many coordinates, misses V ; it has the form U (Γ, ∆) = {v : v[Γ] = 1, v[∆] = 0} with
Γ = w−1 (1) ∩ E, ∆ = w−1 (0) ∩ E for a finite E ⊆ Fm. Since U (Γ, ∆) ∩ V = ∅, every v ∈ V
satisfies Γ ▷ ∆, so Γ ▷ ∆ ∈ Th(V ); and w ∈ U (Γ, ∆) violates it. So w ∈  / Mod(Th(V )). This is
Lemma 6.26(a), formalized as Carnap.Val_mrel. Duality. By Lemma 9.2, Mod = Val and Th
are mutually inverse inclusion-reversing bijections between the fixed points on the two sides, i.e.
between Scott relations and closed subsets of 2Fm .

    The Stone-duality reading after Theorem 9.10 needs only Stone’s theorem (Stone, 1936), the
observation that the clopen set of the clause γ∈Γ ¬γ ∨ δ∈∆ δ is {v : v |= Γ▷∆}, and conjunctive

normal form in B. scott_duality.py checks Corollary 9.9 on 300 random sequent sets over
four formulas, and Theorem 9.10 exhaustively over three formulas (all 256 sets of valuations are

Proof of Theorem 9.11. For every substitution σ and valuation v, (v ◦ σ)(φ) = v(σφ), so

                                 v ◦ σ |= Γ ▷ ∆    iff   v |= σΓ ▷ σ∆.                               (2)

(a) (⇒) Let ⊢ be structural and v ∈ Val(⊢). For Γ ▷ ∆ ∈ ⊢ we have σΓ ▷ σ∆ ∈ ⊢, so v satisfies
it, so v ◦ σ |= Γ ▷ ∆ by (2). Hence v ◦ σ ∈ Val(⊢). (⇐) Let Val(⊢) be substitution-invariant
and Γ ▷ ∆ ∈ ⊢. For every v ∈ Val(⊢), v ◦ σ ∈ Val(⊢) satisfies Γ ▷ ∆, so v |= σΓ ▷ σ∆. Thus
σΓ ▷ σ∆ ∈ Th(Val(⊢)) = ⊢, the last equality by Corollary 9.9 since ⊢ is a Scott relation. The
duality statement is the restriction of Theorem 9.10: a closed substitution-invariant V equals
Val(Th(V )), so Th(V ) is structural by (⇐).
    (b) An assignment into Lv = ⟨Fm, v −1 (1)⟩ is a homomorphism Fm → Fm, i.e. a substitution
σ, and it satisfies Γ ▷ ∆ in the matrix iff not (σΓ ⊆ v −1 (1) and σ∆ ∩ v −1 (1) = ∅), iff v |= σΓ ▷ σ∆,
iff v ◦ σ |= Γ ▷ ∆. So Lv validates Γ ▷ ∆ iff v ◦ σ |= Γ ▷ ∆ for all σ. If ⊢ is structural and
Γ ▷ ∆ ∈ ⊢, this holds by (a). If Γ ▷ ∆ ∈    / ⊢, the position [Γ : ∆] is coherent (a witness inside it
would give Γ ▷ ∆ by (Wk)), so Theorem 9.8 gives v ∈ Val(⊢) with v[Γ] = 1, v[∆] = 0, and the
identity assignment in Lv violates Γ ▷ ∆. Likewise, a coherent position [X : Y ] is realized by
some v ∈ Val(⊢), and the identity assignment in Lv designates X and no member of Y .

Proof of Proposition 9.16. (⇒) If P = v µ(v)v̄, the three conditions hold valuation by valuation:
