                                                  232
Lemma H.1 (Elementary consequences of Gaifman coherence). Let P satisfy (G1), (G2). Then:
(i) P (¬φ) = 1−P (φ); (ii) if ⊢ φ ↔ ψ then P (φ) = P (ψ); (iii) if ⊢ φ → ψ then P (φ) ≤ P (ψ); (iv)
max(P (φ), P (ψ)) ≤ P (φ∨ψ) ≤ P (φ)+P (ψ); (v) P (φ)+P (ψ)−1 ≤ P (φ∧ψ) ≤ min(P (φ), P (ψ)).

Proof. (i) Apply (G2) and (G1) to φ ∨ ¬φ. (ii) φ ∨ ¬φ and ψ ∨ ¬φ are both provable, and both
pairs are provably exclusive; so P (φ) + P (¬φ) = 1 = P (ψ) + P (¬φ). (iii) ψ is provably equivalent
to φ ∨ (ψ ∧ ¬φ), a provably exclusive disjunction, so P (ψ) = P (φ) + P (ψ ∧ ¬φ) ≥ P (φ) by (ii)
and (G2). (iv) The lower bound is (iii). For the upper bound, P (φ ∨ ψ) = P (φ) + P (ψ ∧ ¬φ) ≤
P (φ) + P (ψ) by (ii), (G2) and (iii). (v) The upper bound is (iii). For the lower bound,
1 − P (φ ∧ ψ) = P (¬φ ∨ ¬ψ) ≤ P (¬φ) + P (¬ψ) by (i), (ii), (iv).

Proof of Theorem 9.17. (⇐) If P (φ) = µ([φ]): a provable φ lies in every complete consistent
theory, so [φ] = SL ; and if ⊢ ¬(φ ∧ ψ), then [φ] ∩ [ψ] = ∅ and [φ ∨ ψ] = [φ] ∪ [ψ]. (⇒) By the
completeness theorem, [φ] = [ψ] iff ⊢ φ ↔ ψ, and [φ] ∩ [ψ] = ∅ iff ⊢ ¬(φ ∧ ψ). Every clopen subset
of SL is compact, hence a finite union of basic clopens, hence of the form [φ]. So µ0 ([φ]) := P (φ)
is a well-defined function on the clopen algebra (Lemma H.1(ii)), with µ0 (SL ) = P (⊤) = 1,
nonnegative, and finitely additive by (G2). It is countably additive on the clopen algebra: if
      F
[φ] = n [φn ], compactness of [φ] and openness of the [φn ] give a finite subcover, and disjointness
makes all other [φn ] empty. By Carathéodory’s extension theorem µ0 extends to a probability on
the σ-algebra generated by the clopens. Since L is countable there are countably many clopens,
and they form a basis, so every open set is a countable union of clopens and this σ-algebra is the
Borel σ-algebra. Uniqueness: the clopens form a π-system generating it (π–λ theorem).

Proof of Theorem 9.18. Every sentence is provably equivalent to one built from atomic sentences
by ¬, ∧, ∃, and both P (Lemma H.1(ii)) and truth in N respect provable equivalence; so it suffices
to treat such sentences. We show P (φ) = [N |= φ] by induction on the number of logical symbols,
simultaneously for all such sentences. Atomic sentences are quantifier-free. A true one has P = 1
by hypothesis. If σ is false, ¬σ is a true quantifier-free sentence, so P (σ) = 1 − P (¬σ) = 0.
Negation: P (¬φ) = 1 − P (φ) (Lemma H.1(i)). Conjunction: if P (φ), P (ψ) ∈ {0, 1}, the
bounds of Lemma H.1(v) coincide and give P (φ ∧ ψ) = min(P (φ), P (ψ)). Existential: numerals
i = S · · · S0 contain no logical symbols, so each instance φ(i) has fewer logical symbols than
∃x φ(x), and by induction P (φ(i)) = [N |= φ(i)]. By Lemma H.1(iv), applied repeatedly,
maxi≤n P (φ(i)) ≤ P ( i≤n φ(i)) ≤ i≤n P (φ(i)), so P ( i≤n φ(i)) = maxi≤n P (φ(i)) ∈ {0, 1}.
                        W            P                    W

By the Gaifman condition, P (∃x φ(x)) is 1 iff some φ(i) is true in N, iff N |= ∃x φ(x), because
every natural number is the value of a numeral.

Proof of Corollary 9.19. We use three properties from Garrabrant et al. (2016): a market is a
computable sequence of rational pricings (Pn ); by Limit Coherence the limit P∞ (φ) = limn Pn (φ)
exists and is a coherent probability relative to Γ; and by Non-Dogmatism Γ ⊬ ¬φ implies
P∞ (φ) > 0. (We cite these properties by name; we have confirmed the statements but not the
theorem numbering of the arXiv version.) By Limit Coherence, P∞ is Gaifman-coherent and
gives 1 to every theorem of Γ, in particular to every true quantifier-free sentence, which Q ⊆ Γ
proves. Suppose it satisfied the Gaifman condition. Then P∞ = δTh(N) by Theorem 9.18. Two
arguments refute this. Via Non-Dogmatism. By the Gödel–Rosser theorem (Gödel, 1931; Rosser,
1936), the consistent r.e. Γ ⊇ Q is incomplete: some σ has Γ ⊬ σ and Γ ⊬ ¬σ. Let ψ be whichever
of σ, ¬σ is false in N. Then Γ ⊬ ¬ψ (as ¬¬σ is equivalent to σ), so P∞ (ψ) > 0 = δTh(N) (ψ). Via
∆2 . Since (n, φ) 7→ Pn (φ) is computable, “Pn (φ) > 12 ” is decidable. If the limit were δTh(N) ,
then φ ∈ Th(N) iff ∃N ∀n ≥ N Pn (φ) > 21 (a Σ2 condition) iff ∀N ∃n ≥ N Pn (φ) > 12 (a Π2

                                                233
condition): if P∞ (φ) = 1 both hold, and if P∞ (φ) = 0 neither does. So Th(N) would be ∆2 ,
contradicting Tarski’s theorem that Th(N) is not arithmetical. The general statement. The
∆2 argument used only that the credence is the pointwise limit of a computable sequence of
rational-valued functions, that it is coherent, and that it gives 1 to every true quantifier-free
sentence; so no such credence satisfies the Gaifman condition. The quantifier-free hypothesis is
needed. Let T be the theory of the one-element structure, in which S0 = 0 = 0 + 0 = 0 · 0. T is
decidable, so δT is computable and coherent; and it satisfies the Gaifman condition with respect
to the numerals, because the only element is the value of a numeral. It fails the hypothesis only
by giving P (0 = S0) = 1.

H.4    Contexts
Proof of Theorem 9.22. Note first that, since Ci = Thi ◦ Modi , every set of the form Thi (K) is
Ci -closed (Lemma 9.2: Thi Modi Thi = Thi ), and Thi Modi (T ) = T for every Ci -closed T . (a)
For every i and φ: cΓ |= i:φ iff Modi (Ti ) ⊆ Modi (φ) iff φ ∈ Thi Modi (Ti ) = Ti . Hence cΓ satisfies
Ki ∪ Γi ⊆ Ti . It is bridge-compatible: if cΓ |= jk :φk for all premises of a bridge rule, then
φk ∈ Tjk , so ψ ∈ Ti by (Br), so cΓ |= i:ψ. (b) Let c |= Γ be a model and Ui = Thi (ci ). Each Ui is
Ci -closed and contains Ki ∪ Γi . The family (Ui ) is closed under bridges: if φk ∈ Ujk for all k,
then c |= jk :φk , so c |= i:ψ by bridge-compatibility, i.e. ψ ∈ Ui . By leastness of Der(Γ), Ti ⊆ Ui ,
so every m ∈ ci satisfies Ti : ci ⊆ Modi (Ti ) = cΓi . (c) (⇒) If φ ∈ Ti and c |= Γ is a model, then
by (b) every m ∈ ci ⊆ cΓi satisfies φ. (⇐) If φ ∈   / Ti , then cΓ ̸|= i:φ by (a), and cΓ is a model of
Γ.

Proof of Remark 9.24. With Mi = Fix(Ci ) \ {Li } we have Thi ◦ Modi = Ci : if Ci (X) ̸= Li ,
then Ci (X) is itself a model and Thi Modi (X) = {T ∈ Mi : X ⊆ T } = Ci (X); otherwise
                                                       T

Modi (X) = ∅ and Thi (∅) = Li . So Theorem 9.22 applies, and cΓi = Modi (Ti ) is nonempty iff
Ti is proper, since then Ti ∈ cΓi , while no proper closed theory contains Li . A falsum with
Modi (⊥i ) = ∅ is exactly a Ci -explosive ⊥i , and then Ti ̸= Li iff ⊥i ∈
                                                                        / Ti . Without explosiveness
the two readings come apart: let Ci be the identity closure (no learned rules), Ki = {⊥i } and
no bridges into i; then ⊥i ∈ Ti = {⊥i } = ̸ Li , yet {⊥i } ∈ cΓi . repair_checks.py (B) checks the
equivalence on 3,000 random finite closure systems with an explosive falsum.

Proposition H.2 (Equilibria; non-monotone bridges). [T6 Props 4.3, 4.4, as repaired] 
                                                                                       Call S = (Si )
an equilibrium if Si = Ci Ki ∪ Γi ∪ {ψ : (· · · ⇒ i:ψ) has all premises in S} for all i (the
monotone case of Brewka and Eiter 2007, with each context accepting exactly the closure of its
knowledge base). (a) Der(Γ) is the least equilibrium, and there is an equilibrium with ⊥i ∈     / Si
for every i ∈ D iff the system is D-coherent. (b) Let context i have consistent Ki with Ki ⊬ p
and no premises (Γi = ∅), and add the single non-monotone bridge “i:p if not i:p” (negation
as failure). Then Ki is consistent, so the context is coherent without its bridge, yet there is no
equilibrium. So existence for default-style bridges needs an extra condition, such as stratification
or the absence of odd loops through negation as failure; stratification is sufficient, not necessary,
and coherence alone does not supply it.

Proof of Proposition H.2. Write R(S)i for the right-hand side of the equilibrium equation, and
T = Der(Γ). (a) T ⊇ R(T ): each Ti is Ci -closed and contains Ki , Γi and, by (Br), the head of
every bridge rule whose premises lie in T . Conversely the family R(T ) contains K ∪ Γ, is locally
closed, and is bridge-closed: a rule whose premises lie in R(T ) ⊆ T has its premises in T , so its
head lies in R(T ) by definition. By leastness of T , T ⊆ R(T ). So T = R(T ) is an equilibrium.


                                                 234
Any equilibrium S = R(S) is locally closed, contains K ∪ Γ and is bridge-closed, so S ⊇ T . Hence
a D-consistent equilibrium forces T to be D-consistent, and conversely T itself is an equilibrium.
(b) If p ∈ Si , the rule is inapplicable, so Si = Ci (Ki ) ̸∋ p; if p ∈
                                                                      / Si , the rule fires and p ∈ Si .
Either way Si ̸= R(S)i .

Proof of Theorem 9.26. Projection lemma. For a consistent closed theory T over atoms A and
B ⊆ A: Mod(T )|B = ModB (T ∩ L(B)). The inclusion ⊆ is clear. For ⊇, let a B-valuation w
satisfy T ∩ L(B), and suppose T together with the literals over B true in w were unsatisfiable.
By compactness, T ⊢ ¬(l1 ∧ · · · ∧ lr ) for some literals lk true in w; this sentence lies in T ∩ L(B)
and is false in w, a contradiction. By the lemma, mutual conservativity on an edge {i, j} says
Mod(Ti )|Ai ∩Aj = Mod(Tj )|Ai ∩Aj . Gluing. Root the tree at i0 and let mi0 |= Ti0 be given; assign
the atoms of Ai0 accordingly. Visit the remaining vertices in breadth-first order. When c is
visited, its parent p has been visited and its children have not, so p is the only visited neighbour
of c and the tree path from c to any visited vertex passes through p. If an atom a ∈ Ac is already
assigned, it lies in Ad for some visited d; by running intersection it lies in every Ae on the path
from c to d, in particular in Ap . So the assigned atoms of Ac lie in Ac ∩ Ap . After p was visited
the global assignment restricted to Ap was a model mp |= Tp , and assigned atoms never change.
So the current assignment on Ac ∩ Ap is mp |Ac ∩Ap ∈ Mod(Tp )|Ac ∩Ap = Mod(Tc )|Ac ∩Ap , and some
mc |= Tc agrees with it; extend the assignment by mc . Atoms in no Ai are assigned arbitrarily.
At the end the assignment restricted to each Ac is mc |= Tc .

    contexts_local_global.py checks the theorem on one fixed three-node path cover with
7,131 random local-theory systems and on 2,000 random tree covers with 2–5 nodes, with no
failure; a negative control without running intersection fails in 437 of 2,000 cases.

Relation to the context-tree calculus of L7 §8 (proof sketch). L7’s calculus has stipulations
(local axioms), imports π(c):φ ⇒ c:φ for φ in the import filter Fc , discharge c:ψ ⇒ π(c):A → ψ,
and side-conditioned export c:φβ (t), π(c):σβ (t) ⇒ π(c):εβ (t). All are monotone bridge rules, so it
is an MC system, and Theorem 9.22 is its completeness theorem for local-models semantics with
bridges read as compatibility constraints; there an exported ε shrinks the parent’s belief state.
L7’s own semantics differs: the models of the root are fixed by background and observations
and do not depend on exports, and bridges are required to be sound; L7 proves soundness for
it assuming every step and export instance is sound. The two semantics coincide when the
local consequence is the complete base logic and every step and export instance is sound: then
Tc = Th(ModL7 (c)) for every c, where ⊆ is L7’s soundness proposition, and ⊇ goes by induction
down the tree (at the root by local completeness; an idealized child imports exactly Fc ∩ Tπ(c) ; a
supposition child imports all of Tπ(c) and its assumption). Without that hypothesis they differ: if
an unsound export yields @:Q = 5 while background and observations prove Q = 3, MC derives
@:⊥ and cΓ@ = ∅, as Theorem 9.22 requires, while L7’s model class of the root stays nonempty. For
supposition contexts with full import, the canonical chain always satisfies cΓc = cΓπ(c) ∩ Mod(A).

H.5     Learned calculi
Existence of the Leibniz congruence. The join of a family of congruences of A is the
transitive closure of their union. If each congruence in the family is compatible with D, then a
and b related by the join are connected by a chain a = c0 θ1 c1 · · · θr cr = b with each θk in the
family; each step preserves membership in D, so the join is compatible. The join of all compatible
congruences is therefore the largest one.

                                                  235
Proof of Theorem 9.30. (a) Claim: Ω(T ) = h−1 (ΩA D), where h−1 (θ) = {(a, b) : (ha, hb) ∈ θ}.
The preimage of a congruence under a homomorphism is a congruence. It is compatible
with T : if (ha, hb) ∈ ΩA D and a ∈ T , then ha ∈ D, so hb ∈ D and b ∈ T . Maximality:
let θ′ be any congruence of Fm compatible with T . The kernel ker h is compatible with T ,
as T = h−1 (D) is a union of ker h-classes, so θ′′ = θ′ ∨ ker h is compatible with T . Let
θA = {(ha, hb) : (a, b) ∈ θ′′ }. It is reflexive because h is onto, and symmetric. It is transitive:
if (ha, hb), (hb′ , hc) ∈ θA come from pairs in θ′′ with hb = hb′ , then b θ′′ b′ because ker h ⊆ θ′′ ,
so a θ′′ c. It is compatible with the operations: if (xl , yl ) ∈ θA come from (al , bl ) ∈ θ′′ , then for
an operation f , (f (x̄), f (ȳ)) = (hf (ā), hf (b̄)) with f (ā) θ′′ f (b̄). And it is compatible with D:
if (ha, hb) ∈ θA comes from (a, b) ∈ θ′′ and ha ∈ D, then a ∈ T , so b ∈ T and hb ∈ D. Hence
θA ⊆ ΩA D, and θ′ ⊆ θ′′ ⊆ h−1 (θA ) ⊆ h−1 (ΩA D). Isomorphism. Define [a]Ω(T ) 7→ [ha]ΩA D . By
the claim it is well defined and injective; it is onto because h is; it is a homomorphism because h
is; and it maps T /Ω(T ) onto D/ΩA D because a ∈ T iff ha ∈ D. If ⟨A, D⟩ is reduced, ΩA D is
the identity and the right-hand side is ⟨A, D⟩. (b) A maximal consistent theory of CPC is v −1 (1)
for a Boolean homomorphism v : Fm → 2. This v is onto, since v(p ∨ ¬p) = 1 and v(p ∧ ¬p) = 0.
The matrix ⟨2, {1}⟩ is reduced: the only congruence of 2 besides the identity identifies 0 with 1,
which is not compatible with {1}. Apply (a).

    repair_checks.py (D) checks a non-surjective example for the remark after Theorem 9.30:
in the four-element Heyting chain 0 < a < b < 1, h(p) = a generates {0, a, 1} =   ∼ H3 , which is
reduced, so the reduced Lindenbaum matrix of h−1 (1) is ⟨H3 , {1}⟩, not the intended four-element
matrix.

Verification of Example 9.32. (i) h : Fm → H3 is a Heyting homomorphism with h(p) = 12 ,
h(⊥) = 0, h(⊤) = 1, so it is onto. T = h−1 (1) is an IPC-theory by soundness of IPC for Heyting
algebras, and p ∈/ T . T is maximal among theories omitting p: let ψ ∈     / T , so h(ψ) ∈ {0, 12 }. If
h(ψ) = 12 then h(ψ ↔ p) = 1, so ψ ↔ p ∈ T and any theory containing T and ψ contains p. If
h(ψ) = 0 then h(¬ψ) = 1, so ¬ψ ∈ T and any theory containing T and ψ contains ⊥, hence p.
So every theory strictly above T contains p, and T is a point. It is not maximal consistent, since
h(p ∨ ¬p) = 12 ∨ 0 = 12 puts neither p nor ¬p in T . ⟨H3 , {1}⟩ is reduced: a congruence identifying
1                                                                  1
2 with 1 is not compatible with {1}; one identifying 0 with 2 also identifies 0 → 0 = 1 with
1
2 → 0 = 0, hence everything, and is not compatible either. By Theorem 9.30(a) the reduced
Lindenbaum matrix of T is ⟨H3 , {1}⟩. (ii) In a one-element structure ∀x∃y φ and ∃y∀x φ are
equivalent for every formula φ, so every instance of the schema is sound there; with equality, a
structure in which every instance of the schema holds must have one element (take R(x, y) to
be x = y: ∀x∃y x = y holds in every structure, and ∃y∀x x = y says there is one element). So
the models of first-order logic plus the schema are exactly the one-element structures, and a
designated context asserting two distinct objects has none.


I    Proofs for Section 10
Lemma 10.6, Proposition 10.7, Theorem 10.8, Proposition 10.11, Proposition 10.17, Lemma 10.20,
Theorem 10.22(a), Theorem 10.24, Proposition 10.28 and Theorem 10.36 are proved in full in the
main text. This appendix proves the remaining results of Section 10 and verifies Example 10.16.
Notation is as in Section 10. Throughout, ClR is a closure operator (extensive, monotone,
idempotent; Lemma 2.3), and for a first-order complete h and finite Γ ∪ {y} ⊆ Dh we use freely
that Γ |=h y iff Th ∪ ρh Γ |= ρh y iff Th ⊢ ρh Γ → ρh y (Gödel completeness).

Proof of Theorem 10.10. (a) Let the oracle-answered escalations of an honest prover be s1 , s2 , . . . ,

data lie in B: then VS consists exactly of the hu with u ∈      / {s1 , . . . , si−1 } when si is queried, and
si is escalated. For the lower bound, take practice distributions supported on B and identical for
all hypotheses (shadow-measurable), and the honest prover that queries s1 , . . . , sn−1 in order.
Under target hsi the step si is invalid, and the answers to s1 , . . . , si−1 are the same (“yes”) under
hu∗ and hsi . So the law of the transcript up to the verifier’s answer in round i is the same under
both targets, and δ ′ -soundness under hsi gives Phu∗ [si accepted in round i] ≤ δ ′ . Summing, the
expected number of these honest queries that are not accepted (escalated or rejected) under hu∗ is
at least (1−δ ′ )(n−1) = (1−δ ′ )(1/W ∗ −1) for the uniform prior; a verifier that never rejects, such
as Vtg,δ , escalates them. With δ = W ∗ δ ′ = δ ′ /n, the bound of (b) is n(ln n + ln(1/δ ′′ ))/δ ′ , and the
ratio to the lower bound is at most 2(ln n + ln(1/δ ′′ ))/(δ ′ (1 − δ ′ )) = O((ln(1/W ∗ ) + ln(1/δ ′′ ))/δ ′ )
for n ≥ 2 and δ ′ ≤ 1/2. This is the argument of T1 Thm 3.2 and Cor 4.5 (Section 4).

I.2    Identification
Proof of Proposition 10.13. Write the interaction as a sequence of rounds. In round k + 1 the
learner (and any prover whose strategy depends on the target only through its shadow) issues
queries Qk+1 drawn from a kernel that depends only on the history Hk and its own coins; then
the environment issues a datum Dk+1 . By the assumptions, the conditional law of Dk+1 given
(Hk , Qk+1 ) is K(sh, Hk , Qk+1 ) for a kernel K and the shadow sh of the target: practice data
depend on Stgh∗ and the history; escalation and link answers are Stgh∗ -labels; object data depend
on Vh∗ and the history; and paradoxes are computed by the learner from the fixed family A and
the hypotheses themselves, independently of the target. (Since h ≡g h′ includes agreement on
coherence, the same A satisfies the designation promise under both targets.) Let Ph be the law
of the interaction under target h. By induction on k, Ph and Ph′ agree on (Hk , Qk+1 ): the step
from k to k + 1 composes the same kernels. Hence they agree on all cylinder sets, and so on the
whole sequence. Any learner’s output is a function of the history and its coins, so it has the same
law under h and h′ . The closure-level version is the same argument with |= in place of Stg .

Proof of Lemma 10.14. Write D = Dh and T = Th .
   (i) ⊆: let v = vM ◦ ρh with M |= T . Since T ⊢ ρh (∼x) ↔ ¬ρh (x), v(∼x) = 1 − v(x). If
Γ ⊆ v −1 (1) is finite and Γ |=h y, then M |= ρh Γ and T ∪ ρh Γ |= ρh y, so v(y) = 1.
    ⊇: let v be a coherent valuation and Θ := T ∪ {ρh (x) : v(x) = 1}. Suppose Θ is inconsistent.
By compactness some finite Γ ⊆ v −1 (1) makes T ∪ ρh Γ inconsistent, so Γ |=h y for every y ∈ D.
Pick x ∈ D (here D ̸= ∅ is used); one of x, ∼x has v-value 0, call it y; but Γ |=h y forces v(y) = 1,
a contradiction. So Θ has a model M . If v(x) = 1 then M |= ρh (x). If v(x) = 0 then v(∼x) = 1,
so M |= ρh (∼x) and hence M |= ¬ρh (x). Thus v = vM ◦ ρh ∈ Vh .
   (ii) (⇒) is (i) ⊆, together with the domain clause in the definition of |=h . (⇐) If Γ ∪ {y} ⊆ D
and Γ ̸|=h y, then T ∪ ρh Γ ∪ {¬ρh y} is consistent, by completeness; a model M of it gives
v = vM ◦ ρh ∈ Vh with v(Γ) = 1 and v(y) = 0.
   (iii) A is h-coherent iff ⊥ ∈ / ClRh (ρh A) iff T ∪ ρh A is consistent iff it has a model M , iff
vM ◦ ρh is a member of Vh with v(A) = 1. For the second equivalence: if A is coherent, pick



                                                     238
x ∈ D; in a model of T ∪ ρh A one of x, ∼x is false, call it y, and then A ̸|=h y. If A is incoherent,
T ∪ ρh A is inconsistent and A |=h y for every y ∈ D.
    The converses. If Vh ̸= ∅, D is the common domain of its members and (ii) recovers |=h .
Coherence of finite subsets of D recovers |=h , since for Γ ∪ {y} ⊆ D, Γ |=h y iff T ∪ ρh Γ ∪ {¬ρh y}
is inconsistent iff Γ ∪ {∼y} is incoherent. That neither holds unconditionally: two hypotheses
with inconsistent theories and different domains both have V = ∅; and two hypotheses that differ
only on steps avoiding every member of a fixed A agree on coherence on A.

    The key step in Theorem 10.15 is the following lemma.

Lemma I.1 (Upper bracket). Assume the hypotheses of Theorem 10.15, and let h ∈ H have
Dh ⊇ D∗ . Call an h∗ -datum the restriction of some u ∈ Vh∗ to a finite subset of D∗ .
  (i) If |=h |D∗ ̸⊆ |=h∗ , some h∗ -datum refutes h.
 (ii) If |=h |D∗ ⊆ |=h∗ , no h∗ -datum refutes h, and h is coherent on every finite A ⊆ D∗ on which
      h∗ is coherent.

Proof. (i) Take (Γ ▷ y) ∈ |=h \ |=h∗ with Γ ∪ {y} ⊆ D∗ . By Lemma 10.14(ii) for h∗ , some u ∈ Vh∗
has u(Γ) = 1 and u(y) = 0. Let v := u|Γ∪{y} , an h∗ -datum with dom v ⊆ Dh . If v extended to
some vM ◦ ρh ∈ Vh , then M |= Th ∪ ρh Γ and M ̸|= ρh y, contradicting Γ |=h y. So v refutes h.
   (ii) Let v = u|F with u ∈ Vh∗ and F ⊆ D∗ finite, and let Γv := {x ∈ F : v(x) = 1} ∪ {∼x : x ∈
F, v(x) = 0}, a finite subset of D∗ (which is negation-closed). Suppose v extended to no member
of Vh . Then Th ∪ ρh Γv is inconsistent, since a model of it would induce an extension of v (using
Th ⊢ ρh (∼x) ↔ ¬ρh (x)). Pick x ∈ D∗ . Then Γv |=h x and Γv |=h ∼x, and both steps have their
occurrences in D∗ , so Γv |=h∗ x and Γv |=h∗ ∼x. But u makes Γv true, so by Lemma 10.14(ii) for
h∗ it makes both x and ∼x true, which is impossible. For coherence: if h were incoherent on
A ⊆ D∗ , then A |=h x and A |=h ∼x for x ∈ D∗ , hence A |=h∗ x and A |=h∗ ∼x, and T ∗ ∪ ρ∗ A
would be inconsistent.

Proof of Theorem 10.15. (a) If h ≈ h′ , then Dh = Dh′ (both are recovered from the common
relation), Vh = Vh′ by Lemma 10.14(i) (both are the coherent valuations of the same relation,
for the same negation), and h, h′ are coherent on the same sets by Lemma 10.14(iii), applied to
A ∩ Dh (partial-reading convention). So h ≡∞ h′ . The converse is part of the definition of ≡∞ .
Nonempty domains are needed: with empty readings, a consistent and an inconsistent theory
have the same (empty) |= but different V.
    (b) By (a), ≡∞ and ≈ coincide; apply the closure-level version of Proposition 10.13.
    (c) Every h ∈  / U ∗ is eventually refuted. If |=h∗ ̸⊆ |=h , some s ∈ |=h∗ \ |=h is presented in
(P) and refutes h. Otherwise |=h∗ ⊆ |=h ; then Dh ⊇ D∗ , because {x} |=h∗ x for x ∈ D∗ ; and
since all steps of |=h∗ have occurrences in D∗ , |=h∗ ⊆ |=h |D∗ , with strict inclusion as h ∈     / U ∗ . So
|=h |D∗ ̸⊆ |=h∗ , and by Lemma I.1(i) some h∗ -datum refutes h; it is presented in (O). No h ∈ U ∗
is ever refuted. Practice data lie in |=h∗ = |=h |D∗ ; Dh ⊇ D∗ as before; and by Lemma I.1(ii)
no object datum refutes h and h is coherent on every designated context (which lies in D∗ and
is h∗ -coherent). As H is finite, after finitely many data the survivors are exactly U ∗ , and the
intersection of their relations, restricted to D∗ , is |=h∗ . Refuting h by a practice datum s requires
a certificate that s ∈
                     / |=h , i.e. that Th ∪ ρh Γ ̸|= ρh y; this is co-r.e. and in general not semi-decidable
(for Th = ZFC), so (c) describes the ideal version space.
    (d) Practice. The data are the elements of Stgh∗ , and a datum s refutes h iff s ∈            / Stgh . So
complete presentation eventually refutes exactly the h with Stgh∗ ̸⊆ Stgh . Since {x} ▷ x needs


                                                    239
zero rule applications, ({x} ▷ x) ∈ Stgh∗ for each x ∈ D∗ , and practice survivors have Dh ⊇ D∗ .
Objects and paradoxes. Among practice survivors, Lemma I.1 shows that some presented object
datum refutes h iff |=h |D∗ ̸⊆ |=h∗ , and that paradoxes refute none of those with |=h |D∗ ⊆ |=h∗ .
Conclusion. As H is finite, the survivors are eventually exactly S g . h∗ ∈ S g , and every member
of S g contains Stgh∗ , so the intersection of the survivors’ g-step relations is Stgh∗ . The upper
bracket holds by definition of S g . For the lower bracket, let (Γ ▷ y) ∈ ChD∗ (Stgh∗ ), witnessed by
an argument with lines in D∗ ⊆ Dh and inferences and links in Stgh∗ ⊆ Stgh ⊆ |=h . Lemma 10.6
under h and monotonicity give Γ |=h y. If h∗ is step-expressive on D∗ at gap g, the two brackets
meet, so |=h |D∗ = |=h∗ and h ∈ U ∗ ; conversely every h ∈ U ∗ with Stgh ⊇ Stgh∗ is in S g . Effectivity.
Practice refutation is decidable because Stgh is. An object datum v refutes h iff Th ∪ ρh Γv is
inconsistent (notation of Lemma I.1), which is semi-decidable since ClRh is r.e.; paradoxes are
found by search. Each non-member of S g is refuted by a finite datum with a finite certificate,
and H is finite, so the budgeted version space equals S g after finitely many data and a finite
budget. Example 10.16 shows that survivors can have strictly weaker meaning.

Verification of Example 10.16. Both hypotheses are propositional (a fragment of first-order logic)
and share the reading; D∗ = Dh consists of occurrences read as p0 , ¬p0 , p3 , ¬p3 , and ∼ maps the
occurrence read as pi to the one read as ¬pi and back, so D∗ is negation-closed. R∗ consists
of the zero-premise steps ▷τ for τ ∈ T ∗ and all steps Π ▷ φ with Π finite and Π tautologically
implying φ; Rh consists of the tautological steps only. Then ClR∗ (Γ) is the set of tautological
consequences of Γ ∪ T ∗ and ClRh (Γ) that of Γ, so both hypotheses are first-order complete, with
Th = ∅.
    Let 1 ≤ g ≤ 3. Since Rh ⊆ R∗ , Stgh ⊆ Stgh∗ . Conversely, let Γ ▷ y be in Stgh∗ , via an
R∗ -derivation π of ρ(y) from ρΓ with at most g applications. If ρ(y) ∈ ρΓ, the step is in Stgh
with zero applications. Otherwise the last step of π is tautological, because axiom steps conclude
members of T ∗ , which are not readings of occurrences in D∗ . So π uses at most g − 1 ≤ 2 axiom
steps, and ρ(y) is a tautological consequence of ρΓ ∪ T ′ for some T ′ ⊊ T ∗ . We claim ρ(y) is then
a tautological consequence of ρΓ alone. Otherwise some valuation v of p0 , p3 satisfies ρΓ and
falsifies ρ(y) (all four formulas mention only p0 , p3 ). Let pi → pi+1 be a chain axiom missing
from T ′ , and extend v by v(pk ) := v(p0 ) for k ≤ i and v(pk ) := v(p3 ) for k ≥ i + 1. Every axiom
of T ′ is then of the form pk → pk+1 with both sides equal, so v satisfies T ′ ∪ ρΓ and falsifies ρ(y),
a contradiction. Hence the single tautological step ρΓ ▷ ρ(y) is in Rh , and Γ ▷ y ∈ St1h ⊆ Stgh . So
Stgh = Stgh∗ .
    (p0 ▷ p3 ) ∈ |=h∗ , since T ∗ implies p0 → p3 ; it is not in |=h , since p0 ̸|= p3 . So |=h |D∗ ⊊ |=h∗
and h ∈ / U ∗ . Every object datum extends to an h-admissible valuation, since Mod(T ∗ ) ⊆ Mod(∅)
gives Vh∗ ⊆ Vh ; and every A on which h∗ is coherent is h-coherent, since Th ⊆ T ∗ . So h survives
every presentation in the sense of Theorem 10.15(d), although h ∈        / U ∗ . At g = 4, the derivation
from p0 by the three axiom steps and one tautological step puts (p0 ▷ p3 ) in St4h∗ \ St4h , and
practice refutes h.

Proof of Proposition 10.19. Let ωvN be the set of von Neumann natural numbers, with successor
SvN (x) = x ∪ {x}, and let ωZ be the range of the function z on ωvN defined by recursion,
z(0) = ∅ and z(SvN n) = {z(n)} (Replacement), with successor SZ (x) = {x}. ZF proves that both
(ωvN , ∅, SvN ) and (ωZ , ∅, SZ ) are Dedekind–Peano systems: the successor is injective, ∅ is not a
successor, and every subset containing ∅ and closed under successor is everything (for ωZ , by
induction along z). Define +• , ×• on each by the recursion equations. By Dedekind’s categoricity
theorem (Dedekind, 1888), provable in ZF, there is a unique f : ωvN → ωZ with f (0) = 0 and
f (SvN n) = SZ f (n); it is a bijection, and by induction it preserves + and ×.
    For an arithmetic formula φ(⃗v ) let φvN and φZ be its translations: quantifiers relativized
to ω• , and 0, S, +, × interpreted as above. By induction on terms and then formulas, ZF ⊢
∀⃗n ∈ ωvN (φvN (⃗n) ↔ φZ (f⃗n)). Atomic formulas use that f commutes with the operations and is
injective; connectives are immediate; for ∃m ψ use that f is surjective. So for closed arithmetic
x, ZF ⊢ ρvN (x) ↔ ρZ (x), and for Γ ∪ {y} ⊆ Dar closed, Γ |=vN y iff ZF ⊢ ρvN Γ → ρvN y iff
                                                                                V

ZF ⊢ ρZ Γ → ρZ y iff Γ |=Z y.
       V

    Occurrences with contextual constants (“let n be a number”) are read with a fresh constant
c and the axiom c ∈ ωvN , respectively c ∈ ωZ . They are not equivalent sentence by sentence,
since c denotes different sets under the two readings, but the consequence relations still agree:
Γ |=vN y iff ZF ⊢ ∀⃗n ∈ ωvN ( ΓvN (⃗n) → y vN (⃗n)) (deduction theorem and generalization over
                                  V

the fresh constants), iff ZF ⊢ ∀m                ⃗ → y Z (m))

Γ |=Z y. Finally, “1 ∈ 3” is not an arithmetic sentence; under ρvN it is true, and under ρZ it is
false, since {{{∅}}} ̸∋ {∅}.

I.3   Objects versus paradoxes
In the correction games, VS is the set of surviving hypotheses. A learner is deterministic; the
environment may choose the target adaptively, as long as some target is consistent with all its
answers, and an item is legal only if it is legal for such a target.

Proof of Theorem 10.22(b), (c). (b) Fix r ≥ 1. A (−obj) item s ∈ At \ h∗ is also a legal (−bagr )
item B = {s}, and it deletes the same hypotheses (h ∋ s, i.e. h ⊇ {s}). (+) items are common
to both games. So against any learner, the object environment’s moves are a subset of the bag
environment’s, and every bag-game strategy achieves in the object game at most its bag-game
                                (r)
worst case. Hence Mobj ≤ Mbag . At r = 1 a legal bag is a set B ⊆ At with |B| ≤ 1 and B ̸⊆ h∗ ,
i.e. B = {s} with s ∈ At \ h∗ , so the games coincide.
    (c) Let Ldim(∅) = −1, and for nonempty C ⊆ 2S let Ldim(C) be the largest d such that there
is a complete binary tree of depth d whose internal nodes are labelled by elements of S and
such that every root-to-leaf path is realized by some h ∈ C. A path is realized if, at each node
labelled s, it goes right iff s ∈ h (Littlestone, 1988). For s ∈ S write Cs1 := {h ∈ C : s ∈ h} and
Cs0 := C \ Cs1 . If Ldim(C) = d ≥ 0, then min(Ldim(Cs0 ), Ldim(Cs1 )) < d for every s. Otherwise


                                                  241
both subclasses would have trees of depth d, and a root labelled s above them would give a tree
of depth d + 1 for C.
     Upper bound. Announce At := {s : Ldim(VS1s ) ≥ Ldim(VS0s )}. A (−obj) item s ∈ At leaves
    0
VSs , and a (+) item s ∈/ At leaves VS1s . In both cases the remaining class is the one of smaller
Littlestone dimension, which is below Ldim(VS). The target always survives, so Ldim(VS) ≥ 0,
and there are at most Ldim(H) corrections.
     Lower bound. The environment maintains VS with a tree of depth d = Ldim(VS). While
d ≥ 1, let s label the root. If s ∈ At , it answers (−obj) s and commits to VS0s . This is legal:
any h ∈ VS0s has s ∈ At \ h, and VS0s ̸= ∅ because it contains the hypotheses realizing the
left subtree. If s ∈/ At , it answers (+) s and commits to VS1s . Either way the new version
space contains a subtree of depth d − 1. So every learner suffers at least Ldim(H) corrections,
and Mobj (H) = Ldim(H). This is Littlestone’s theorem, transcribed. Equivalence queries with
arbitrary hypotheses (Angluin, 1988) are the same game.

i ̸= j falsifies the remaining one. So the set of all the bi together with A is inconsistent, while A

Proof of Proposition 10.25. Let the blocks be X1 , . . . , Xk , each of b steps, and let H consist of
the sets that omit exactly one step from each block. Write CX for the surviving candidate
culprits of block X, and call X unresolved if |CX | ≥ 2.
    Mobj ≤ k. Announce S minus the culprits identified so far. Then At ⊇ h∗ , so no (+) item is
legal, and each (−obj) item is an unidentified culprit, which resolves its block.
    Mobj ≥ k. The environment always answers inside one unresolved block X: with (+) on
some c ∈ CX \ At , legal for culprits in CX \ {c}, or, if CX ⊆ At , with (−obj) on some c ∈ CX ,
committing to culprit c. Each answer changes only one block and resolves at most one, and while
some block is unresolved an answer is available. Each of the k blocks must be resolved, so at
least k corrections occur.

some block is unresolved. The environment answers as follows.

   • Otherwise every unresolved block has all its candidates accepted. Pick one, X. If |CX | > r,

     CX ← B, and min(r, |CX | − 1) drops from r to r − 1. If |CX | ≤ r, answer B = CX , which
     is legal and deletes no survivor; Φ is unchanged.

Proof of Proposition 10.26. Mobj (H) = 1 means some first announcement A is such that every
legal feedback leaves a single hypothesis. A feedback item s against A (either kind) leaves exactly
the hypotheses h with s ∈ A △ h. The environment may return any s ∈ A △ h∗ for any h∗ ∈ H,
so the sets A △ h, h ∈ H, are pairwise disjoint. In the bag game, announce A. A (+) item s ∈   /A
lies in exactly one A △ h and pins the target. A bag B ⊆ A with |B| ≤ r leaves the h with
B ̸⊆ h, i.e. with B ∩ (A △ h) ̸= ∅. Each element of B lies in at most one such set, so at most
r hypotheses survive. Then announce the survivors one at a time. Any feedback against an
announced h ̸= h∗ deletes h: a (+) item s ∈ h∗ \ h deletes the hypotheses lacking s, and a bag
B ⊆ h with B ̸⊆ h∗ deletes the hypotheses containing B. So at most r − 1 further corrections
occur, at most r in all. (If |H| ≤ r, plain elimination costs at most r − 1.)

I.4   Frege, Russell, Zermelo
Proof of Theorem 10.30. Refutations are sound, so a refuted set is incoherent. (a) I contains
every support and is strictly shortest. (b) A coherent c has no refutation; an incoherent c has
one of size k(c), found by every search with budget t ≥ k(c). (c) For t ≥ t0 , by (b), the unrefuted
members of R are exactly the coherent ones. (d) Normalizing wt does not change the arg max. For
every c ∈ R, covt (c)/∥wt ∥1 −cov∞ (c) =          i∈c (wt (i)/∥wt ∥1 −w∞ (i)) ≤ ∥wt /∥wt ∥1 −w∞ ∥1 → 0,
                                               P

where cov∞ (c) := i∈c w∞ (i). Let c∗ be the unique coherent maximizer of cov∞ , and γ > 0
                     P

its margin over the other coherent members (R is finite). Once t ≥ t0 and the ℓ1 distance is
below γ/2, c∗ has strictly larger coverage than every other coherent member, so REPt = c∗ .
Pointwise convergence alone would not suffice: the members of R are typically infinite, and
mass escaping along instances inside some c breaks the convergence of coverages. (By Scheffé’s
lemma, pointwise convergence of probability vectors to a probability vector is enough.) (e) Coh
is downward closed, so any superset of the incoherent c1 ∪ c2 is incoherent. If supp(wt ) ⊆ c1 ∩ c2 ,
then covt (c1 ) = covt (c2 ) = ∥wt ∥1 , and the lexicographic comparison reduces to ℓ.

Proof of Lemma 10.31. Membership of the menu formulas in the classes (Table 13):
   • POS: V, S, INT, UNI, PAIR;
   • STRAT ∩ Φ: V, EMP, INT, UNI, PAIR, DIFF, CMP;
   • SEP: INT, DIFF, ZR;
   • Z ∩ Φ: INT, DIFF, ZR, EMP, PAIR, UNI.
The formulas S, R and ZR contain x ∈ x and so admit no stratification.
    (F1) Comp(R) gives y with ∀x (x ∈ y ↔ x ∈      / x); instantiating x := y gives y ∈ y ↔ y ∈  / y,
hence ⊥.
    (F2) Let {u} be a one-element structure with u ∈ u. Every atomic formula (x = y or x ∈ y)
is true under every assignment, so every positive formula, built from atoms by ∧, ∨, ∀, ∃, is true.
Hence ∀x (x ∈ u ↔ φ) holds for every positive φ and all parameters, and y := u witnesses every
positive comprehension instance. Extensionality holds trivially.
    (F3) The finite and cofinite subsets of N form a countably infinite Boolean algebra FC; let
e : N → FC be a bijection and put x ∈ y : ⇐⇒ x ∈ e(y). For parameters a, b, the extensions of
the stratified menu formulas are N (V), ∅ (EMP), e(a) ∩ e(b) (INT), e(a) ∪ e(b) (UNI), {a, b}
(PAIR), e(a) \ e(b) (DIFF) and N \ e(a) (CMP). All are in FC, hence equal e(y) for some y.
Injectivity of e gives Extensionality.
    (F4) In the hereditarily finite sets with true membership, INT, DIFF and ZR define subsets
of a ∈ HF, which are in HF; EMP, PAIR and UNI define ∅, {a, b} and a ∪ b, which are in HF.
Extensionality and Foundation hold.
    (F5) POS ∪ Z and STRAT ∪ Z contain V and ZR. Comp(V) gives v with ∀x (x ∈ v);
Comp(ZR) with a := v gives y with ∀x (x ∈ y ↔ x ∈ v ∧ x ∈        / x), i.e. ∀x (x ∈ y ↔ x ∈/ x), and
(F1) applies. POS ∪ STRAT contains S and CMP. Comp(S) gives s with ∀x (x ∈ s ↔ x ∈ x);
Comp(CMP) with a := s gives y with ∀x (x ∈ y ↔ x ∈         / s), i.e. ∀x (x ∈ y ↔ x ∈ / x), and (F1)
applies.
    (F6) SEP ⊊ Z by the lists. S ∈ POS \ (STRAT ∪ Z), CMP ∈ STRAT \ (POS ∪ Z), V ∈
STRAT \ Z, ZR ∈ Z \ (POS ∪ STRAT), and DIFF ∈ Z \ POS. So the three classes are pairwise
incomparable.



                                                 244
Proof of Theorem 10.32. (i) is Theorem 10.30(a), since NC contains every support and is shortest.
(ii) is (F1); NC is the only incoherent member, so t0 is the size of that refutation. (iii) NC is
incoherent; POS, STRAT and Z are coherent (F2–F4); SEP ⊊ Z is coherent but not maximal;
and POS, STRAT and Z are pairwise incomparable (F6), hence all maximal. They are pairwise
jointly incoherent by (F5).
     (iv) By Theorem 10.30(c), for t ≥ t0 the learner REPt maximizes (cov, −ℓ) over the coherent
classes POS, SEP, STRAT and Z, whose description lengths increase in that order. Let W be the
total weight and supp the support, with all weights in the support positive; cov(c) = W iff supp ⊆
c. Support ⊆ DC. STRAT and Z contain DC, so both have coverage W , and STRAT beats Z on ℓ.
POS has coverage W iff the support avoids DIFF and EMP; SEP iff it avoids UNI, PAIR and EMP.
The maximal coverage is W , and the shortest class achieving it is selected: POS if it qualifies, else
SEP if it qualifies, else STRAT. So STRAT is selected iff the support meets both {DIFF, EMP}
and {UNI, PAIR, EMP}, as claimed; the full support DC selects STRAT. ZR ∈ supp ⊆ DC∪{ZR}.
POS and STRAT miss ZR, so their coverage is below W . Z has coverage W , and SEP does iff the
support avoids UNI, PAIR and EMP. Since SEP is shorter than Z, SEP is selected in that case
and Z otherwise. The full support selects Z. Support DC ∪ {ZR, V}. cov(STRAT) = W − w(ZR);
cov(Z) = W −w(V); cov(POS) = W −w(DIFF)−w(EMP)−w(ZR) < cov(STRAT); cov(SEP) =
W − w(V) − w(UNI) − w(PAIR) − w(EMP) < cov(Z). So Z is selected iff w(ZR) > w(V), and
ties go to the shorter STRAT. The partial example. For support {INT, UNI, PAIR, ZR, V} with
w(V) > w(ZR): cov(POS) = cov(STRAT) = W − w(ZR) > W − w(V) = cov(Z), and SEP misses
UNI, PAIR and V. POS ties STRAT and is shorter, so POS is selected. All 93 sub-supports
(the nonempty subsets of DC, alone, with ZR, and with ZR and V) were checked against these
conditions by T4-checks/verification_checks.py (V2), with no mismatch.

Proof of Proposition 10.33. For n ≥ 1 let pn be the sentence “some object has exactly n elements”.

for n ∈/ A and IA ⊢ pn for n ∈ A, and IA ∪ IB is inconsistent whenever A ̸= B. IA is consistent.
Let MA have domain {e0 , e1 , . . . } ∪ {an : n ∈ A}, where every ei has no elements, an has
exactly the elements e0 , . . . , en−1 , and there is no other membership. Then pn holds in MA iff

defining formula has empty extension for all parameters, and e0 witnesses it. Maximal extensions.
Consistency has finite character, so by Zorn’s lemma each IA extends to a maximal consistent set
JA of instances. If A ̸= B, then JA ∪ JB ⊇ IA ∪ IB is inconsistent, so JA =      ̸ JB and the two are
incompatible. Every JA contains Comp(x ̸= x). There are 2ℵ0 sets A. There are only countably

It is maximal: if ιk ∈ / S, then Sk ∪ {ιk } is inconsistent, so S ∪ {ιk } is. Consistency of a finite set
of sentences is Π1 , so S is computable from the halting problem, i.e. ∆2 , and by Shoenfield’s

limit lemma it is the pointwise limit of a computable sequence of guesses. This does not conflict
with Incurvati and Murzi (2017): being ∆2 does not make S recursively axiomatizable.

I.5   The robust core
Proof of Theorem 10.35. (a) Every inference and link of the argument is a non-noise practice
item, so by RCHg it lies in StgSV ⊆ Stgσ ⊆ |=σ , or is a link valid under σ, for every σ. Validity
puts every line used in Dhσ , and unused premises are dropped. Lemma 10.6 for hσ gives the
conclusion in ClRσ (ρσ Prem). If Tσ proves the premises, then since ClRσ is consequence over Tσ ,
it proves the conclusion.
    (b) Each datum is consistent with each hσ :
   • practice steps lie in StgSV ⊆ Stgσ ;
   • a “yes” is given only on StgSV ⊆ Stgσ , and a “no” only outside             g       g
                                                                           σ ′ Stσ ′ ⊇ Stσ , for steps and
                                                                       S

     links alike;
   • designated contexts are σ-coherent, so no paradox for hσ exists;
   • an SV-truthful datum at the precise object M is v(x) = vMσ (ρσ (x)) for super-determinate
     x, the same value for every σ; so v is the restriction of vMσ ◦ ρσ ∈ Vhσ .
Budgeted searches find only certificates, so no hσ is ever refuted and {hσ } ⊆ VSt . Unanimity
then gives acceptance ⊆ σ Stgσ = StgSV ⊆ |=σ for every σ. Convergence. Let H be finite and
                               T

every element of StgSV eventually presented. Any h lacking some element of StgSV is refuted when
that element is presented, so after finitely many data every survivor contains StgSV . The hσ
survive, so the intersection of the survivors’ relations is contained in σ Stgσ , and therefore equals
                                                                          T

StgSV . Effectivity. For infinite Σ the robust core can be undecidable. Let the vague sentences be
w0 , w1 , . . . ; let σn read wm as the ∆0 sentence “machine m does not halt within n steps” over a
base theory proving all true ∆0 sentences; and let each Rσ contain the zero-premise steps ▷δ
for true ∆0 sentences δ, a decidable set. Then (∅ ▷ wm ) ∈ St1σn iff machine m does not halt
within n steps. A false ∆0 sentence is refutable, hence underivable in a sound calculus. So
(∅ ▷ wm ) ∈ St1SV iff machine m never halts, which is Π1 -complete. The idealized verifier is an
algorithm only for finite Σ or under extra uniformity.
     (c) By (b), under these data every hσ remains a possible target forever. (c1) If an acceptance
set A is contained in |=σ for every σ, then A ⊆ σ |=σ = |=SV . (c2) The same argument works
                                                     T

with Stgσ . If H = {hσ }, all members survive by (b), and the verifier’s accepted set is exactly
       g           g
  σ Stσ = StSV at all times. The gap. Let Σ = {σ}, let Rσ be natural deduction for classical
T

propositional logic, and let ρ(x) = q and ρ(y) = q ∧ (q ∧ q). Then ({x} ▷ y) ∈ |=σ . No single
rule application derives q ∧ (q ∧ q) from q. ∧I would need the premise q ∧ q; →E would need
an implication with that consequent; ⊥E would need ⊥; and the classical reductio rule needs a
subderivation of at least one further step. So ({x} ▷ y) ∈   / St1σ , while two ∧I steps suffice.

     (d) By (b), the accepted set is contained in StSV , so steps outside it are never accepted. (d1)
Let s ∈  / |=σ for some σ ∈ Σ. A “yes” on s would be false under σ, so no SV-truthful answer
licenses s. Any library extension must keep Rσ sound for Mod(Tσ ), so it cannot make s valid
under σ. Any expansion into σ-valid steps would make s σ-valid by Lemma 10.6. So while
σ ∈ Σ and s is unchanged, s stays outside StgSV ; only shrinking Σ (a definition) or changing the
step (lemma-incorporation) removes it. (d2) Let s = (Γ ▷ y) ∈ |=SV . Adding the derived step
ρσ Γ ▷ ρσ y to each library Rσ keeps Rσ sound, because the step is σ-valid. It puts s in St1σ ⊆ Stgσ



                                                 246
for every σ, so s ∈ StgSV . Alternatively, an expansion of s into steps of StgSV , when one exists, is
accepted step by step (Proposition 10.11).

Proof of Proposition 10.37. (a) Each |=σ , restricted to finite subsets of Dσ := Dhσ , is reflexive
({x} |=σ x for x ∈ Dσ ), monotone, and closed under cut (Γ |=σ x and Γ ∪ {x} |=σ y give Γ |=σ y),
because ClRσ is a closure operator. These properties are preserved by intersection over σ, on
                           T
the common domain σ Dσ . (b) Let the argument have steps (inferences and links) s1 , . . . , sn ,
and let Ei := {σ : si ∈ |=σ }. Under every σ ∈ i Ei , Lemma 10.6 gives (Prem ▷ ym ) ∈ |=σ .
                                                       T

So, by the union bound, the (measurable) event that the argument is valid has probability at
least µ( i Ei ) ≥ 1 − i µ(Eic ) ≥ 1 − nε. Tightness. Take a base theory deciding order between
         T                P

numerals; a vague predicate “heap”; sentences qk = “k grains make a heap” for k = 0, . . . , n; and
sharpenings σc , c = 0, . . . , n, defining “heap”(x) as x ≥ c. The argument is qn ▷ qn−1 ▷ · · · ▷ q0 .
Under σc , the step qk ▷ qk−1 fails iff k ≥ c > k − 1, i.e. iff c = k, and the overall step qn ▷ q0 fails
iff c ≥ 1. Give σ1 , . . . , σn mass ε each and σ0 mass 1 − nε (for ε ≤ 1/n). Each step is valid with
probability exactly 1 − ε, and the argument exactly with probability 1 − nε. At ε = 1/n, the
argument is valid under no sharpening in the support of µ. T4-checks/robust_core_and_mdl.py
checks the cases n = 3, 5, 10.

Proof of Proposition 10.38. (a) A step s = (Γ ▷ y) ∈ A0 can be refuted only by a model of T
in which ρ∗ Γ holds and ρ∗ y fails; such a model exists iff s ∈   / |=T . By completeness of monster
presentation, s survives iff s ∈ |=T , so A∞ = A0 ∩ |=T . By Gödel completeness (Gödel, 1930),
each surviving step has a derivation, in any complete calculus C for T , of ρ∗ y from ρ∗ Γ. For an
argument chained from surviving steps, replacing each step by such a derivation and concatenating
yields a C-derivation of the ρ∗ -image of the conclusion from the images of the premises. This is
Lemma 10.6 with the derivations made explicit.
    (b) Here A∞ = {χ ▷ θ : PA ⊢ χ → θ}, which is r.e. but undecidable: with χ the sentence 0 = 0,
membership decides PA-provability of arithmetic sentences. Let C be a complete calculus for
PA satisfying the effectivity assumptions, and suppose A∞ ⊆ StgC for some g. Since StgC ⊆ |=PA
and A0 ∩ |=PA = A∞ , we get A∞ = A0 ∩ StgC , which is decidable because A0 and StgC are.
Contradiction.
                  S
    (c) Let A0 = j≤J inst(Sj ). Under schema-level selection with complete monster presentation,
Sj survives iff none of its instances is refuted, iff all its instances lie in |=T . For each surviving
Sj , the template πj instantiates to a derivation of each instance with at most g rule applications,
and its formulas have size at most |πj | times the size of the instance. Choosing the size bound
of Definition 10.2 to be at least maxj |πj | times the input size, every surviving step is in StgC .
The templates are finitely many derived rules of C, whose instances are the surviving practice.
Pure propositional schemas. Let S have metavariables A1 , . . . , Ak and mention no atoms, with
all instances valid. Its generic instance S[Ai := pi ], for distinct atoms pi , is valid, so it has a
derivation π in a complete propositional calculus with schematic rules. Applying the substitution
pi 7→ φi to every formula of π yields a derivation, with the same number of applications, of
S[Ai := φi ], because schematic rules are closed under uniform substitution. So π is a uniform
template. Failure in PA. Let θ(n) be ¬Prf PA (n, ⌜0 = 1⌝). Each instance θ(n̄) is a true statement
about a numeral, decided by a primitive recursive relation, and is therefore provable in PA (by Σ1 -
completeness, since PA is consistent). Suppose a template with a schematic letter for the numeral
instantiated to a derivation of θ(n̄) for every n. In a standard Hilbert calculus, substituting a
fresh variable x for the schematic letter turns it into a derivation of θ(x), and generalization gives
PA ⊢ ∀x θ(x), i.e. PA ⊢ Con(PA). This contradicts Gödel’s second incompleteness theorem.

J     Proofs for Section 11
The following results are proved completely in the main text: Propositions 11.3, 11.4, 11.10, 11.19,
11.22 and 11.40, Theorems 11.8, 11.16, 11.28 and 11.38, and Lemma 11.20. The minimal-repair
drift stated after Theorem 11.28 (T3 Prop 4.2) is immediate: enlarging a tolerance enlarges an
interval, so coherence is preserved upward. Proposition 11.33 and the numerical claims marked
(computed) are computations, reproduced by the scripts in research/theory/T3-checks/; they
are not proved here. Everything else is proved below. Throughout, w is a world, dom = dom Mw ,
and δ(F × G) := {λ[D:=µ] : λ ∈ F, µ ∈ G}.

J.1    Preliminaries on the filters of Definition 11.2
Lemma J.1 (Filter bases). (a) For c = DEF(D, λ◦ , G) with parent p, the family Bc = {δ(F ×
G) ∩ dom : F ∈ Fp (w), G ∈ G} is a filter base: X ∈ Fc (w) iff X ⊇ B for some B ∈ Bc , and c is
proper at w iff every member of Bc is nonempty. (b) For c = SUP(A) with parent p, X ∈ Fc (w)
iff X ⊇ F ∩ ∥A∥w for some F ∈ Fp (w). (c) If the parent filter is principal at a point λ, then in
(a) one may take F = {λ}: X ∈ Fc (w) iff X ⊇ {λ[D:=µ] : µ ∈ G} ∩ dom for some G ∈ G.

Proof. (a) δ(F1 × G1 ) ∩ δ(F2 × G2 ) ⊇ δ((F1 ∩ F2 ) × (G1 ∩ G2 )), and F1 ∩ F2 ∈ Fp (w), G1 ∩ G2 ∈ G.
So Bc is directed downward, and the filter it generates consists of the supersets (in dom) of
its members. It contains ∅ iff some member is empty. (b) Likewise {F ∩ ∥A∥ : F ∈ Fp (w)} is
closed under finite intersections. (c) If Fp (w) is principal at λ, every F ∈ Fp (w) contains λ, so
δ({λ} × G) ⊆ δ(F × G), and {λ} ∈ Fp (w).

J.2    Proofs for §11.2
Proof of Proposition 11.5. (a) The root filter is principal at λ∗ , where patm = 1.0 × 105 . The
frame has no phases, and by hypothesis the model exists at λ∗ [patm :=0]; by Lemma J.1(c) with
the principal path the child filter is principal there; hence c |= patm = 0. Both filters are principal,
hence proper, and by Proposition 11.3(b) each context’s theory is consistent.
    (b) Under limit semantics the only generator is {λ∗ [mr :=0]} ∩ dom = ∅, so the limit child
is improper. Under germ semantics the generators are {λ∗ [mr :=µ] : 0 < |µ| < η} ∩ dom =
{λ∗ [mr :=µ] : 0 < µ < η}, all nonempty, so the germ child is proper (Lemma J.1(a)). In the
model at mr = µ > 0, a = Φ/µ, so |a| > K whenever µ < |Φ|/ max(K, 1); the generator with
η = |Φ|/ max(K, 1) lies inside ∥|a| > K∥.
    (c) Let E = {µ : λ∗ [D:=µ] ∈ dom}. By Lemma J.1(c), cgerm |= Q ∈ I iff there is G ∈ G with
q(µ) ∈ I for all µ ∈ G ∩ E. “For every open I ∋ q(λ◦ ) there is G ∈ G with q(G ∩ E) ⊆ I” is the
definition of q(µ) → q(λ◦ ) along the trace of G on E. (Intervals with endpoints named in L, e.g.
rational ones, suffice for convergence.) The limit child is principal at λ∗ [D:=λ◦ ], so its value of
Q is q(λ◦ ).

Proof of Lemma 11.7. (a) Let F = ∥φ∥w ∈ Fp (w) and G ∈ G. Every element of δ(F × G) ∩ dom
has the form λ[D:=µ] ∈ dom with λ ∈ F ; it differs from λ only in coordinates in D, so by
D-stability it satisfies φ. Thus this generator lies inside ∥φ∥w , and ∥φ∥w ∈ Fc (w). Laws are
D-stable because they hold on all of dom. An arithmetic formula in the constants pi , i ∈   / D, is
evaluated in the standard real sort with pi interpreted as λi , so its truth value depends only on
      / , which points differing only on D share.
(λi )i∈D

    (b) Let λ, λ′ ∈ dom differ only on D, with Mw (λ) |= φ. Take the actual point λ∗ = λ and the
ideal value λ◦ = λ′D . Then @ |= φ, and the limit child’s only generator is {λ[D:=λ′D ]} ∩ dom =
{λ′ }, so its filter is principal at λ′ and c |= φ says Mw (λ′ ) |= φ. Exchanging the roles of λ and λ′

    (c) Every generator {λ[k:=0] : λ ∈ F } ∩ dom of the child lies inside ∥k = 0∥, so c |= k = 0

J.3    Proofs for §11.3: realizability
AUX contexts are identified with their parents throughout. For a set M ⊆ K and a sentence A,
∥A∥ in (R1) denotes {N ∈ K : N |= A}. Since SUP contexts have only SUP descendants, the
parent of a DEF context is never a SUP context.

Proof of Theorem 11.12(a). Observation 1 (collapse). By (R1) and induction along SUP chains,
Mc = Mb ∩ A∈Asm(c) ∥A∥ for every SUP context c with base b. Hence (c, Γ, φ) satisfies (R3) at
              T

Mc iff every N ∈ Mb with N |= Asm(c) ∧ Γ satisfies φ, i.e. iff (b, Γ ∪ Asm(c), φ) satisfies (R3)
                                   V          V

at Mb . So, given (R1), the judgments of J hold iff Mb ⊆ {N : N |= Th∗ (b)} for every non-SUP
context b.
     Observation 2 (anchoring lemma). In any realization, Mc ̸= ∅ for every c ∈ Anc. We induct
along the inductive definition of Anc. The root is nonempty by (R0) and certified contexts by
(R5). Suppose c enters through an informative use (c, β, t) with Mπc =    ̸ ∅, and suppose Mc = ∅.
Then Mc |= ϕβ (v) for every v ∈ V (vacuously), so (R4) gives Mπc |= σβ → εβ (v) for all v. Since
(πc, ∅, σβ ) ∈ J, (R3) gives Mπc |= σβ . Any N ∈ Mπc then satisfies {σβ } ∪ {εβ (v) : v ∈ V0 },
contradicting informativeness, since N ∈ K.
    (⇒) For c ∈ Anc, Mc =  ̸ ∅ by Observation 2, and any N ∈ Mc satisfies Th∗ (c) by Observation 1.
    (⇐) For each c ∈ Anc choose Nc ∈ K with Nc |= Th∗ (c), and put Mc = {Nc }. Put Mc = ∅
for every non-SUP context outside Anc (these are DEF contexts, since @ ∈ Anc), and define
SUP contexts by (R1). Then (R0), (R1), (R2) and (R5) hold by construction, since @ ∈ Anc
and Ccert ⊆ Anc. (R3) holds by Observation 1: at anchored b because Nb |= Th∗ (b), and at non-
anchored b vacuously. For (R4), take a use (c, β, t). If πc ∈/ Anc, then Mπc = ∅ and (R4) is vacuous.
If πc ∈ Anc, then c ∈ Anc because β is informative, so Mc = {Nc }. The judgment (c, ∅, Q = t̄)
lies in J ∗ unchanged (c is not a SUP context), so QNc = t; by standard names, Mc |= Q = v̄ only
for v = t. Finally (πc, ∅, εβ (t)) ∈ J gives Nπc |= εβ (t), so Mπc |= σβ → εβ (t).

Proof of Theorem 11.12(b). Fix a frame realization at w.
    Filter anchoring lemma: every c ∈ Anc is proper at w. The root is proper, and certified
contexts are proper by assumption. Suppose c enters through an informative use (c, β, t) with
πc proper, and suppose c is improper. Then c |=w ϕβ (v) for every v, so bridge soundness
gives πc |=w σβ → εβ (v) for all v. The judgment (πc, ∅, σβ ) holds, so πc |=w εβ (v) for all v
(Proposition 11.3(a)). By informativeness, {σβ } ∪ {εβ (v) : v ∈ V0 } has no model in K for some
finite V0 ; every Mw (λ) is in K, so the truth set of the finite conjunction is ∅. That set lies in
Fπc (w), contradicting properness.
    Conclusion. Let c ∈ Anc. For a SUP descendant c′ = SUP(Ak ) of . . . of SUP(A1 ) of c,
iterating Proposition 11.4 and using Proposition 11.3(a) shows that c′ |=w χ iff c |=w i Ai → χ.
                                                                                       V

Hence each judgment (c′ , Γ, φ) holds iff c |=w Asm(c′ ) ∧ Γ → φ, and so Th∗ (c) ⊆ Thw (c).
                                                 V             V


                                                 249
Since Th∗ (c) is finite, the intersection of the truth sets of its members lies in Fc (w), and it is
nonempty because c is proper. Any λ in it gives a model Mw (λ) ∈ K of Th∗ (c). No compactness
is used. The argument does not depend on whether paths are principal or germs.

Proof of Theorem 11.12(c). CE1. Ccert = {c}, so Anc = {@, c}; Th∗ (@) = {p1 = 1} and
Th∗ (c) = {p1 = 0} are each satisfiable in K (the constant p1 may denote any real), so the
criterion holds. In a frame realization, the root judgment forces λ∗1 = 1. The certified c is proper,
so λ∗ [2:=0] ∈ dom and Fc is principal at that point, whose first coordinate is 1; hence c |= p1 = 1,
and c ̸|= p1 = 0 since Thw (c) is consistent. CE2. Two siblings with the same declaration have the
same generators, hence the same filter. Both are certified, hence proper; the judgments Q = 1
and Q = 2 would put ∥Q = 1∥ ∩ ∥Q = 2∥ = ∅ in this proper filter. The criterion holds because
each of {Q = 1}, {Q = 2} is satisfiable. Both counterexamples are also brute-forced over all
frames on Λ = {0, 1}2 (realizability_frame.py).

   The exact frame criterion under limit semantics, summarized in the main text after Theo-
rem 11.12, is the following.
Proposition J.2 (Frame realizability under limit semantics). [T3 Prop 2.7, added after verification]
(brute-force checked) Assume every DEF context in J uses limit semantics, every used bridge is an
informative value-export bridge, and SUP contexts have only SUP descendants. For a candidate
actual point λ, define the points of the non-SUP contexts by λ@ = λ and λc = λπc [Dc :=λ◦c ].
Let S(λ) be the least set of non-SUP contexts that contains @ and Ccert and is closed under (i)
parents: c ∈ S ⇒ πc ∈ S; (ii) informative bridges: (c, β, t) ∈ U and πc ∈ S ⇒ c ∈ S; (iii)
coincidence: πc ∈ S and λc = λc′ for some c′ ∈ S ⇒ c ∈ S. Then J is frame-realizable iff there
is λ ∈ Λ such that every point x ∈ {λc : c ∈ S(λ)} lies in Λ and {Th∗ (c) : c ∈ S(λ), λc = x}
                                                                  S

has a model N ∈ K with pN   i = xi for all i.

    The criterion is non-local in two ways: it quantifies over the actual parameter values, and
it conjoins the theories of all forced-proper contexts that sit at one parameter point. CE1 fails
it because c’s point shares p1 with the root, and CE2 because the two siblings share a point.
realizability_frame.py compares it with brute-force frame realizability over all frames on
Λ = {0, 1}2 (up to three DEF and two SUP contexts, 0–2 bridge uses per context): 8,000 instances,
0 mismatches.

collapse into the base by Proposition 11.4.

all their collapsed theories, and pNi = xi .

J.4    Proofs for §11.3: blame localization and hygiene
Proof of Corollary 11.17. (a) Let w be a world at which d is proper. The root is proper at
every w ∈ Wρ , and Wρ ̸= ∅. If every Ax in the cone were true at w and every Step, Imp and
Exp instance in it sound at w, Theorem 11.16 (which uses only the items of the derivation
tree, i.e. the cone) would give d |=w ⊥, i.e. ∅ ∈ Fd (w). (b) A derivation of d ⊩ ⊥ contains
only the items in its tree, and no rule of Definition 11.15 takes ist-claims, or a SUP context’s
undischarged conclusions, as premises. (c) Let πd be proper at w with πd |=w ωd . Take a
generator δ(F × G) ∩ dom of Fd (w). Since F ∩ ∥ωd ∥ ∈ Fπd (w) and πd is proper, there is
λ ∈ F ∩ ∥ωd ∥. Then Eλ = {µ : λ[D:=µ] ∈ dom} ∈ G, and since G is proper, G ∩ Eλ =           ̸ ∅.
For µ in it, λ[D:=µ] ∈ δ(F × G) ∩ dom. So every generator is nonempty, and d is proper by
Lemma J.1(a).



                                                   251
Proposition J.3 (Propagation of ⊥ along informative bridges). [T3 Cor 2.6, propagation clause] Let
c be a DEF context with an informative value-export bridge β cited for c, and suppose c ⊩ ⊥,
πc ⊩ σβ and πc ⊩ ωc are derived. Suppose R̂ contains ex falso (⊥ ▷ ψ) and the kernel of πc
is refutation-complete for finite sets (it derives ⊥ from any finite set of derived sentences that
has no model in K). Then πc ⊩ ⊥ is derivable by adding finitely many synthetic Exp instances.
Iterating along an anchoring chain reaches the root or a certified context. Moreover, fix a world
w at which πc is proper: (i) if c is proper at w, some item of c’s own derivation of ⊥ is unsound
at w; (ii) if c is improper at w, all of c’s own items are sound at w, and some item of the
derivation of πc ⊩ ωc is unsound at w; (iii) without the certificate premise in Exp, in case (ii),
if the derivation of πc ⊩ σβ is sound at w, some synthetic Exp instance is unsound at w: the
bridge applied to an ill-posed context.

Proof. Informativeness gives t1 , . . . , tk with {σβ , εβ (t1 ), . . . , εβ (tk )} unsatisfiable in K. Ex falso
gives c ⊩ Q = t̄i for each i, and k Exp instances give πc ⊩ εβ (ti ). Refutation completeness gives
πc ⊩ ⊥. Iterate for πc along the chain by which c entered Anc.
   (i) is the contrapositive of Theorem 11.16 in c. (ii) If c is improper at w, so is every context
below it (SUP filters refine their parent’s, and a DEF child of an improper context has the empty
generator δ(∅ × G) ∩ dom). Every set lies in an improper filter, so every Ax in c’s subtree is
true at w, and every Step, Imp and Exp instance concluding in that subtree is sound at w. By
Corollary 11.17(c), πc ̸|=w ωc , so by Theorem 11.16 the derivation of πc ⊩ ωc contains an item
unsound at w. (iii) Without ωc , suppose the derivation of σβ is sound at w, so πc |=w σβ . If
every synthetic instance were sound at w, then, as c |=w Q = t̄i trivially, πc |=w εβ (ti ) for all i,
and the finitely many truth sets would have empty intersection in the proper filter Fπc (w).

Proof of Theorem 11.18. (a) Let c′ = SUP(A) be the child of c in which the reductio is carried
out. By Lemma J.1(b), Fc′ (w) ⊇ Fc (w), and ∥A∥ ∈ Fc′ (w). So the premises Kc (true in c) are
true in c′ , A is true in c′ , and every c-sound instance has its truth set in Fc (w) ⊆ Fc′ (w), i.e. is
c′ -sound. By induction along the derivation (filter closure, Proposition 11.3(a)) every line is true
in c′ , so c′ |=w ⊥, and Proposition 11.4 gives c |=w A → ⊥, i.e. c |=w ¬A.
     (b) A derivation of ⊥ from Kc is also a derivation of ⊥ from Kc ∪ {B} for every B, in
particular for B = A and B = ¬A. The set of refuted sentences is then all sentences, whatever
the world.
     (c) From A → q and ¬A → q, case analysis gives q, contradicting ¬q; so K is unsatisfiable.
The valuation A = q = 0 satisfies {A → q, ¬q}, and A = 1, q = 0 satisfies {¬A → q, ¬q}. DA
derives q from A and A → q (modus ponens) and then ⊥ from q, ¬q; D¬A is the same with ¬A.
As a derivation (used premises, steps, conclusion), DA is identical to the reductio of A from the
consistent set K ′ = {A → q, ¬q}, and that reductio is sound because K ′ ∪ {A} is unsatisfiable.
So a test that depends only on the derivation and accepts every sound reductio from a consistent
premise set accepts DA ; likewise D¬A with K ′′ = {¬A → q, ¬q}. The accepted conclusions ¬A
and ¬¬A are jointly unsatisfiable. (hygiene.py checks these facts by truth tables.)
     (d) Suppose N |= A. Every leaf of the reductio is in Kc ∪ {A}, hence true in N ; every step
has true premises and an instance true in N , hence a true conclusion. So N |= ⊥, which is
impossible; thus N |= ¬A. If both A and ¬A were refuted by reductios whose instances N
satisfies, then N |= ¬A and N |= ¬¬A. For the counterexample: if c |= q, the zero-premise
instance ▷ q is c-sound; with Kc = {¬q}, every SUP(B) derives q and ¬q, hence ⊥ by the sound
instance q, ¬q ▷ ⊥, so every B is refuted; a model of ¬q exists but violates the instance ▷ q. For
existence: if c is proper at w, c |=w Kc and the finitely many instances used are c-sound at w,


                                                     252
then the truth sets of the members of Kc and of the instance conditionals φi → ψ all lie in
                                                                                    V

Fc (w); their intersection lies in Fc (w) and is nonempty, and any λ in it gives N = Mw (λ).
    (e) By (b), each A yields both labels; they are jointly unsatisfiable, a fixed function of Kc ,
and the same in every world. If Kc is consistent and R̂ logically sound, a model of Kc satisfies
every instance, so by (d) no A and ¬A are both refuted.

Proof of Theorem 11.27. (a) In a child with canonical stipulation pD = 0 (e.g. k = 0), the
smallness parameter (e.g. s = kv02 /g) is 0, so c ⊩ s ≤ η is derivable soundly in the child, whatever
the world. An Exp rule that accepts it therefore exports Q(λ∗ ) ∈ [Q(0) ± ϵ] from the value of Q
at the ideal point alone. This is an export rule whose information is a single sample at 0 ̸= λ∗ ,
so by Theorem 11.21(d) it is unsound on any class closed under adding smooth functions, for
every ϵ. The ping-pong numbers are those of Theorem 11.25 (computed).
    (b) With k and g both stipulated in the child, the child’s range is R0 (g ′ ) = v02 sin 2θ/g ′ , which
takes any positive value as g ′ varies, while the parent condition kv02 /g ≤ η is evaluated at the
actual g and holds for the steel ball (0.0276). The export R ∈ [R0 (g ′ ) ± ϵ] is false for g ′ far from
g.
    (c) By Theorem 11.16 and Lemma 11.7(a), it suffices that each Exp instance is sound at every
w. Let p = πc and suppose c |=w Q = v̄, p |=w σβ and p |=w ωc . By Lemma J.1(a) there are
F1 ∈ Fp (w) and G ∈ G with δ(F1 × G) ∩ dom ⊆ ∥Q = v̄∥. Let F = F1 ∩ ∥σβ ∥ ∩ ∥ωc ∥ ∈ Fp (w)
and fix λ ∈ F . Since λ ∈ ∥ωc ∥, Eλ = {µ : λ[D:=µ] ∈ dom} ∈ G. For µ ∈ G ∩ Eλ , λ[D:=µ] ∈
δ(F1 × G) ∩ dom, so Q(Mw (λ[D:=µ])) = v. Hence {µ : λ[D:=µ] ∈ dom, Q(Mw (λ[D:=µ])) =
v} ⊇ G ∩ Eλ ∈ G, and the schema’s antecedent holds at λ; as Mw (λ) |= σβ , the schema gives
Mw (λ) |= εβ (v). So F ⊆ ∥εβ (v)∥, i.e. p |=w εβ (v).
    The rope. dom = {mr > 0}, D = {mr }, limit semantics. The child’s generator {λ∗ [mr :=0]} ∩
dom is empty, so the child is improper and proves a = 0 by (vacuously) sound steps. The
schema’s antecedent requires λ[mr :=0] ∈ dom, which never holds, so the schema is a theorem of
the frame. The parent proves mr ≤ η when the actual mr ≤ η, and Exp without ωc would export
|a| ≤ ϵ, whereas |a| = |Φ|/mr ≥ |Φ|/η > ϵ for η < |Φ|/ϵ. With the certificate requirement, ∥ωc ∥
must be empty (no λ has λ[mr :=0] ∈ dom), so the proper root cannot soundly derive ωc .

Lemma J.4 (Asymptotic schemas below a principal parent). [T3 Remark after Thm 3.9] Read the
child judgment “Q ≃ v” as: c |=w |Q − v̄| < r̄ for every rational r > 0. Call β an asymptotic
schema for the declaration (D, λ◦ , G) if, for all w, all λ ∈ dom Mw with Mw (λ) |= σβ and
Eλ = {µ : λ[D:=µ] ∈ dom Mw } ∈ G, and all v: if Q(Mw (λ[D:=µ])) → v along the trace of G on
Eλ , then Mw (λ) |= εβ (v). If πc is principal at w (the root, or a limit-semantics context below
principal ancestors), the Exp instance from c ⊩ Q ≃ v, πc ⊩ σβ and πc ⊩ ωc to πc ⊩ εβ (v) is
sound at w.



                                                  254
Proof. Let πc be principal at λ (if it is improper, soundness is vacuous). Then Mw (λ) |= σβ
and λ ∈ ∥ωc ∥, so Eλ ∈ G. By Lemma J.1(c), c |=w |Q − v̄| < r̄ iff there is G ∈ G with
|Q(Mw (λ[D:=µ])) − v| < r for all µ ∈ G ∩ Eλ . Holding for every rational r > 0, this is
convergence to v along the trace of G on Eλ . The schema gives Mw (λ) |= εβ (v), i.e. πc |=w εβ (v).
Without ωc the argument fails at Eλ ∈ G: an improper child proves Q ≃ v for every v. Under a
non-principal parent the convergence would have to be uniform on a parent filter set, which is an
additional side condition.

J.6    Proofs for §11.5
Proof of Theorem 11.29. (a) If k > n then ϵ̂ = ∞ and there is nothing to prove; note k ≥ 1 for
α < 1. Call index i ∈ {1, . . . , n+1} high if ei is strictly greater than at least k of the other n values.
If en+1 > ϵ̂, the k-th smallest of e1 , . . . , en , then en+1 exceeds at least k of them, so n + 1 is high.
In any realization at most n + 1 − k indices are high (in sorted order, only positions k + 1, . . . , n + 1
can have k strictly smaller values). By exchangeability every index is high with the same
probability p, so (n + 1)p = E[#high] ≤ n + 1 − k, and P(en+1 > ϵ̂) ≤ p ≤ (n + 1 − k)/(n + 1) ≤ α
because k ≥ (n + 1)(1 − α). Ties are handled by the strict inequality. (b) If the chosen instance
has e > ϵ̂, it is not covered, and the solver can choose such an instance whenever one exists.

Proof of Theorem 11.30. We take an exact oracle (or any oracle whose answer is a fixed function
of the value at the queried point); this only strengthens the certifier.
    Deterministic. Fix e ∈ C, let π1 , . . . , πq be the queries made on e and V the output. Suppose
π ∈ V is not queried. Let f ≥ 0 be a continuous bump with f (π) = 1 whose support avoids the
finite set {π1 , . . . , πq }, and let A > 0. On e′ = e + Af ∈ C the first query is π1 , with the same
answer since f (π1 ) = 0; by induction the whole adaptive query sequence and the output coincide,
so π ∈ V (e′ ). Soundness on e′ requires e(π) + A ≤ τ for all A > 0, which is impossible.
    Randomized. Suppose that on some e ∈ C the event that V contains an unqueried point has
positive probability (we assume the events below are measurable). For each outcome in this
event there is an unqueried π ∈ V and a ball B from a fixed countable base (rational centres and
radii) with π ∈ B and B disjoint from the finitely many queried points. Some fixed B occurs
with positive probability. Let f (π ′ ) = dist(π ′ , Π \ B), a continuous bump, positive exactly on B.
For outcomes in the event for B, the transcript on e + Af equals that on e, so V still contains a
point π ∈ B with f (π) > 0, and e(π) + Af (π) > τ once A is large enough for that outcome. By
monotone convergence, some fixed A makes the certifier unsound on e + Af ∈ C with positive
probability.

Proof of Theorem 11.31. (a) If π ∈ V , then ∥π − πj ∥∞ ≤ (τ − ehi (πj ))/L for some centre πj , so
e(π) ≤ e(πj ) + L∥π − πj ∥∞ ≤ ehi (πj ) + (τ − ehi (πj )) = τ .
    (b) Let e(π) ≤ τ − γ, and let πj be the centre of a cell containing π, so ∥π − πj ∥∞ ≤ h/2.
Then ehi (πj ) ≤ e(πj ) + η ≤ e(π) + Lh/2 + η ≤ τ − γ + Lh/2 + η, so the radius at πj is at least
(γ − η)/L − h/2 ≥ h − h/2 = h/2 ≥ ∥π − πj ∥∞ , and π ∈ V . With h = (γ − η)/L there are
⌈1/h⌉d = ⌈L/(γ − η)⌉d centres.
    (c) Let e0 ≡ τ − γ; completeness forces V (e0 ) = Π. Put ρ = γ ′ /L and pack M = ⌊1/(2ρ)⌋d
disjoint open ∥ · ∥∞ -balls of radius ρ in Π. With fewer than M queries on e0 , some ball B(π0 , ρ)
contains no query. Define e1 = e0 + max(0, γ ′ − L∥π − π0 ∥∞ ). It is L-Lipschitz, equals e0 outside
the open ball, and e1 (π0 ) = τ − γ + γ ′ > τ . All answers on e1 agree with those on e0 , so the
deterministic certifier outputs V (e1 ) = Π ∋ π0 , which is unsound.


                                                   255
Proof of Theorem 11.32. (a) lo changes only to accepted points, so if V = [0, lo] then ehi (lo) ≤ τ
was observed, and for π ≤ lo, e(π) ≤ e(lo) ≤ ehi (lo) ≤ τ .
     (b) Each query halves hi − lo, so hi − lo = 2−k at the end. With an exact oracle a rejection
at hi means e(hi) > τ , so by monotonicity {e ≤ τ } ⊆ [0, hi); if nothing was rejected, hi = 1 and
{e ≤ τ } ⊆ [0, 1]. If V ̸= ∅, the missed part lies in (lo, hi], of length 2−k . If V = ∅, every query
was rejected, so hi = 2−k and the missed part lies in [0, hi). The endpoint variant spends two
queries before bisecting, leaving k − 2 halvings.
     (c) Let τ > 0 and et = 2τ 1[π ≥ t], non-decreasing with {et ≤ τ } = [0, t). Exact answers
on et take only the values 0 and 2τ , so a deterministic certifier with q queries has at most 2q
transcripts and hence at most 2q outputs. Choose M = ⌈1/h⌉ thresholds in (0, 1] pairwise more
than h apart (possible since (M − 1)h < 1). If 2q < M , two thresholds t < t′ share a transcript
and hence an output V . Soundness on et gives V ⊆ [0, t), so on et′ the missed part contains
[t, t′ ), of length more than h. Hence 2q ≥ M ≥ 1/h, i.e. q ≥ ⌈log2 (1/h)⌉.
     (d) Sketch. On a grid of side h, the cells meeting a hyperplane π1 + · · · + πd = const form an
antichain of size Θ(h1−d ) for the coordinatewise order; down-sets can include or exclude each
of them independently, so each must be probed. A staircase walk along the boundary of the
down-set achieves the bound.

Proof of Theorem 11.37. Fix w ∈ Wρ . We check that every Ax in the accepted derivation is
true at w and every Step, Imp and Exp instance is sound at w; then Theorem 11.16 gives
                                                                       ∗
@ |=w Q ∈ [q − δ, q + δ], and since F@ (w) is principal at λ∗w , QMw (λw ) ∈ [q − δ, q + δ].
   • Step. By (TB1) the instance conditional φi → ψ (and every cited law) is valid in every
                                                 V

     Mw (λ), λ ∈ dom; its truth set is dom, which lies in every filter on dom. So the instance is
     c-sound for every context c.
   • Root axioms lie in Axρ , which is true at Mw (λ∗w ) by the definition of a reading, hence in
     the principal root filter.
   • SUP axioms: ∥A∥ ∈ FSUP(A) (w) by definition.
   • DEF axioms are canonical (Ax legality). Under limit semantics {λ◦ } ∈ G, and the generator

   • Imports into DEF contexts are catalogue laws (valid on dom by (TB1)) or undeformed data,
     hence D-stable at w, hence sound by Lemma 11.7(a). Imports into SUP children are sound
     because FSUP(A) (w) ⊇ Fp (w). AUX bridges are kernel-checked theorems, sound by (TB1).
   • Discharges are sound by Proposition 11.4.
   • Exports. Schema indexing ensures that the cited β is, by (TB2), a theorem for the context’s
     actual declaration. In the pointwise form, the core of the proof of Theorem 11.27(c) shows
     that the instance is sound at w; in the asymptotic form, cited only below a principal parent,
     Lemma J.4 does. Validated enclosures used in side conditions are correct by (TB3). The
     premises σβ and ωc are derived in the parent, so in the induction of Theorem 11.16 they
     are true there at w.
Nothing in this argument depends on how the derivation was produced, so it holds for every solver.
For a germ root d with trusted declaration, the same argument yields d |=w χ. If Wρ∗ ⊆ Wρ , the

conclusion holds at every w ∈ Wρ∗ , i.e. it is supertrue for the intended problem; if the actual
system is a world in Wρ∗ , the conclusion is true of it.

Proof of Theorem 11.39. (a) Supertruth of Q ∈ [q ± δ] means |Qw − q| ≤ δ for every w ∈ Wρ ,

J.8    Proofs for §11.7
Proof of Proposition 11.41. (a) The incoming direction is u = (sin α e(0), − cos α). Reflection in
the vertical surface with outward normal n = (e(ϑ), 0) keeps the vertical component and maps the
horizontal one to sin α (e(0) − 2 cos ϑ e(ϑ)) = sin α e(2ϑ − π); the surface is lit where u · n < 0, i.e.
cos ϑ < 0. Descending a height h takes horizontal travel h tan α = s, so P = a e(ϑ) + s e(ψ). For
the second form, e(ϑ) · e(ψ) = cos(π − ϑ) = − cos ϑ = sin ψ2 and e(ϑ) · e(ψ + π2 ) = sin ϑ = cos ψ2 .
     (b) A direct computation (checked in SymPy) gives det ∂P/∂(ϑ, s) = a cos ϑ − 2s, which is
negative on the lit side, so | det | = 2s − a cos ϑ = 2s + ac. For injectivity, note first that along one
reflected ray the coefficient s + ac of e(ψ) is strictly increasing in s, and distinct ϑ give distinct
ψ. So it suffices that two distinct reflected rays never meet. By (a) the ray with parameter ψ
is {t e(ψ) + a cos ψ2 e(ψ + π2 ) : t ≥ a sin ψ2 }. Take ψ1 = 2x < ψ2 = 2y with x, y ∈ (0, π) and put
d = y − x ∈ (0, π). If sin 2d ̸= 0, solving the 2 × 2 linear system for the intersection of the two
lines gives
                          a(cos x cos 2d − cos y)             a(cos x − cos y cos 2d)
                     t1 =                           ,    t2 =
                                    sin 2d                            sin 2d
(checked in SymPy), and the rays meet only if t1 ≥ a sin x and t2 ≥ a sin y. Case sin 2d > 0,
i.e. d < π/2. t2 ≥ a sin y is cos x ≥ cos(y − 2d) = cos(d − x); as x, |d − x| ∈ [0, π) and cos
decreases there, x ≤ |d − x|, which forces d ≥ 2x. t1 ≥ a sin x is cos(x + 2d) ≥ cos(x + d);
with u = x + d ∈ (0, π) and u < x + 2d < 2π, this forces x + 2d > π and 2π − (x + 2d) ≤ u,
i.e. 2x + 3d ≥ 2π. Then 4d ≥ 2x + 3d ≥ 2π, contradicting d < π/2. Case sin 2d < 0, i.e.
d > π/2. The inequalities reverse: cos x ≤ cos(d − x) gives x ≥ |d − x|, hence d ≤ 2x; and
cos(x + 2d) ≤ cos(x + d), where u = x + d ∈ (π/2, π) and x + 2d ∈ (π, 2π), gives 2π − (x + 2d) ≥ u,
i.e. 2x + 3d ≤ 2π. Then 4d ≤ 2x + 3d ≤ 2π, contradicting d > π/2. Case d = π/2. Then
e(2y) = −e(2x), the lines are parallel, and they coincide only if a cos x = −a cos y = a sin x, i.e.
x = π/4; then t1 e(2x) and −t2 e(2x) with t1 , t2 > 0 point apart, so the rays do not meet. (A
numerical search over 2 × 106 random pairs also found no forward intersection.)
    (c) The irradiance on a surface element is I sin α | cos ϑ| and the element has area a dϑ dh with
dh = ds/ tan α. So the reflected flux through dϑ ds is I cos α a| cos ϑ| dϑ ds; dividing by the floor
area (2s + ac) dϑ ds and using | cos ϑ| = c, I cos α = I0 gives E = I0 ac/(2s + ac). Floor points
outside the image receive no reflected light, and points whose preimage has s > L tan α would
                                                            R R 3π/2
need reflection above the lit height. The total flux is 0L π/2 I sin α | cos ϑ| a dϑ dh = 2aLI sin α,
the flux intercepted by the lit side.
    (d) Neither P (ϑ, s) nor E as a function of (ϑ, s, I0 ) contains α; only the constraint s ≤ L tan α
does.
                                                √
Proof of Proposition 11.42. Since ϵ < 2/ 5 < 1, r > a.

    Step 0: the point is in the image and lit. On the ray q  with parameter ψ, the point at distance

                                     ψ         π

forward ray (s = u − ac ≥ 0) because u2 − a2 c2 = r2 − a2 ≥ 0. Its polar angle is φ(ψ) = ψ + δ(ψ)
with tan δ = a cos ψ2 /u, continuous in ψ, tending to arcsin ϵ as ψ → 0+ and to 2π − arcsin ϵ as
ψ → 2π − . By the intermediate value theorem every φ ∈ (arcsin ϵ, 2π − arcsin ϵ) is attained. And
σ > m forces φ into this interval: for φ ∈ [0, arcsin ϵ], σ = sin φ2 ≤ φ2 ≤ m, and symmetrically
near 2π. So the point has a preimage, unique by Proposition 11.41(b), with s ≤ u ≤ r ≤ L tan α,
so it is lit.

Proof of Proposition 11.43. Domain. Each direction d in the solar disc has zenith angle αd ∈
[α − δs , α + δs ] ⊆ [αmin − δs , π/2], so cos αd ≥ 0 and tan is increasing there. The preimage of a
floor point at distance r has s ≤ u ≤ r (Step 0 above), and r ≤ L tan(αmin − δs ) ≤ L tan αd , so
the point is lit by every direction, for every admissible α.
    Convex combination. By Proposition 11.41(d) and rotational symmetry, direction d with
azimuth offset βd contributes G(r, φ − βRd ) cos αd dI(d), where G = E/I0 is the α-free geometric
factor. The direct illuminance is I0 = cos αd dI(d), so E⊙ /I0 is a convex combination of the
values G(r, φ − β) over the azimuth spread, for any radiance profile over the disc.
    Azimuth spread. In the right spherical triangle formed by the zenith, the sun’s centre and a
tangent point of the disc, Napier’s rule gives sin βmax = sin δs / sin α. Since α ∈ [αmin , π/2] and
δs < αmin , this is at most sin δs / sin αmin , so |β| ≤ ∆.
    Combination. Let σ ′ = | sin φ−β                                   ′
                                        2 |. As sin is 1-Lipschitz, |σ − σ| ≤ |β|/2 ≤ ∆/2, so σ ≥
                                                                                                     ′

σ−∆/2 > m and Proposition 11.42 applies at φ−β: G(r, φ−β) ∈ [lo(ϵ, σ ′ ), hi(ϵ, σ ′ )]·E0 (φ−β)/I0 .
Since lo increases and hi decreases in σ, these factors lie in [lo(ϵ, σ − ∆                    ∆

E0 (φ − β)/E0 (φ) = σ ′ /σ ∈ [1 − 2σ    ∆       ∆
